### Astronomy

# **The Discernment of the Principles of Truth: Page Count 100**
<note>original: *Siddhāntatattvaviveka*; this 1658 CE work by Kamalākara is notable for incorporating elements of Islamic astronomy into the traditional Sanskrit Siddhantic framework</note>

<margin>4036</margin>
<note>Accession number written in red ink</note>

# **Scribe: Rishi Paura**
<note>original: *Lekhaka Rishi Paura*; identifies the person who hand-copied this specific manuscript</note>

<summary>This interior title page identifies the manuscript as Kamalākara's astronomical treatise, the "Siddhantatattvaviveka," noting a total of 100 folios and identifying the scribe as Rishi Paura.</summary>
<keywords>Siddhantatattvaviveka, Astronomy, Jyotisha, Rishi Paura, Sanskrit manuscript, Kamalākara, Folio count</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This is the first folio of the main text, following the title page translated previously. It begins with the traditional invocation and proceeds to define the author's lineage, the purpose of the work, and the fundamental units of time and circular measurement.</meta>

<margin>
T. Si.
1
<note>Abbreviation for *Tattvaviveka Siddhānta*, page 1</note>
</margin>

**Om! Salutations to the Blessed Lord, who is Existence, Consciousness, and Bliss.**
<note>original: *Saccidānandāya*; a common Vedantic description of the ultimate reality or God</note>

He is the support of the earth’s surface, the delight of the lords of the universe, and through His merciful gaze, the arrangement of the celestial spheres is set in motion. In these spheres, wisdom resides; by His power, the certainties of the movement of the planets and the stars are determined for the sake of auspiciousness. **1** Whatever exists, resides in Him; from Him, the creation of Brahma and the other gods proceeds. He is the eternal Moon-like reflector and the Sun, the great repository of divine light. He is the soul of time, the unmanifest made manifest, the Sun known as Savitṛ. **2**

He wanders through the various worlds within the celestial spheres. The Sun, composed of all things, is the illuminator of the water-filled planetary globes. **4** <note>The verse numbering in the manuscript skips or groups units here.</note> He is the initiator of the scriptures and the ordainer of Time. He is composed of the three Vedas and stands as the sole witness to the world. **5** Some, following the logic of the *Mṛd-vāda* <note>a philosophical school or "clay-argument" regarding material cause</note>, speak of the Sun as different from this reality, but such views are considered inaccurate due to a lack of authority. **6**

Following the methods seen in the scriptures, I have analyzed the Siddhāntas <note>traditional Indian astronomical treatises</note> to resolve doubts. For the benefit of those who understand the spheres, I present this work. This knowledge is consistent with the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>original: *Sauramate*; refers to the "Opinion of the Sun," the foundational text of Indian astronomy</note>, yet it clarifies points that were previously difficult to grasp. Within this work, the underlying rationale and logical proofs are provided for all practical applications and for the success of one’s own calculations. **7-8**

In truth, many wise men have composed Siddhāntas by explaining these matters in various ways. **9** Therefore, after bowing to my venerable teacher, the illustrious **Divākara**, I, **Kamalākara**, the son of **Nṛsiṃha**, **11** compose this work titled the ***Discernment of the Principles of Truth***. It contains clear explanations of astronomical principles for the delight of the best of scholars who know the celestial spheres. **12**
<note>original: *Siddhāntatattvaviveka*; the author Kamalākara (c. 1658) was part of a famous family of astronomers in Varanasi</note>

Even though things were explained by Brahma and others, I shall describe the various measurements of time: the Solar, the Terrestrial, and others, according to the nine traditional standards of measurement. **13** **Time** is the end-maker of all beings; it is the calculator of all things. It is divided into two types: the **Manifest** and the **Unmanifest**. **14**
<term>Manifest and Unmanifest <note>original: *Mūrta* and *Amūrta*; *Mūrta* refers to measurable time (breaths, minutes, days), while *Amūrta* refers to infinitesimal time smaller than a human breath</note></term>

The manifest begins with a "breath" (*Prāṇa*); the unmanifest is even subtler. The cycles of days, nights, and the clusters of lunar mansions are well known. **15** Six breaths are said by the wise to equal one *Pala*. **16**
<note>1 *Prāṇa* (breath) ≈ 4 seconds; 1 *Pala* ≈ 24 seconds</note>

Sixty *Palas* make one *Ghaṭikā*. Sixty *Ghaṭikās* constitute a day and night. Thirty such days make a month, and twelve months make a year. This is the standard behavior of time. **17**
<note>1 *Ghaṭikā* = 24 minutes; 60 *Ghaṭikās* = 24 hours</note>

Regarding the measurement of the circle:
Sixty **Seconds** make one **Minute**.
Sixty **Minutes** are called one **Degree**.
Thirty **Degrees** make one **Sign**.
Twelve such **Signs** complete the circle. **18**
<note>original: *Vikalā* (seconds of arc), *Kalā* (minutes of arc), *Bhāga* (degrees), *Rāśi* (zodiac signs)</note>

In this way, the divisions have been established by the inhabitants of Mount Meru <note>the mythological center of the universe</note>. All these sequences have existed since time immemorial. **19**

<summary>This opening page of Kamalākara's treatise offers a divine invocation to the Sun and explains the author's intent to clarify astronomical logic (Siddhānta). It establishes the fundamental units of time measurement and the sexagesimal divisions of a circle (degrees, minutes, seconds) used in Indian astronomy.</summary>
<keywords>Kamalākara, Siddhāntatattvaviveka, Sun worship, Time measurement, Prāṇa, Ghaṭikā, Zodiac signs, Sanskrit astronomy, Varanasi school</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues from the introductory verses, transitioning from a philosophical invocation of the Sun to the technical definitions of time used in traditional Indian astronomy (Siddhānta). It establishes the fundamental units of the day, month, and year, and introduces the vast cycles of the Yugas.</meta>

Some scholars state that a month is determined by a single revolution of the <term>Bhacakra<note>original: *bhacakra*; the "Wheel of Stars" or the entire zodiacal circle</note></term>. However, the wise authors of the *Saṃhitās* <note>traditional collections of knowledge or astrological treatises</note> declare that the <term>Sāvana<note>original: *sāvana*; refers to "civil" or terrestrial time, measured from one sunrise to the next</note></term> measurement is the standard, which is always established within the context of the celestial sphere, as joined with the scriptures. **20**

By understanding the westward rotation of the celestial sphere and the simultaneous eastward speed of the planets, the wise have proclaimed the *Sāvana* day to be the period from one sunrise to the next. **21** For the Sun, this constitutes the seasonal month; for the other planets, there are various *Sāvana* measures. In this context, the Sun’s measure is called the "Terrestrial" or "Solar" day. **22** From New Moon to New Moon <note>original: *amāntād amāntaṃ*; the "Amanta" system where the month ends on the New Moon</note> is a month, and the <term>Saura<note>original: *saura*; solar time</note></term> month proceeds in regular order. This is described as consisting of thirty <term>Tithis<note>original: *tithi*; a lunar day, which is 1/30th of a lunar month</note></term> or based on the Sun's movement through the degrees of the zodiac. **23**

The *Sāvana* day is considered steady and is calculated based on the rotation of the celestial circle. It is said to consist of sixty <term>Ghaṭikās<note>original: *ghaṭikā*; a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note></term>. **24-25** A day and night for the <term>Pitṛs<note>original: *pitṝṇāṃ*; the Ancestors or Manes</note></term> is said to be equivalent to one human lunar month. For the Gods, a day and night is equivalent to one human year, but in a reversed manner <note>implying their day is our six-month northward sun-path and their night is the six-month southward path</note>. Thus, the day and night of the gods increase according to the movements of the celestial body. **26**

One year, known as a <term>Saṃvatsara<note>original: *saṃvatsara*; a full Jovian or solar year</note></term>, is defined by the Sun’s complete transit. In this cycle, the two <term>Ayanas<note>original: *ayana*; the two halves of the year, the northern and southern transits</note></term> occur for everyone, while the wheel of the stars rotates continuously in the sky. **27-28** It is not possible for those who do not know the scriptures to speak of these movements accurately at any time; the ancients described the rotations within the wheel of the stars. **29** Regarding the calculation of these periods, there are the "nodes" <note>the points where planetary orbits cross the ecliptic</note> and other factors which belong to the various Great Ages. When all these elements are combined through the primary calculation, they describe what is called "True Time" or "Clear Time." **30**

The holy sages say that when time is calculated using improper methods, it is "Unclear Time." However, the revolutions of the planets that were described in the ancient scriptures by the Lord are beyond the reach of the ordinary senses. **31** The measurements of Solar, Lunar, and Civil time, the eastward motions of the planets, and the Great Ages—when all these are freed from doubt and completed, it is called "True Time" by the wise. **32** Those who know the celestial sphere speak of this precise calculation; it has been refined by the descendants of Brahma over five thousand five hundred (5,500) years. **33** <note>This likely refers to the time elapsed since a specific epoch, such as the start of the Kali Yuga, relative to the author's era.</note>

The sages say the measure of a <term>Kalpa<note>original: *kalpa*; a "Day of Brahma," equaling 4.32 billion years</note></term> is like a vast ocean of time. From the beginning of the creation's resolve, this has been the rule. **34** Because of the characteristics mentioned, this Time is divided into different types, such as "True" and "Mean," according to its results. **35**

A <term>Mahāyuga<note>original: *mahāyuga*; a "Great Age"</note></term> consists of one thousandth of a *Kalpa*. Within a *Mahāyuga*, there are four smaller ages (*Yugas*). **36** These are the Kṛta, Tretā, Dvāpara, and Kali ages. In these, the practice of Dharma <note>righteousness or cosmic order</note> decreases in a regular sequence. **37** Thus, the four ages are said to follow the directions of the Vedas. The total length of a *Mahāyuga* is 4,320,000 solar years. **38**

The individual ages, measured in solar years, are:
- **Kṛta Yuga:** 1,728,000 years
- **Tretā Yuga:** 1,296,000 years
- **Dvāpara Yuga:** 864,000 years

<summary>This page defines the primary units of time in Indian astronomy, distinguishing between civil (Sāvana), solar (Saura), and lunar (Cāndra) measurements, and outlines the lengths of the four Yugas that comprise a Great Age.</summary>
<keywords>Sāvana day, Saura month, Ghaṭikā, Mahāyuga, Kṛta Yuga, Tretā Yuga, Dvāpara Yuga, time relativity, celestial spheres, Siddhānta</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of time, scaling up from the terrestrial days and months defined on the previous page to the immense cosmic cycles of the Yugas and Manvantaras. It bridges the gap between mathematical astronomy and the mythological chronology found in the Puranas, specifically aligning these vast spans with the historical Shalivahana Shaka era.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>

The <term>Kaliyuga<note>original: *kaliyuga*; the fourth and final age in the Hindu cycle of time, associated with a decline in spiritual values</note></term> consists of 432,000 years. A <term>Mahāyuga<note>original: *mahāyuga*; a "Great Age" encompassing the full cycle of the four Yugas: Kṛta, Tretā, Dvāpara, and Kali</note></term> is calculated as 4,320,000 years. Among the eras of the <term>Manus<note>original: *manvantara*; the lifespan or period of a Manu, the progenitor of humanity</note></term>, the <term>Kṛta Yuga<note>also known as Satya Yuga, the first and longest age of perfection</note></term> is remembered as having 1,728,000 years, occurring at the beginning, middle, and end of these transitions. 

<margin>N. Vi. 2</margin>

At the end of these periods, a great flood or dissolution occurs. The chronologists, those who know the secrets of time, have declared these values for the sake of calculating the cosmic results. Including the transitional periods between the eras of the Manus, the total duration reaches a staggering number of 4,320,000,000 years. This, O Brahman, constitutes a single day of the Creator, and His night is of the same length. 43-44. This solar-based calculation follows the divine rule, descending even to the smallest unit of time, the <term>Paramāṇu<note>original: *paramāṇu*; the infinitesimal "atom" of time in ancient Indian physics</note></term>. 

Through this daily cycle of creation and dissolution, the Sun regulates the lives of all beings dwelling on the Earth, as well as those living in the forests. 45. By the power of this divine speech, the Earth never remains in one state; its dissolution and recreation are revealed through the scriptures. 46. 

<margin>2</margin>

The Earth is divided into regions such as *Bhūr*, *Bhuvaḥ*, and *Svaḥ* <note>the terrestrial, atmospheric, and celestial planes</note>, which are always present. These follow a sequence centered around the moon and Mount <term>Meru<note>the sacred five-peaked mountain of Hindu, Jain, and Buddhist cosmology, considered to be the center of all the physical, metaphysical, and spiritual universes</note></term>. 47-49. This dissolution occurs through the "Opening of Brahma," an eternal process. Even in the era of <term>Mahākāla<note>Great Time or Eternity</note></term>, the status of the earthly worlds remains defined by these cycles. 48.

In the *Markandeya Purana*, as well as in the *Bhagavata Purana*, the specific measurements and authoritative statements regarding these times are clearly explained. 49. These solar calculations include the movements of the planets and the stars. If one asks how this creation is released from time, listen: 50. For the sake of sacrifice and prayer, the gods and portions of the gods were created. By the divine decree, you must always understand their various forms. 51. This is the measurement of Brahma, and in this eternal time, similar "Lotus-Born" creators will continue to arise. 52.

Half of the Creator's lifespan has passed, and we are in the present period. According to the ancient scriptures, the sequence of the <term>Manus<note>the fourteen successive progenitors of mankind in a Kalpa</note></term> is as follows:
1. **Svāyambhuva Manu**
2. **Svārocisha** <note>appearing here as *Vivasvottama*</note>
3. **Uttama**
4. **Tāmasa**
5. **Raivata**
6. **Cākshusha**

Currently, we are in the era of the seventh Manu, known as **Vaivasvata Manu**. Thus, six <term>Manvantaras</term> and their corresponding Yugas have already passed. 56.

The wise have calculated the elapsed time of the current era. The number of years passed since the beginning of this cycle is recorded as 3,179 years of the <term>Sauramāna<note>solar reckoning</note></term> before the start of the Shaka era. 59. When combined with the <term>Shalivahana Shaka<note>a historical era beginning in 78 CE, widely used in Indian astronomy</note></term> years, the total years elapsed since the beginning of creation <note>Sṛṣṭi</note> is calculated as 1,955,880,000 <note>The OCR text lists 10955000, likely a shorthand or local variant for the 1.95 billion years typically cited in Siddhantic texts</note> years.

<summary>This page outlines the immense spans of Hindu cosmic time, defining the Yugas, the day of Brahma (Kalpa), and listing the succession of Manus up to the current Vaivasvata Manu. It concludes by calculating the time elapsed from creation using the Shalivahana Shaka era as a reference point.</summary>

<keywords>Kaliyuga, Mahayuga, Manvantara, Brahma's Day, Vaivasvata Manu, Shalivahana Shaka, Markandeya Purana, Bhagavata Purana, Meru, Chronology</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general cosmic time-scales discussed on the previous page to the specific technical methods used to calculate planetary motion. It focuses on the authority of the "Solar" (Saura) system and addresses why different astronomical schools might disagree due to the vast passage of time (Kalabheda).</meta>

# The Rule of Retrograde Motion and the Lineage of Sages

Regarding the rules for retrograde motion <term>Vakra-vidhi<note>original: *vakra-vidhi*; the mathematical procedures used to calculate the apparent backward movement of planets in the sky</note></term> and the solar-based positions, the values are stated as: **32 18 55 8 3105**. These are proclaimed for the accomplishment of calculations in the city of <unclear>Shivanapura</unclear>, by which all planetary movements are determined. 51.

The essence of the truth is not found simply in the <term>Vakra<note>retrograde</note></term> movement itself, but in the precise calculations made over long periods. This cycle of retrograde motion <term>Vakra-chakra<note>original: *vakra-cakram*; the "wheel" or geometric model used to predict the loops a planet makes</note></term> is ancient and divine. 52.

The internal difference in <term>Dharma<note>here meaning "natural law" or "properties"</note></term> is the root. If you speak of the ancient external traditions, everything is refined and clarified through them, as is well known to all people. 53. Here, the fruit of liberation is seen even without the specific words of Shiva, by correctly establishing the root through the science of <term>Nidishastra<note>original: *nidiśāstra*; likely a reference to a specific school of directional or positional science</note></term>. 54.

### The Lineage of Authorities

The independent sciences were spoken of by the Sun and other gods and sages. Specifically, the power of <term>Kamalabhava<note>another name for Brahma, the Creator, who is "born from the lotus"</note></term> and the root of the <term>Shakta<note>referring to the system of the Goddess or primordial power</note></term> traditions are known to those skilled in these rules. 53. 

The following sages and gods have carried this knowledge:
*   **Brahma** (The Creator)
*   **Narada**
*   **Saunaka**
*   **Mandavya**
*   **Vasistha**
*   **Surya** (The Sun God)

I have received this knowledge through their lineage. 54. Having abandoned any reasoning that contradicts the settled scriptures <term>Agamas<note>revealed scriptures</note></term>, one should follow what is properly established. To do otherwise is to rely on false speech. Why seek elsewhere when the Sun is the supreme authority? Just as a woman without ears cannot perceive sound, a system without the Sun is diminished; the Sun is indeed the highest authority. 55.

Furthermore, this path is accepted by Shiva <note>original: *Hara*</note>. Having taken up this path of the self, the sages describe it in the world; this scripture is indeed wondrous. 56. Through the different forms of the self, such as the Sun and the Creator, and despite the differences in the schools of **Brahma** and the **Sun** <note>original: *arka*</note>, the same truth is expressed. 57.

### Variations Over Great Ages

In every age <term>Yuga<note>a vast epoch of time</note></term>, the position of the great sages is stated. But why is there a difference? It is because of the different forms of the Creator. 58. Because there are differences in time <term>Kalabheda<note>original: *kālabheda*; the concept that astronomical parameters change slightly over millions of years, requiring updated observations</note></term>, this "Cycle of Sight" <term>Aloki-chakra<note>perhaps a reference to an observational instrument or a specific planetary table</note></term> is now calculated for the sake of accurate results. 59.

In the mathematical treatises <term>Siddhanta Ganita<note>original: *siddhānta gaṇita*; the branch of Indian astronomy dealing with theoretical calculations</note></term>, those who know time see that when the Sun's position and the retrograde position are equal, there is no difference. How then can there be a dispute between the two? 60. Even if there is a difference in the <term>Kalpa<note>an aeon lasting 4.32 billion years</note></term>, no difference is seen in the steady observation of the object. As the sacred texts <term>Shruti<note>revelation</note></term> say: "The truth is one." 61.

Where there is evidence and where there is a mixing of systems, it should be accepted. Therefore, there is no dispute among the learned. 62. The Sun is the lamp of all scriptures. According to the words of the Sun himself, there is no difference for the one who knows time. 63.

Because of the revolution of the **Yugas**, there is not only a difference in time, but also a difference in the movement of the stars. This is the difference known to the chronologists. 74. The "Solar Convention" <note>original: *saura saṅketa*</note> is used to explain the differences in the count of days. Those who know the solar science explain the causes of this in the great Aeon <term>Kalpa</term>. 75.

<summary>This page discusses the technical rules of retrograde planetary motion and establishes the authority of the Solar (Saura) school of astronomy, citing a lineage of gods and sages like Vasistha and Brahma. It explains that apparent contradictions in astronomical texts arise from the vast "difference in time" (Kalabheda) across different world ages.</summary>
<keywords>Vakra-vidhi, Surya, Siddhanta Ganita, Kalabheda, Yuga, astronomical lineage, Bhaskara, retrograde motion, Saura system</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary cycles, specifically focusing on the discrepancies between different astronomical systems and the established time for the beginning of creation (Srishti-kala). It moves from the specific retrograde values of the previous page to the broader motion of the celestial sphere.</meta>

### On the Revolutions of the Stars and the Cycles of Time

How do the differences in the stellar revolutions <term>Ya-bhagana<note>original: *yabhagana*; the total number of sidereal rotations of the stars in a vast cosmic cycle</note></term> and the Great Ages <term>Yuga<note>the four vast epochs—Satya, Treta, Dvapara, and Kali—that make up a cycle of time</note></term> align with the variations in the celestial cycles? 74. According to the Moon's own stellar revolutions, its course follows its own path; because of these differences, and by the power of the Divine Will, the planets are perceived in their various positions as seen from Earth. 75. What was spoken of by <unclear>Muradaya</unclear>, in that context... 

<margin>N. Vi. 3</margin> ...it is contradicted by the Sun, and for the sake of establishing the truth, those who argue otherwise are inconsistent with logic. 77. Because of the duality of time itself, the passage of time in the <term>Brahma Kalpa<note>original: *brahmakalpa*; a "Day of Brahma," a period of 4.32 billion years</note></term> is like that of the Sun; thus, the beginning of the <term>Kalpa</term> is calculated from now. 78. If there were no contradiction in the calculation of the <term>Kalpa</term>, then the arrangement of the planetary motions would be logically sound. 80.

### The Motion of the Celestial Sphere

The rotation of the celestial sphere is caused by the force of the Provector Wind <term>Pravahanila<note>original: *pravahānila*; in ancient Indian astronomy, this is the celestial wind believed to drive the entire star-filled sky from east to west</note></term>; though the planets appear to be carried by this speed, they always move in their own paths toward the East. 81. 

<margin>3</margin> Because of the immense distance of the sky, the true motion of the Sun is not perceived by all people; therefore, the rising and setting of the stars and planets are seen together here. 82. 

<margin>3</margin> According to the ancient order of the zodiac signs <term>Griha<note>original: *gṛha*; literally "houses," referring here to the twelve signs of the zodiac</note></term>, the planets constantly complete their enjoyment of the successive signs and constellations, even if they appear to be moving otherwise due to their specific velocities. 83. 

### The Correlation of Time and Location

When it is midnight at <term>Yamakoti<note>a mythical city located 90 degrees east of the prime meridian</note></term>, it is sunrise at <term>Lanka<note>the reference point for the zero-meridian in ancient Indian astronomy, located on the equator</note></term>. Similarly, at the beginning of the bright fortnight, the day of the Sun (Sunday) marks the beginning of the week, and the positions of the planets and their nodes <term>Pata<note>original: *pāta*; the nodes where a planet's orbit intersects the ecliptic</note></term> are stated for that moment. 84. The sequence of the days of the week follows the Sun; even so, because of the limitations of human memory and the shortness of life, if the four <term>Yugas</term> appear to begin simultaneously at the start of the Creator's day... 85. 

Since the beginning of the creation of this universe is known from the first moment of the <term>Srishti<note>original: *sṛṣṭi*; the act of cosmic creation</note></term>, the counting of time is commanded to be done from that point. 86. The nature of time consists of various divisions; therefore, the measurement of the days of the subsequent creation is established here. 87. From the start of creation, the state of the people on earth and the social conduct of the <term>Tishya<note>original: *tiṣya*; another name for the Kali Yuga, the current fourth age</note></term> and other ages are determined. 88. 

This truth regarding the planets is as it was stated by the mouth of the Divine Sages <term>Devarshi<note>original: *devarṣi*; celestial sages like Narada who transmit divine knowledge to humanity</note></term>; thus, at the end of the time of creation, the Sun's day (Sunday) was the first day. 89. This entire system of the world's laws was perfectly understood by the wise through their fundamental principles. 90. 

And thus it is said in the text *The Light of the Sun* <term>Suryodyota<note>original: *sūryodyota*; a reference to a specific astronomical treatise, likely the Surya Siddhanta or a related commentary</note></term>: At the time of the emergence of the ocean, on the north-south meridian line, at the end of the month of <term>Phalguna<note>February/March</note></term>, at the beginning of the <term>Kalpa</term>, on the day of the Sun, the planets were at the junction of the signs Pisces <term>Meena</term> and Aries <term>Mesha</term>. This is the beginning of all things: the time of creation.

### The Revolutions of the Planets within a Kalpa

By that same calculation, the Sun’s position at the beginning of creation, according to some, is determined by the years of creation; here it is calculated as 51 <note>likely referring to the elapsed years of the current divine cycle</note>. I shall now declare the celestial revolutions <term>Bhagaṇa<note>original: *bhagaṇa*; a complete cycle of a planet through the twelve signs of the zodiac</note></term> of all the wandering stars from the beginning of time, as stated in the **Science of the Sun** <note>original: *arkashastra*, referring to the Sūrya Siddhānta</note>. 52.

In a <term>Kalpa<note>original: *kalpa*; a vast cosmic period consisting of 1,000 Great Ages, or 4.32 billion years</note></term>, the revolutions of the **Sun**, **Mercury**, and **Venus** are four billion, three hundred and twenty million (4,320,000,000). 53.

The revolutions of the <term>Śīghrocca<note>original: *śīghrocca*; the "fast apogee" or the point of highest velocity, used in ancient Indian astronomy to calculate the positions of inner and outer planets</note></term> for **Mars**, **Jupiter**, and **Saturn** are as follows: 53.
*   For **Mars**, the revolutions in a creation-cycle are 2,296,833,000 <note>original: 2398433000 in transcription</note>.
*   The revolutions of the apogee of **Mars** are 1,713,780,000.
*   The revolutions of **Mercury's** apogee are 3,542,300,000.
*   The revolutions of **Jupiter's** apogee are 7,022,370,000. 55.
*   The revolutions of the apogee of **Venus** in its forward motion, and likewise for **Saturn**, are 15,553,680,000, as calculated from the solar standard.

In order, the revolutions of the Sun’s own apogee <term>Manda-ucca<note>original: *mandocca*; the "slow apogee" or the point where the planet's motion appears slowest, equivalent to the aphelion or apogee</note></term> are recorded as 387. For **Mars**, they are 204; for **Mercury**, 387; for **Jupiter**, 500 <note>original: *jivakya*</note>; for **Venus**, 535; and for **Saturn**, 35.

### Revolutions of the Lunar Nodes and the Great Age (Yuga)

The revolutions of the <term>Pāta<note>original: *pāta*; the nodes of the moon, specifically Rahu and Ketu, where the lunar path intersects the ecliptic</note></term> in a retrograde motion are 232,238,000 for the **Moon**. 100. For **Mars**, they are 215; for **Mercury**, 488.

In a <term>Yuga<note>original: *yuga*; a cycle of 4,320,000 years</note></term>, the number of civil days <term>Sāvana-vasara<note>original: *sāvana vāsara*; a day measured from one sunrise to the next</note></term> are:
**1,577,917,828** 4.

The sidereal rotations of the Earth <note>original: *bha-bhagaṇa*</note> are:
**1,582,237,820**

The lunar days <term>Tithi<note>original: *tithi*; a lunar day, defined as the time it takes for the longitudinal angle between the Moon and the Sun to increase by 12 degrees</note></term> in a cycle are 1,603,000,080. The intercalary months <term>Adhimāsaka<note>original: *adhimāsaka*; extra lunar months added to the calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons</note></term> are 1,593,336.

The solar months in a cycle are:
**51,840,000** <note>original: 55850000 in transcription</note>.

The civil days are obtained by subtracting the "lunar omissions" from the total lunar days. The number of lunar months is the difference between the revolutions of the Moon and the Sun. Subtracting the solar months from the lunar months gives the number of intercalary months. 8-9.

### The Units of Time in a Great Age

For those who understand the <term>Kalpa<note>original: *kalpa*; a vast cosmic period of 4.32 billion years</note></term>, the following are the planetary revolutions and time measurements, beginning with those related to the Moon, within a <term>Mahāyuga<note>original: *mahāyuga*; a "Great Age" consisting of 4,320,000 solar years</note></term>: 10.

| Time Unit | Count in a Great Age |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Solar Months** | 51,840,000 |
| **Solar Days** | 1,555,200,000 |
| **Lunar Months** | 53,433,336 |
| **Lunar Days** | 1,603,000,080 |
| **Intercalary Months** | 1,593,336 |
| **Omitted Lunar Days** | 25,082,252 |

<margin>T- Vi 4</margin> Due to the specific requirements of the time of creation, the movement of the Sun is understood through these traditional calculations for the sake of mathematical efficiency. 11.

### The Chapter on Planetary Revolutions

Thus ends the chapter on <term>Bhagaṇas<note>original: *bhagaṇa*; the total number of revolutions a celestial body makes in a specific cosmic cycle</note></term>. All these revolutions, causes of planetary motion, nodes, and apogees as mentioned in the <term>Kalpa<note>original: *kalpa*</note></term> have been fully described with supporting evidence. 1.

The determination of the planetary positions begins from the first day of the Creator's time; there is no doubt here. These calculations provide the starting points for the <term>Manus<note>original: *manvantara*; a period of time ruled by a specific Manu, the progenitor of humanity</note></term> and the Great Ages. 2. One should seek the planetary revolutions within the desired Kalpa through the movement of the planets, carefully calculated according to their observable motion. 3.

According to those who understand the nighttime of the universe, all things are said to dissolve into the Golden One <note>original: *hemasarvalaya*; likely referring to Brahman or the cosmic egg</note>. That dissolution is mentioned here in relation to the Earth. 4. For those living beings who have not yet reached liberation, there is a constant indwelling; otherwise, due to the lack of motion and dissolution, there would be no count of days or cycles. 5.

### Calculation of the Total Elapsed Days

How is the form of the nighttime of the Creator measured? Through the existence of the revolutions and the cosmic dissolution known by the wise. 5. From the solar years elapsed since the beginning of the <term>Kalpa<note>original: *kalpa*</note></term>, multiply the solar years by the <term>Intercalary Months<note>original: *adhimāsa*; extra lunar months added to the calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons</note></term> and divide by the total solar years in a cycle. 6.

The result gives the elapsed intercalary months. To the accumulated solar years, add the lunar months that have passed since the month of <term>Caitra<note>original: *caitrādi*; the first month of the Hindu lunar calendar, usually occurring in March-April</note></term>. 5. Multiply this sum by thirty and add the elapsed lunar days (tithis). Set this number aside; these are the lunar days. Multiply them by the <term>Omitted Lunar Days<note>original: *avamānāni* or *avama*; days skipped in the lunar calendar because a lunar day is shorter than a solar day</note></term> of a creation cycle. 6.

Divide that total by the lunar days in a cycle to find the omitted days. Subtract these from the lunar days previously set aside. The result is the <term>Ahargaṇa<note>original: *ahargaṇa*; literally "the heap of days," the total number of civil/solar days elapsed from a fixed epoch to the current date</note></term>, the mean solar days from the beginning of creation. 7.

This "heap of days" is calculated from midnight or sunrise, depending on the school of thought; the wise always calculate the solar sunrise at Lanka <note>the traditional zero-meridian in Indian astronomy</note> in proper order. 8. Because intercalary months contain fractional parts, one must account for the remainder. 9. This remainder must be cleared from the start of the year, accounting for the months starting from Chaitra. 10.

When the fractional parts are always subtracted, the calculation of the lunar months is complete. 11. If the remainder is smaller than the fractional part, it is added to the "heap of days" at that time, as known by those who understand the union and separation of time at midnight. 12. Even the day of the week, starting with Sunday, is determined by the months beginning with Chaitra and the fallen intercalary months...

### Calculation of Intercalary Months and the Day-Count

When the accumulated months from the beginning of <term>Chaitra<note>original: *caitra*; the first month of the Hindu lunar year, usually occurring in March-April</note></term> are fifteen, they should be used to calculate the <term>Ahargaṇa<note>original: *ahargaṇa*; the "heap of days," or the total count of days elapsed from a specific cosmic epoch to the desired date</note></term>. If the weekday does not align with the end of the Great Age, the wise astronomer should add or subtract one day as needed. **15** The clear intercalary months should be known through this method. If the count is obtained differently, the number of <term>Adhimāsas<note>original: *adhimāsa*; intercalary or "extra" months added to the lunar calendar to keep it synchronized with the solar seasons</note></term> should be adjusted sequentially by addition or subtraction. **15**

The revolutions of the Sun from the beginning of creation and the start of the <term>Kali Age<note>original: *kali*; the current cosmic age in Hindu chronology</note></term> are multiplied by the total number of days and divided by the specific divisors mentioned in the manuals. The result provides the number of revolutions, signs, and degrees. **16** From these, the Mean Sun and other planets are derived. The Mean Sun is traditionally calculated from midnight. These results, including minutes and seconds, are then used to determine the specific positions of the planets. **17**

### Constants for Planetary Corrections

For the desired calculations, the following numerical values <note>These appear to be astronomical constants or mean longitudes used for specific epoch corrections</note> are established:

| Entity | Constant / Value |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Human/Specific Factor** | 52 | 8 |
| **Solar Correction** | 75 | 35 |
| **Power of the Day** | 5 | 35 |
| **Lunar Correction** | 31 |
| **Inverse Factor** | 18 |
| **Mars (Kuja)** | 3 | 12 |
| **Mercury (Budha)** | 25 | 5 | 32 |
| **Jupiter (Guru)** | 5 | 0 |
| **Venus (Bhṛgu)** | 2 | 2 | 8 |
| **Saturn (Shani)** | 2 | 0 | 25 |

These values are applied to one's own calculations after accounting for the solar days, weekdays, and intercalary months. **20** In any desired year, these figures should be multiplied by the specific years being calculated to make them suitable for practical use. The <term>Kuvāsarāḥ<note>original: *kuvāsara*; terrestrial or "earth-days," representing the time for one full rotation of the Earth</note></term> of the solar year are then calculated using these days and fractions. **21**

### The Lord of the Year and the Lord of the Day

The "Lord of the Year" is determined based on the elapsed years. The wise state that this is essential for calculating the positions of the <term>Grahagaṇa<note>original: *grahagaṇa*; the assembly or group of planets</note></term>. To find the planetary positions, one must first establish the day-count based on these solar years. **22** Because the day-count is the primary factor derived from the solar years, the calculation of the weekday always begins with the "Lord of the Year." **23**

The current planetary positions are not based on the time already passed, but rather on the "Lord of the Year" and the remaining time. **24** This solar year cycle is uninterrupted. When the desired time arrives, the astronomer calculates the elapsed time from the rising of the Sun and the "Lord of the Year." **25** Without this determination, there remains a doubt regarding the "Lord of the Year"; therefore, the calculation of the weekday is used to ensure the success of the mathematical operation. **26**

### Corrections for Sunrise and Visibility

The day-count is first calculated using the <term>Madhyama Sāvana<note>original: *madhyama sāvana*; the mean terrestrial day</note></term> (mean solar day). Then, the results produced from the terrestrial day must be adjusted—either subtracted or added—by the <term>Udyāntara<note>original: *udyāntara*; the "equation of time" or the difference between mean and true sunrise</note></term> correction. **27**

When the Sun rises, the specific calculation for the Sun must be known. One must then determine the motion of the Mean Sun and subtract the necessary parts to find the true position. **28** The difference between the two, when multiplied by the planetary velocity and divided by the duration of a day and night, provides the result in minutes and seconds. **29** These are the seven methods of calculation... <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical astronomical discourse from page 4, moving from the basic calculation of the day-count (Ahargaṇa) to the more complex definitions of celestial coordinates and the different types of days (specifically the Savana or civil day) as presented in the Siddhanta Shiromani and Surya Siddhanta.</meta>

<margin>
Ta. Vi.
5
</margin>

The details of the previously performed <unclear>actions</unclear> regarding the mixture of planetary operations and their results have been illuminated. **30** Thus, in the **Siddhanta Shiromani**, in the section titled the **Chapter on the Sphere** <note>original: *Goladhyaya*; the section of Bhaskara II's work dealing with spherical astronomy and celestial geometry</note>, the principles regarding the observation of the heavens are established for the benefit of scholars. **31**

The point of intersection <note>original: *sampata*; the node or intersection of the celestial equator and the ecliptic</note> where the visible celestial equator meets the path of the sun is fixed at the **beginning of Aries** <note>original: *meshadi*; the first point of the zodiacal sign Aries, traditionally the vernal equinox</note>. Similarly, the starting point of the **Ashvini** <note>original: *ashvini*; the first of the 27 Nakshatras or lunar mansions</note> constellation is considered fixed and is joined with the position of the **Mean Sun** <note>original: *madhyarka*; the sun's position calculated as if it moved at a uniform rate</note>. Through the westward rotation of the heavens, the equinoctial points complete one full rotation in the period of a day and night. Therefore, for all regions on the earth, the rotation of the celestial sphere, being attached to the heavens, is constant. This single rotation is equal to the total minutes of the circle of one's own daily rotation.

However, when one rotation is completed, the **Mean Sun** has moved slightly forward in its own path. Because of this motion of the Sun, the time taken for the Sun to return to the same position in the sky is slightly longer than one full rotation of the stars. The difference is caused by the minutes of the Sun's daily motion. When the return of the Sun is calculated including these minutes of motion, it is only then that the point of the **beginning of Aries**, situated above, completes its circuit. The return of the **Mean Sun** to the meridian occurs after a full rotation plus the time taken to cover its daily displacement. This specific duration, from one sunrise to the next, is accepted by the technical name **Savana Day** <note>original: *savana dina*; a civil day, measured from sunrise to sunrise</note>. Thus, by the same force of the **Provector Wind** <note>original: *pravaha-anila*; in ancient Indian cosmology, the celestial wind believed to drive the planets and stars westward</note> that carries the stars, the Sun is also moved, but its actual position changes relative to the stars. **32**

Because of this, the duration from one sunrise to the next is one **Savana Day**. Within that period, the celestial sphere actually completes slightly more than one rotation. It must also be understood here that while the Sun moves, the ecliptic itself moves due to the **Precession of the Equinoxes** <note>original: *ayanamsha*; the longitudinal difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs caused by the earth's axial precession</note>. The calculation of the day must account for this shift. If one considers only the rotation of the celestial circle without the Sun's additional motion, the count of days would be different. This has been stated by the **Blessed Sun** himself in his own treatise, the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>original: *kha-siddhante*; referring to the Surya Siddhanta, one of the most authoritative ancient Sanskrit texts on astronomy</note>, where he defines the days based on the Sun's transit through the equinoctial points. Thus, the rotations are understood through the Sun’s motion. **33**

Therefore, a single **Savana Day** consists of one full rotation of the stars plus the time corresponding to the Sun's daily motion. Every **Savana Day** represents one rotation of the heavens and the time elapsed as the Sun moves from its previous position. When the daily motion is accounted for, a single revolution of the equinox is completed. In terms of precise time, the number of **Savana** rotations is slightly different because of this constant shifting of the Sun.

<summary>This page defines the Savana (civil) day as the time between two sunrises, explaining that it exceeds a single sidereal rotation due to the Sun's own forward motion and the precession of the equinoxes (Ayanamsha), citing the Siddhanta Shiromani and Surya Siddhanta.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion on the Sāvana (civil) day-count, building on the previous page's explanation of the celestial sphere's rotation and the Sun's daily motion. It specifically addresses the discrepancies between "mean" and "actual" time calculations.</meta>

<page-num>51</page-num>

The rotations involving the Sun and the earth's movement <note>original: *Sāvyākabhubhramāḥ*; referring to the combined motion of the earth's rotation relative to the Sun</note> are considered. If the **Mean Sun** <note>original: *madhyārka*; the theoretical sun moving at a constant speed</note> has reached the desired point at the intended time, then, due to the difference in time, the **Civil Day-count** <term>Sāvana Ahargaṇa|The total number of civil days elapsed since a fixed epoch, usually the start of the Kali Yuga</term> is established from the union of three factors. In that civil day-count, the rotations are not merely the cycles of the Mean Sun, but the sum of three elements: the total rotations of the star-sphere, the Sun’s own revolutions, and the minutes of the Mean Sun converted into time-units <note>original: *asavaḥ*; breath-units or sidereal seconds</note>.

The **Civil Day-count** derived from these three is considered the **Actual** <note>original: *vāstava*</note> count. It is established through distinct methods. Through the **Rule of Three** <term>Trairāshika|A mathematical proportion: if A gives B, what does C give?</term>, the position of the Mean Sun at the time of sunrise is determined as the **Actual** position. In some systems, this is calculated using the "Mean Civil Day," but the result must correspond to the actual time of sunrise. Some may argue that to achieve this "actual" state, a specific correction for the **Equation of Time** <term>Udayāntara|The difference between mean solar time and true solar time caused by the earth's orbital eccentricity and the tilt of its axis</term> is necessary.

To this, it is said: My preferred method for reaching the "actual" result does not rely on whether a specific day is counted as "real" or "not real" in a purely theoretical sense. For instance, in the previously mentioned system, the day-count is defined by one rotation, one revolution, and the intersection of the Mean Sun’s minutes. In the **Non-Actual** <note>original: *avāstava*; here referring to the mean or simplified calculation</note> method, one simply considers the day-count as one revolution and the basic minutes of the Mean Sun. The difference between the **Actual Civil Day** and the **Non-Actual** one is a period of twenty-seven <unclear>units</unclear>, during which time units like **Lunar Days** <note>original: *tithi*</note> and smaller fractions of time may be lost or diminished.

Furthermore, there may be a greater or lesser internal difference. According to those who follow the "Network of Time" <note>original: *tanjāla*; likely referring to a specific school of astronomical calculation</note>, this difference must be clearly defined. In the **Sidereal Year** <note>original: *nirayana-varṣa*; a year measured relative to fixed stars, without accounting for the precession of the equinoxes</note>, if one only adds the extra cycles of the celestial circle to the mean measure, the Civil Day-count will not be accurate.

Those who speak clearly on the matter state that the civil day is determined by the Sun’s position including precession <note>original: *sāyana*; tropical longitude</note> and its corresponding time-units. If one calculates the **Equation of Time** <note>original: *udayāntara*</note> based on the Sun, even that might be considered "not real" if it does not lead to the **Actual Day-count**. This would contradict the sacred traditions. In reality, in the solar and lunar systems, the cause of the day and night is the Sun’s own motion. Just as the civil days are determined by the movement of the lunar day through twelve degrees of separation, they are established as "actual" at the desired time through mutual proportion. This is not the case in the purely sidereal system. Therefore, the day-and-night cycle depends on its own motion, and the proportions derived from it at a given time are not "actual" unless they align with the reality of the celestial sphere. This is established through the **Intercalary Months** <note>original: *adhimāsa*; extra months added to the lunar calendar to align it with the solar year</note> and the **Omitted Days** <note>original: *avama*; days skipped in the lunar calendar to maintain alignment</note>. Through the relationship of the remaining fractions of these omitted days, the Civil Days are calculated as they appear at the horizon at the time of the Mean Sun’s rise. This is what makes them **Actual**.

<summary>This page analyzes the mathematical definition of a Civil Day (Sāvana), arguing that a truly "actual" day-count must account for the Sun's specific motion and the Equation of Time (Udayāntara) rather than relying on simplified mean rotations.</summary>

In this way, the risk of **imprecision** <note>original: *sthūlatā*; referring to mathematical rounding errors or conceptual vagueness</note> arises; therefore, this is untenable. Indeed, the Solar and Civil measures are used here for the sake of bringing about the calculation, but the hand-measurements and other observational tools used in the Vedas are not the "Actual" **Civil Day-count**. Furthermore, regarding the simultaneous existence and non-existence of two points in time, there is an objection regarding the distinction between the two, because a contradiction arises in describing a single entity as having both properties. 

Thus it is said: even the **Solar** measures must be considered by you according to the **Mean** measure. Where there is an actual existence of a quantity, the calculation of those days for the purpose of the Civil day-count is considered "unreal" at the time of sunrise. Therefore, the day-count which is calculated from the time of the solar sunrise at the creation <note>original: *sṛṣṭi*; the start of the current cosmic cycle</note> is established as "Actual" <note>original: *vāstava*</note>, as it is characterized by the specific time of the Sun’s rising for that birth-epoch. 

It cannot be said that the day-count previously established by an "unreal" mean civil measure is invalid at the moment of sunrise. How could it be considered unreal? From the movement of the **Mean Sun** <term>Madhyārka|The theoretical Sun moving at a constant speed along the celestial equator</term> and the subsequent addition or subtraction of the difference in its movement, the **True Day-count** <term>Sphuṭāhargaṇa|The day-count adjusted for the Sun's actual, non-uniform motion</term> is achieved. If the days were not considered as whole units, how could they be "Actual"? Since the day-count is counted in whole units of days, why is it accepted as the **Mean** at the time of sunrise? In that system, if the count is based on those specific sunrises, how could the planetary weekday be correctly determined? 

Furthermore, at the start of the **Solar Year**, the application of the **Equation of Time** <term>Udayāntara|The correction required to synchronize the mean solar day with the actual sunrise</term> and other factors would be inconsistent. In the context of the calculation, when the result is derived from the mean motion and its subsequent corrections, it becomes **True** <note>original: *spaṣṭa*</note>. Through the logic of the results, it is highly refined. How then can this be contested? In that system, the rule of the **True** motion of the Sun, which follows a fixed path on its eccentric circle <term>Pratimaṇḍala|The off-center circle used in ancient astronomy to model the varying speed of planets</term>, is unavoidable. 

Now, as for what is called the "Actual Single Measure" by the ignorant: in that path, the **Equation of Time** is not established by two equal measures, but by the difference in time at the end of six months, resulting in a different mathematical conclusion. This is a heterogeneous calculation. <note>original: 35; likely referring to a specific mathematical value in the calculation</note> Therefore, in another set of day-counts, there is a heterogeneous planetary calculation, and there too, an intercalary difference of 35 occurs. 

Moreover, there is a great contradiction: the minutes of the difference in motion between the planet and the Mean Sun are not refined when the two are uncorrected. By giving the **Equation of Time** correction, the logic of the apogee and perigee is addressed through the Sun's motion. It is said that in a part of a day, the Sun moves... <note>original: 350; likely referring to the Sun's approximate movement in minutes or a related constant</note>

<meta>This page continues the technical discourse on the calculation of the Civil Day-count (Ahargaṇa) and its philosophical underpinnings. The author, likely Kamalakara, is critiquing the methodology of the 12th-century astronomer Bhāskarācārya, asserting that his "rational explanation" contradicts the traditional authority of the sages and gods. They call the **Mean Civil Day** <term>Kudina|A terrestrial or "civil" day measured from one sunrise to the next</term> the movement of the mean sun (1). As many motions as there are in a cosmic cycle, so many are the **Solar Civil Days** (3). In the same way, in that union, there are also the revolutions of the celestial spheres (2).

Whatever the interval between the single Civil Days, there is a specific motion of the planets; even the solar nature of the time is not lost, for that time is known as the "Rational Explanation" <note>original: *vāsanā*; the underlying physical or geometric logic of a mathematical rule</note> by those who know the definitions.

The **Mean Sun** <term>Madhyārka|The theoretical Sun that moves at a constant rate, rather than the true Sun which appears to speed up and slow down</term> is identical to its own motion (3). When the time of the movement is equal, without the union, you yourself should speak of the sunrise; from that sunrise, the Mean Sun is known. If the sunrise occurs when the body is dead <note>original: *mṛttane*; likely a corrupt OCR reading for a technical term regarding the solar disc</note>, the single Civil Day would be contradicted (4). Considering that this is established from a state of equality, these wise men understand it through the study of the **Celestial Sphere** <note>original: *gola*</note> (5).

Therefore, regarding the current **Day-count** <term>Ahargaṇa|The total number of days elapsed from a fixed epoch to a given date</term>, the mean Civil Days should be adjusted by the correction of the sunrise. This is what is stated here. This matter should be contemplated with a sharp mind by those who know the truth of the essence of the Sphere. By this, the account of the distance is explained. Beyond that, the name of the "actual" day-count does not reach its highest point if one ignores the difference in the Civil Days (6).

As the great teacher **Bhāskarācārya** <note>A famous 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>, by his own force, began to act in a way that is **contrary to the opinions of all the gods and sages**, he put forward a "Rational Explanation of the Sphere" that is excluded from proper consideration. Even though his own view has been accepted by many who merely pride themselves on his name, it has proceeded on an **incorrect path** <note>original: *aśuddha-mārga*</note> and should not be respected in any way by the disciplined scholars of today. This is because of the contradiction with the world as defined in the scriptures, regarding the calculation of the Civil Day. How could the determination of the Sun and other planets be done correctly using such Civil Days? This is not known by those who follow the authority of the **Solar Sciences** <note>original: *Arka-śāstra*</note> (7).

In this calculation, the **Solar Years** <note>original: *sauravatsara*</note> that have passed since the **Day of Brahma** <term>Brahmadina|A vast chronological period in Hindu cosmology, often equated to a Kalpa</term> are to be considered. From that time, the Solar years are measured. These elapsed years are: 

->**1,553,920,000** (15)<-

The calculation of the **Mean Positions** <note>original: *madhyama-nayana*</note> must be performed here with these elapsed periods. Through this, the planets—starting with the Sun—are established in their mean positions (10). Without knowing the nodes of the Moon, and the beginning of Aries, it is measured as such (1). There is no difference in the years from the beginning of the Day of Brahma (11). Twelve (12) revolutions are known... the planets are joined with the current zodiac signs. The life-breaths and other measures (12). The correction of the **True Sun** <note>original: *sphuṭārka*</note> is done starting from Aries. At that time... the lunar months are always calculated as they were previously described by the great sages (13). This is the fault that arises from the lack of a transition...

As has been stated, Aśvinī and the rest are to be understood right here.

<meta>This page continues the transition from the mathematics of time into the physical structure of the cosmos. The **Revolution of the Stars** <term>Bhagaṇa|A full 360-degree rotation of a celestial body</term> is indeed <unclear>to be understood</unclear> in two ways: it is fixed, yet it is also moving 31. The sphere, which is the seat of the zodiac and the stars, moves around the **Pole of the Ecliptic** <term>Kadamba|The point 24 degrees from the celestial pole around which the zodiac rotates</term>; it is bound by the **Celestial Wind** <term>Pravaha-vāyu|The driving force of the heavens</term> and controlled by the center of the poles 32. It rotates here due to the power of the Sun’s own motion... as was previously stated regarding the ancient celestial sphere 33.

The clear **Equatorial Circle** <term>Viṣuvat-vṛtta|The celestial equator</term> is said to begin with Aries and the other signs; because of the movement of the star-sphere, its own shifting is also recognized 34. Thus, by the masses of the stars, the measure of the movement proceeds in order. Thus, in the celestial sphere, the circle is divided by the Ecliptic Pole and other markers 35. This movement of the star-sphere is known as the "motion of the equinoxes" <note>original: *ayana-calana*; referring to the precession of the equinoxes</note>, though some are confused by the nature of this rotation 36. From the positions of the poles, the stars are born from their own nature; at the time when there is no shifting of the signs, they follow the path of the pole 37.

Now, that sphere has moved, because it is known to move by itself; it is seen here to rotate from the previous sign 38. Then it is seen rotating from the subsequent sign. For the sake of establishing what is fixed, the **Pole Star** <term>Dhruva-tārā|The North Star, Polaris</term> is perceived by the mind 39. With that, there is a dispute regarding the shifting; it is indeed a cause of confusion. If everything is fixed in the position of the pole, why would there be motion of the celestial sky by the rotation of the Ecliptic Pole? 40.

That truth is found at the peak of **Mount Meru** <term>Meru|The sacred mountain at the center of the world, serving as the Earth's axis</term>, which is immovable at the center of the earth. On that mountain, the golden power of the gods is always within the sight of the eyes 41. Having imagined the poles below, they speak of them as being not-fixed. Thus, the celestial sphere has been described, which rests upon the support of the stars 42.

I shall now speak clearly of the **Seven Planetary Spheres** <term>Graha-gola|The nested shells or orbits of the planets</term>. They are produced by their own nodes, their slow-motion points, and their fast-motion points 43. The spheres known as the "highest point" <note>original: *ucca*; apogee</note> and "lowest point" <note>original: *nīca*; perigee</note> are suitable for the calculation of the "true" positions of the planets. All of these together constitute the great Sphere of the Planets 44. How could the planets be arranged one above the other? These spheres in the sky are great supports called "winds" 45.

Now, they are to be known in their proper order, starting from **Saturn** <term>Śani|The slowest and outermost planet in ancient astronomy</term> and staying within their limits. From the weightless mirror of the sky was produced **Mars** <note>The text here omits Jupiter, jumping from Saturn's sphere toward Mars</note>, and from that the **Sun** 46. From that **Venus**, then **Mercury**, and from that the **Moon**—this is clear. In the intervals between each pair of these, the **Celestial Musicians and Spirits** <note>original: *Siddha-Vidyādhara*</note> 47 have their dwellings; these spheres of the sky stand always as their own supports. In all the connected spheres, the uppermost moves by its own motion around the pole 48. The one below moves the sky; the one in the middle moves in the atmosphere. Because of the motion of the sphere situated below, a contrary motion is perceived 49. Of the one moved by its own power...

...in any way 50. There is no purpose for this other calculation to be applied to the motion. The observer who is devoted to the calculation of the degrees—those stars are fixed at the horizon even without them 51. One must understand these things clearly: when positioned in the **Observational Sphere** <term>Dṛg-gola|The visible celestial sphere as seen by an observer on Earth</term>, the other spheres also appear moved because they depend on that specific state 52.

Each of these spaces should be known in relation to the Moon. In order, the different spheres are situated at varying distances 53. The layer that extends from the earth’s surface to the limit of movement is called the **Sphere of Fire** <term>Vahni-gola|The atmospheric layer associated with heat and terrestrial energy</term>; it does not exist beyond that halfway point 54. Beyond that are the realms of the pure ones: the World of Creation, the World of Penance, and then the World of Truth <note>original: *Jana-loka, Tapas-loka, Satya-loka*; these are the upper three of the seven planetary/celestial worlds in Vedic cosmology</note>, which is established as eternal 55.

Fixed at the poles, the measures of the planets and signs are moved by a specific power; however, the **Sphere of the Stars** <term>Bhagola|The celestial sphere containing the fixed stars and zodiac signs</term> also moves by its own power to its own specific place 56. All spheres, because they are infinite, move by their own power from their respective positions. They exist only for the sake of the rotation of the void <note>original: *kha*; referring to the space or ether</note>, and are not subject to a forced or external rotation 57.

**Aryabhata’s statement** <note>original: *Āryabhaṭo-kti*; referring to the 5th-century astronomer Aryabhata I</note> is heard by the learned: that the head <insert>of the sky</insert> seems to move while the person is standing still, but this is because of the rotation of the Earth; thus, the idea of a **Driving Wind** <term>Pravaha|A theoretical celestial wind or force traditionally thought to push the stars</term> is redundant 58. Because everything is without a fixed support in the lower regions, everything stays in its own place; this is said to refute those who think things would fall 59. Thus, this "unreal rotation" <note>apparent motion of the stars caused by Earth's rotation</note> has been accepted with effort by some, even though the movement is actually caused by the connection of the fields 60.

Indeed, for the sake of understanding time, these spheres are produced by time itself. The sphere called the **Star-Sphere** should not be considered the same as the "Driving Wind" 61. From the Driving Wind to the Fire Sphere, there are spheres known as **Foreign** <note>original: *Yavana*; this term literally means "Ionian/Greek" and may refer here to atmospheric theories influenced by Greek astronomy, or a specific technical classification</note> which are extremely clear and devoid of impurities 62.

Just as the Earth's atmosphere is universal, men should know that the Driving Wind is also all-pervading. These clear spaces, which are called the **Wind Spheres** <term>Pavana-gola|The atmospheric layers dominated by air/wind</term>, are fixed in their places 63. These spheres become increasingly rarefied, one after another, and their movements vary. Near the poles, the wind and the sky are said to be much thicker than elsewhere 64. They are all distinct by their nature; the planetary bodies move in their own places according to their own rules, even though they appear to be fixed together in a rotation 65.

The rays of the Sun are not obstructed in any way. Because of the proximity of the Sun, the intensity of the rays is great 66. Thus, from the Earth's horizon to the Star-Sphere, there are seven levels of light; beyond that is the Great Void 67. Therefore, the spheres belong to the sky-makers; in all of them, the Star-Sphere is lost to view once one moves beyond its half-way point 68. As it stands in its own place, endowed with the rays of the Sun, the sky appears blue; this is due to the nature of the light in the upper reaches 69.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of celestial and terrestrial spheres. In the world, there is a perception of blue all around; this is an illusion <note>original: *saṃbhramaḥ*; meaning a delusion or mental whirl</note> regarding the **Earth-sphere** <term>Bhūgola|The physical, spherical planet Earth</term>. To common people, the "mountain-sphere" <note>the solid, rocky Earth</note> does not appear as a center 70. However, in the study of logic, it is said that all centers are fundamentally one.

From this perspective, how can a center be different? It is the great **Sphere of Water** <term>Jala-gola|The hydrosphere or the mass of water surrounding/on the Earth</term> that is vast. Just as there is a sensation of rotation, the waters also constantly rotate; this is the established principle 73. This mass, composed of earth, fire, wind, and ether <note>original: *mṛd-agni-anila-ākāśa*; the four classical elements</note>, is like a **Wood-apple fruit** <note>original: *kapittha-phala*; a common Indian simile for the Earth's spherical shape</note> or a pearl, appearing round from all centers 74.

The Earth is stable by the power of others <note>likely referring to divine or cosmic forces</note> and is situated at the bottom of all spheres. In the middle of the **Cosmic Egg** <term>Brahmāṇḍa|The universe, literally the 'Egg of Brahma'</term> is the Earth-sphere 75.

### The Power of Support

It possesses a supreme power that is characterized by weight. There is no other external support for this Earth; it exists upon its own support 76. Otherwise, if we sought a support for the support, we would face the "fear of infinite regress" <term>Anavasthā|A logical fallacy where a premise requires an endless chain of prior causes</term>. Therefore, one must go to the point where power itself resides 77. 

From the very beginning, one must imagine this power for the sake of simplicity <note>original: *lāghava*; the principle of parsimony or 'lightness' in logic</note>. The wise have spoken of this in terms of heaviness, the sun’s heat, and smoke 78. Even if the Earth is seen moving through the sky, it is established as it truly is 79. What one person calls "upward," another on the opposite side calls differently; yet this Earth remains a stable and diverse creation 80.

As people travel across the surface of the Earth, they perceive it according to their own position 81. Because the Earth is a round sphere, people remain stable in their own places in every direction 82. They see the Earth in the middle of their vision. This Earth, which is above the water, is seen as bright and meaningful 83. The wise say the truth is pure: the Water-sphere exists, and it is vast, and there is no error in this 84.

The Earth-sphere is partially submerged in water and partially situated outside of it. The "knower of spheres" <note>an astronomer or cosmologist</note> understands that more of the Earth exists above the water 85. Because of the rotation of the Water-sphere, what is inside the water may eventually come outside, much like the internal parts of an egg 86. When the mass of the Earth is above the water on a single surface, the wise describe it as a **Hemisphere** <note>original: *ardha-gola*</note> 87. 

Now, many further questions and answers are provided according to different schools of thought 88. On this Earth, there is the Salt Ocean. At the coastline, the sky appears the same to all who stand there 89. Because of the Earth’s dimensions, the sky is seen clearly there; for those on the shore, the situation is equal 90. 

Now, to resolve any contradictions, the beneficial parts are described. The internal divisions of the Earth are equal parts, each resting on its own nature 91. These are the **Underworld Realms** <term>Pātāla|The seven subterranean realms in Hindu cosmology</term>, filled with hundreds of divine medicines and heaps of various gems...

### The Axis and the Four Cities

Mount Meru <note>the sacred mountain at the center of the world in Hindu cosmology</note> passes through the middle of the **Earth-sphere** <term>Bhūgola|The physical, spherical planet Earth</term> and emerges on both sides. At the top of this axis, located at the center, dwell the ancient Great Sages and the Gods 13.

In the same way, at the bottom, the Asuras <note>original: *asurāḥ*; often translated as demons or titans, they are the inhabitants of the lower realms</note> reside, occupying the opposite side. Within the circle of the Earth's equator <term>Nirukta-deśa|The equatorial region where the day and night are of equal length</term>, there are four primary cities:

*   **Lanka** is situated in the middle of the Earth.
*   To the East is **Yamakoti**.
<margin>t. vi. 5 <note>A reference likely pointing to a specific verse or chapter in a commentary</note></margin>
*   To the West is the city called **Romaka**.
*   Directly opposite Lanka, "below," is **Siddhapura**.

In the North is **Meru**, and in the South is the other pole. Thus, there are always six primary locations situated around the Earth's core 16. One should imagine themselves at any one of these locations. All beings, whether gods or humans, are situated "upright" relative to their own position, even if they appear "upside down" <note>original: *adhasthāt*; meaning below or underneath</note> to those on the opposite side of the sphere 17.

### Calculating the Sphere

For those who understand the **Sphere of the World** <term>Loka-gola|The cosmic or terrestrial sphere</term>, the prime meridian <note>original: *rekhā*; literally 'line', referring here to the line of zero longitude passing through Lanka and Meru</note> is the reference for one's own country 18. By understanding the division of countries, one calculates the **Gnomon** <term>Śaṅku|A vertical rod used in ancient astronomy to measure shadows and determine latitude</term>.

To everyone, Meru always appears to be in the North. Similarly, for those at the other pole, Meru appears to be in the South 19.

When the sun rises in Lanka, it is midday in **Yamakoti**, sunset in **Siddhapura**, and midnight in **Romaka** 1-2. This is the constant rule for the equatorial regions. A true "Knower of the Sphere" <term>Gola-vid|An expert in spherical astronomy</term> understands that "East" is simply the direction in which the sun is first seen at any given location 3-4.

### The Mathematics of the Circle

Just as we determine the circumference of the Earth's sphere from its diameter, the knowledge of the diameter leads to the result of the circumference 5. According to the tradition of the **Sūrya Siddhānta** <term>Sūrya Siddhānta|A foundational Sanskrit treatise on Indian astronomy, dated to the 4th-5th century CE</term>, the calculation is made precise.

Without knowing the proportion of the diameter to the circumference, the true size of the Earth cannot be known 7. The ancient sages followed a specific rule for this. The compassionate Sun God <note>referring to the divine source of the Sūrya Siddhānta</note> provided the rule:

**The square of the diameter multiplied by ten gives the square of the circumference.**

<note>This describes the ancient Indian approximation of Pi (π) as the square root of 10, or approximately 3.1622</note>

Therefore, the diameter is the square root of the circumference's square divided by ten 9. By utilizing the **Sines** <term>Kramajyā|The trigonometric Sine function</term> and their inverse equivalents, one can determine the exact measurements of the planetary orbits and the Earth's dimensions 10. These calculations are refined by the division of the circle into degrees and minutes to avoid errors in time and position 11-12.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical and geographic description of the Earth. Having previously established the positions of the four great cities (Lanka, Yamakoti, Romaka, and Siddhapura) and Mount Meru, the text now transitions to the specific mathematical ratios used to calculate the Earth’s dimensions, citing the authority of the mathematician Bhaskara.</meta>

### The Mathematical Ratio of the Sphere

The diameter <term>Vyāsa|The straight line passing through the center of the Earth-sphere</term> and the circumference <term>Paridhi|The total distance around the Earth's surface</term> are related in a fixed proportion. In the works of **Bhaskara** <note>original: *bhāskarīyaiḥ*; referring to Bhaskaracharya, the 12th-century Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>, these measurements are calculated and inscribed using a specific formula. By examining his methods, we find the remainder of the calculation. 14

Through a division using the factor of eight, twenty-four, five, and thirty-two <note>original: *8। 24। 5। 32*; these likely refer to specific fractional parts or sexagesimal units used in ancient trigonometry</note>, the method taught by Bhaskara is correctly applied to determine the circle's diameter. From this, the true nature of the circular root is known. 15 Some observers see a width of 28,308 <note>a value related to the Earth's measure</note>, which is fixed and consistent with the views of the **Solar School** <term>Saura|The tradition or astronomical school following the Surya Siddhanta</term>. 16 To ensure the understanding of his students and to resolve disputes, this calculation is performed for the sake of clear solar realization. 17

### Methods of Calculation

Square the diameter and multiply it by ten; then, the square root of that result provides the circumference. Conversely, the diameter can be found by reversing this process. 18 Alternatively, square the circumference, then divide that square by ten; the square root of the result will give the diameter through the reverse operation. 19 Whenever the square root must be extracted, one should follow the prescribed method of calculation. 20

The circumference is described as being the **square root of ten** <note>original: *dashamūla*; a common ancient approximation for Pi (π) where π ≈ √10, or roughly 3.162</note>. 21 When the desired diameter is multiplied by this value, the result is produced. In some calculations, the square is 53,293. 22 Thus, the square of one's own diameter results in the circumference in many ways. However, it must be noted that in some traditions, the diameter and circumference are not considered perfectly fixed. 23

### The Dimensions in Yojanas

In the measurement of hundreds of **Yojanas** <term>Yojana|A traditional unit of distance, roughly 5 to 9 miles depending on the text</term>, the Earth’s measure is given as 39,600. 25 According to the Solar and Manu traditions, there are variations in these counts because of different standards of measurement. This difference in numbers is explained by their own teachers. In the calculations of the **Romakas** <note>original: *romaka*; referring to the Greco-Roman or Alexandrian astronomical tradition</note>, the math follows a different method. 26 That which follows the measurement of the inhabited world is used for the measure of the Earth; those who speak otherwise do so out of ignorance of the mathematical methods. 27

### The Meridians and Local Positions

The North-South line <term>Yāmyottara-rekhā|The prime meridian or local longitude line</term> passes through one’s own location and connects to **Meru** <note>the North Pole</note>. 30 Just as Meru is the starting point, the locations of other cities are determined by these lines. 31 Where a line is drawn according to its own degrees of latitude, those degrees define that city's position and no other. 32 From the circumference of one’s own side to the desired location in yojanas, the distance is calculated from the middle. 33

To the South of Meru, the city of **Lanka** is situated at a distance of 22 degrees <note>original: *bhāgaiḥ*; degrees</note>; it is well-known that the line passes through there. 34 The line connecting the rising point of Meru and one's own country is established with great effort. 35
