### Calculating the Longitude Difference

The value of **Lanka** <note>The theoretical point on the equator used as the starting point for Indian longitude</note> is taken as the base. From the <term>Meru|The North Pole</term>, the divisions of the Earth are calculated in 30 parts. To find the difference between one's own location and the prime meridian, use the following method:

Multiply the distance in <term>Yojanas|A traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-13 kilometers</term> by the <term>Kalā|Minutes of arc; 1/60th of a degree</term>—specifically 38—and divide by the Earth's circumference of 360. 38-39 This result gives the <term>Deśāntara|Longitude difference</term> in minutes. If the result is positive, it is added; if negative, it is subtracted from the calculation of the <term>Sphuṭārka|The true position of the Sun</term>. 40

Time is infinite, and it is only through these mathematical corrections that the Sun's position becomes "true" or accurate for a specific observer. 41 By applying these corrections to the Moon and other planets, the coordinates are refined for one's own country. 42

### The Sacred Line of the Meridian

The scholars have declared that the line of the meridian passes from the abode of the **Rakshasas** <note>referring to Lanka</note> to the mountain of the gods <note>Mount Meru</note>, passing through the middle of the world. It passes through **Rohitaka**, **Avanti** <note>Ujjain</note>, and the nearby sacred lakes. 45 This is established by the authority of all astronomical treatises.

To find the distance between two places in <term>Yojanas</term>, one must calculate the difference between the observed time of the Sun at the prime meridian and at one's own location. 48 When the Sun is at the meridian in the East, it will be at a different relative position in one's own place. By dividing the circumference of the Earth by these units of time, the distance in *Yojanas* for the longitude difference is determined. 49

One should calculate the results in minutes (<term>Liptā</term>) based on the Earth's circumference. For those living to the west of the meridian, the correction must be subtracted; for those to the east, it is added, to find the true position on the surface of the Earth. 50

### On the Visibility of the Planetary Spheres

Because of the Earth's surface, the sight of a person is obstructed in certain directions. Above the Earth, the vision of the observer is cut off; as much as the sphere of the Earth moves forward, that same amount is obscured in the planetary sphere. Within that own sphere, the moving celestial body is seen by men. When the sight of the Earth and the sight of the planet meet in the sky, the rising and setting of the planet occurs.

Therefore, the wise astronomer should know that everything regarding the rising and setting of planets, such as the Moon, is established through the geometry of these spheres. Just as the Earth does not move through the planetary spheres by touch, the movement and the speed of the rising and setting are spoken of by the people. The Earth's disk is perceived within the blue sphere <note>original: *nīlo-vari-golaka*; likely referring to the blue sky or the atmosphere</note> according to its form. 

Because of the difference in height—being high or low—the rising of the sun and planets in the sky appears different. Depending on where the observer resides on the Earth, the vision is cut off at different points. Because of the rotation of the planets and the specific position of the observer on the Earth's surface, the rising and setting are perceived differently. This is caused by the difference in the observer's line of sight.

**59**

Due to the wondrous nature of the Earth's position and the observer's sight, the state of the planets is determined in mathematical calculations. By this method, the wise determine the minute discrepancies <term>truṭi|a very small unit of time or a slight observational error</term> for the sake of establishing the exact time of planetary visibility. 

**60**

### The Sphere of Air and Atmospheric Phenomena

Above the sphere of the Earth <note>original: *ku-golād*</note>, there is the air <term>vāyu|the element of wind or the atmosphere</term>, which is consistently cold. Above this, it is said by some that the rising and setting of the celestial bodies are affected by the nature of the vapors. Just as fire produces vapor, so too does the air produce upward-moving vapors. These vapors, dark like the skin of a deer, travel through the sky for many years. 

It is said that the planets move through these layers with great speed. Similarly, the falling of sun-rays, the appearance of lightning, and the wondrous **City of the Gandharvas** <note>original: *gandharvapura*; a traditional term for a mirage or *fata morgana* occurring in the atmosphere</note> are explained by these atmospheric conditions. Comets <term>ketavo|meteors or comets</term>, which are seen as givers of omens or results, also exist in this world. 

These phenomena—great diseases and atmospheric changes—are all caused by the interaction of these vapors with the heat and light of the sun-rays. Below the celestial bodies, these vapors are situated. External colors and scents <note>original: *gandhaka*; literally sulfur or fragrant substances, here referring to atmospheric particles</note> go everywhere from the Earth due to the combination of these vapors. According to the science of fire <note>original: *agni-śāstra*; possibly referring to thermodynamics or the study of light and heat</note>, stars and other celestial bodies are observed within their spheres. 

Similarly, through the application of cold or by the force of the wind, stones that are hard and firm <note>original: *pāṣāṇaiḥ*; referring to meteorites</note> fall from the sky. These stones wander through the atmosphere, driven by the power of the air, until they fall to the Earth.

### On Atmospheric Omens and Planetary Orbits

In the natural world, a sound is often heard upon the Earth arising from flames or the emission of vapors <note>original: *vashpa*; refers to steam or geothermal gases</note>. This is said to occur in the practical realm of our ground. Through the emission of these vapors, even if a certain location is desired, earthquakes may occur among the mountains and elsewhere; this is the definitive conclusion. 

Due to the influence of the Sun's hot rays, these various vapors become unevenly distributed. Because of these vapors, many people may face death; this is famously known in the world by the name **"The Great Plague"** <note>original: *mahamari*; refers to an epidemic or pestilence believed to be caused by atmospheric corruption</note>. Furthermore, when one observes the rising of the Sun through such vapors, it is seen as an ill omen. This results in rains occurring out of season, or eclipses and other celestial events appearing at the wrong time or in the wrong place. 

During such times, one should perform deeds to alleviate hunger and distress through the power of righteousness (*dharma*), and the Lord shall destroy the famine. This has been stated here in brief; it is explained in great detail throughout the scientific scriptures (*shastras*). 

### The Dimensions of the Planetary Spheres

Now, the secrets of the orbits of all the planets are heard, revolving constantly within the "Tree of Time." The spheres of the Universal Egg <note>original: *Brahmanda Gola*; the "Egg of Brahma," or the manifest universe</note> are arranged by the seven celestial bodies. Their proportions are given in <term>yojanas</term> <note>a traditional Indian unit of distance, approximately 8 to 9 miles</note>. Based on the number of revolutions (*bhaganas*) completed by the planets in a <term>Kalpa</term> <note>an immense aeon of time in Hindu cosmology</note>, the circumferences of their respective orbits are determined. By dividing these by the number of civil days (*kudinas*), the daily motion of the planets in *yojanas* is likewise found.

Within the center of the sky, where the spheres are located, the mean distance of the Sun's orbit is established. Listen now to the measurements of these orbits as derived from the Sun-Sciences (*Arka-Shastra*), which define the path of the planets moving in the middle of the circle:

| Planet | Orbital Circumference (in *yojanas*) |
| :--- | :--- |

| **The Moon's path** (*Chandaja*) | 1,053,205 |
| **Venus** (*Bhriguja*) | 2,884,539 |
| **The Sun (Refined/Outer)** | 5,331,500 |
| **Mars** (*Kuja*) | 146,505 |
| **Jupiter** (*Guru*) | 5,397,565 |
| **Mercury** (*Rau*) | 1,276,255 |
| **Saturn** (*Shani*) | 248,820,000 |

Above all these, the sphere of the universe rotates, driven by the <term>Pravaha</term> wind <note>the celestial wind believed to drive the rotation of the star-sphere</note>. It stands at the very top of the structure...

### On the Calculation of Planetary Orbits

Thus, from the desired calculations, the greatness of the positions is established. Due to the vastness of the divisions and the smallness of others, and because of the time taken for a single revolution <term>bhagana; a complete 360-degree circuit of the zodiac</term>, the planet is dependent on its specific period. Moving through a great circle over a long period of time, the planet's own path and its rays are spread according to the scale of its revolution. By moving through these great divisions, each planet follows its own course.

Because of this, **Saturn** <note>original: *shanaishchara*; "the slow-mover"</note> is the highest and slowest. The motion of **Venus** is not the same; its distance in <term>yojanas; a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 to 13 kilometers</term> and its division into minutes of arc <note>original: *kala*</note> would be different according to the system of the venerable **Brahmadeva**.

Thus, the hypotenuse <term>karna; in this context, the radial distance from the center</term> of the celestial sphere should be known according to the stated method. The orbits in this system, measured in tens of thousands of yojanas, are as follows:

The disk-spheres <note>original: *vimba-gola*; the physical volume or diameter of the planetary bodies</note> of the planets are fixed within the northern celestial sphere. A learned man, following the teachings of the lords of the solar path <note>original: *kranti-devaih*</note>, states their diameters in yojanas as follows:

*   **The Sun:** 7,500 yojanas is the diameter of its orb.
*   **The Moon:** 480 yojanas.
*   **Mars:** 175 yojanas.
*   **Mercury:** 1,531 yojanas.

These dimensions of the orbs are provided according to the Solar Method <note>original: *arka-yukti*</note>.

There is a disagreement regarding the minutes of arc among some teachers. I have seen the orbs with my own eyes through the tubes <note>original: *dhru-goh*; likely referring to observational instruments or sighting tubes used before the telescope</note>. <insert>Therefore, the position of the orb is appropriately joined to the higher place.</insert> The true nature of the orbs arises from the divisions of the sky. Therefore, the authority of the calculations regarding the Sun and others should be accepted by the wise.

The diameters of Mars and the other planets are also determined by the multiplier of their respective hypotenuse <note>distance</note>. Thus, in the **Surya Siddhanta** <note>The "Sun Treatise," one of the most authoritative ancient Indian astronomical texts</note>, the measurements in yojanas for Mars and the others are given in order.

### Calculations of Visual Diameters

In sequence, the values are: **twenty** (20), **forty-five** (45), **fifty-two and a half** (52:30), **sixty** (60), and **thirty-seven and a half** (37:30). These are measured according to their own positions in the sky.

These values were declared as "true" in the *Karnasara* <note>The "Essence of the Hypotenuse," a mathematical astronomical treatise</note>. The sage **Shakalya** also spoke of these true values (54). In the calculation of the positions of **Mars** and the other planets, one must determine the distance-hypotenuse through their respective strengths. From the sum of the two hypotenuses <note>The distances at the furthest point (apogee) and nearest point (perigee)</note>, half of that sum is known as the <term>sphuta-karna; the "true hypotenuse" or the actual distance from the observer to the celestial body</term> at its peak (57).

Because of the suitability of the results, this method is established for bringing about the calculation of the Sun and the Moon. (58) Here, the mean distance in <term>yojanas; a traditional unit of distance, approx. 8-13km</term> is considered the <term>trijya; the standard radius used in calculations, usually the sine of 90 degrees</term> for the true yojanas. Thus, from the true distance, the radius measure is adjusted to be greater or lesser (59) so that the true yojanas of the diameters of the <term>bimba; the visible disk or orb of a planet or star</term> can be found. The explanation for the orbs of the planets as described in the *Surya Siddhanta* <note>original: *Saur-ukta*; "as spoken in the Solar Treatise"</note> is now joyfully declared (60).

### On the Diameters of the Planetary Orbs

Regarding the true calculation: the yojanas and arc-minutes <note>original: *kala*</note> of the diameter of the orb-sphere are well-known in the solar system. From the sum of the distances, the "true hypotenuse" is derived. Here, the mean distance in yojanas is defined as the radius-measure.

Now, if the radius is used for this distance, then the yojana-diameter of the disk yields the arc-minutes of the disk. In this context, the mean distance-hypotenuse acts as the divisor, and the radius acts as the multiplier. By using these two, the measure of the disk situated at the distance of the hypotenuse is calculated according to the radius-measure. This same logic should be understood for the calculation of the solar disk's properties.

Furthermore, one should divide the mean yojana-hypotenuses by the specific terminal hypotenuse and its half. The result is the true distance in yojanas. The rule for the diameter of the planets in yojanas is that they should be divided by the true distance. The result is the visual diameter expressed in arc-minutes. 

In this context, the "ancients" <note>referring to earlier astronomers like Aryabhata or Brahmagupta</note> used the word "hypotenuse" (*karna*) to signify the diameter. According to the school of **Bhaskara** and others, the diameters of the planetary orbs in yojanas are as follows:

### Table of Planetary Diameters (Bhaskara’s System)
| Planet | Diameter in Yojanas |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Mars** | 1,845 |
| **Mercury** | 286 |
| **Jupiter** | 9,459 |
| **Venus** | 1,110 |
| **Saturn** | 29,550 |

Now, according to the *Surya Siddhanta* and the school of the sage **Shakalya**, the yojanas of the planets are calculated at their highest point of distance...

#### Defense of the Spherical Model

This follows the sequence of the calculations regarding the motion of the planets. Because of the following of calculations by those who lack knowledge of the <term>Golajñāna; the "science of the celestial globe" or spherical astronomy</term>, and due to the pride they take in their own systems (*nijatantra*), I—the debater—declare that the distances heard by the ears (61) are stated as four times higher by them. By following their calculation methods on a different path, (62) one might think, "I always know the planets." He who stands below appears to be going above; how can an ignorant person understand this without a contradiction? (63)

Those who are immersed in the knowledge of the globe see the results clearly. Because of the obstruction of those groups, what is the difference between the four directions? The motion of the planets is restrained by their own perigee and apogee. (64) By knowing the spherical geometry through the circles and calculations, you have become proficient. Having abandoned those incorrect views, how can an ignorant man remain in this sphere? Otherwise, the eye of the globe-calculator is useless. (65)

Is it not the case that at a time not sanctioned by the <term>Tantras; astronomical treatises</term>, even when the Sun has entered the sign of Leo (*Mṛgeśa*), you are unable to see the planetary disk? This should be understood. (66) The great sages have explained the various spheres through the science of the Earth and the scriptures. Because of the contradiction of one who is restrained by his own limited power, (67) the confusion regarding the knowledge of the globe is destroyed. All of this is stated to clear up the confusion regarding planetary positions. Furthermore, when the Sun is at its lowest point (*nīca*), it is still in its own high station (*ucca*) relative to the Earth. (68) Even from its own high station, it is situated below; this is a great wonder! This is due to the union of the Earth and the Sun's rays. (69) Whether moving upward or downward, the view is always considered as half.

<meta>This page continues the astronomical discourse, moving from the mathematical calculations of planetary distances to a philosophical and physical description of the celestial sphere (Gola). <margin>Tattva Viveka 13</margin>

Mostly contradictory are the things spoken by those who are confused; they are deceived by the delusions of my own mind. [70] But this mass of light, through the state of being made into a chariot by the rays of the Sun, and also for Mars, would be unbearable. [71] The Sun moves there, and for those two, there is a commonality. One does not carry it out; it is not found in the sacred texts. [72]

->**The Nature of the Celestial Sphere**<-

Some say, "I am the unfolding sphere," situated in the atmosphere of the sky. In this structure, it remains with the stars at its edges. [73] In this circular globe, the circumference of the entire calculation is considered to be on the **equator** <term>equator; original: *viṣuvan-maṇḍala*; the great circle of the celestial sphere lying in the same plane as the Earth's equator</term>. [74] To the north and south of the equator, the center is observed; from there, directed upwards, lies the **ecliptic** <term>ecliptic; original: *krānti-maṇḍala*; the sun's apparent path among the stars</term>. [75]

In this circle made of iron—the ecliptic—iron wires are woven and fixed in the form of the heavens; thus it is called a "hand-made" instrument. [76] On the ecliptic, the fixed signs beginning with **Aries** <note>original: *Meṣādyāḥ*; refers to the twelve signs of the Zodiac</note> are situated in the eastern direction in their proper order. [77]

### The Order of the Planetary Spheres

With the **earth-globe** <note>original: *bhū-gola*</note> situated in the center, the seven spheres are placed at the tips. The great mass is attached to a single axis, fixed in the great circle. [78]

| Order | Planet |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Highest** | **Saturn** |
| | **Jupiter** |
| | **Mars** |
| | **Sun** |
| | **Venus** |
| | **Mercury** |
| **Lowest** | **Moon** |

Below these, in order, the centers of the eclipses are joined. The outer and middle parts are a globe of flames, facing outward. [79] It rotates at all times, made of iron, accompanied by the central wind; it moves by its own speed, rotating from the west together with the body of the wind. [80]

### Refutation of the Literal Iron Model

The various coverings of cloth and the iron forms spoken of by Brahma, the Sun, the Moon, and the sages, are said to arise from the delusion of intellect and the contradictions of Bhrigu. [81] I have not accepted the iron globe as a reality, even though it is used by masters for calculation. [82] Because, if it were a form of iron heated by the rays of the Sun, life would not be possible in such a body. And how could the **Pole Star** <note>original: *Dhruva*</note> be considered fixed? [83]

That also is false. Since the Pole Star is well-known and seen by men, the appearance and shape of the globe is an union perceived by the rotation of the directions. [84] The **celestial sphere** <note>original: *ākāśa-gola*</note> is superior, created by the Supreme Lord for the purpose of rotation. What is the need for ironwork? Was it not made by the Lord's power? [85]

Furthermore, the stars and planets do not require an iron globe as a support. Their rotation is forced by their own power; it should be said that their rotation is under the control of the cosmic rotation. [86] There is no need for iron here; the globes of the Sun and others stay separate because they are distinct entities. [87] The power of the pivot at the poles is the support; it is the rotation of the poles...

How is it explained that this [celestial sphere] is supported by an iron globe, fixed with pins for rotation around the **poles**? <term>poles; original: *dhruva*; the fixed points around which the heavens appear to turn</term> [88] This excellent celestial sphere exists in the sky, containing the stars and planets; here, in your model, the stars are to be fixed onto iron rods and pins. [89]

Because of the lightness of the Sun's rays, the single **celestial sphere** <note>original: *bhagolaka*</note> always appears like a pair. How is it moved by the **pivots** <term>pivots; original: *kadeva*; likely referring to the two celestial poles or axial points</term>? What force have you accepted there—is it the rays of the Sun? [90]

Furthermore, in this region of the iron-like circle, the pivot is indeed made of iron. The rods connected to the stars and planets revolve all around, originating from the pole. [91] Why is it said that the movement of the two pivots is "covered" or hidden? Why is this not accepted in the vast form of the actual universe? In the iron model you have made, there is no obstacle caused by such a covering. [92] By observation, that appearance is like the celestial sphere of Indra himself, presented by you. Set aside the result of what you saw through the delusions of your previous notions. [93]

The connection to the pole is at the edge of the sky. Even if the spheres are moved by the pivots, they move in accordance with the Earth because of the **Pravaha wind**. <term>Pravaha; the great cosmic wind in Indian cosmology believed to drive the rotation of the celestial bodies</term> [94] At that time, how could an iron sphere move in accordance with the pole when struck by the *Pravaha* wind, without some attraction toward the Earth? [95] 

If there were no attraction from the pole, and the lines were merely balanced in the globe following the pole, then how would the rotation of the planetary spheres occur upon an iron pin? [96] Following the pole... even there, the rotation of the two spheres would not occur in a mere diagram without attraction. Therefore, there must be a strong attraction involved here. [97] Due to the pole, the rotation is caused by the *Pravaha* wind; by the turning of the pin, those spheres supported by it also move. In that same way, the mechanism supported by the poles is manifest. [98]

Moreover, the **magnet-sphere** <note>original: *nīlāyaso golo*; literally "blue-iron sphere," a common term for a lodestone or magnet</note> is bound by the *Pravaha* wind. Likewise, the surrounding spheres also move by the wind, through their own light. [99] That iron ecliptic, within the pivots and in the inner part, moves below by its own power. It is said to move according to the rotation of the pole. [100]

The magnet attracts the iron globe when it comes near. Here, the movement of the iron must be accomplished by the two pivots. [1] When the zodiac moves by the motion of the pivots in the iron ecliptic, then the iron parts must act according to their own nature. [2] If it is accepted in this circle of iron that the pole moves, then how would the magnet-sphere not be moved by the pivots in that same way? [4] For it is well known among men that a magnet situated below attracts the iron, and that "iron is born of iron" <note>This likely refers to the sympathetic attraction between magnetic and non-magnetic iron.</note> through attraction. [5] Since the great sphere is fixed above, the attraction from below...

They move around the two **pivots** <term>pivots; original: *kadamba*; the celestial poles or axial points around which the spheres rotate</term> by the rotation of the sphere. Then there are the two parts called the North and South, and the two called the Corners. At the union of these two, at the two poles, the movement goes through the sky. Half of it is different in its logic and position. [19] At the point of their union, the position is established by those who know the time. That which is in the South and North is the same, O gentle one. [20]

From there, by half-parts, rising toward the North and descending toward the South, and joining them with two pins, the sphere of the **Pravaha wind** is placed in the space between. [21] Using the two poles and the degrees of the earth, the circles of day and night should be made there, following the North and South directions. [22] Half of the meridian circle of that sphere... by the degrees... in the picture of the pivots... in the North and South direction...

<margin>10</margin> From the **Pole Star** <note>original: *dhruva*; the fixed North Star used as a reference point</note>, in the north, it should be joined; in the north, with the highest degrees, the **Celestial Sphere** <term>Celestial Sphere; original: *bhagola*; the imaginary sphere of stars surrounding the Earth</term> is first placed. That should be constructed by the wise using its two pivots. [23] Between the two **ecliptic poles** <term>ecliptic poles; original: *kadambaka*; the points around which the zodiac rotates</term>, at a distance of <gloss>80</gloss> degrees in this world, the **Zodiac** <term>Zodiac; original: *rashi-chakra*; the wheel of twelve signs</term> is placed in the sphere-instrument. Its twelve parts are the signs, made equal by three... [24]

Here, the tips of the **latitudes** <term>latitudes; original: *shara*; literally 'arrows', referring to the planetary distance from the ecliptic</term> must be extracted... the sphere which is the refuge of the countless stars. From the two ecliptic poles, the line of the diameter <note>original: *jinahasta-sutra*; likely referring to a specific measurement line in the instrument</note> [25] is the movement of the stars. At the position of the two poles, in the circle of stars, all is moved by the **wind** <note>original: *vayu*; specifically the *Pravaha* or 'provector' wind mentioned on the previous page</note> according to the arrangement of its own region. [26]

The **treatises** <note>original: *shiddhanta*; astronomical scientific texts</note> are to be made well-attainable. From the inclination of that position... the movement of the celestial circle is calculated from the mathematics. [27] Thus, from the refuge of the Celestial Sphere, through the two ecliptic poles, the moving sphere of the planets is formed. In the Celestial Sphere, the circles of the signs are all made as one. [28]

This is seen in its own form, situated in its own sphere. To understand the true nature of the spheres, the wise should construct **three spheres** <note>original: *golaka-traya*; likely representing the celestial equator, the ecliptic, and the local horizon</note> for the sake of perceiving the objects as previously stated. This is the "piercing of the sphere" <note>original: *gola-vedha*; the method of observational verification</note>. In various directions, places, and times, many instruments are produced according to the proportions of the observed reality. [29-30]

The mean and true positions, the local celestial sphere, are to be investigated. Through the varied motion of the **nodes** <note>original: *pata*; the points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> and other factors, [31] all these are produced from good calculations in a proper place, and they all rely on one principle. Through the reasoning of time, it is not possible to calculate everything perfectly. [30] <note>The numbering in the manuscript repeats 30.</note>

What belongs to one and what is to be given to another is not known even by the experts in the world. **Foolish people** <note>original: *mudhah*; a common polemic term for those who refuse to update their calculations</note>, out of pride, say that there is no **correction** <term>correction; original: *bija*; literally 'seed', a numerical adjustment applied to ancient formulas to match modern observations</term> required over time. [31]

By **Brahma, the Sun, and the Moon** <note>original: *Brahmarka-chandrai*; the three traditional sources of astronomical knowledge</note>, the creation was said to be stable, yet every moment it is perceived as distinct. Therefore, one must state the motion of one’s own "seed" <note>the *bija* correction</note>. When that is calculated, I do not speak falsely. [32]

Thus, in the latter half of the verse, as it is explained in detail, so is your expansion in the cities of the mountains. [33] Why do they specifically destroy... if the "seed" taken by the Creator would result in gross devotion? [34]

If it were fixed, then this knowledge would be firm through the instruments. Surely, from this, there is no exhaustion of the degrees anywhere in the measurement. [35] For the sake of attaining the "fruit" of the planets, perform the mathematics according to the logic. For the sake of wanting to see that, it should always be as something visible. [36]

What is seen in a **mirror** <note>original: *adarsha*; used here as an analogy for the reflection of celestial truth in an instrument</note> and so on is a varied sight. By looking with the eye, that which is visible through the instrument is the same as that seen in a mirror. [37] It might be the power of the eye, or not; it is from the union of the two. In those moments... [38]

Following the path ahead by the process of clearing obstacles, therefore by you... the reversal of the power in its own place... it becomes attached here.

Tattva-Viveka
15
</margin>

...having placed a mirror in a clean spot, one becomes focused on that location. [38] Because of its purity, and because the rays are without a body <note>original: *virashmiyo-pityā*; referring to the subtle, non-solid nature of light</note>, the rays simply pass through. Only where they strike, according to their own power in another place, are they grasped by the minds of embodied beings. [39] In that mirror, there is a specialized logic of reflection; the reflection of the rays is seen in the mirror. Those rays, having turned back by their own motion, are perceived as being attached to the solar disc itself. [40] Thus, the reflection is seen in the mirror, and similarly in water. This is a great philosophical consideration regarding the heart <note>original: *hṛdavasya*; implying a deep intellectual or intuitive understanding of optics</note> which has been explained in its own context. [41] Generally, visibility does not exist in the same way as the solar rays themselves, because sight arises from the eye. The solar rays that have struck a wall go along with that wall, which is why the object is visible. [42]

->**OM. Thus ends the Chapter on Mean Motion in the Siddhānta-tattva-viveka composed by the illustrious Kamalākara. OM.**<-

The imperceptible movement of the wandering planets in the sky has been described for the sake of determining good and bad results. However, that is not possible without the calculation of **Sines** <term>Sines; original: *jyā*; the primary trigonometric function in Indian astronomy, representing a half-chord</term>. Therefore, I shall now explain the computation of Sines along with its underlying principles. [1] Because even those Sines require operations such as finding **Square Roots** <term>Square Roots; original: *varga-mūla*; the basis for solving the Pythagorean theorem in chord calculations</term>, I, the best among those who know the principles, shall first explain that mathematical reasoning. [2]

The **Square** <term>Square; original: *varga*; the product of a number multiplied by itself</term> of two numbers is their product. It has been illustrated by the ancients through the digits. To find the square, place the number on the board. [3] Multiply the last digit by twice the others, and place the results in their respective places. Then, having set aside the last digit, the remaining process is repeated by those seeking the result. [4] The square of the last digit is placed above; the sum of all such operations becomes the total Square. By reversing this process, the knowledge of the **Root** <note>original: *mūla*; the square root</note> is achieved. [5]

Mark the places of the digits with horizontal lines, designating them as **Odd** and **Even** <note>original: *viṣama* and *sama*; referring to the traditional Indian algorithm for square roots which treats digits in pairs</note> in sequence. [6] Subtract the square of the last digit from the odd place; then, double that root and divide the next even place. Subtract the square of the quotient from the next odd place, repeating this again and again. [7] This process is the "Root of all" <note>original: *sarva-mūla*; the general algorithm</note> until the digits are exhausted. When the digits of the last place are combined, the resulting figure is called the Root. [8]

There are many numbers that are perfect Squares; that is well known. However, for those numbers that are **Non-Squares** <term>Non-Squares; original: *avarga*; numbers whose roots are irrational or not integers</term>, I now explain the method for finding their roots. [9] One should take the number whose square root is desired and multiply it by a very large, perceived "denominator" <note>original: *cheda*; here used as a multiplier to increase precision</note> that is itself a perfect square. [10] The square root of that product should then be divided by the root of the multiplier; this is known as the **Approximate Root** <term>Approximate Root; original: *āsanna-mūla*; a decimal or fractional approximation of an irrational root</term>. By using a very large multiplier, even more precise knowledge of the root is obtained by the wise. [11] Even if the chosen multiplier is small, it does not lead to a large error for common people. [12]

If the root is slightly more or slightly less than the true value, the following method is used for greater precision: [13] Divide the original number by the **Approximate Root**; the result is then averaged with that same approximate root. This result is what the wise call the **Precise Approximate Root** <term>Precise Approximate Root; original: *sphuṭā-āsanna-pada*; a refined value closer to the true irrational root</term>. [14] Thus, in mathematics, this has been explained by the sages for the sake of calculating the Sines of an arc...

<meta>This page continues the transition from the previous discussion into the technical mathematical foundations of the "Chapter on Sines." Having established the need for precise calculations for astronomical instruments, Kamalākara now explores the theory of square roots, specifically focusing on the problem of "non-square" numbers (irrationals) and how to approximate their roots using fractions.</meta>

### On the Calculation of Squares and Roots

In the calculation of those forms and colors, the two squares are produced. Again, in the method for finding the difference between those two squares, there is a doubling of the product of the two forms and colors. [18] When it stands as such, in the case of a negative term, it is the subtraction of the square from the square of the form; the difference is identified as having the nature of the consumed quantity. [18] From the multiplication by the two squares of sixteen, and then from what is obtained—when multiplied by sixteen—the result is sixty-four. [18]

The **square root** <term>Square root; *mūla*; the value which, when multiplied by itself, produces the original number</term> that is obtained includes a fractional part. After that, once the fractional part is divided by eight, the proximate root is found. [19]

Now, regarding the nature of roots of fractional non-squares: there is a confusion shown to those who have not reflected deeply on the nature of what is seen. [20] These methods were previously established by the words of the great masters **Aryabhata** and **Bhaskara**. [21] In the subject of square roots, there are those numbers which are of a "single nature" <note>original: *ekātmakam*; referring to perfect squares like 1, 4, 9</note> and those that are not. Those that are not linear in their root—whatever they may be—their squares become perfect squares, such as nine and others. [23]

Those numbers for which a "single-natured" root does not exist are the non-squares, such as two, three, five, eight, and so on. [24-25] Therefore, a perfect square has a two-fold root, while a non-square has a root that is essentially of a single, non-integer nature.

Now, among perfect squares, for those where we consider the method of bringing forth the root, the square of a quantity is considered. For those which are near-square quantities—such as five and others—one assumes an excess in the root. Because a perfect square was not seen previously, and a non-square is different from that, a square that is greater or lesser than the intended quantity is accepted. By taking a fractional part through intelligence, one attempts to make its square equal to the original. 

How can the true nature exist there? If there is an absence of such a thing, then listen: where the root of non-squares like 2, 4 <note>The text likely means 2 or 5, as 4 is a square</note>, or 5 is spoken of, it may be an integer or a fraction. Because of the nature of the square of an integer or the square of a square, a non-fractional root is impossible for these numbers. 

Now, a **fraction** <term>Fraction; *bhinna*; literally "broken" or "divided"</term>, when multiplied by itself, does not lose its fractional nature; this is proven by logic. Since the square of a fraction must necessarily be a fraction, it is possible for these numbers to have a fractional root. When a number divides another without remainder, its square also divides the square of that number without remainder. By that logic, if it does not divide without remainder, its square will also not divide the other's square without remainder. Thus, it is certain that in the square of a fraction, the fractional nature is not lost.

Consider this example: if one does not divide exactly, and in the previous part a result of 20 was obtained, with a remainder of 4. When multiplied by a small amount, say 250, and divided by that same amount, the result is 58. Thus, with the inclusion of the small fractional parts, the number becomes 27;8 <note>This notation indicates a whole number and a fractional remainder, typical of the sexagesimal or fractional style in Indian mathematics</note>. The square of this is 550;25. Here, if the fractional parts are not taken, the number is 29;8, and its square is 15/25. Here, as the word "fractional part" implies, if the square is not exactly eight, then it is not exact. One might say a fractional part is slightly more or slightly less than the truth, but even by taking successive fractional parts, one cannot perfectly reach the square.

<summary>Kamalākara discusses the mathematical theory of square roots, distinguishing between perfect squares and non-perfect squares (irrationals), and explaining why the roots of non-squares must be expressed as fractional approximations.</summary>
<keywords>Square root (mūla), Perfect Square (vargika), Non-square (avargika), Aryabhata, Bhaskara, Fractions (bhinna), Approximation, Tattva-Viveka</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical investigation into the nature of square roots and fractions. Having previously mentioned Aryabhata and Bhaskara, he now moves into a rigorous logical proof regarding why the square root of a non-square number cannot be a simple fraction. He utilizes the concept of "irreducible" (dṛḍha) fractions to show that if a fraction is in its simplest form, its square will never result in a whole number.</meta>

<page-num>16</page-num>
<meta>Marginal note in left margin: Tattva-viveka 18; Modern pencil marking: 16</meta>

### On the Impossibility of Rational Roots for Non-Squares

Because such a possibility is non-existent, it is not proper to imagine a fractional part by which a square would become an integer. This might be the case if, in the **square** <term>varga; the product of a number multiplied by itself</term> of a quantity possessing parts (a fraction), there were a "loss of parts" <note>Kamalākara is arguing that squaring a fraction doesn't magically make the fractional remainder disappear to produce a whole number</note>. But this is not so. In the square of a fractional quantity, the remainders do not vanish through the method of "row-arrangement" or by the rule of "multiplication of segments." 

<margin>Tattva-viveka 18</margin> If a square is divided by a divisor and is cleared without remainder, then the fractional parts would indeed be lost; however, such a loss of parts does not occur here. In that case, consider the group of one among those multiplied by twenty and doubled; it is not established. From the square of thirty, which is cleared without remainder by one, it is combined; therefore, a fractional part of eleven-sixtieths would emerge. Therefore, it is not at all proper to claim that in the square of any imagined fractional quantity, the loss of parts leads to a "partless" (whole) number.

The wise, for the sake of clear understanding, state the matter otherwise: where a **fractional dividend** <term>bhājya; the number to be divided</term> is greater than the divisor, its square is also necessarily greater than the divisor. This is not within our current subject of inquiry. Furthermore, it is well known that whether the **dividend** and **divisor** <term>hāra; the number by which one divides</term> are **reduced** <term>apavartita; simplified by a common factor</term> or unreduced, the resulting quotient remains the same. 

Therefore, since an unreduced fraction and a reduced fraction yield the same result, their squares must also yield the same result. Even if the dividend and divisor are not **irreducible** <term>dṛḍha; literally "firm," referring to a fraction in its lowest terms where the numerator and denominator are co-prime</term>, one can make them entirely irreducible by using a common factor. The investigation is as follows: if an irreducible dividend is divided by an irreducible divisor until no remainder remains, the head of the irreducible dividend is of the same class as the divisor. Because of this, reduction is possible. However, if they are irreducible, no further reduction is possible, and thus seeking a "result without remainder" is useless.

Now, consider the possibility in the square of an irreducible dividend composed of two segments. When dividing by an irreducible divisor until no remainder remains, the square of that consists of:
1. One part equal to the square of the irreducible divisor.
2. A second part consisting of the remainder of that irreducible dividend.
3. A third part which is the square of the seen remainder.

If, in the square of a dividend made of three segments, each segment or their sum is cleared without remainder by the square of the divisor, then the square of that fraction would be a whole number; otherwise, it is not possible. To explain: the first segment, being of the same class as the square of the irreducible divisor, would certainly be cleared by it. If the second segment were also sometimes cleared by it, the third segment—which is the square of the remainder—would remain. Because it is of a different class than the square of the irreducible divisor, it cannot be cleared. Thus, the square of an irreducible dividend consisting of three segments cannot be cleared without remainder. If there were a remainder when dividing the second segment by the square of the divisor, then even if that remainder and the square of the third segment’s remainder could not be cleared individually by the square of the divisor, there might be a possibility of clearing them without remainder when they are combined. However, this could never happen if the second segment...

<summary>Kamalākara presents a mathematical proof using the logic of irreducible fractions to demonstrate that the square of a fraction (in its lowest terms) can never result in a whole number, thus explaining the nature of irrational square roots.</summary>
<keywords>square root, varga, bhājya, hāra, dṛḍha, irreducible fractions, Tattva-viveka, rational numbers, Indian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical proof from page 16, where Kamalākara argued that squaring a fraction cannot result in a whole number because the "parts" (the denominator's influence) are never fully eliminated. Here, he extends this into the realm of "irreducible" fractions and concludes that the roots of non-square numbers must be understood geometrically rather than numerically.</meta>

### The Geometric Nature of Irrational Roots

In this context, the **square of the given remainder** <term>dṛṣṭa-śeṣa-varga</term> constitutes a third segment of the total square. This is joined with the **square of the given divisor** <term>dṛṣṭa-hara-varga</term> <note>The text uses both 'svara' and 'hara' interchangeably here to refer to the denominator or divisor</note> which is equal to the specific square of the second segment. Through a detailed investigation of the multiplier of the given remainder's square, we find the following: if there is a second segment of the same class as the given divisor, and it is multiplied by one or more parts of that divisor, then that part must also necessarily be of the same class as the given divisor.

Furthermore, if we consider the square of the divisor, it remains of the same class. If the third segment—which is the **square of the given remainder** <term>dṛṣṭa-śeṣa-varga</term> and of the same class as the divisor—is not divisible by the square of that divisor, it is because it is obstructed by the very nature of being a square. The remainder resulting from the first operation, multiplied by one or more parts, is not cleared by the square of the divisor. Because both possess these inherent properties, the third segment (the square of the remainder) cannot be reconciled as equal with the square of the divisor.

This is established because the given divisor, even when multiplied, remains what it is. Only if the third segment—the remainder's square—were equal to the divisor's square would a whole number result, but this is impossible in these cases. If the multiplier of the square of the given remainder were a number such that it was equal to the divisor, then the remainder would be the divisor itself, which contradicts it being a "remainder." Any square number formed by the product of two such squares <note>meaning the remainder squared and the divisor squared</note> would lead to a situation where the square root would have to be reducible by the divisor again.

Therefore, in the present case, we see that the remainder and the divisor are **irreducible** <term>dṛḍha; literally "firm" or "reduced to simplest terms"</term>. Since the square of the remainder is seen to be linked to the divisor, no matter what given remainder we search for, we will not find one whose square is perfectly cleared (divided) by the given divisor. 

Consequently, the square of the remainder in the third segment can never be equal to the square of the divisor in the second segment. Therefore, since the **square of the dividend** <term>bhājya-varga</term> is not cleared without remainder, it is different from a perfect square. Its root, being of a nature that is not a whole number or a fraction, is described as **geometric** <term>rekhātmaka; literally "consisting of lines"</term>.

This is just as in the case of a triangle where the base and the upright are of different types; even when they are combined, the junction of the **side** <term>bhuja</term>, the **upright** <term>koṭi</term>, and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> remains a geometric relationship. Just as a square is formed by the hypotenuse of a right-angled triangle, so too is a square formed by the difference of the side and upright. In this same way...

<summary>Kamalākara provides a mathematical proof that the square root of a non-square number cannot be a rational fraction, concluding that such roots are "rekhātmaka" or geometric lengths (irrationals).</summary>
<keywords>square root, irrational numbers, geometric lines, irreducible fractions, remainders, Kamalākara, Indian mathematics</keywords>

--- Page 36 ---
<meta>This page continues the mathematical inquiry from page 16, where Kamalākara explored the limits of rational numbers. Here, he transitions into geometry, providing a formal proof for the square of the hypotenuse (the Pythagorean theorem) and discussing the ontological status of irrational numbers—those "approximate roots" that exist as physical lines but defy perfect numerical representation.</meta>

# The Proof of the Square of the Hypotenuse

## Geometric Construction and the Area of a Square

The area of this figure consists of four triangles formed by the **base** <term>bhuja</term> and the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term>, combined with the area of the small internal square. Thus, it is established that the total area is equivalent to the sum of the squares. This is expressed as: Base 1, Perpendicular 1, Square of the Base 1, Square of the Perpendicular 1. From the base and perpendicular, by adding the two squares, the area of such a square is produced. 

<margin>Folio 17</margin> It is proved that from the sum of the squares of these two, the **square of the hypotenuse** <term>karṇa-varga</term> is the measure of the area. The logic that establishes the square of the hypotenuse as the sum of these two is well-known.

## The Problem of Non-Square Roots

Here it must be asked: in the case of the sum of these non-square values, does the "root" <note>the side of the square</note> of that area truly exist or not? If it is joined to the hypotenuse, then it is established. By the connection of the two lines of the base and perpendicular, the magnitude of the hypotenuse is determined. However, if the first result obtained is not a perfect square, then there is no "absolute" certainty in the numerical result. 

Therefore, without doubt, if we imagine a root for a **non-square area** <term>avarga-kṣetra</term> of a right-angled square, the hypotenuse line itself is the only "true" representation. In the pursuit of such knowledge, if the circumference or measure is not a whole number, then it is established through logic as being an "additional" or fractional value. It is known by the wise as a value consisting of units and parts, because it is impossible for the square of a chosen fractional value to exactly equal a non-square whole number. 

## The Nature of the Approximate Root

Whatever the **true root** <term>vāstava-mūla</term> prescribed by the scriptures might be, it is known only as an **approximate root** <term>āsanna-mūla</term> <note>literally "a root that has come near"</note>. The wise say that this is the only way to proceed, as there is no other path to the truth. Understand that the root of a non-square number is "broken" or fractional <note>original: "khaṇḍitam"</note>, not a whole number. Therefore, in mathematics, because a perfectly whole root is impossible for such numbers, its validity is recognized through its "nearness" or proximity to the truth. One should contemplate that the root is "near" because it is as close as possible to the actual physical line. 

Even if the numerical value of the hypotenuse, seen as a physical line <term>rekhātmaka</term>, is known to some extent, it lacks a perfect numerical definition when derived from the sum of the base and perpendicular. Thus, seeking knowledge through an "approximation" is entirely appropriate. This follows the logic that it is better to accept a near-truth than to suffer the total loss of meaning <note>A reference to the legal/logical maxim 'sarvanāśe samutpanne ardhaṃ tyajati paṇḍitaḥ': when total destruction is imminent, the wise man gives up half to save the rest</note>. This proximity is preferred in the practical use of fractions and divisions, through diligent effort. Even when using formal proofs, we find that the determination of the value remains fractional. For instance, just as people accept measures such as "one-half" or "one-third," the hypotenuse, as a physical line, is measured by the relationship between the base and the perpendicular, even when its root remains a non-square value.

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof of the Pythagorean theorem (the sum of the squares of the base and perpendicular equals the square of the hypotenuse) and discusses the concept of "approximate roots" (irrational numbers), arguing that they are physically real as lines even if they cannot be perfectly expressed as whole numbers.</summary>
<keywords>Pythagorean theorem, bhuja (base), koti (perpendicular), karna-varga (hypotenuse square), asanna-mula (approximate root), irrational numbers, geometry, Indian mathematics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the transition from the mathematical properties of the hypotenuse and irrational roots to their physical and philosophical implications. The author, Kamalākara, uses geometric proofs to challenge the "indivisible atom" theory held by the Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika school of logic (the Tārkikas), arguing instead for infinite divisibility. He then moves into a formal Euclidean-style series of definitions for lines and circles.</meta>

# The Refutation of the Indivisible Atom

## On Magnitude and Composite Parts

By the very measure of a magnitude that possesses qualities, there must be a flaw in the idea of a self-contained measure that cannot be divided. According to the reasoning regarding the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> and the sum of squares, the nature of being **composite** <term>sāvayavatva</term> <note>literally "having parts"</note> is perceived. Because of this, the proof of a "true" indivisible unit fails due to the lack of a fundamental, partless basis. 

If magnitudes are stated to be the finality of measurement, then the proof of magnitude itself is seen through its parts. Following the previous reasoning, **infinite parts** <term>anantāvayava</term> are established. Therefore, among these, because they possess the quality of "having parts," there is no point where the progression of parts ends. It cannot be said that there is a single, ultimate part that initiates all things.

## The Paradox of the Mountain and the Mustard Seed

If parts exist, then the distinction between "large," "small," and "extremely subtle" is made. It is observed that there is a difference in magnitude between **Mount Meru** <note>the cosmic mountain in Indian cosmology</note> and a **mustard seed** <term>sarṣapa</term>. One should not claim that this is merely an imaginary distinction. According to the logical arguments stated, even in the smallest parts, if we perceive two sub-parts, the same logic of divisibility applies, making the "indivisible" state impossible to maintain. 

If one were to assume a partless foundation, there would be no evidence for it. One might argue: "Does not the flaw of infinite regression apply equally to both the Mountain and the Seed if both are composed of infinite parts?" To this, we say: No. Their equality cannot be established simply by the word "infinity." Rather, it is the specific **magnitude** <term>parimāṇa</term> measured in those two masses that differs. Because of the specific measure of their parts, they are not equal. One does not find a fixed equality everywhere just because "infinity" is present elsewhere. Through these parts, the logical establishment of "large" and "small" is achieved.

## Critique of the Logicians

How is it that the **Logicians** <term>tārkika</term> <note>referring to followers of the Nyāya and Vaiśeṣika schools</note>, abandoning direct perception and clinging only to false inference, accept the **atom** <term>paramāṇu</term> as being **partless** <term>niravayava</term>? (20-21) 

A vast amount of time has been wasted by those who claim to know logic; because they do not understand the nature of **non-square roots** <term>avarga-mūla</term> <note>irrational numbers</note>, they have fallen into the hellish error of the "indivisible atom." (22) Furthermore, it is not contradictory for a magnitude to be divisible. Therefore, we should dismiss this contradictory obsession.

# Definitions of Geometry

## The Nature of the Line

That which always has length but never possesses width, and is extremely subtle, is known by the wise as a **line** <term>rekhā</term>. This is of two types: the first is the **curved** <term>vakra</term> and the other is the **straight** <term>sarala</term>. 

Where the action of the line is joined at the end, that point is invisible to the dull-witted and cannot be seen. A curve is that which moves in a threefold or varied way... 

<margin>Folio 18</margin> ...that which has neither [extra] length nor width [beyond its path]. (25) From a **center** <term>kendra</term>, by the intelligence of the mathematician, a line that always moves in the middle is drawn. That which is produced as the **radius** <term>vyāsārdha</term> <note>literally "half-diameter"</note> becomes the primary curved line [when rotated]. (26) 

## The Circle and the Sphere

When a cord is rotated equally all around, that is a **circle** <term>maṇḍala</term>. The line that stays in the middle of the circle, passing through the single center, is the cord-line. (27) This is known by the wise as the **diameter** <term>viṣkambha</term> or **width** <term>vyāsa</term> passing through the middle. A line that [partially] crosses the circle is perceived by the learned as a **chord** <term>jiyā</term>. (28) 

Thus, everything extending from the center according to the measure of the radius, forming a consistent mass, is a **sphere** <term>gola</term>. This is what should be contemplated...

<summary>Kamalākara refutes the Nyāya-Vaiśeṣika theory of indivisible atoms using geometric logic, arguing that if irrational roots (like the hypotenuse) exist, matter must be infinitely divisible. He then provides formal definitions for lines (straight and curved), circles, diameters, and spheres.</summary>

# Section 26: The Trigonometric Principles

<margin>T. Vi. 19</margin>
In the two operations performed, where the "arm" of the arc is established, 51
at the two ends of that given arc, the "bowstring" <term>siñjinī</term> <note>original: "siñjinī"—literally the string of a bow, the standard Sanskrit term for a sine or chord</note> is known as the Sine. When the hypotenuse is doubled, the arc of contact is established 52
there. Half of that arm-arc's segment, the half-chord, is called the Sine. From that calculation, the half-chord of the remaining arc is derived. 53
Through these Sines, the practical operations of geometry are traditionally remembered. Therefore, many types of "arms" <note>sides of a triangle</note> are calculated here in various ways. 54
As far as those lines extend horizontally and vertically, to that extent, they are considered Sines. Within this circle, the difference between those distances is noted. 55

From the vertical line, the **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> is derived in sequence. 
The **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> acts as the hypotenuse within the sector of the diameter <note>the quadrant</note>; it is the square root of the sum of the squares of the sine and cosine. 56
The square of the radius, when diminished by the square of the Sine <term>bhujajyā</term>, 
leaves the square of the Cosine. Indeed, the Cosine is the square root of that remaining value. 57
When those two—the Sine and Cosine—are established, they are placed in sequence. 
When one is subtracted from the radius, the opposite result is obtained. 58
That which resides between the Sine and the arc is the **Arrow** <term>bāṇa</term>, also known as the **Versine** <term>utkramajyā</term> <note>literally "the Sine in reverse order"</note>. In this way, the Sine and Versine are generated from the arms of the arc. 59

In any triangle, the square root of the sum of the squares of the Sine and Cosine is the hypotenuse. 
The "Arrow" <term>śara</term> <note>another term for Versine, measuring the height of the arc</note> is the result of those calculations. 60
The diameter is diminished by the Arrow, and from that, the Sine of the arm is derived. 
The square of twice the Sine, when subtracted from the square of the diameter, and its root taken, 61
results in a value that, when subtracted from the diameter and halved, gives the Versine. 
Alternatively, the square of the Sine divided by the Arrow, plus the Arrow itself, 
is certainly the length of the **diameter** <term>vyāsa</term>. 62

Or, if one obtains the Versine from the Sine, the Sine can be obtained from the Versine. 
The Versine's half is multiplied by the remaining diameter, and after seeing the result, 63
one can find the proof here: the Radius is twice the Sine of the half-arc. 
The diameter diminished by the Arrow is the sum of the Cosine and the Radius. 
The Arrow is the difference between the Cosine and the Radius. 
The difference of their squares is the square of the Sine. 
The root of that is the Sine <term>bhujajyā</term>; thus it is proven. 

Through these methods, the root of the product of the Arrow and the remaining diameter is the Sine. 
This is the calculation used by the masters. 
When the squares are considered, the difference between the square of twice the Sine and the square of twice the Radius 
results in the square of twice the Cosine. 
Twice the Radius minus twice the Cosine is twice the Versine. 
Following this logic, the identity of the Versine is confirmed. 
Furthermore, the square of the Sine is equal to the product of the sum and difference of the Cosine and the Radius. 
If divided by the Arrow, it yields the sum of the Cosine and Radius, which is the diameter minus the Arrow. 
Thus, it is proven that the diameter is the sum of the Arrow and that result. 
Just as in the mathematical "Infusion" <term>bhāvanā</term> <note>a method of composition/derivation used in algebra</note> of the greater and lesser roots, these values are established by the wise through logical derivation.

# Section 27: The Composition of Sines and Cosines

<margin>
T. Vi.
20
</margin>

<margin>
20
</margin>

Indeed, for calculations performed elsewhere, regarding the sum and difference of the arcs and their respective Radii: by the rule of "Composition," the Sines of the sum and difference of the arcs are established. Whether the arcs are within the same quadrant or have passed into another, the Sines are determined for the desired time or position. As the wise clearly understand from the two types of Composition <note>The "Additive" (Samāsa) and "Subtractive" (Antara) Bhāvanā</note>, this is not otherwise. I shall now describe this:

When the Sine of one arc is multiplied by the Cosine of the other, and divided by the Radius, the results are the Sines of the sum and difference of those two arcs. When the Sines and Cosines are cross-multiplied and then divided by the Radius, the sum or difference of those products gives the Sine of the sum or difference of the arcs. This method of calculation is made exceptionally clear through the "Composition" methods in the *Siddhānta Śiromaṇi* <note>original: "Siddhānta Śiromaṇi"—the crown jewel of treatises by Bhāskara II</note> by the lord of astronomers <note>referring to Bhāskara II</note>.

Later, the author of the *Marīcikā* <note>Muniśvara, who wrote the *Marīci* commentary</note> explains this based on the original roots. Just as there are "Lesser" and "Greater" roots in algebra <term>kaniṣṭha and jyeṣṭha</term> <note>These are auxiliary terms used in Pellian equations, here applied to trigonometry</note>, the **Sine** <term>bhujajyā</term> and **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> are treated as the Lesser and Greater roots. Through the established nature of Composition, the addition and subtraction of these roots allows for the calculation of the Sine of any desired arc. 

In this context, the Sine is defined as the Lesser Root, and the Cosine as the Greater Root. It is established that the square of the Sine subtracted from the square of the Radius results in the square of the Cosine. 

In the practical application of these rules <insert>to ensure the multiplier is correct</insert>, if we take the square of the Radius as the constant "additive" <term>kṣepa</term>, then the Sine and Cosine relationship perfectly mirrors the algebraic "Composition" of roots. The calculations are shown as follows:

| Component | First Arc | Second Arc |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Lesser Root** <gloss>Sine</gloss> | Sine 1 | Sine 2 |
| **Greater Root** <gloss>Cosine</gloss> | Cosine 1 | Cosine 2 |
| **Additive** <gloss>Square of Radius</gloss> | Radius² | Radius² |

Through the cross-multiplication of these: (Sine 1 × Cosine 2) and (Sine 2 × Cosine 1). Their sum or difference, when adjusted by the Radius, produces the Sine of the combined arc. Because the results are necessarily tied to the square of the Radius, one must divide by the Radius at the end to return to the standard scale. Thus, the Lesser Root <gloss>the new Sine</gloss> is perfected through the relationship with the Radius.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical derivation of the addition and subtraction theorems for Sines (sin(A±B)) using the "Bhāvanā" method of algebraic composition. It frames trigonometry through the lens of Pellian equations, citing Bhāskara II and Muniśvara as authorities.</summary>

<keywords>Bhāvanā, Composition, Sine, Cosine, Bhāskara II, Siddhānta Śiromaṇi, Marīci, Kaniṣṭha, Jyeṣṭha, Radius, Trigonometry</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical justification for the trigonometric "Composition" (Bhāvanā) rules. It moves from general principles to specific algebraic cases (Pellian-style equations) to prove how the Sines and Cosines of sums and differences are derived. The author is reconciling the methods of Bhāskara II with algebraic proofs.</meta>

As for that which was stated regarding the sum and difference of the products of Sines, when divided by the Radius: it follows that the Sine of the difference of two arcs is produced specifically from the difference of those products. This is established by the proof starting with "of the two arcs," and so on. In this very way, the Cosine <term>koṭijyā</term> <note>literally "the Sine of the complementary arc"</note> related to the sum and difference of the arcs is also established. However, calculating it in that manner involves a certain "heaviness" <note>original: "gauravāt"—mathematical complexity or tediousness</note>. For the sake of "lightness" <note>original: "lāghavāt"—mathematical elegance or brevity</note>, it is better known through the square of the Sine and the Radius.

Thus, by treating the Cosine as the "Lesser Root" <term>kaniṣṭha</term> and the Sine of the arm as the "Greater Root" <term>jyeṣṭha</term>, the Sine of the sum and difference of the arcs is obtained. Here, the logic is sound because the final Cosines and Sines of the unit arcs are the ultimate components. Thus, the explanation of the proof for the Sines of the sum and difference through "Composition" <term>bhāvanā</term> is provided with proper reasoning. Otherwise, if one were to say this "Composition" was not intended, then how would the words of Bhāskara <note>original: "Bhāskarīya vacana"—referring to the 12th-century mathematician Bhāskara II</note> be consistent? 

Therefore, that proof is achieved through the "Composition of Sums and Differences" <term>yogāntara bhāvanā</term> via a different path. To explain: in various technical contexts, the square of the Radius <term>trijyā varga</term> minus the square of the Sine is the square of the Cosine. 

Here, four cases or "positions" <note>original: "pakṣa"</note> are established:

1. One position is where the Radius is the "Greater Root," the Cosine is the "Lesser Root," and the square of the Sine is the "Additive" <term>kṣepa</term> <note>The constant term in a quadratic indeterminate equation</note>.
2. A second position is where the square of the Radius minus the square of the Cosine equals the square of the Sine; here, the Radius and Sine are the roots and the square of the Cosine is the Additive.
3. The third position is that the sum of the squares of the Sine and Cosine equals the square of the Radius. Here, the Sine and Cosine are the roots, and the square of the Radius is the Additive.
4. Or, the fourth position is where the Cosine is the Lesser Root, the Radius is the Greater Root, and the square of the Sine is the Additive.

Through the "Composition of Sums and Differences" of the Lesser Roots, Greater Roots, and Additives of their respective classes within these four cases, the different paths for reaching the desired Sines and Cosines are realized. For the sake of brevity, one might divide by the square of the twentieth part, which also yields the Sine of the difference of the unit arcs. 

Why is this not accepted in its literal form? Because the relationship to the Sine is the same in the "Composition of Sum and Difference." We do not merely desire a relationship with the Sine; rather, our goal is the nature of the Sine of the unit arc as the Lesser Root. Therefore, by whatever factor the square of the Radius is divided, the square root of that divisor is used to divide the Lesser Root, which we call the Sine of the difference of the arcs. 

If one speaks of the nature of the Sines and Cosines through internal composition, the resulting Sine of the difference of the arcs is declared by the wise to be pure and refined. Indeed, rules for arcs are not capable of creating rules for Sines without the "Composition of the Sines of Sums and Differences." 

The statement regarding the difference of the Sines of two arcs is different from this; it lacks a proving factor. In the third type of Composition mentioned, the sum and difference are calculated through the squares...

Two values exist as a single **Lesser** and **Greater** pair. There, by another additive, the Lesser and Greater Roots are calculated in relation to a desired unstated additive. **First Additive × Second Additive = [New] Additive**
1. (Nature × First Lesser Root²) + First Additive = First Greater Root²
2. (Nature × Second Lesser Root²) + Second Additive = Second Greater Root²

The product of these: 
(Nature² × First Lesser² × Second Lesser²) + (First Lesser² × Nature × Second Additive) + (Second Lesser² × Nature × First Additive) + (First Additive × Second Additive) = (First Greater² × Second Greater²)

In this case of the product of the squares, when it is combined with the product of the additives, it becomes great. When divided by the **Nature**, it is exactly what is desired. In the sum of the desired Lesser Roots, this is what is desired: the product of the **Nature** and the Lesser Roots plus the product of the Greater Roots. For the purpose of knowing the **Sines** <term>jyā</term> of the desired arcs <term>iṣṭa-cāpa</term>, and for the sake of establishing the Sine of the sum and difference of two arcs, the Sine of one is added to the Sine of the other. One Sine shall be the sum; by subtracting and adding the subtractor and the remainder, it becomes well-known. Therefore, the difference between them is sought. 

In the absence of one Sine, it is equal to the Sine of that day. When these two Sines are considered, in the absence of the **Sine of the Base** <term>bhuja-jyā</term> <note>The Sine of the vertical arc or the first component of the calculation</note>, its **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> <note>Literally the "Sine of the complement," corresponding to the modern cosine function</note> is at its maximum. When the Sine of the other is at its maximum, the difference is found; but in the absence of that, due to the absence of the absence, there is an absence of the Cosine of the other. There, the Sine is at its maximum. Therefore, for the Sine of their sum, having subtracted the Cosine of the difference, the Sine of the other is measured by its own Sine. This is established by the difference spoken of for the two. 

Indeed, there, the established result is not zero; rather, it is joined to the difference. In that established state, that very precise Sine of the difference, which is the multiplier, is changed by the mentioned method. Because of the impropriety of the places of sum and difference through the reversal of the subtractor and the subtracted, without the algebraic operation <term>bīja-kriyā</term>, the **Radius-sine** <term>trijyā</term> <note>The Sine of 90 degrees, often used as the constant "R" in Indian trigonometry</note> is obtained by the sum of the "Gross" <term>sthūla</term> and "Precise" <term>sūkṣma</term> differences. 

By knowing the Cosine of the day in order, and the Sine of the precise sum, the difference of that is the Sine of the difference spoken of for those two. That should be subtracted from the Sine of the precise sum measured by the Radius. The result will be the precise Sine of the sum measured by the different Cosine. From the difference measured by the "Gross Sum Sine" diminished by the "Precise Sum Sine," that difference is desired by me as the Sine of the other measured by the Radius. In order to calculate the desired difference, the Sine of the other is known here. By this, the difference between the "Gross" and "Precise" sums regarding the Sine of the other is determined.

->**The Proof of Success is Light**<-

"Success is indeed light," <note>original: "jyoh siddhireveti"</note> spoken with the intention of victory. Having explained the meaning according to that intention, the errors of those wandering from the true nature of things are dispelled through a **Second Proportion** <term>dvitiya-anupata</term>. Here, this brightness is achieved through the ease of demonstrating the proof. In the absence of the "shadow" <note>original: "chaya" — often referring to the gnomon shadow or a trigonometric function like the Tangent</note> of another text, there exists a different shadow. 

Where there is an absence of the **Sine** <term>jya</term>, the **Cosine** <term>kotijya</term> reaches its maximum value, being measured by the **Radius** <term>trijya</term> <note>This describes the trigonometric identity where if the Sine of 0° is 0, the Cosine of 0° is equal to the Radius (1 in modern terms)</note>. Since a single Sine measured by something other than this is not obtained, one asks: what is obtained by the desired Cosine? The result is an extremely subtle single Sine. This is proven through **Agreement and Difference** <note>original: "anvaya-vyatireka" — the logical principle of presence and absence</note>. In the opinion of other texts, that subtle single Sine is also obtained as being very precise. 

There exists a single Sine measured by a different Cosine; in the interval between them, there is a difference measured by that different Cosine. When that is present, the single Sine which is added becomes measured by the different Cosine. To achieve this within the text, a **Proportion** <term>anupata</term> is used: if the difference measured by the Radius is known, then for the desired Sine, what would it be? The result is a mixture of the extremely subtle Sines. This is established through the logical steps of the previous proportion.

In the same way, in the calculation of other Sines, the difference between the two Sines is the root. By reversing the subtrahend and the subtractor, this is established. Because of the absence of the Cosine of the precession <note>original: "ayanantara-kotijya"</note>, it remains unproven by the stated proportion. Even if that light has arisen, it is not joined with a difference of Sines measured by zero. Where there is a difference between the subtle Sines, and the arc is measured by a different Cosine, the difference between the Sines should not exist. This is clear.

Therefore, we state the following regarding the geometric construction: On level ground, draw a circle with a diameter equal to the **Radius** <term>trijya</term>. Mark the circle with the divisions of minutes <term>kala</term>. Divide it into four quarters and mark the East, West, South, and North. From the East-West line, in a **Clockwise** <term>savya</term> direction, draw the Sine of a small arc; in a **Counter-clockwise** <term>apasavya</term> direction, draw the Sine of a large arc. 

According to the rule of the wise, the Cosine associated with that arc should be drawn extending to the North-South line. Then, from the point where the Sine of the small arc meets the East-West line, one should draw a line to the end of the Cosine of the small arc. That distance to the North-South line is the Cosine of the arc's difference. This is the method for clearly calculating the Sines of both small and large arcs. By determining the difference between the Cosines, which forms one side of a triangle, the **Hypotenuse** <term>karna</term> is produced. This Hypotenuse can also be formed from the Sine and Cosine associated with a single arc.
