->**The Derivation of Sines and Cosines**<-

The second part consists of the **Square of the Second Sine** <term>dvijyā-varga</term> and the **Square of the Second Cosine** <term>dvikoṭi-varga</term>. The product of these is equal to the square of the square of the **Radius** <term>trijyā — the "Total Sine," often a fixed value in Indian astronomy used as the radius of the calculation circle</term>. <note>In modern terms, this relates to the identity $(R \sin \theta)^2 + (R \cos \theta)^2 = (R^2)^2$ when scaled</note>. In this manner, four distinct segments are established as being equal to one another.

Through the equality of the positive and half-segments, from the remainder, the **Squares of the Sines of a single arc** are derived. These consist of the First Sine, the Second Cosine Square, the First Sine, the Second Sine, and the First Cosine. <note>original: "ā-jyā-dvi-ko-va" — abbreviations for ādyajyā (first sine) and dvitīyakoṭijyā (second cosine)</note>. Here, the middle segment is twice the product of the two Sines and two Cosines. <insert>It is distinct</insert> and is equal to twice the product of the Sine and Cosine of the arcs. 

Similarly, the **Sine of a single arc** is the square root of: the First Sine, the Second Cosine Square, the Second Sine, and the First Cosine. <insert>vidya — knowledge/method</insert>. Thus, the desired **Cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> multiplied by the Sine is proven. 

<margin>N.Vi. 23</margin> Regarding the calculation of the **Sine of the difference between two arcs** <term>antara-cāpajyā</term>, one must consider the fixed lines of the Radius. There, the **Sines** <term>bhujajyā</term> of the two arcs located on the circumference should be set in the same direction. From these, the two corresponding **Cosines** are determined. This is the nature of the calculation. In this context, regarding the Sine of the arc of difference, the "base" is the Sine and the "upright" is the difference between the two Cosines. The **Hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> is the diagonal line derived from these two. 

In this matter, the Sine and Cosine of the arc of difference are also possible through the interaction of the base and the upright. To achieve this, one should draw a line on the Second Sine from the center. From that same fixed line, like the determination of the Sine, a second Hypotenuse should be drawn. That is the **Versine** <term>utkramajyā — the "inverted sine" or 1 - Cosine</term> of the arc of difference. 

The distance between the base of that line and the end of the Hypotenuse is the measure of the **Versine of the arc of difference**. This serves as the "upright." From these two, the Hypotenuse established previously is produced. This is visible in the diagram where the Hypotenuse is the segment of the arc of difference. Even from the knowledge of the Versine related to the Sine of the arc of difference, the result is accomplished by the rule of "half the square of the Radius multiplied by the Versine." 

Therefore, from the previously known "base" and "upright," even from the Sine of the segment of the resulting arc, the Sine of the arc of difference is proven through the inverse method. This is just like the calculation of the Sine of a single arc. When the segments are arranged, the Versine of the arc of difference is also found. Using the **Radius** <term>vyāmsārdha — half the diameter</term> as the measure, the directions are marked. 

The Sine belonging to the **Full Chord** <term>sampūrṇa-jya</term> of the arc is doubled to find the desired arc. Half of that is the Sine belonging to the arc. By this method, the practice of using Sines in calculations is established. Now, regarding the division, the total chord belonging to the full arc is multiplied by the Radius... <unclear>illegible</unclear>... thus the full arc is marked.

### The Proof of the Sine-Generation Rules

Its proof arises from the mutual relationship of the **cosines** <term>koṭijyā — the "upright sine" or the horizontal component of an arc</term> and the desired sines. For that purpose, and for the sake of clarity in a well-drawn sphere-circle, consider each angle in a sequence of two arms. 

One **sine** <term>bhujajyā — literally "the arm-sine," representing the vertical component</term> is always formed by an extremely small angle. In the triangle formed by the other hypotenuse, for the boundary of that arm, from the **unequal angle** <term>viṣamakoṇa — an oblique or non-right angle</term>, the hypotenuse in the cosine is its own. This is what I have determined. 

Thus, the three sine-strings of the arc are also marked in a diagram of a circle with a radius equal to **half the Radius** <term>trijyārdha — half the standard calculation radius</term>. First, one should draw the East-West line. Then, from the point known as the East, in one direction, using the **North-shadow** <term>uttaracchāyā</term>, the previous sines should be marked. The cosine resulting from them should be extended to the Western point. From there, a transverse line representing the sine of the further arc should be drawn. 

Thus, two triangles are shown in the demonstration: one where the base is the **small sine** <term>laghu-bhujajyā</term> and the **cosine** <term>koṭijyā</term> of the earth-sphere radius, and another where the arms are the **sine** and **cosine** of the further arc. From these two triangles, the calculation of the **intercepts** <term>ādhā — the segments on the base</term> is performed as previously stated. In the intersection of the three directions, the difference in the horizontal intercepts and the vertical sines, as well as the difference in cosines, results in the **sine-hypotenuse** <term>jyā-karṇa</term>. From these, the derivation of the further sine of the arc is achieved through the method of **sine-segments** <term>khaṇḍa-jyā</term>. 

In this regard, there are many methods—six paths for those of sharp intellect. Now, following the vertical and horizontal rows, the sines and cosines are calculated through a transverse multiplication known as the **Diamond-Like Composition** <term>vajra-bhāvanā — a method of cross-multiplication used to derive the sine of the sum of two arcs</term>. This is self-evident. Just as the "Lesser Composition" is used in worldly calculations for solving equations, this **Sine Composition** <term>jyā-bhāvanā</term> is taught here for the sake of proving the sine of a single combined arc. 

This has been explained not just as the nature of Sine Composition, but its proof is also established through the methods of **Algebraic Composition** <term>bījagaṇitīya-bhāvanā — rules for combining solutions to quadratic equations, specifically the Pell equation</term>. Thus, the difference between the sines of a combined arc is made clear to the wise. This second method of proving Sine Composition is also established by its own nature. This should be understood by scholars with even a little effort. For those who follow the intentions of the Universal Ruler, this proof of the combined arc is declared. In this small demonstration of our own composition, we offer a handful of water and flowers <note>This is a traditional Sanskrit closing metaphor (añjali), signifying the completion of a dedicated task</note>.

Now, I shall explain the movement of the **Sine** <term>dorjīva — another term for bhujajyā</term>: 

In a triangle, the sine of the degrees of the arm is five. The measure of the arm and cosine is multiplied by ten and divided by the **Total Radius** <term>trijyā</term>; this results in the primary multiplier. The sine of the arm and the sign are divided. When the square of the result is subtracted, the math-trained expert finds the **Enlivener** <term>sañjīvinī — likely a specific rule or auxiliary formula</term> of the degrees of the arms. 

When these are mutually multiplied, the difference of the cosines of the two... <unclear>nu-ro</unclear>... multiplied by two and divided by the radius... the sine of the arm is derived... the square of the square of the arm-sine multiplied by the king... when subtracted from the sum of the squares... the square root is the remainder of the Sine.

### The Calculation of the Sine and the Fifth Part

Divided by the square of three, the result obtained for the **sine of the fifth part of the arm** <term>pañcāṃśaka-bhujājīvā — the sine of 1/5th of a specific arc, likely 18° or 72° depending on the context of the quadrant</term> is calculated in reverse. From the square root of the sum of the squares of the **sine** <term>kramajyā</term> and the **versed sine** <term>utkramajyā</term>, this demonstrated value becomes the **Sine** <note>original: "siñjinī", a synonym for "jyā" derived from the Arabic 'jib' or 'sinus'</note>.

Alternatively, it is the square root of half the product of the **Radius** <term>trijyā</term> and the **versed sine**. The sine of half of ten <note>i.e., the fifth part</note> is produced by the difference of the squares of the inverse sines, divided by the versed sine.

If the parts are greater than the "nine-sky" <note>the number 90, representing the degrees in a quadrant</note>, then the true value is found by subtracting 90. The **true** <term>sphuṭa — the "corrected" or accurate value</term> sine is obtained through repeated calculation. From that, the segment of the sine is multiplied by the square of the **Radius** or by the square of the half-diameter. By subtracting the result from the square of the arm-arc, the square of the sine of the fifth part is determined. 

One should divide the square of the fifth part by the square of the **sine of three signs** <term>tribhajyakā — the sine of 90 degrees, which is equal to the Radius</term>. Subtracting the resulting fraction from the sine, the remaining value is the sine of the fifth part. By this method of calculation, the sines of the various parts are established. This is the rule for finding the sines of arcs such as three degrees and so on.

### The Geometric Proof using Triangles

Now, the method of proof for these rules is as follows: In a geometric figure <note>the "field" or kṣetra</note> constructed using half the **Radius**, the remaining portion of the **cosine** <term>koṭijyā — the "upright" component</term> is doubled. Thus, it takes the form of the **base** <term>bhuja</term> and the **upright** <term>koṭi</term>.

<note>The diagram here illustrates the relationship between the chords of a circle and the right-angled triangles formed by the radius and the sine/cosine components.</note>

The **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> is formed by the two of them. This forms a **right-angled triangle** <term>jātya-kṣetra — a "legitimate" or standard triangle</term>. In a second instance, where the sine of the arm and the cosine are the base and upright, the Radius serves as the hypotenuse. From the sum of the base and upright, a perpendicular line is dropped to the ground of the hypotenuse; that is the sine of the desired arcs. By the ratio of these similar triangles, the calculation of the sine is perfected. Therefore, the sine of the three-sign arc <note>90 degrees</note> is the base, and the doubled Radius is the hypotenuse. In this way, the sines and cosines of the desired portions are clearly discerned.

When the value of the difference of the squares of the **cosine** <term>koṭi — the vertical side or cosine of an angle</term> and the other side is divided by the measure of the side, and that is multiplied by the measure of the **cosine** and doubled by ten, then the **sine** <term>jyā — the half-chord or sine of an angle</term> of the single sign <note>30 degrees</note> is joined with half the **cosine-sine** <term>koṭijyā — the cosine</term>.

By dividing these two, the two sines produced from the half-segments are obtained in order. Thus, the new **sine** of the difference of the **sine** and **cosine** is produced.

By this calculation, the **cosine** of the divided part is the result of the difference. Because the sine of the divided part is established here, the proof is as follows:

# Calculations for Ritual Altars

In the case of the elevated pit <note>original: "tauccādye-kuṇḍe"</note>, for its quadrilateral <term>caturbhuja — a four-sided shape</term> form, by taking the approximate square root <term>āsannamūla — a method for calculating roots of non-perfect squares</term>, the width <term>vyāsa</term> is **23|58|30**. For a quadrilateral of one cubit <term>hasta — a unit of measurement roughly 18-24 inches</term>, it is correctly written and expanded in other texts. By squaring and dividing by five, the desired circular pit's width is produced from that root. Taking the approximate root, the width is **27**. For one cubit, the square of the area is **23|37|9**. Following the sequence of the area's own calculations, multiplied by thirty-two and divided, the result of the squared area is produced from the root. By taking the approximate root, the measurements are **19** finger-breadths <term>aṅgula — a unit approximately the width of a finger</term> or **28**.

In a seven-sided <term>saptavibhuja — a heptagon</term> pit, according to the square of its area and divided by its own direction, the side <term>bhuja</term> is derived from the root. That figure should be combined with its own parts to determine the width. Now, having divided the square of the area, the width is found from that root. There, in a pit of one cubit, the side should be **12|28**. The width is approximately **58|00** as derived from the root. This is the method for the pit of that specific shape.

Just as with the seven-sided pit, for the one-hand pit, the side should be **12|46** and the width **22|50**. This is one method for the vulva-shaped pit <note>original: "yoni-kuṇḍa" — a ritual pit shaped like a leaf or a heart, symbolizing the womb of the earth</note>. Alternatively, for the area of the vulva-shaped pit, from the square of the base, **16|00**, multiplied and then divided by **32|23**, the width is derived from the root. The side is derived from the square. For a one-hand width, the measurement is **2|52** and for the other part **17|58**. This is the second method for the vulva-shaped pit.

In a similar way, for a hexagonal <term>ṣaḍasra — a six-sided shape</term> pit, the square of its area is multiplied by three. From that root, the measurement of the side is produced. By another method, the square of the area is divided and the width is determined; that may be either the side or the width. By taking the approximate root for a one-cubit pit, the two sides are **12|53** and the width in finger-breadths is **22|48**. Those who are skilled in the science of architecture <term>śilpa — traditional arts and construction</term> know this well.

In the same way, for an octagonal <term>aṣṭāsra — an eight-sided shape</term> pit, the area is multiplied by seventy-five and divided by fifty-three; from that root, the side is surely found. The multiplier is **9|23|50** <note>These are sexagesimal units: 9 units, 23 minutes, 50 seconds</note>. That multiplier determines the width and the side here in the eight-cornered shape. For a one-cubit octagon, from the closest approximate root, the side is **10|38|1** and the width is **10|55**.

The same logic should be applied to the sixteen-sided pit. Now, for the lotus-shaped <term>padma — a circular pit with petal designs</term> pits, multiplying by their own areas and dividing by **3600** and **44|29**, the width is found from that root, and the side is found as in the octagon. For a one-cubit lotus pit, the width is **21|10|25** and the other measure is **8|16|38**. By these multipliers, for the three-fold octagonal machine-pit <note>original: "aṣṭayantra-trayama-kuṇḍa"</note>, the multipliers are **0|6|17**. When the area is multiplied by these and the root is taken, the side is produced. The width of that octagon is determined by its own multiplier. There, for a one-cubit measure, the side is **7|32|55** and the width is **20|3|12**. Thus, with the expanded results, for the beautiful pit, multiplying by **780** and dividing by **87**, the width is found here. The multipliers are **0|35|16**.

# Calculations for Ritual Altars

For the heptagonal pit <note>original: "saptavāra-tribhujaḥ" — literally seven-sided, though sometimes referring to specific layered geometries</note>, following the calculated root, the value for the excellent pit is **1200**. For a pit of one cubit <term>hasta — a standard ritual unit of measure, approx. 18-24 inches</term>, the side <term>bhuja</term> and diameter <term>vyāsa</term> are determined by the sum of the parts.

Thus, for the seven-sided pit, the multiplier is **10 | 32 | 15 | 5**. The diameter and side for a one-cubit measurement are **13 | 13 | 3 | 12**.

The diameter is **21 | 11 | 56**. Now, for a one-cubit pit, by the approximate method, the diameters are **31 | 12** and **13 | 16**. In the seven-sided pit, according to the experts in area <note>original: "phalavide" — those who know the results or areas</note>, the calculation is **323** divided by the square of fifteen <term>tithivarga — 225, as 15 is the number of lunar days (tithis) in a fortnight</term>. The diameter and side should always be twelve finger-breadths and forty-five units **12 | 45** <term>aṅgula — a finger-breadth</term> for the fire pit.

## The Method of Construction

Now, the method for achieving these shapes:
In a **circular pit** <term>vṛttakuṇḍa</term>, the standard measurement is half of its own diameter. 
In a **crescent-moon pit** <term>ardhacandra — semi-circular</term>, the measurement is its own diameter... <note>The text here describes the transition of shapes from circles to polygons of six, seven, or eight sides.</note>

To expand the pits to six, seven, or eight sides, one should draw a circle and mark the respective divisions. Each part should be marked from the circumference of the circle so that the full diameter reflects the desired shape of the pit. The sides should be attached to the circle; having seen them there, the patron of the sacrifice <term>yajamāna — the person sponsoring the ritual</term> gains merit through these qualities. The divisions of the circle are the primary tools for establishing the sides. Through knowledge of these divisions, the ends of the sides are established.

By dividing equally and marking those points, one prepares the **womb-shaped pit** <term>yonikuṇḍa — a pit shaped like a leaf or vulva, symbolizing the source of creation</term>. Drawing three lines from the heart of the shape, or by making a half-rotation of the compass <note>original: "arddha-bhramaṇād" — referring to drawing an arc with a string and peg</note>, one should draw a circle and a half-circle. Where the ends of those sides meet, lines should be drawn. The remainder becomes the womb-shaped pit. This first type is shaped like a leaf-half.

Following the same measurement for a four-sided pit, from the desired corner, the sides should be increased from the half-circle. This creates the womb-shaped pit. From the previous diameter and sides, the group of pits is calculated. There, with a half-triangle, one should increase from the center. In all pits, the width should be made according to their respective shapes. By these two methods, the patron calculates the finger-breadths. Other pits are produced according to their own shapes.

For the primary pits, one should project **1** <note>original: "jino" — possibly a symbolic number or specific unit</note> and calculate with finger-breadths according to the form. From the width mentioned previously, one should calculate the two sides. One should draw the two sides of the semi-circle correctly. Divided into four parts, or by the third and fourth divisions, and by the measure of half that part, a circle is produced through rotation. When it lies outside the circle, one should draw lines from those divisions. That section of wood <note>likely referring to the ritual staff or boundary</note> should be bent on all sides. The pit, consisting of eight parts, is known as the "Universal" pit <note>original: "paryākhyeya"</note>...

## Geometric Principles of the Square Pit

## The Crescent Moon and Triangle Pits

Now, for the **crescent moon pit** <term>ardhacandra-kuṇḍa</term>, the area is calculated by doubling the result. From that doubled area, one half is taken. From that doubled area, the calculation is performed: the square of the doubled area <term>varga</term> is multiplied by eight and then divided. The root of this becomes the measure of the diameter <term>vyāsa</term>. 

Next, for the **equilateral triangle** <term>samatribhuja</term>, two similar triangles are formed on both sides. In reverse, or by their union, the area is found. The area is already known as desired. In that case, the side <term>bhuja</term>, half-side, and altitude <term>koṭi</term> produce the area.

Therefore, the square of the area is divided by the sides to find the root, which becomes the side. In a square-shaped pit, the side is doubled. This is the rule for the divisions. In the case of the equilateral triangle, the root is multiplied by three. By this inverse method, the square of the side is divided by twelve to find the proportion of the division. For example, in the "Sun-hand" (12-finger) measure, the previously mentioned side has a square of 15. The root of this, multiplied and divided, yields the area of 25. ### Calculations for the Triangle and Octagon

If the sides are equal, it is not divided into two parts like half of an octagon or half of a square. Its area <term>phala</term> is already known from what was written in the table. In that case, let the setting be as follows: the measure of the side itself is the unknown quantity <term>yāvantāvat</term> <note>original: "yā 1" — 'yā' is the standard abbreviation for 'yāvantāvat,' used in Indian algebra exactly like the modern 'x' to denote an unknown variable.</note>. 

The area of this equilateral triangle <term>sama-tribhuja</term> is the square of the unknown <note>original: "yā va" — shorthand for 'yāvantāvat-varga,' or $x^2$.</note> multiplied by the values 4;83;55. For the two halves of an octagon, the area is the square of the unknown multiplied by 3;2;115. For the equilateral triangle, the calculation is the square of the unknown multiplied by 5;53;38. This is the successful result. 

With this method, having added them together, we have the square of the unknown multiplied by 5;53;38. To find the area of the triangle, the coefficients are multiplied by the sun-measure <note>original: "arka" — the number 12</note>, resulting in the multipliers 4800 and 311.

The second method for determining the multipliers should be known as follows: <insert>the square of the unknown 5;5</insert>. In a quadrilateral situated within the diagonals, the area of the two sides adjacent to one corner is the same as the area of three such quadrilaterals. This is known from the writing in the table. In that case, according to the rule, the area itself is the measure of the diagonal of the quadrilateral. 

Let the measure be the unknown <term>yāvantāvat</term> (1). The area of half the square of this is the square of the unknown multiplied by 1/2. The square root of this is the side of the quadrilateral <term>bhuja</term>. Therefore, the area there is the square of the unknown multiplied by 1;41;42. The sum of the two areas is the square of the unknown multiplied by 1;91;42. 

By equating this with its own result, we have the square of the unknown multiplied by 1;91;42. Here, by doubling the result for the multipliers, we get the coefficients <insert>six-fold multiplier 1800</insert> and 3600. 

In the equilateral triangle, the same rule applies to the hand-measure <term>hasta</term>. There, for one equilateral triangle, the square of the stated area is the square of the unknown multiplied by 3. The root of this is the total area in the "six-born" <note>original: "ṣaḍja" — likely referring to a hexagon or a specific six-part geometric division</note> calculation. Or, for one who knows the square of six, the square of the total area is the square of the unknown multiplied by 27. Therefore, by the reverse method, the square of the area is divided by twenty-six <insert>258</insert>. The root of that is the measure of the side, and half of that is the actual side. 

The square of that, divided by sixteen, gives the area <insert>81/16</insert>. This is the square of the area doubled... Thus, for an octagon <term>aṣṭāsra</term>, the sine <term>jyā</term> of the half-part of twenty-two degrees and thirty minutes (22° 30') <note>original: "sārdha-dvāviṃśati-bhāga-jyā" — This is a precise trigonometric value for constructing a regular octagon, as 360° / 16 = 22.5°.</note> is used.

The side is doubled. There, the square of the sine of the "arrow-veda" <note>original: "śara-veda" — using the Katapayadi system where 'śara' (arrows) = 5 and 'veda' = 4, referring to a specific numerical value or degree</note> part is 5. The root of this is the sine of the "arrow-veda" part... from the radius <term>vijyā</term>... the versine <term>utkrama-jyā</term> of the "arrow-veda" parts is 17;36. 

If the measure of the diameter <term>vyāsa</term> is the unknown (1) <insert>the unknown 3</insert>, and the side is half the diameter (1), then by the operation of the square-divider, the radius multiplied by the sine is 17;36. Half of that is 8;48. The sine of the two-and-a-half part is a quarter... 0;3;59. The root of that is twice the sine of the two-and-a-half part. Now, the octagonal form is 45;59. 

The sine of the side of the octagon is 0;22;57. Here, one side is a side, and two sides are as mentioned. From that triangle-like shape, the octagon is formed. There, the single area is the square of the unknown multiplied by 5;14. The total area of the octagon is the square of the unknown multiplied by 42;124. Therefore, the area divided by 42;124 and its square root will be the diameter in an octagon. Through this practice, the area should be multiplied and divided by the parts of the triangle... as stated for the two octagons. Here, by the divisions of the parts, the area of the octagon is explained in one way.

Through this, for the sake of correctly demonstrating the results, [this] instrument is used <note>original: "memaiṣa-yantra", possibly a variant of a "Meṣa" or Ram-instrument, or a scribal error for a specific measuring device</note> for the ritual pits <term>kunda: a consecrated fire pit used in Vedic rituals</term>. Here, for the half-diameter, the particular knowledge for the wise is 1.6. This concludes the **Inquiry into Ritual Pits**.

Now begins the **Classification of the Method for Deriving Sines**. Having performed the direct calculation of sines <term>jīvā: the chord-half or sine, a fundamental unit in Indian trigonometry</term> and making the radius sixty, the direct sine is expanded for practical mathematical use.

In the derivation of these sines, whatever value has reached the sequence of the table is established. Even without the sequence, the astronomers, in their mastery of sine-mathematics, can determine the value at any specific time. The difference between the elapsed and the upcoming segment <term>bhogya-khaṇḍa: the "to-be-traversed" portion of the sine table</term> is multiplied by the arc and divided by sixty. By adding that to the established sine, the [precise] sine is found. This is considered the half-chord.

The sine is cleared [of fractions]. The portion of the sine that consists of arc-degrees is established. The remainder is multiplied and divided by the difference between the sines, and the result is obtained in minutes and so on. This becomes the beginning of the arc degree. That which is converted into minutes consists of seconds.

The **Standard Sine** <term>kramajyā: the sine function starting from 0°</term> which is associated with the sequence, when added to sixty, becomes the **Cosine** <term>koṭi-jyā: literally the "end-point sine" or cosine</term>. It is said that if the standard sines are placed in reverse order, the **Versed Sine** <term>utkramajyā: the "inverse" sine, or 1 - cos(x)</term> is produced by the segments. This is calculated using the reverse segments as written in the *Shiromaṇi* <note>Bhāskara II's Siddhānta Shiromaṇi</note>. The segments to be traversed for the sine are calculated as follows:

# On the Determination of True Sine Segments and Planetary Circumferences

Something more shall now be said regarding the nature of the sine segments <note>original: "jyakhandas"</note>. By the method of agreement and difference <note>original: "anvaya-vyatireka"; a logical framework used to establish cause and effect</note>, how could the proportion <term>anupāta</term> not be established? Just as the holder of the earth <note>likely referring to the support of the calculation</note> is considered in other ways, so too is the sine of the complement <term>kotijyā</term> derived. Through the union of the achieved and the destined <note>original: "siddha-bhogya-yuti"</note>, the sine of the signs is not fully realized by the Rule of Three <term>trairāśika</term> alone. 

Furthermore, when considering the clear correction <term>sphuṭā-karaṇe</term> of the segments to be traversed, one must account for the strength of the segments. The segments used in the Rule of Three are divided by the parts. What is described here is the method for calculating the segments as given in the memory of the ancients. 

### The Lineage of Astronomical Knowledge

In this pursuit of clear mathematical action <term>spashta-karma</term>, I follow what was spoken by the Sun himself <note>A reference to the *Surya Siddhanta*, believed to be revealed by the Sun God</note>, and transmitted through the tradition of men: from the son of Jishnu <note>referring to Brahmagupta</note>, to Aryabhata and Bhaskara. By my own intellect and through the realization of the circle <note>original: "gola-samstha"</note>, I explain that which was not previously stated for the sake of clarity.

->**The Planetary Circumferences**<-

Because they are not stated perfectly elsewhere, I shall now declare the circumferences <term>paridhi</term> <note>these refer to the dimensions of the epicycles used to calculate planetary orbits</note>. 

| Planet | Circumference (Degrees, Minutes, Seconds) | Additional Factors |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sun (Ravi)** | 13° 40' 28'' | Defined by "Kha-Sagara" (40) and "Trishat" (30) |
| **Mars (Bhauma)** | 3° 14' 5'' 22''' | "Shakra-Rama" (143) and "Sapta-Arnava" (47) |
| **Jupiter (Guru)** | 33° 5' | "Muni-Danta" (327) |
| **Saturn (Shani)** | 45° 26' | "Mrida-Ashvi" (211) |
| **Venus (Kavi)** | 11° 50' | "Dard-Shora" (110) |

<note>The text uses "Bhuta-sankhya," a system where words represent numbers: e.g., "Sagara" (Oceans) = 4, "Kh" (Void) = 0, "Ashvi" (Twins) = 2.</note>

### Calculations for the Equation of Center

Regarding the circumference of the planets, one must find the square root <term>pada</term> of the sum of the squares. The circumferences for Jupiter and others are increased or decreased based on their position in the odd or even quadrants. 

For the calculation of the <term>manda-phala</term> <note>the Equation of Center, which corrects for the eccentricity of the orbit</note>, the following maximum values are given:

*   **Sun:** 2° 10' 31''
*   **Moon:** 4° 57' 53''
*   **Mars:** 13° 15'
*   **Jupiter:** 4° 40'
*   **Saturn:** 8° 5'
*   **Venus:** 37° 40' <note>This high value reflects the outer limits of the planetary epicycle.</note>

The learned astronomer, having known the circumference and the square of the degrees, should multiply them to find the true position. This method, rooted in the teachings of the Sun, allows for the transformation of mean motion into the observed reality of the heavens.

# On the Dimensions and the Rule of Proportion

...the measure is considered two-fold. || 33 || For the first <note>likely the Sun or the starting point of the calculation</note>, the measure is twelve (12). For Jupiter <note>original: "Gurau"</note>, the measure is four hundred and fifty-seven (457). Similarly, for Venus <note>original: "Bhargavena"</note>, it is sixty-five hundred and forty (6540). Regarding the solar sine at the horizon, the sine is 210. Using the Radius <term>trijyā</term> as the standard, a proportion <term>anupāta</term> of the circumference is established. By the power of this, in the center of the circle, each of the four quadrants <term>pada</term> is formed. In the odd and even quadrants, the measures are determined in the circle to achieve the side <term>koṭi</term>.

### Calculations in the Quadrants

In an odd quadrant, the result is produced; in an even quadrant, it is stated as the side. Then, the sine <term>doḥ-jyā</term> and cosine <term>koṭi-jyā</term> of each are multiplied by the parts of the circumference and divided by the degrees of the circle respectively. These results are called the "Side-Results" <term>phala</term>. These results, when multiplied and then divided by the Radius <term>trijyā</term>, produce the corrected values.

Whether the center is in the signs beginning with Capricorn <note>original: "mṛgādi"; the first six signs starting from the winter solstice</note> or signs beginning with Cancer <note>original: "karkādi"; the signs starting from the summer solstice</note>, the sum or difference of the cosine and the result-sines becomes the corrected side here. The sine is the base <term>bhuja</term>. The square root of the sum of their squares <note>original: "vargayuteḥ padam"; a clear reference to the Pythagorean theorem used in Indian astronomy</note> is the Hypotenuse <term>śruti</term> or <term>karṇa</term>.

### Refinement of the True Motion

Just as in the case of the Sun's center, the Hypotenuse is derived from the square root of the sum of the squares of the sine-result and the cosine. The sine of the anomaly <note>original: "kendra"</note> is multiplied by the maximum correction and divided by the Hypotenuse <term>karṇa</term> to find the precise result. From this, the difference between the mean <term>madhyama</term> and the true <term>sphuṭa</term> position of the planet is found in degrees.

When the sine-result is divided by the Radius and multiplied by the Hypotenuse, the precise result is achieved. This is the "Fast Method" <term>śīghra-mārga</term> which should be performed by astronomers to remove all doubt. 

->**The Refined Circumference**<-

The circumference calculated in this way becomes "True" <term>sphuṭa</term>. The arc of the sine-result <term>doh-phala-cāpa</term> is equal to the result stated. When divided by the Radius and multiplied by the Hypotenuse, the sine of the base becomes true. This arc is known as the "True Sine-Correction." To destroy the confusion born of delusion <note>original: "mūḍha-bhrama-jñāna-vibhañjanārtha"</note>, I have explained this through the seven-fold divisions of the circumference. This single calculation <term>sakṛt-phala</term> is the primary method based on the movement of the Hypotenuse.

<keywords>Jupiter (Guru), Venus (Bhargava), Radius (Trijya), Hypotenuse (Karna/Shruti), Sine (Bhujajya), Cosine (Kotijya), Quadrants (Pada), True Position (Sphuta)</keywords>

# On the Dimensions of the Slow Epicycles

By the grace of the Teacher, through the rule of proportion <term>anupāta</term> <note>a mathematical method similar to the 'rule of three' used to find unknown values based on ratios</note>, the dimensions of the epicycles <term>paridhi</term> produced in the even quadrants are determined. For those skilled in calculating results <term>phala</term>, I shall now state the desired dimensions of these epicycles for the even and odd quadrants. For the Moon <note>original: "Vibhu"</note>, the measure is 32 degrees and 50 minutes. For the Sun, it is 37 degrees and 50 minutes. The epicycles produced from the odd quadrants for Mars and the others are now stated:

For Mars, the values are 32, 28, 11, and 48. At the end of the second even quadrant, the values are 14 degrees and 52 minutes. For the Sun <note>original: "Rimi-didhiti"</note>, when the values are decreased, the retrograde degrees are 37 and 51. For the Moon, in the even quadrant, it is 23 degrees and 39 minutes. For Mercury, the measure is 30 degrees and 15 minutes. From the degree of Mercury, the value is 35 degrees and 55 minutes. For Jupiter <note>original: "Guru"</note>, it is 11 degrees and 20 minutes. For Venus <note>original: "Bhṛgu"</note>, it is 55 degrees and 57 minutes. At the end of the fourth quadrant for Saturn, and for the Sun at the end of the even quadrant, the scriptures state 13 degrees and 9 minutes. For Mars, it is 29 degrees and 12 minutes. For Mercury, it is 6 degrees and 11 minutes. In the case of Jupiter, it is 26 degrees and 3 minutes. For Venus, the measure is 12 degrees and 12 minutes. For Saturn, it is 11 degrees and 4 minutes, with 50 minutes.

These measurements for the Sun and others are taken as half of the sum of the second and third quadrants. This wonderful and varied reasoning for the results <term>phala-vāsanā</term> <note>the 'logic' or 'rationale' behind why these mathematical corrections work</note> should not be discarded nor is it common knowledge; it arises from the power of the position of the celestial sphere <term>gola-sthiti</term>. Regarding the appearance of the epicycles in odd and even quadrants, the wise say that the measurement at the end of an odd quadrant is the same as the beginning of an even one, used to know the true epicycle.

The difference between the odd and even epicycle is multiplied by the sine <term>bhuja-jyā</term> and divided by the radius <term>tri-jyā</term>. This result is then subtracted from or added to the even circle to find the True Epicycle <term>sphuta-paridhi</term>. This calculation, performed by the ancients for the sake of precise results, is presented here. In all methods, the final result remains the same.

Having seen the readings at the end of the quadrants and their agreement with the spoken word, this has been accepted by the wise. What was previously hidden is now expanded upon with proof and devotion. This description of the planetary sphere as stated by the noble ones <note>original: "Arya"; possibly referring to Aryabhata or the lineage of astronomers</note> is provided here to ensure the logic of the calculations.

# On the Determination of True Planetary Positions

Even when calculations are performed according to the Solar system <note>original: "Saurai," referring to the Surya Siddhanta, a foundational Sanskrit astronomical text</note>, if the true position is not attained, one must rely on the position of the slow-apsis <term>manda-lagna</term> <note>the point in a planet's orbit furthest from the Earth</note>. In reality, that which is conceived according to the Solar school allows us to understand the nature of the planets. Because of the various arrangements of the texts, the words of the best of sages may appear contradictory. In matters where the sacred scriptures <term>shruti</term> are the ultimate proof <term>pramāṇa</term>, what need is there for logic? Yet, if logic follows the path of the sages, it is desirable.

Thus, the rationales <term>upapatti</term> of the scriptures are presented to the world for the sake of well-being. By applying the corrections to the various quadrants, the results are obtained. For the common practice of astronomers, the true position <term>sphuṭatā</term> should be calculated for the year. The results of the calculation methods taught by the school of Brahma <note>the Brahma-Siddhanta</note> were stated by Bhaskara <note>Bhaskara II, the 12th-century mathematician</note> and others, yet some details were not explicitly indicated.

In the first and subsequent anomalies <term>kendra</term> <note>the angular distance of a planet from its apogee</note>, the natural calculation is obtained. The mean planet <term>madhya</term> <note>the theoretical position of a planet moving at a constant speed in a perfect circle</note>, when corrected by the slow-correction <term>manda-phala</term> <note>the "equation of the center" which accounts for the planet's elliptical orbit</note>, becomes the "true-slow" position <term>manda-sphuṭa</term>. This is also called the corrected position in certain contexts. 

Through the sine <term>jyā</term> of the anomaly of the planet, the calculated results are found. For the Sun and the Moon <note>original: "Ravi-candrayoḥ"</note>, this calculation is straightforward. In the various quadrants <term>pada</term>, the arc is added or subtracted in the proper order. For the Sun and Moon, the "true-slow" position is the final true position, as they lack the "fast" motion or stations <note>original: "stambhatayor-abhāvāt"; the Sun and Moon never appear to go retrograde, unlike the other planets</note>. Thus, there is no need for a fast-correction <term>śīghra-sphuṭa</term> for them.

The process of correction is as follows: By applying the "slow-correction" to the mean position, one obtains the "true-slow" position. Then, by applying the "fast-correction" <term>śīghra-phala</term> <note>which accounts for the Earth's own motion, effectively the parallax</note>, the planet becomes truly corrected <term>sphuṭa-graha</term>. 

According to the authority of the Solar system, these operations are performed. If one asks why the "fast-anomaly" is calculated like the "slow" one, it is because the sines of the arcs of their respective quadrants must be added to the true apsis <term>sphuṭocca</term>. The "slow-correction" is calculated from the true Sun. If one applies the correction in reverse, the mean position is found. This is the method favored in the Solar tradition.

### The Rationale for the Mean Position

Now, hear the derivation <term>upapatti</term> of the mean position. Within the diameter of the celestial circle, the difference between the "slow" and "fast" bodies is seen. Just as the center of the Earth <term>bhū-gola</term> is the center of the universe, the anomaly of the planet in the celestial sphere <term>ākāśa-gole</term> follows a similar logic. The planets move in the sky, and their positions are observed from the center of the observer's horizon...

<summary>This page explains the mathematical transition from a planet's theoretical "mean" position to its "true" observed position using two major corrections: the Manda-phala (slow correction) and the Shighra-phala (fast correction). It distinguishes the Sun and Moon from other planets because they never exhibit retrograde motion (stations).</summary>

# The Geometry of the Eccentric Circle and Planetary Motion

From the center of the Earth <term>bhūkendra</term>, a second center is established at a distance equal to the maximum sine of the correction <term>antyaphalajyā</term> <note>This refers to the eccentricity of the orbit, where the center of the planet's path is shifted away from the Earth's center.</note>. This second center belongs to what is known as the **eccentric circle** <term>pratimaṇḍala</term>. Following the order of the signs starting from Aries <note>original: "ajādya," referring to Mesha or Aries</note>, a second "true" path is established. A vertical line, known as the **meridian line** <term>sūtra</term>, is drawn through the center; the point where it reaches the upper part of the circle is the **apogee** <term>ucca</term> <note>the point furthest from Earth</note>, and where it reaches the lower part, it is called the **perigee** <term>nīca</term> <note>the point closest to Earth</note>. 

The wise should understand that these two lines—the one through the apogee and perigee and the horizontal line—pass through the center. Because the planet is sometimes distant from the center of the Earth <term>garbha</term> and sometimes near, the distance between the two circles must be accounted for by the learned. At every point, the distance between the mean orbit and the eccentric orbit is governed by the sine of the correction.

The disk of the planet <term>vimba</term> moves daily upon this eccentric circle according to its anomaly <term>kendra</term> <note>the angular distance of the planet from its apogee</note>. Through this daily motion, the planet's position is determined. To find the true position <term>spaṣṭa</term> from the center of the Earth, one must project this onto the zodiac <term>bhacakra</term> starting from Aries. 

### Calculation of the Sine and Cosine Components

For the purpose of calculation, the anomaly is measured from the apogee. The **sine component** <term>bhujajyā</term> is defined as the horizontal distance from the apogee-perigee line. Then, the **cosine component** <term>koṭi</term> should be known. The cosine is the vertical distance. For the anomalies in the quadrants of Capricorn and Cancer <note>original: "mṛga-karki-kendre"</note>, the cosine is added to or subtracted from the radius. 

The **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> <note>the actual distance from the Earth's center to the planet</note> is the square root of the sum of the squares of the sine and cosine components <note>original: "kṛti yoga mūlaṃ," a Sanskrit phrasing of the Pythagorean theorem: $\sqrt{Bhuja^2 + Koṭi^2}$</note>. This hypotenuse represents the distance from the Earth's center to the planet's position. 

Where the line of the mean position and the line of the cosine intersect, there the distance to the center of the planet is found. In this manner, the true position is derived from the mean. This method is consistent with the teachings of the masters. The mean planet travels on the mean circle, while the true planet exists on the eccentric circle <term>pratimaṇḍala</term> or the epicycle <term>nīcocca-vṛtta</term>. 

The intersection of the vertical and horizontal lines through the center of the circle allows us to define the planet's place. The circumference of the **epicycle** <term>nīcocca-paridhi</term> is the scale used to determine the exact deviation from the mean path. Whether using the eccentric model or the epicycle model, the resulting position of the planet's disk remains the same.

<keywords>Pratimandala (eccentric circle), Karna (hypotenuse/distance), Bhuja (sine), Koti (cosine), Uccha (apogee), Nicha (perigee), planetary disk, Indian trigonometry</keywords>

From those results, the lines are drawn through the centers in sequence. Where the vertical line is, there the horizontal line exists; by the elevation of the anomaly, the horizontal line is established. From that point, we shall draw the line going horizontally from the perigee. By using the **radius** <term>tribhajyā</term> <note>literally "three-sine," the standard radius of 3438 minutes used in Indian trigonometry</note>, at the points known as **Capricorn** <term>mṛga</term> and **Cancer** <term>karki</term>, the meeting point of the vertical sounds is found. From that, the vertical side is found by the sum or difference of the **radius** and the **cosine-correction** <term>koṭiphala</term>.

The horizontal line is its base, which is simply the **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term>. From these two, the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> <note>the actual distance from the observer to the planet</note> is produced. This explains the distance from the center of the Earth's disk to the center of the planet's disk. Regarding the anomaly, the **radius** <term>tribhajyā</term> is the vertical side, and the sum of the vertical components is the base-sine; thus it is obtained from the planet's disk. Similarly, from the vertical line...

...there is a single correction for the **retrograde** <term>vakarī</term> motion. From the center of the Earth to the limit of the disk, the vertical side and the **cosine-correction** <term>koṭiphala</term>—or the base and its **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term>—are the terms to be known. The **radius** <term>trijyā</term> is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> of the base-sine. That sine is the **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term> produced from the vertical. When the sector of the mean longitude is at the place of the base, then as far as its own disk reaches, there is a second method. From the center of the Earth, the vertical side and the **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term> are the base in the middle. For the sake of its own correction, the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> is produced from the two vertical lines. Whatever correction is in the mean minutes, that is given from the Earth's center, inclined by the **radius** <term>tribhajyā</term>. From the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>śravaṇa</term> <note>another term for the hypotenuse/distance</note>, if the **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term> is the base, then the **radius** <term>tribhajyā</term> is the divisor of the **hypotenuse** <term>śravaṇa</term>.

When the **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term> is known in that quadrant, <insert>sine-sum-radius</insert> <insert>sine-sum-radius</insert> the **radius** <term>trijyā</term>, the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, and the multiplier must be used. Here, by that **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term>, and from the **radius** <term>trijyā</term> and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, by the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <insert>minutes</insert> <insert>folio 36</insert> the known **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term> becomes the **sine-of-the-correction** <term>phalajyā</term>. In the circumference, from these two **sines-of-the-correction** <term>phalajyā</term>, by the method of knowledge, the wise say that the **sine-of-the-correction** <term>phalajyā</term> is derived from the **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term>. In the same way as stated, the **radius** <term>tri-guṇa</term> is the multiplier for the **sine-of-the-correction** <term>phalajyā</term> arising from that method. By the proportion resulting from the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, these two are always corrected. From the **sine-correction** <term>dohphala</term> comes the "mild" <term>manda</term> <note>referring to the equation of the center, the correction for the elliptical/eccentric shape of the orbit</note> correction; that itself is the **sine-of-the-correction** <term>phalajyā</term>. This method, by the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, is done for the "mild" <term>mṛdu</term> correction. The proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> was not previously performed because the addition was for the sake of finding the extreme difference. This is described there as being joined or subtracted, because it is properly reasoned and unrefuted. For the sake of the planet, the astronomers describe this. By the power of the rising signs, the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> and the corrected circumference are obtained from the straight line of the proportion. This has been determined by the principles of the observation. 

In the first place of the **Tattvaviveka**, from the calculation and logic, since the tip of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇāgra</term> is established, how could the disk at the tip of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇāgra</term> be otherwise? Some say that the **sine-of-the-correction** <term>phalajyā</term> comes from the **sine-of-the-anomaly** <term>dorjyā</term>, and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> as stated, when combined with the **radius** <term>triguṇa</term>, is for the sake of the "mild" <term>manda</term> distance.

Regarding the results for the two planets, some say that the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> <note>the angular correction based on the planet's position on its epicycle</note> produced from the proportion of the chord is itself the cause of the destruction <note>original: "nāśakṛte," likely meaning the elimination of error</note> of the discrepancy, because of the equality of the two factors. This is the view seen and spoken of by others. However, that opinion appears to be based on their own intuition. Since the **sine of the anomaly** <term>dorjyā</term> <note>the sine of the planet's displacement from its apogee</note> would be the **maximum sine-correction** <term>antyaphalajyakā</term> starting from the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, the multiplier is taken accordingly. 

The **hypotenuse of the slow-motion** <term>mandakarṇa</term> is not used here for the sake of the **sine-correction of the slow-motion** <term>mṛdudoḥphala</term> alone; rather, there is a rejection of that view. By means of the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, the **true-state** <term>sphuṭatva</term> of the **sine of the anomaly** <term>dorjyā</term> is also calculated. Thus, the results for the two planets are equal to the **maximum sine-correction** <term>antyaphalajyakā</term> here.

The **sine-correction** <term>phalajyā</term> produced from the mean calculation in this system is always equal to the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> produced from the **true anomaly** <term>sphuṭakendra</term> through the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>. Thus, I shall speak in a way that is free from doubt, just as it was stated by the tradition. Whatever doubt was expressed there: imagine the **hypotenuse** <term>śravaṇa</term> <note>synonym for karṇa/hypotenuse</note> at the end of the calculated anomaly. The **mean planet** <term>madhyagraha</term> is situated on the **mean line** <term>madhyasūtra</term>. 

Where the **vertical side** <term>koṭi</term> is, there—from the **true planet** <term>sphuṭavidvān</term>—the distance is horizontally equal to the **vertical line** <term>koṭisūtra</term>. At that point, the **mean anomaly** <term>madhyakendra</term> is measured starting from the **apogee** <term>ucca</term>. On the **eccentric circle** <term>pratimaṇḍala</term>, at the place of the **vertical line** <term>koṭisūtra</term>, there is the **anomaly** <term>kendra</term> of the apogee. From that **mean line** <term>madhyasūtra</term>, and from the connection of those two **verticals** <term>koṭi</term>, the angle that is the object of discussion is formed. In one place, the rule for those two is established by the power of the **multiplier** <term>guṇa</term>.

When the **sine-correction** <term>phalajyā</term> is calculated from the **anomaly** <term>vikendra</term>, the **sine of the anomaly** <term>dorjyā</term> is found through that **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>. The **sine of the anomaly** <term>doḥphala</term> made from the **mean line** <term>madhyasūtra</term> is the **sine-correction** <term>phalajyā</term> in the **mean circle** <term>madhyakavṛtta</term>. Similarly, it is understood through the **true planet** <term>sphuṭavidvān</term>. Even if the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> is calculated from the **apogee** <term>ucca</term>, in the case of the **perigee** <term>nīca</term>, the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> stays at the intersection of the **vertical line** <term>koṭisūtra</term>.

At the end of that **maximum sine-correction** <term>antyaphalajyā</term>, the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> arising from the **true anomaly** <term>sphuṭakendra</term> is known to be equal to the **maximum result** <term>antyaphala</term>. In this way, there is no doubt. From the **true planet** <term>sphuṭagraha</term> corrected by the single result obtained through the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, what is the **sine of the anomaly** <term>dorjyā</term> here? It is of another kind. The **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> which is corrected by the result, occurs in the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> arising from its own **true anomaly** <term>sphuṭakendra</term>. 

Once the result is obtained, it should be calculated as before. Therefore, I have imagined all this myself. After the result is corrected, the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> arises from the path of the **true anomaly** <term>sphuṭakendra</term>. From that correction, the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> is indeed as stated. This is the **sine-correction** <term>phalajyā</term> arising from the mean. Thus, when the **true divisor** <term>sphuṭahāra</term> is used, the **sine-correction** <term>doḥphala</term> obtained by that method is the **sine-correction** <term>phalajyakā</term>. This occurs only once. When there is no division by the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> in that calculation, then for the planet, the **disk** <term>vidhu</term> of the **mean planet** <term>madhyakheṭa</term> is calculated from the **center of the Earth** <term>bhūmigarbha</term>. Thus, you should understand the **mean center** <term>madhyakendra</term> and the **Earth center** <term>bhūmikendra</term> on the line.

<margin>Si.Ta.Vi. 36</margin>
<margin>36</margin>

# Chapter on the True Positions of Planets

In this specific triad <note>referring to the three stages of planetary calculation</note>, the **mean planet** <term>madhya-kheṭa</term> <note>the average position of a planet moving at a constant speed</note> becomes the **true planet** <term>sphuṭa</term> when calculated along the line of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇasūtra</term> <note>the actual distance from the observer to the planet</note>. For determining auspicious times such as marriages, the **true-mean position** <term>spaṣṭa-madhya</term> is used, as it is derived from the mean but accounts for certain primary corrections. The result produced by the proportion of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> and the **radius** <term>triguṇa</term> <note>literally "three times ninety," meaning the sine of 90 degrees</note> is what determines the actual angular correction. 

Thus, through these means, the true positions are established on the **ecliptic** <term>krāntivṛtta</term>. Those who are experts in the celestial spheres <note>original: "golavid"</note> understand that the true position is calculated by adding or subtracting the correction from the mean. This is directly observable in the heavens. 

The experts say that the **mean planet** <term>madhya</term>, when corrected by the **slow-correction** <term>manda-phala</term> <note>equivalent to the equation of center, correcting for the elliptical nature of the orbit</note>, becomes the **slow-true planet** <term>manda-sphuṭa</term>. When that position is further corrected by its own **rapid-correction** <term>śīghra-phala</term> <note>equivalent to the equation of the equant or parallax, correcting for the Earth's motion</note>, it becomes the **true planet** <term>sphuṭa</term>. This process of refinement makes the planet "true" or "clear" in its position. 

The underlying **logic and rationale** <term>vāsanā</term> for these corrections is provided to clarify the differences between the various celestial spheres. However, for those of small intellect, explaining the deep rationale is considered a fault rather than a virtue, as it may lead to confusion. 

Without the power of the Sun, nothing can be known for certain. I shall now describe the natures of the celestial spheres which are the causes of these planetary corrections—the forms that exist in the space of the heavens. These spheres are defined by the distance between the **apogee** <term>ucca</term> <note>the point furthest from Earth</note> and the **perigee** <term>nīca</term> <note>the point closest to Earth</note>. 

The experts in the spheres know that the distance from the **center of the Earth** <term>kugarbha</term> to the **maximum apogee** <term>parocca</term> defines the **sphere of apogee** <term>uccagola</term>. Similarly, half the distance from the Earth's center to the **maximum perigee** <term>paranīca</term> defines the **sphere of perigee** <term>paranīcagola</term>. 

The Sun travels within the space between these two spheres. The distance from the Earth's center to the planet’s path is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>. When we consider the sphere of the planet itself, we speak of the **internal hypotenuse** <term>saudara-karṇa</term> <note>literally "uterine" or "inner" hypotenuse</note>, which is determined by the radius of that specific sphere.

The center of the **eccentric circle** <term>pratimaṇḍala</term> <note>a circle whose center is offset from the Earth's center to account for irregular motion</note> is described by experts as being distinct from the Earth's center. The distance between the Earth's center and the center of this eccentric circle is exactly equal to the **maximum correction** <term>antyaphala</term>. This geometry allows us to understand why the Sun appears to move at varying speeds from our perspective on Earth.

<summary>This page explains the transition from mean to true planetary positions using the manda (slow) and śīghra (rapid) corrections. It introduces the geometric model of apogee/perigee spheres and the eccentric circle (pratimaṇḍala) centered away from the Earth.</summary>

# The Construction of the Celestial Spheres

Due to the stability of the divine arrangement <note>original: "devaka-sthairya-vaśād"</note>, the motion of the sphere and its various parts are determined. The **eccentric sphere** <term>prativṛtta-gola</term> <note>a model where the planet moves on a circle whose center is offset from the Earth</note> is not produced spontaneously; rather, it is calculated based on the motion of the **apogee** <term>ucca</term> <note>the point in an orbit farthest from the Earth</note>. Because the apogee itself moves, the eccentric sphere also moves accordingly.

Through the **line of sight from the center of the Earth** <term>ku-garbha-dṛk-sūtra</term> <note>an imaginary line connecting the observer at Earth's center to the planet</note>, the position of the **true planet** <term>sphuṭa-graha</term> is determined within the zodiac. In this system, two forms are imagined for one's own benefit and the benefit of others: the sphere of the apogee and the eccentric sphere. These spheres are calculated to account for the observed irregularities in planetary speeds.

### The Epicycle of Apogee and Perigee

Now, let us consider the region of the eccentric circle. The **true apogee** <term>sphuṭa-ucca</term> exists there, as does the **perigee** <term>nīca</term> <note>the point in an orbit closest to the Earth</note>. I shall explain this clearly: the **epicycle of apogee and perigee** <term>nīcocca-vṛtta</term> <note>a small circle whose center moves along the circumference of a larger circle</note> is the path where the planet's anomaly <term>kendra</term> travels. 

This celestial sphere, along with its rays of light, is positioned such that the **mean orbit** <term>madhya-kakṣā</term> and the epicycle intersect or relate to one another. From the center of the Earth to the planet's position on its orbit, the distance is known as the **hypotenuse** <term>śravaṇa</term> or <term>karṇa</term>. 

### Variations in Planetary Calculation

Just as the **celestial sphere** <term>bhagola</term> and the sphere of the apogee are in motion, the spheres designated as "apogee-perigee" <note>original: "nīcocca-saṃjña"</note> also move in all directions due to the stability of the celestial forces. The distance between the center of the Earth and the planet's actual position is not constant. 

<note>The text here contrasts the simpler motion of the Sun with the more complex motions of planets like Mars.</note>

Regarding the Sun <note>original: "arka"</note>, the calculation of the result <note>the equation of center</note> is similar to that of the Moon. However, for planets such as **Mars** <term>kuja</term> and the others, the **slow-correction** <term>mṛdu-phala</term> <note>the adjustment for the planet's elliptical-like path</note> is required. 

The great masters <note>original: "ācārya-varyā"</note> state that:
1. The **mean planet** <term>madhya-graha</term> moves on its own orbit.
2. The **slow-true position** <term>manda-sphuṭa</term> is calculated on the **hypotenuse-circle** <term>karṇa-vṛtta</term>.

Some scholars argue that the mean planet is not the "true" mean in every sense, but rather a calculation tool. To find the final **true position** <term>sphuṭa</term>, one must understand the remaining correction <note>the "śeṣa-phala"</note> which is derived from the distance between the mean and the slow-true positions.

<summary>This page describes the geometric relationship between the eccentric circle and the epicycle (nīcocca-vṛtta) used to model planetary motion. It explains how the true position of a planet is derived from the mean position using the line of sight from the Earth's center and the hypotenuse (distance).</summary>

<keywords>eccentric sphere, epicycle, apogee, perigee, true planet, Mars, Sun, Indian astronomy, mathematical modeling</keywords>

Ka-37</margin>

# Refining the Equations of Planetary Motion

Alternatively, one may intelligently conceptualize the orbit and apply the rule for sines <note>original: "doṣīya-vidhi"; referring to the calculation of the sine (jyā) of the anomaly</note> to the **hypotenuses** <term>śruti</term> <note>the distance from the Earth to the planet at a given point in its orbit</note>. When the result of the equation is known, it is usually expressed in terms of the **radius** <term>trijyā</term> <note>original: "tri-guṇa"; literally "three-times-arc," a standard term for a radius of 3438 minutes</note>. However, these values are truly established upon the circle of the **slow hypotenuse** <term>manda-karṇa</term> <note>the distance from the Earth to the planet during its slow or 'manda' anomaly</note>. Therefore, to obtain the **true results** <term>sphuṭa</term>, these values must be multiplied by the radius and divided by that same hypotenuse. These corrected values are then suitable for determining the planet's position at the place of the equation.

Following the teachings of **Bhaskara** <note>Bhaskara II (1114–1185 CE), a preeminent Indian mathematician and astronomer</note>, the circumferences <term>paridhi</term> are corrected through the slow hypotenuse. When the **sine of the equation** <term>phala-jyā</term> is determined, it becomes the **true sine of the base** <term>sphuṭa-bhuja-jyā</term>. By applying the correction for the slow hypotenuse, the result is in accordance with Bhaskara’s school of thought. Because of the differences in the orbits, there is a difference in the results. Some say this leads to a contradiction in one's own objectives, yet this logic is stated to clarify the intellect; however, such claims are refuted here by valid reasoning.

The **anomaly** <term>kendra</term> <note>the angular distance of a planet from its apogee</note> is established through the procedure of the area of the hypotenuse. Regarding the definition of that hypotenuse, it is said that the result is like a geometric field. Even so, the **corrected circumference** <term>sphuṭa-paridhi</term> is calculated by the ratio of the hypotenuse. This method is used because the slow hypotenuse is considered the most refined measure for determining the divisions of the degrees. These measures of the circumference are suitable for the distance of the hypotenuse.

Furthermore, if you claim in the astronomical treatises that this distance of the hypotenuse is the true measure for the divisions of the degrees, then for the sake of the result of the **sine of the base** <term>doḥ-phala</term>, a specific ratio is taught. This uses the degrees of the planet’s own part and the radius. The true equation of the center is derived from the **corrected epicycle** <term>sphuṭa-paridhi</term>. This is stated in the verse beginning "By its own..." <note>original: "svena-iti padya"; likely a reference to a specific verse in Bhaskara's Siddhanta-siromani</note> by the learned Bhaskara.

Moreover, if the previous circumference was considered "true," then the sine of the equation calculated by that method would not be true. Therefore, this rule of sines and the circumference only attains its full extension through the detailed calculations of the mathematicians. This rule of the sine is logical. If the calculation is performed using the measure of the degrees of the circle generated by the hypotenuse, then the result—the **maximum equation** <term>antya-phala</term>—is corrected as stated, just like the perigee and apogee <note>original: "nīca-ucca-vat"</note>.

Without this specific refinement, there would be a discrepancy in calculating the **equation of the conjunction** <term>śīghra-phala</term> <note>the 'fast' anomaly correction, primarily used for the inner and outer planets relative to the Sun</note>. To avoid the burden of excessive calculation, this should be understood by the wise. If one's objectives are contradicted by the results, the difference is due to false interpretations of Bhaskara's teachings. Those who rely on impure methods are lost, just as one who destroys their own wealth. In this way, the true positions are established.

### The Decision Regarding the Inclined Orbit

Now, I shall provide the decision for the scholars regarding the doubt concerning the **inclined orbit** <term>vimandala</term> <note>the orbit of a planet that is tilted relative to the ecliptic</note>. Through the rotation of the **eccentric circle** <term>pratimandala</term>, the planet moves within the inclined orbit. This results in the planet’s motion through the signs of the zodiac, starting with Aries, measured in minutes and seconds. By the rotation of the eccentric circle alone, the planet reaches its position in the zodiac.

Is the movement of the planet observed from the inclined orbit or from the eccentric circle? It cannot be from the eccentric circle alone. Because the rotation is centered on the Earth, the motion is observed through the inclined orbit. This involves the **node** <term>pāta</term> <note>the point where the planet's inclined orbit intersects the ecliptic</note>. When the planet is at the position of the node, its position is described as being together with the node...

It defends the methods of Bhaskara II against alternative interpretations and introduces the relationship between the eccentric circle and the inclined orbit (vimandala) for calculating true longitude.</summary>

<margin>38</margin>

# On the Calculation of True Motion and Retrograde Paths

Having applied the necessary corrections <note>original: "datta-saṃskāra"; referring to mathematical adjustments for longitude</note>, the astronomer should calculate the position of the planet along with its **nodes** <term>pāta</term> <note>the two points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note>. Through the sequence of the planet’s own quadrant, and by following the rules of the celestial sphere, the **true planet** <term>sphuṭa-graha</term> is established on its own circle. It must be understood that the planets, as perceived in their orbits, reside specifically within the **inclined orbit** <term>vimaṇḍala</term> <note>the actual secondary circle on which the planet moves, tilted relative to the ecliptic</note>.

I shall now describe the precise method for determining these positions. One must multiply the result of the **sine of the anomaly** <term>koṭi-jyā</term> by the radius and divide by the **hypotenuse** <term>śruti</term>. This process corrects the planet's position across the various quadrants. When the node is added or subtracted correctly, the result is the most accurate position of the heavenly body. These extremely subtle calculations are performed so that the planets, which are typically calculated on the ecliptic for the sake of simplicity in fruit-based astrology <note>original: "phalārtha"; referring to predictive astrology</note>, are instead understood in their true physical location on the inclined orbit.

### The Thresholds of Retrograde Motion

The daily motion of a planet is considered **retrograde** <term>vakra</term> <note>appearing to move backward against the stars</note> based on its distance from its **apex of fast motion** <term>śīghrocca</term>. The specific degrees of **anomaly** <term>kendra</term> at which the planets begin this backward motion are as follows:

*   **Mars**: 184 degrees
*   **Mercury**: 155 degrees
*   **Jupiter**: 130 degrees
*   **Venus**: 153 degrees
*   **Saturn**: 115 degrees

When these values are subtracted from the full circle <note>360 degrees</note>, the remaining degrees <note>185, 155, 130, 153, 115</note> indicate the points where the retrograde motion ends and the planet resumes its **direct motion** <term>mārga</term>. Even while in retrograde, the planet’s movement is calculated relative to the direction of the signs, just as the nodes move.

<margin>38</margin>

<margin>35</margin> The <unclear>kṛṇa</unclear> and the daily motion of Mars and the other planets are influenced by their **fast anomaly** <term>cala-kendra</term>. The **true daily motion** <term>sphuṭa-bhukti</term> is always considered greater than the **mean daily motion** <term>madhyama-bhukti</term> when the planet is in certain positions of its orbit. This occurs because the center of the planet’s epicycle is rotating toward its furthest point.

The difference between the planet's position today and its position at the same time on the following day is what we call the **true daily velocity** <term>sphuṭa-bhukti</term>. If the position for the next day is greater than the current one, the motion is direct; if it is less, it is retrograde. By calculating the difference between the **corrected mean motion** <term>mṛdu-sphuṭa-bhukti</term> and the results of the fast anomaly, the astronomer arrives at the true daily path.

In the first quadrant, if the equation of the result is increasing, the velocity is added to the mean motion. However, in the second quadrant, even if the result appears positive, one must consider the decreasing nature of the equation. Thus, the true motion is found by subtracting or adding the daily change in the equation of the center to the corrected mean motion.

<summary>This page defines how to calculate a planet's true position on its inclined orbit (vimandala) and provides the specific degree thresholds for the retrograde motion of the five visible planets.</summary>
<keywords>inclined orbit, retrograde motion, true daily motion, Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, Saturn, anomaly, nodes</keywords>

# On the Equations of Velocity and the Onset of Retrograde Motion

Following the sequence for determining the **true motion** <term>sphuṭa-bhukti</term> <note>the actual daily speed of a planet as seen from Earth</note>, one finds that when the **difference in the equation** <term>phalāntara</term> <note>the change in the correction factor known as the equation of center</note> is added to the **mean motion** <term>mṛdu-bhukti</term>, the velocity increases. However, in cases where the difference in this equation is equal to the mean motion itself, the two values cancel each other out. At that moment, because there is no true motion remaining, the planet appears to be **stationary** <note>original: "samo bhavataḥ"; literally "becomes equal" or unchanging</note> both before and after this point.

When the planet is **near its perigee** <term>nīcāsanna</term> <note>the point in its orbit closest to the Earth</note>, the velocity is affected by the decrease in the mean motion. Because the difference in the equation is negative, it must be subtracted from the true motion. Thus, the preceding true position is actually greater than the subsequent one. This occurs because the true motion is diminished by the difference in the correction. In the **third quadrant** <term>tṛtīyapada</term> of the orbit, even though the equation technically increases, the difference remains negative because it is subtracted from the mean motion, which is already at its limit. Therefore, the subsequent position of the planet will be less than the previous one.

### The Dynamics of Retrograde and Direct Motion

Furthermore, when the negative difference in the equation becomes equal to the mean motion, the result is the absence of true motion. At this juncture, the planet is stationary. Near the perigee, the planet then begins its **direct motion** <term>mārga-gati</term> <note>forward motion through the zodiac</note> once the subsequent motion becomes greater than the previous. 

In the **fourth quadrant** <term>caturthapada</term>, due to the decrease in the equation, the difference becomes positive. When this positive difference is added to the mean motion, the true motion increases, making the subsequent position greater than the previous one. Thus, through the sequence of the increase or decrease in the equation's difference, the true position of the planet is established. This has been explained by the ancient teachers. It is impossible to determine these specific times of stationary motion solely through the mean motion; one must account for the difference between the two. 

### Bhaskara’s Critique and the Celestial Sphere

As stated by **Bhaskara** <note>referring to Bhaskara II, the 12th-century mathematician and author of the *Siddhanta Shiromani*</note> in his own treatise <term>tantra</term>, these calculations must be consistent with the **celestial sphere** <term>gola</term> and mathematical reasoning. He argues against certain older views regarding the intersection of the planet's disk with the ecliptic. If one were to follow those flawed methods, there would be a contradiction with the observed mathematics of the sphere. 

<note>The text here becomes highly technical, discussing the "loss of the equation" (phala-nāśa) and the intersection points (sampāta) of the planetary orbits.</note>

The wise astronomer should contemplate these intersections according to the principles of the celestial sphere. The intersection point occurs before or after the mean time depending on the planet's motion. As the **Sun** <term>ravi</term> travels, the loss of the equation is calculated so that the motion near the intersection is refined. This allows the astronomer to understand the "rising" and "setting" of the planetary equations and the subtle shifts between mean and true motion.

<summary>This page discusses the mathematical conditions for a planet's stationary points, retrograde motion, and direct motion, emphasizing the relationship between mean motion and the difference in the equation of center. It references Bhaskara II's insistence that mathematical models must align with the physical reality of the celestial sphere.</summary>

<keywords>true velocity, mean motion, equation of center, retrograde motion, Bhaskara II, perigee, celestial sphere, quadrants, planetary stationary points</keywords>
