# The Geometry of the Celestial Spheres and Orbits

By one’s desire, the combined results of the two parts <note>likely referring to the two equations: the equation of center and the equation of anomaly</note> are joined to find the position of the planet. At the point of intersection, the planet's position is determined through the **eccentric circle** <term>prativṛtta</term> and the **high-low circle** <term>nīcocca-vṛtta</term> <note>the epicycle</note>. From a specific point within the creation, a line is drawn from the **center of the Earth** <term>garbha-sūtra</term> extending outward; by this rule, the **true planet** <term>spaṣṭa-kheṭa</term> is established. The mean position is located there as well, though it is further refined by the equations.

Somehow, through the movements within these circles, the knowledge of the number of planetary spheres is obtained. Just as the **celestial sphere** <term>bhagola</term> <note>the sphere of the fixed stars</note> is arranged by the Sun-god, it exists as a complete sphere. The sphere known as the **ecliptic** <term>kadambādika</term> <note>literally "pertaining to the Kadamba or pole of the ecliptic"</note> is situated at an interval of six signs <note>180 degrees</note>. Within that sphere, the point where the center of the ecliptic lies is where the **oblique circle** <term>vimaṇḍala</term> <note>the secondary circle or orbit inclined to the ecliptic</note> is located. In each of these, from the center of the Earth, the tip of the sine of the equation is calculated as previously stated.

### The Nested Spheres of the Sun and Planets

The sphere known as the **eccentric** <term>pratimandala</term> is described as follows: just as in the celestial sphere, the two spheres of the Sun and the eccentric are considered **limbs of the sphere** <term>aṅga-gola</term>. Even though they exist within the eccentric spheres, they remain within the oblique circle. This sphere moves through the two **nodes** <term>pāta</term>. Above that is the sphere of the nodes, which is suitable for calculating the "latitude" <note>original: "shakha-nayana"; literally "bringing the branches" or deviations</note>. The **sphere of the apogee** <term>parocca-gola</term> is situated above the two nodal points. 

Through the movement of the **nodal sphere** <term>vipāta-gola</term>, the **slow corrected position** <term>manda-sphuṭa</term> <note>the planet's position corrected for the equation of center</note> is found. When the planet is joined with the node, it moves within the oblique circle. From the movement of the sphere, the unknown factors become possible. The difference between the **oblique circle** <term>vimaṇḍala</term> and the **slow corrected planet** <term>manda-sphuṭa-graha</term> on the celestial circle is what defines the **latitude** <term>śara</term> <note>literally "arrow," the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</note>. 

The sphere of the **planets beginning with Mars** <term>kujādikānām</term> <note>the star-planets: Mars, Mercury, Jupiter, Venus, and Saturn</note> exists in its own right. Situated there, the planet moves in its own eccentric circle as the **slow corrected position** <term>mṛdu-sphuṭa</term>. In its own orb, the planet moves. One center is the **slow hypotenuse** <term>manda-karṇa</term> which is moved by the center of the orbit. The second center is at the intersection of the **epicycle** <term>nīcocca-vṛtta</term> and the **eccentric circle** <term>prativṛtta</term>. 

The sphere known as the **superior sphere** <term>vara-gola</term> belongs to the oblique and eccentric circles. Above the previously mentioned spheres, the sphere of the apogee moves. From the center of that region, the line called the **radius** <term>ralla</term> <note>original: "rallākhyo"; possibly a local or specific technical corruption for a radius or specific chord</note> extends. In that place, the nodal sphere exists. In the case of the Moon, there is a specific nodal sphere. At the center of the eccentric sphere, the distance from the center of the Earth's sphere is determined. From the middle of that sphere, the deviation from the ecliptic is called the **latitude** <term>apama</term>. In the eccentric circle, the maximum latitude occurs at a distance of three signs <note>90 degrees</note> from the node. It is said that for others, the eccentric circle is the position of their own sphere. 

<summary>This page describes the complex geometric nesting of celestial spheres (the celestial, eccentric, and oblique circles) used to model planetary motion. It explains how the planet's latitude and true position are derived from the intersection of these spheres and the line extending from the Earth's center.</summary>

<keywords>celestial sphere, eccentric circle, epicycle, planetary latitude, nodes, Sun's sphere, Mars, Earth's center, astronomical geometry</keywords>

# Calculations of the Nodes and Celestial Latitude

Through other means of proof, one who wishes to know the nature of the globe <note>original: "sagole vibhāvatan"</note> finds that in the northern and southern halves of the **ecliptic circle** <term>kadamba-vṛtta</term> <note>literally "the circle of the poles of the ecliptic"</note>, the true positions of the planets are established as described in the traditional teachings on motion. The **true position** <term>sphuṭa</term> is explained according to the parts stated by other authorities. The agitation of the planets and the nodes of the planets <term>pāta</term> <note>the points where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic</note> are calculated through their respective motions in the celestial sphere.

<margin>
Reference:
T. V. 
K. 25
</margin>

The minutes of the arc stated by other teachers for the nodes, and those stated in the **Surya Siddhanta** <term>Sauri</term> <note>literally "of the Sun," referring to the seminal astronomical text</note>, even if expressed in seconds, are multiplied by the sine of the latitude and divided by the specific planetary distance <term>karṇa</term>. These become the **true nodes** <term>sphuṭa-pāta</term> in one's own sphere of calculation. By applying the result of the inverse calculation, the true position of the node is refined. This refined position, measured from the beginning of **Aries** <term>Meṣa</term> in the ecliptic circle, is used by the astronomer to determine the **celestial latitude** <term>śara</term> <note>literally "arrow," the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</note>. 

Therefore, those who know the globe <term>golavida</term> should calculate the latitude from the **corrected true position** <term>mṛdu-spaṣṭa</term>. Following the logic of the sphere, I have described this in my own treatise. This knowledge of the latitude, which I have stated, should be understood as distinct from errors; it is not something to be ignored by the learned. The nodes of all the planets, starting from Aries, are calculated through their swift motion. The "arrow" <term>bāṇa</term> <note>another term for latitude</note> described by others is not always true for all purposes.

<margin>
39
</margin>

By following the path of the globe without contradiction, the means of calculating the latitude is perfected for all planets, including Mars, Saturn, and the son of the Moon <note>Mercury</note>. These are calculated after being refined by the corrections for their **higher apsides** <term>calocca</term>. The minutes of the daily motion are used to find the true positions of the planets. The results for the Sun, including its motion and its conjunctions, are seen here. The intersection of the true solar motion and the celestial equator <term>viṣuvat</term> provides the basis for these calculations. 

The motion of the planets is established for the purpose of determining the **lunar day** <term>tithi</term> and the **half-lunar day** <term>karaṇa</term>. For this, only the **true positions of the Sun and Moon** <term>sphuṭa-candrārka</term> are used. The corrections stated in one's own tradition must be applied. Without this, the calculations for the day of the Moon and the weekday would be incorrect. The fixed **astrological segments** <term>karaṇas</term>—such as Śakuni, Catuṣpāda, Nāga, and Kiṃstughna—are determined by the specific degrees of the Moon's waning phase.

The elapsed and remaining time in **nāḍikās** <term>nāḍikā</term> <note>a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</note> is calculated based on the motion of the Sun at that time. Those who are skilled in these calculations should thus determine the true nature of the lunar days. This is how the motion of the planets, multiplied by the six-nāḍikā period and divided by sixty, yields the true result. All this mathematics was spoken by the **All-Knowing Sun** <term>Śrī Sūrya</term> for the benefit of the world. What was refined by the ancient sages <term>muni</term> may sometimes be crude; therefore, one must look to the words of the divine seers <term>devarṣi</term> and use good discrimination to judge strength and weakness. 

The work I have spoken of cannot be attained without knowing the globe. Through the study of the globe, the success of these calculations is achieved. This should be contemplated by those of good intelligence. At the time of a planetary conjunction, one must observe the celestial sphere to see which planet stands above or below in the circle of the signs. Such a time is determined by the intersection of the two circles; this intersection is the **node** <term>pāta</term>. Even the nodes for the planets as stated in the Surya Siddhanta must be understood in this way.

<vocab>
- **Sphuta (स्फुट):** The "true" or "corrected" position of a celestial body, as opposed to its mean position.
- **Graha (ग्रह):** A "grasper" or planet, traditionally including the Sun, Moon, five visible planets, and the nodes.
- **Pata (पात):** A node; the point where a planet's orbit crosses the ecliptic (the Sun's path).
- **Gati (गति):** Motion or daily velocity of a planet.
- **Tithi (तिथि):** A lunar day, defined as the time it takes for the longitudinal angle between the Moon and Sun to increase by 12 degrees.
- **Bhagola (भगोल):** The celestial sphere or the sphere of the fixed stars.
- **Kadambavrtta (कदंववृत्ते):** The circle passing through the poles of the ecliptic.
- **Gola (गोल):** The globe or the science of spherical astronomy.
</vocab>

# The Calculation of the Precession of the Equinoxes

The celestial circle moves according to its own designated time, and as it rests within its own support, it undergoes a specific motion. Having gone a certain distance westward from the point of equilibrium, it then turns back. Through these degrees of oscillation, the celestial positions are refined. This movement is stated by the divinities and the ancient sages <note>original: "daivatairmunibhiḥ"</note> in their own treatises as the true method for determining the **Precession of the Equinoxes** <term>Ayanamsha</term> <note>the angular difference between the tropical and sidereal zodiacs, crucial for aligning the calendar with the seasons</note>.

In a great cycle, the total number of **revolutions** <term>bhagaṇa</term> of this precession is stated to be 600. To find the current position: take the years elapsed since the beginning of the **current age** <term>Yugadi</term>, multiply them accordingly, and divide by 1,200,000. From this result, the degrees and minutes of the **Precession-Point** <term>Ayanagraha</term> <note>literally "the precession planet," treating the shifting equinox as a mathematical body</note> are determined. 

By calculating the "arm" or sine of these degrees, the specific Precession degrees are obtained. These degrees determine the shift from the beginning of **Aries** <term>Meṣa</term> in the celestial circle. Because of the power of these seven corrections <note>referring to the traditional mathematical refinements applied to planetary positions</note>, the motion known as "the shaking" <term>calākhya</term> is understood by astronomers to be the cause of the shifting solstices. 

<margin>
Specific Value:
12 
degrees
</margin>

Without this calculation, the "unseen results" <note>original: "adṛṣṭaphala," referring to the merit gained from rituals performed at the precise astronomical moment</note> of religious acts cannot be achieved. Therefore, when the Sun enters a new sign—the **Transit** <term>Saṅkrānti</term>—one must use these Precession degrees to find the true time. If one ignores this, as some of the older authorities like **Muñjāla** <note>a famous 10th-century Indian astronomer who refined the theory of precession</note> have discussed, the calculation will be in conflict with observation. Wise astronomers should accept the refined calculation of the equinoxes. 

Through the square of the **sine of the maximum obliquity** <term>jinajyakā</term> <note>the sine of 24 degrees, the traditional Indian value for the tilt of the Earth's axis</note>, and by applying the mathematical rules of the "True Chapter" <term>Sphuṭādhikāra</term>, the exact intersection of the ecliptic and the equator is found. This has been explained by the great sages in their own treatises to ensure the determination of results is accurate. 

Even the observers in the **City of the Romans** <term>Romaka-pattana</term> <note>referring to the West/Alexandria, often cited in Indian texts as a reference point for longitudinal calculations</note> have refined the Precession based on the intersection of the celestial equator and the ecliptic. This method is the best means to reconcile theory with observation. When the Sun reaches these twelve points of transit, it is a time of great spiritual merit. 

The learned astronomer, the **Knower of the Sphere** <term>Golavid</term>, should understand the Earth’s circumference in **Yojanas** <note>a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</note> and the true diameter of the planetary orbits. By using the **Precession-inclusive** <term>Sāyana</term> positions, the "true distance" <term>karṇa</term> and the sines are calculated. This method, following the ancient **Solar Tradition** <term>Sauri-rīti</term> <note>the system of the Surya Siddhanta</note>, is the path of the great seers for achieving success in astronomical science with great effort.

# The Determination of True Planetary Distances

The arc produced by the planet’s own motion is situated within the radius. By using half the sum of the radius and the hypotenuse, and multiplying it by the medium-distance hypotenuse, then dividing by the radius, the **True Distance in Leagues** <term>sphuta-yojana-karna</term> <note>the physical distance of a planet from the Earth's center measured in yojanas, an ancient Indian unit roughly equal to 8-9 miles</note> is derived. This distance is distinct from the mere positions on the celestial sphere. In this matter, the views of the venerable Vishwarupa are also considered. By the words of the ancient texts, for all living beings, the distance to the interior of the orbit is specifically stated. That distance remains consistent with the principles of the lineage of the sages.

This differs from what was stated in the **Crest-Jewel of Siddhantas** <note>original: "Siddhānta Śiromaṇi," the influential 12th-century work by Bhaskara II</note>. Regarding the calculation of these distances from the measure of leagues, I shall speak according to the scientific tradition. For a planet situated on the sphere of the sky, the true distance is obtained when the orbit is multiplied by its hypotenuse and divided by the radius. Thus, the distance from the center of the Earth is calculated, as the planets wander through the air by the force of the winds. The true distances in leagues for the planets—Mars and the others—are calculated by these mathematical proportions.

Regarding the diameters of the planetary bodies, such as the visual size of **Venus** <term>Bhṛgu</term>, there is a discussion here. When Venus is at its highest point <term>ucca</term>, its diameter appears smaller, and when it is at its lowest point <term>nīca</term> <note>perigee, or the point closest to Earth</note>, it is larger. Some scholars claim the diameter of Venus is equal to half the diameter of the Moon, but this contradicts observation in the world. 

Therefore, the methods stated by **Bhaskara** <note>Bhaskara II</note> are considered incorrect here because they lead to contradictions with observation. When the calculations of the **Yavanas** <term>Yavana</term> <note>referring to Greek or Islamic astronomers whose works influenced later Indian scholars like Kamalakar</note> are properly applied, the true distance in leagues is clearly understood. Those who do not understand the difference between the high and low points of the orbit are merely confused by their own pride in their knowledge.

The determination of these visual sizes is for the purpose of identifying the true effects of the planets. To achieve the results of the planetary influences, one must follow the solar methods and the testimony of the ancient scriptures. Even if something is fit to be seen, if it is not perceived through the correct mathematical path, it is like looking at the sky in darkness. From this great work, all results will be obtained. Time, which is the soul of the universe, manifests in many ways. For the sake of successfully performing religious rites, one must master this knowledge of time.

<meta>This page continues the technical astronomical exposition from Kamalakar’s *Siddhanta Tattva Viveka*. While the previous page focused on calculating planetary distances (yojanas), this page shifts to "Gola" (spherical astronomy). It describes the construction of the celestial sphere and the primary circles used to define celestial coordinates. The text maintains the author's characteristic blend of traditional Siddhantic concepts with advanced geometric modeling.</meta>

### OM! Salutations to the divine Lord Soma-Shiva.

The concepts of **direction, place, and time** <note>original: "dig-desha-kala"</note> are essential for all students of this science. Having understood the truth from the peaceful teachers, and by the grace of Lord Ganesha, I shall describe the calculations related to the **Celestial Sphere** <term>gola</term>. For the sake of simplicity, one should imagine the Earth as a single sphere situated at the center of the universe. In this sphere, for the ease of calculation, the wise should visualize the various circles. Using a **sighting rod** <term>shalaka</term> <note>a physical instrument or pointer used in an armillary sphere to represent lines of sight</note>, the motion of the planets within the sphere's interior can be understood by those proficient in mathematics.

Within this sphere, one must first establish the circle known as the **Pole** <term>dhruva</term>. This should be made to extend to the North and South. At the center of the world, the experts designate a circle that passes through the East and West as the **Equator** <term>niraksha</term> <note>literally "without latitude," referring to the terrestrial equator where the celestial pole lies on the horizon</note>. Those regions located directly under this circle are considered the "Equatorial Regions."

The circle that passes through the poles of one's own local position is known as the **Meridian** <term>yamyottara-vritta</term> <note>literally "the South-North circle"</note>. For an observer at the equator, the **Zenith** <note>original: "kha-madhya"</note> lies directly on the equinoctial line. The city of **Lanka** <note>not necessarily the modern island, but a theoretical point on the equator used as the prime meridian (zero longitude) in ancient Indian astronomy</note> is celebrated by the wise as the capital of the gods, situated exactly on the equatorial line. 

From the meridian of one's own place, a transverse line is drawn at the center of the equator. Where the meridian and the equator intersect, the **Prime Vertical** <term>samamandala</term> <note>the East-West vertical circle passing through the observer's zenith</note> is formed. This circle always passes through the East and West points of the horizon. 

Due to the constant rotation of the **Provector Wind** <term>pravaha-vayu</term> <note>the celestial force believed to drive the daily rotation of the stars</note>, the gods at the North Pole see the stars moving clockwise, while the demons at the South Pole see them moving in the opposite direction. 

The circle of the **Zodiac** <term>rashivritta</term> <note>the ecliptic</note> intersects the **Equinoctial Circle** <term>nadika-vritta</term> at two specific points. These points are known as the **Vernal Equinox** <note>original: "mesha-adi," the start of Aries</note> and the **Autumnal Equinox** <note>original: "tula-adi," the start of Libra</note>. When the Sun moves North of the equator, it marks the northern course; when it moves South, it marks the southern course. 

The distance between the equator and the ecliptic at the solstices is known as the **Maximum Declination** <note>original: "parama-kranti"</note>. Some scholars describe these circles in various ways, but one should avoid contradictions by observing the actual positions of the stars in the sky. The circles that pass through the poles and are perpendicular to the equator are called **Diurnal Circles** <term>ahoratra-vritta</term> <note>circles of constant declination that a star follows during its daily rotation</note>.

By measuring the arc along the **Prime Vertical** from the zenith, the wise determine the **Altitude** and the **Zenith Distance** <term>nata-unnata</term> <note>technical terms for the vertical position of a celestial body relative to the horizon</note>. All these circles are constructed within the sphere for the purpose of precise calculation and observation.

<summary>This page defines the fundamental circles of the celestial sphere (Gola), including the Equator, Meridian, Prime Vertical, and Ecliptic, while establishing Lanka as the reference point for longitude.</summary>
<keywords>celestial sphere, Gola, Equator, Niraksha, Prime Vertical, Samamandala, Zenith, Lanka, Equinox, Vishuva, Kamalakar</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of spherical astronomy (Gola) from Kamalākara’s *Siddhānta Tattva Viveka*. While the previous page introduced the basic concept of the celestial sphere, this page delves into the geometric relationship between the center of the Earth and its surface, the intersection of celestial circles, and the calculation of solar positions and latitudes. It addresses the fundamental astronomical problem of parallax—the difference between a "view from the center" and a "view from the surface."</meta>

<margin>
T. Vi.
Folio 41
</margin>

<margin>
41
</margin>

When calculating the true motion within one’s own authority, one must consider the motion as it appears from the center of the **Celestial Sphere** <term>gola-madhya</term>. Because the circles are measured from that center, that which is calculated for the sphere’s surface is not the same as that at its center. One should visualize the center of the sphere. If the calculation is made for the surface, it must follow the rule of the two **Poles** <term>dhruva</term> and the circle known as the **Equator** <term>vishuvakhya-vritta</term>. Just as the two poles of the Earth are fixed, so too are the two **Poles of the Ecliptic** <term>kadamba</term> <note>original: "kadamba-dvayaye"; these are the points 90 degrees from the ecliptic path</note>.

The oblique motion that occurs there must be understood through the rule of the **Radius** <term>tribhojya</term> <note>literally "the sine of three signs," which equals 90 degrees or the total radius in Indian trigonometry</note>. The position of the planets is established where the two circles meet. Within the sphere, there are two circles representing the half-surface; these are known as the "friends" <note>likely referring to coordinate systems that work together</note>, one being the clear middle and the other the polar. By joining these two at a distance of ninety degrees, the **Declination** <term>apaparamosha</term> <note>the maximum distance from the equator</note> is determined.

Just as the equator is fixed by the two poles, so the declination is fixed by the two ecliptic poles. When the Sun, corrected by its daily motion, reaches the end of the **Meridian** <term>unmandala</term> <note>the "rising circle" or the prime vertical at the equator</note>, the degrees of its position are known. At that time, the **Zenith Distance** <term>natamsha</term> <note>the angular distance from the point directly overhead</note> of the Sun or the degrees of the Pole’s altitude can be observed. Through these degrees, the latitude of the place in the northern or southern circle is determined. 

In this sphere, the path does not begin with the sign of Capricorn <note>original: "mṛga"</note> if the motion is observed differently. When seen from the sign of Scorpio <note>original: "kīṭa"</note>, the path is seen as the **Southern Progress** <term>dakshinayana</term> <note>the sun's southward journey between the summer and winter solstices</note>. From the surface of the sphere, the direction and the degrees of the circle are observed through the intersection of the **Prime Vertical** <term>samamandala</term> and the horizon.

This is why there is a distinction between the **Geocentric** <term>bhu-garbha</term> <note>literally "Earth-womb" or center of the Earth</note> position and the **Topocentric** <term>bhu-prishta</term> <note>literally "Earth-back" or surface of the Earth</note> position. A single line originating from the center of the Earth reaches the surface and touches the observer. Because of this, the zenith distance observed by a person on the surface differs from the true arc calculated from the Earth's center. The arc of the **Gnomon** <term>shanku</term> <note>a vertical pillar used to cast shadows for timekeeping and observation</note> is born from the center, while the observer stands on the surface. For the visual sphere <term>driggolaka</term>, one must understand the difference in status between the center and the surface. 

To find the true position, one must subtract the difference caused by the Earth’s radius. The degrees observed on the Earth’s surface are not the true solar degrees; they are the "surface-apparent" degrees. By using the Earth's semi-diameter <note>original: "bhu-khāṇḍa"</note> and the sine of the altitude, the wise mathematician calculates the correction. Multiplied by the sine and divided by the radius, the result is the parallax. In the calculation involving the number 288 <note>original: "gajāṣṭa-netrai"</note>, the minutes of motion are refined. Whether the correction is small or large depends on the rotation of the Earth. In a triangle, if the two sides are known, the **Right Angle** <term>samakona</term> and the **Hypotenuse** <term>karna</term> allow one to find the unknown arc.

<summary>This page explains the geometric distinction between observations made from the Earth's surface versus its center, introducing the concept of parallax. It describes the primary celestial circles (Equator, Meridian, Ecliptic) and provides the trigonometric basis for calculating a planet's true position using the Earth's radius and the observer's latitude.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of spherical astronomy from Kamalākara’s *Siddhānta Tattva Viveka*. The previous page established the difference between the geocentric (center of the Earth) and topocentric (surface of the Earth) perspectives. This page moves into the specific geometric constructions required to calculate the declination and right ascension of celestial bodies, using the intersections of the ecliptic, the equator, and the diurnal circles.</meta>

-> ॥ **Salutations to the Illustrious One** ॥ <-

The relationship between the arc of the "arm" <note>the *bhuja* or the longitude of a planet measured from the nearest equinox</note> and its corresponding **Sine** <term>jya</term> is declared. Within these two, the **Sine** <term>jya</term> acts as the primary factor. Through the nature of the **Radius** <term>trijya</term>, the power of the **Sphere** <term>gola</term> is manifested according to the method of the **Sphere of Declination** <term>kranti-gola</term>.

Until that is fully grasped, I shall speak of its established position based on the traditional mathematical limbs <note>original: "vedanga-sthiti"; referring to the astronomical sciences as a limb of the Vedas</note>. Wherever the **Declination Circle** <term>kranti-vritta</term> is elevated within the center of the sphere, the light is made known. Below that, in the circle where it is situated, the vertical line is equal to the center. On the ground, this is the means for bringing forth the **Sine of Zenith Distance** <term>nata-jya</term>. The center of this is the third point; from that state, the "arm" is positioned. In this way, as long as it moves upward, it reaches the **Meridian** <term>yamya-uttara</term> <note>the north-south line passing through the zenith</note>.

In this context, the **Solstitial Circle** <term>ayan-vritta</term> lies between the two **Poles** <term>dhruva</term>. It is known as the **Meridian** <term>yamya-uttara</term>. There, the middle point of the sun’s path is marked, and the portions of the sun’s degrees at the limit of its wandering are known. From the center, the circles of the **Sphere** <term>gola</term> are brought to equality and become visible. Just as the planet is attached to the poles above, so too are the portions of the **Diurnal Circle** <term>dyujya</term> <note>the circle of daily rotation</note>. At the limit of the wandering, the night and day are produced by the movement of these circles.

Thus, at the central markers, where the portions of the "arm" are made, there are two circles called the **Equator** <term>nadi</term> <note>literally "the circle of tubes/nadi," the celestial equator</term>. These circles are parallel to one another. Within them, a secondary center is raised from the primary center at a distance equal to the **Cosine** <term>kotijya</term> of the planet’s position.

From the sun’s center on the **Ecliptic** <term>kranti-vritta</term>, one should provide the **Sine of the Arm** <term>bhujajya</term>. In the desired circle, that which is limited by the degrees of the "arm" is the very form of the cord situated in the middle of the "arm’s" end. This is known as the **Equator** <term>nadi</term> or the **Equinoctial** <term>vishuvokavi</term>. In the desired circle, at the union of these points, the rod-like motion proceeds from the sun’s center. I shall now describe the decrease and increase of this motion. By the half-cord of the Sine, the arc in the desired circle is formed; from there, the degrees of **Declination** <term>kranti</term> are determined. That which is subtracted from the pole is the northern distance of the **Equatorial Circle** <term>nadivalaya</term>.

The rule for the union between the sun’s center and the end of the arc is the **Sine of the Latitude** <term>vikhshepa-jya</term>. From the **Radius** <term>trijya</term>, the **Cosine** <term>koti</term> and the **Hypotenuse** <term>karna</term> are derived. This is the western declination. Between the center of the arc and the end, the motion stays within the **Equatorial Circle** <term>nadivritta</term>. Beyond that, the degrees of the "other cosine" are certain. Their **Sines** <term>jya</term> proceed from the equatorial cord toward the sun.

The "arm" of the sun’s center should be made into the base of the sine. With the **Radius** <term>trijya</term> as the hypotenuse, the **Maximum Declination** <term>parakranti</term> becomes the "arm" of the sine. From the high point of the node, the **Sine of the Arm** <term>bhujajya</term> in the circular path becomes the **Sine of the Declination** <term>kranti-jya</term>. Thus, the degrees of the "arm" are calculated. Through the multiplication and division of these qualities, the desired position is found. For the planet thus calculated, the direction of the sphere becomes clear. Therefore, its movement is determined by the reverse calculation of the sun's declination.

Now, on the north-south line, the sine of the "arm" from the center of the arc is given. Having established the center, one should imagine the **Day-Night Circle** <term>ahoratra-vritta</term>. From that center, the clear north or south position of the **Day-Night Circle** <term>ahoratra</term> is joined by a cord. From the middle cord, the transverse cord reaching to the limit is the **Sine of the Ascensional Difference** <term>udaya-lavanyaka</term> <note>related to *chara*, the difference in time for rising between the equator and a specific latitude</note>. The square of the **Sine of Declination** <term>kranti-jya</term> is subtracted from the square of the radius, and the square root is the **Cosine of Declination** <term>dyujya</term>.

This indeed is the junction point of the sun’s center and the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvamsha</term> <note>the coordinate along the celestial equator</note>. Situated between the center of the diurnal circle and the middle, it arises upon the earth. The **Sine** <term>jya</term> of the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvamsha</term> at the end of the **Radius** <term>trijya</term> is that which falls from the zenith. These are the minutes of the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvamsha</term> for the day and night.

Now, I shall explain another method for knowing the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvamsha</term> for the wise. Having imagined one’s own middle position, one should mark the North-South direction. If a cord is placed in the desired circle, it is used for the **Equatorial Instrument** <term>nadika-yantra</term>. Here, in the circle called the **Solstitial** <term>ayana</term>, the **Pole** <term>dhruva</term> is the constant. From that polar circumference, a cord is placed in the circle of the directions. That is the work of the **Polar Instrument** <term>dhruva-yantra</term>. The degrees of the "arm" in the circle of the day and night are determined. From that pole, moving north to the limit of the wandering, are the degrees of the Sun.

It describes the use of sines (Jya) and cosines (Kotijya) to map the relationships between the ecliptic, the equator, and the diurnal paths of the planets.</summary>

It follows the discussion of geocentric vs. topocentric positioning and moves into the specific trigonometry of Right Ascension and Declination. The text describes how to project positions from the ecliptic onto the celestial equator.</meta>

<page-num>42</page-num>

The degrees of the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha; the position of a celestial body measured along the celestial equator</term> are determined from the arc of the maximum declination. Even those degrees of declination that have been projected must be understood through their own power. By subtracting the square of the **Sine of Declination** <term>kranti-jiva-kriti</term> from the square of the **Radius** <term>trijya</term>, the wise astronomer calculates the arc-degrees associated with the equinoctial circle.

The degrees of the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha</term> exist in their own measure of the arc. When the degrees of the **Solstice** <term>ayana</term> are brought into union with the equator, those who understand the **Sphere** <term>gola-vid</term> <note>literally "knowers of the sphere"</note> must calculate the difference.

The maximum declination is the primary limit. The complement of the equinoctial degrees <note>the *koti* or cosine value</note> is established there. Here too, one must calculate the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha</term> from the degrees of the arc by the statement of the maximum declination. The resulting degrees are then observed in the division of the circle.

The **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha</term> moves in its own rotation from the declination. Even if it is in the northern circle, the calculation follows the rule of the **Diurnal Radius** <term>dyu-guna</term> <note>the radius of the small circle parallel to the equator on which a star moves</note> and the **Maximum Declination** <term>paramapama</term>. The desired declination is known through the intersection of the equinoctial and the polar points. The declination of a planet <term>kheta-ayama</term> is produced from the maximum declination at the tip of the "arm" <note>the *bhuja* or longitude</note>.

In the context of the two equinoxes, the maximum declination of the equinoctial degrees is also calculated from the **Diurnal Radius** <term>dyujyaka</term> starting from the pole. Here, in the reverse order of the generated declination, the maximum declination is found. Therefore, the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha</term> is determined by the methods of calculation for the benefit of all.

Subtracting the square of the **Sine of Declination** <term>ayamajya-kriti</term> from the square of the **Radius** <term>trijya</term>, one obtains the square root. This root, when divided by the **Radius** <term>trijya</term> and multiplied by the desired factor, gives the degrees of the arc; these are the **Equinoctial Degrees** <term>vishuvan-anshaka</term>.

If the **Cosine** <term>koti-jya</term> is multiplied by the **Radius** <term>trijya</term> and divided by the **Diurnal Radius** <term>dyujyaka</term>, the result in the arc, starting from degrees and minutes, provides the equinoctial position. In this way, the equinoctial degrees are determined from the sine of the arc and the **Diurnal Radius** <term>dyugana</term>.

When the planet is situated in the **Second Quadrant** <note>between 90 and 180 degrees</note>, one must subtract the result from the semi-circle <note>180 degrees</note>. If it has entered the **Third Quadrant** <note>between 180 and 270 degrees</note>, the result must be added to the semi-circle. If it is in the **Fourth Quadrant** <note>between 270 and 360 degrees</note>, it is subtracted from the full circle <note>360 degrees</note>.

Having thus known the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha</term>, the wise should apply the calculation. I speak here of the inverse method for those who take pride in their mastery of the **Sphere** <term>gola</term>.

The **Sine** <term>jiva</term> of the equinoctial degrees, divided by its own complement-generator and multiplied by the **Radius** <term>trijya</term>, gives the arc-degrees in the **First Quadrant** <term>prathame pade</term>. In the second, subtract from the half-circle; in the third, add to the half-circle; in the fourth, subtract from the full circle.

**The Rationale** <term>upapatti</term>:
The declination arises from a portion of the intersection between the equinoctial circle and the **Ecliptic** <term>kranti-vritta</term>. Where the two poles are joined by a thread <note>a mathematical chord or axis</note>, that is the distance between the two circles. This difference is known by the wise as the cause of the rising of the planets.

There is a distinction in name between the equinoctial circle and the ecliptic, but not in their fundamental nature. The **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha</term> is the distance measured from the beginning along the transverse line of the poles. That which is measured along the equinoctial circle is the primary measure. Through the power of these degrees, the declination is known. Those who understand the **Sphere** <term>gola-tattva</term> know that the declination resides on the ecliptic.

The **Sine of Maximum Declination** <term>paramayama-jya</term> multiplied by the **Sine of Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha-jiva</term> and divided by the **Radius** <term>trijya</term> results in the **Sine of Declination** <term>kranti-jya</term>. Because the multipliers and divisors are equal, everything stated here regarding the sequence of equinoctial degrees is mathematically consistent.

<meta>This page continues the mathematical derivation of planetary positions and spherical trigonometry, specifically focusing on the relationship between the celestial equator and the diurnal circles. It follows the previous discussion of Right Ascension and Declination calculations.</meta>

In the sphere of the planets, for the one located in the middle of the **Celestial Equator** <term>vishuvan-mandala; the great circle of the celestial sphere in the same plane as the earth's equator</term>, the measure is determined by its own **Radius** <term>trijya; the standard radius used in Indian trigonometry, often 3438 minutes</term>. Through the use of these radii, the positions are established in the planet-sphere by those who are wise in these matters. If something is known by the mind in a triangle, how can its completion be achieved there without the Sine? Therefore, the perfection of the work should be done with the **Full Sines** <term>purna-jya</term> as understood by one's own intellect. In my view, the most excellent method is that which is fully realized through this logic; thus, the tradition is expanded. 

The relationship between the planetary arm and the hypotenuse is understood through the study of the sphere. Here, in the context of the third placement, from the two known values—the **Cosine** <term>kotijya; the sine of the complement of an arc</term> and the **Sine** <term>jya</term>—one calculates the unknown side. For a known arm and a known hypotenuse, if the unknown arm is to be found, one must set them in their proper places. The Sine of the Cosine is divided by the Radius, and the square root of the sum of the squares of the two sines is equal to the unknown hypotenuse. Those who know this science say that when the Cosine is one side, the result is obtained through the division of the Radius. Through this method, even when there are differences in the observations, the truth is known.

In the sphere, the arc-hypotenuses are also known through the union of the Sines. If there is one at the tip of the hypotenuse, then there are three corners with unequal sides. From a right angle, the two sides are unequal; however, the two sides connected to the tip of the hypotenuse from a right angle are called equal in a **Trapezium-like figure** <term>ardha-sama-bahu; a figure with some equal sides</term>. The specific nature of that arc remains fixed on its side. It is a scalene or **Irregular Triangle** <term>vishama-bhuja</term>. Through the division of the known Sine, the wise should always know the measure of the arc of the side.

Furthermore, on the **Diurnal Circles** <term>ahoratravritta; the small circles parallel to the equator on which stars appear to move daily</term> and the **Equator** <term>vishuvan-mandala</term>, there are various measures. Even if the measures of the circles differ, the number of degrees remains the same. The degrees of the **Right Ascension** <term>vishuvan-ansha</term> which are in the middle of the two polar strings are the same as those measured on the diurnal circle. For the purpose of establishing the precise time, the rising of the signs must be calculated. 

In the circle of the equator, the **Sines of the Diurnal Radius** <term>dyujya</term> are used to find the degrees starting from the time of birth. In that context, one should know the **Twelve Signs of the Zodiac** <term>dvadasha-rashayah</term>. Starting from **Aries** <term>mesha</term>, the signs are fixed along the polar strings. The degrees of the Right Ascension are known by those learned in the science. Now, from the fixed point of Aries, the rising times of the signs are known in the **Equator** <term>unmandala; the six o'clock circle</term>. The time taken for the first part of Aries to rise is its **Rising Time** <term>udaya-kala</term>. In the circle of the signs (the ecliptic), for the three signs starting with Aries, the degrees of the equator that rise with them are called their **Rising Degrees** <term>udayansha</term> in one's own local latitude. These measures for the three signs starting with Aries are calculated as follows...

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of spherical trigonometry in astronomy, specifically the calculation of "ascensional difference" (chara) and the rising times of zodiac signs (udayaprabheda) at different latitudes. It transitions from general sine calculations to local terrestrial measurements.</meta>

53

From the three signs beginning with **Scorpio** <note>original: "keeta-griha-traya"; Scorpio, Sagittarius, and Capricorn</note>, and likewise through the two groups of six signs beginning with **Aries** <note>original: "meshadirashi-shatka"</note>, by calculating in reverse order according to the **equinoctial shadow** <term>palaprabha; the shadow cast by a vertical gnomon at noon on the day of the equinox, used to determine local latitude</term>, the twelve Sines are properly known.

The **Radius** <term>trijya</term> multiplied by the [Sine of declination] and divided by the **diurnal radius** <term>dyujya; the radius of the planet's daily circular path</term> produces the **Sine of ascensional difference** <term>charajya; the difference between the time a celestial body takes to rise at the equator versus a specific latitude</term>. In this way, the degrees of ascensional difference for the rising signs are determined by the wise for their own locations. By multiplying these by the proper factors, the results in **minutes of time** <term>palatmaka; literally "measured in breaths," here referring to units of time</term> are clearly obtained for practical use in the world. Now, for knowing the time of the rising of the signs in one's own country, the ascensional differences for the signs beginning with **Aries** must be subtracted from the **Right Ascensions** <term>lankodaya; the rising times of signs at the terrestrial equator</term>. Conversely, these are added for the three signs beginning with **Scorpio**. Through this method of addition and subtraction, the **Oblique Ascensions** <term>svadeshodaya; the local rising times of zodiac signs</term> are found. Because the celestial sphere sits tilted, that portion of the **zodiacal circle** <term>bhamandala</term> which is seen is either smaller or larger depending on the observer's location. As time passes, the signs from **Capricorn** onwards rise, and the time taken for their appearance is calculated accordingly. When the signs beginning with **Scorpio** reach the horizon, they do so with an increased or decreased duration. For example, the time taken for the rising of **Gemini** <note>original: "mithuna"</note> is reversed when considering the signs in the southern path. These time units, measured in **nadis** <term>nadi; a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>, are used to determine the exact moment a sign crosses the horizon.

In the center of the celestial sphere, the Sun rises at a time that is equal for all. However, in the southern regions, the six signs appear differently. This must be understood. Thus, by the grace of the Sun and other deities, and through the calculations of the Great Sages, the results of the passage of time and space are seen. When one laughs at the sphere <note>an idiomatic expression for observing the celestial rotations</note>, the **longitudinal circle** <term>golaka</term> is seen. If the Sun is positioned in the **Northern Hemisphere** <term>saumya-gola</term>, the duration of the day is always longer, as determined by the observer's latitude. Conversely, in the **Southern Hemisphere** <term>yamya-gola</term>, there is a difference in the time. This total time of 60 **nadis** <term>shastya-nadika; a full 24-hour day</term> is divided. When the Sun is in the northern signs, the **length of the day** <term>dinamana</term> increases and the night decreases; in the southern signs, the day is shorter and the night is longer. At the equinox, they are equal. This variation is always consistent with the Sun's position and the observer's latitude.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical formulas for calculating 'chara' (ascensional difference) and local rising times of zodiac signs, detailing how the length of day and night fluctuates as the Sun moves between the Northern and Southern Hemispheres.</summary>
<keywords>ascensional difference, equinoctial shadow, Oblique Ascension, Northern Hemisphere, Southern Hemisphere, length of day, zodiac signs, Jyotisha</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of spherical astronomy, shifting from the calculation of rising times (ascensions) to the differences between observations made from the Earth's center (geocentric) and the Earth's surface (topocentric). It references the authoritative work "Siddhanta Shiromani" by Bhaskara II to explain these corrections.</meta>

Where the degrees of latitude are equal to the maximum declination, there the horizon and the celestial equator coincide. For those located at the poles, the movement of the planets like **Mars** <note>original: "Kuja"; while Kuja usually means Mars, in this specific geometric context, it refers to the horizon circle or the "earth-born" line of visibility</note> appears to follow a different path due to the rotation of the celestial sphere. In that place, half of the celestial sphere is always visible as it revolves. Just as the degrees increase toward the north, the portion of the horizon that remains visible changes accordingly. For a person standing on the surface of the Earth, the duration of the day is determined by this visible horizon.

For those located on the surface, the visibility of the planets and the stars is slightly different from the view from the **Earth's center** <term>garbha; the mathematical center of the terrestrial globe</term>. In the lands of the mortals, this true position is always calculated through the path described previously. When a planet is located at the eighth house or in certain signs, its appearance varies. For instance, the signs of **the Bow** <note>original: "karmuka"; Sagittarius</note> and **the Deer** <note>original: "mriga"; Capricorn</note> are not seen at all in certain extreme latitudes, while **the Crab** <note>original: "karka"; Cancer</note> and **the Twins** <note>original: "mithuna"; Gemini</note> remain visible for long periods. Near the latitudes of 78 degrees, these signs may never set or never rise depending on the season.

The wise observer understands that at ninety degrees [the North Pole], the signs beginning with **Aries** <note>original: "kriyadi"</note> are seen in a specific manner, and the visibility of the northern and southern paths depends entirely on one's position on the Earth's surface. Scholars say that for those born in these northern lands, there is no doubt that the celestial sphere appears to rotate horizontally. One must understand the visible and invisible portions of the sky through the observation of the celestial path.

The length of the day and night is declared based on whether the sun is above or below the horizon. However, the true mathematical "day" is calculated from the **Earth's center** <note>original: "garbha-sthiti"</note>. Is there a difference in the length of the night or the visibility of the sun for an observer on the surface? Indeed, there is a darkness that prevails when the sun has set below the visible horizon but might still be above the geocentric horizon. If the sun is below the surface horizon but above the center-line, the day length must be corrected. This correction is known as the **topocentric view** <term>prishta-drik; the view from the surface of the Earth as opposed to its center</term>.

The day length mentioned for the horizon passing through the Earth's center is indeed accurate for mathematical theory. However, it is said here that for the purpose of calculating the results of an observation, one must use the method of the **Earth's surface** <note>original: "adhari-tya"</note>. To find the true fruit of the planetary positions, one must apply the correction for the observer's height. In the **Siddhanta Shiromani** <note>The "Crest-Jewel of Siddhantas," a definitive astronomical text written in 1150 CE by Bhaskara II</note>, it is stated that the Earth's diameter must be considered when calculating the rising of **Mars** <note>original: "kuja"; here referring to the horizon or the planet</note> and other bodies for those on the surface of the globe.

I shall now clearly explain the measurement of the day for those living in difficult or distant countries based on the distance from the Earth's surface. One must divide the distance between the two horizons by the root of the difference. By using the distance measured in **Yojanas** <term>yojana; a traditional Indian unit of distance, approximately 8 to 9 miles</term> along the surface of the Earth, the wise astronomer calculates the visible half of the horizon. That which is seen from the Earth's center may not be visible to the eye on the surface. This discrepancy depends on whether the planet is in the northern or southern celestial hemisphere.

What is the nature of this calculation? I shall state the knowledge obtained through the **Cosine** <term>kotijya; the R-cosine or the side of a right-angled triangle</term> and the **Radius** <term>trijya</term>. By multiplying and dividing by the proper fractions and considering the distance in **Yojanas**, the visible portion of the celestial globe is determined. Even for those situated below the surface, the calculation follows the same logic of the Earth's center. Thus, starting from the first sign, one should apply the sequence for the surface horizon. By using the **Radius** and the **Cosine**, and subtracting the necessary **Yojanas**, the result for the **topocentric horizon** <note>original: "ku-prishta-ardha-mandala"</note> is obtained by the learned.

It specifically references the Siddhanta Shiromani to explain how to calculate day length and planetary visibility for observers at high latitudes.</summary>

Without the knowledge of such distances, the sequential movement of the zodiac signs and the calculation of the Sun’s position cannot be determined. This method of dividing the parts <note>original: "bhāga vidhiḥ"</note> is always consistent with the **measure of time** <term>nāḍikā; a unit of 24 minutes</term>.

The arms of the observer at the **Earth's center** <term>garbhīya; geocentric</term> and the visibility from the **Bhairava** <note>likely referring to a specific astronomical school or a fierce observational method</note> perspective show that within one's own celestial sphere, the planets are always positioned in the half-visible sphere. Sequentially, whatever remains here is the night.

For those residing at the **Poles** <term>dhruvaga</term>, the visibility starts from the beginning of the signs. From the start of **the Lion** <note>original: "siṃha"; Leo</note> to the end of the signs, or from the start of the **Scorpion** <note>original: "kīṭa"; Scorpio</note> to the end of **the Bow** <note>original: "dhanu"; Sagittarius</note>, there is a decrease in the day's length for those at the poles.

When measuring in one's own **distance units** <term>yojana</term>, the visibility for humans standing on the surface must be understood. That which is stated regarding the geocentric view is only applicable when the observer is at the **Earth's center** <term>garbha-ja</term>. Therefore, the calculated position of the rising signs is stated as such; otherwise, it would not be accurate. The wise should understand this from the words of the author. Through those distances, the Sun is seen to be always risen in that specific diagram. The visible degrees of the **horizon circle** <note>original: "kacchatra-golaka"</note> would then be combined with the **distance units** <term>yojanas</term> of that place. In all regions, the calculation of the Earth's center and the observer's position is made through these visible degrees. Within the celestial sphere being relied upon, the visibility is calculated in the diagram through the degrees of the **horizon circle**.

When the degrees of visibility located above are greater than the parts of the **horizon circle** in various directions, then the sine of the vertical angle of the horizon's visible degrees <note>original: "koṭi-jyā"; the cosine or the sine of the complementary angle</note> is multiplied by the **radius of the sphere** <term>trijyā</term>. This radiance is doubled when the degrees of visibility are completely processed. <meta>This page continues the technical instructions for astronomical calculations. Having established the difference between the geocentric and topocentric perspectives, the text now details the mathematical "corrections" required to determine the Sun's precise position and the rising sign of the zodiac at any given time.</meta>

In this manner, the portions of the **mid-elevation** <term>madhyonnata; the height of a celestial body when it crosses the local meridian</term> are determined. Where these portions exceed the three circles, a correction <term>saṃskāra; a mathematical adjustment or refinement</term> must be applied in the same or opposite direction, according to the specific rules of the calculation. From these corrected degrees, and through the inverse application of these portions, the arc of the Sun is determined. In this way, whether one calculates forward or inversely from the Sun <note>original: "arka"</note>, the degrees of its position are established.

If the Sun is in the **first quadrant** <term>pada; one of the four 90-degree divisions of a circle</term>, the behavior of the shadow is calculated accordingly. If it is in the **second quadrant**, one must subtract or add six signs. In the **third quadrant**, the calculation involves a reversal, and in the **fourth quadrant**, the process follows the initial method. 

In the first quadrant, the shadow <term>chāyā</term> behaves in a specific way; in the second, the shadow increases while the measure of the day remains steady <note>original: "hraḍimatī"; possibly referring to a specific measurement of duration</note>. In the third, it decreases again, and what was lost is restored through growth. Even if the shadow falls toward the **South**, it is still considered the first quadrant. One should understand the movement of the Sun through these seasonal signs and markers everywhere.

In the eastern part, where the **geocentric horizon** <term>garbhakuja</term> meets the rising zodiac sign, that point is known as the **Ascendant** <term>lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term>. That very Ascendant is what is used in the calculation of the meridian. At any desired **civil time** <term>sāvana; time measured by the Earth’s rotation relative to the Sun</term>, the Ascendant is determined from the Sun’s position at that specific moment. 

One should not attempt this without first refining the position of the Sun. When the Sun’s rays are refined by the movement of the degrees, one must multiply by **sixty** <term>ṣaṣṭi; the base of the sexagesimal system used in ancient astronomy</term>. To find the desired time, use the current rising sign. If the time is multiplied by six and then refined, the two points of the Ascendant can be accurately calculated. 

For those seeking to calculate the time of arrival or departure, one must use the local **rising times** <term>svodaya; the time it takes for a specific zodiac sign to rise at a particular latitude</term>. The remaining degrees should then be multiplied by sixty and divided by the total rising time of the sign. The result, when added to the degrees of the signs, reveals the true position. Through this process, the **equinoctial degrees** <term>viṣuvāṃśaka; degrees of right ascension measured along the celestial equator</term> are determined. 

From the observed time, through inverse calculation, the position of the Sun and the Ascendant become clear. The results for auspicious or inauspicious times are thus made manifest. At any desired time, these degrees—when subtracted or added to the local rising times—reveal the Sun’s position for that specific observation. By observing the difference between the Sun and the Ascendant, and multiplying by the **mean rising time** <term>madhyodaya</term>, the desired civil time is found. All time is ultimately calculated from the rising of the Sun. This Sun, following its civil day, allows the astronomer to bring the entire rotating sphere of the heavens into calculation. The degrees that do not align with the equator are known as the **equatorial degrees** <term>viṣuvāṃśaka; right ascension</term>; these are determined as before, multiplied by the six-fold desired time to reveal the motion of the celestial sphere.

<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for calculating the Lagna (Ascendant). Ta. Vi.

As previously stated, the portions of the **hour angle** <term>nata; the angular distance of a celestial body from the meridian</term> are to be established; these become the degrees of the **Ascendant** <term>lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> derived from the planet. When divided by thirty, the result provides the sign and its degrees. This Ascendant should be refined by the **variable correction** <term>chala-saṃskāra; a mathematical adjustment for the planetary motion or observer's position</term>. This same process is applied to the corrected Sun <note>original: "calārka"</note>.

In the inverse order, according to the stated method, in the **right ascension** <term>viṣuvāṃśa; degrees of the celestial equator</term> and similar measures, the difference between the two is found along with the Ascendants. From the desired **time units** <term>ghaṭikā; a period of 24 minutes</term> from the sunrise, the Ascendant is established in right ascension. If the degrees are greater or lesser than the signs, the remaining Ascendant is to be calculated here. As before, from the hour angle, one's desired time is found. The Ascendant and its related values are more or less depending on the rising of the signs. From the two halves of the day, there is a unity in the middle; as with the signs starting from Aries <note>original: "ajādi"; the beginning of the zodiac</note>, the portions are to be established by the right ascension. For the calculation of the sphere <term>gola</term> starting from Aries, the signs are added to their own respective rising times to find the degrees of the Ascendant. These degrees are listed in the tables <note>original: "koṣṭaka"</note> provided. In a place where the **latitude** <term>palāṃśa</term> is six or seven, the Ascendant is established through these specific means.

On the planetary chart, the degrees of **precession** <term>ayanāṃśa; the shifting of the equinoxes</term> are applied to find the **true Sun** <note>original: "sphuṭa-arka"</note>. Having placed the degrees and so on, multiplied by the northern or southern factors of the Sun, and joined with the six portions, the time units of that location are added to the degrees. Subtracting the degrees from the desired interval, the remaining portions of the signs are found for the mid-point of the day or night; thus the Ascendant is found with the precessional correction <note>original: "sāyanake"</note>. When in that place the calculation is performed using the desired **obliquity** <term>akṣakarṇa</term>, the Ascendant of the Sun is observed at the desired time.

Through the precessional correction, the second method of finding the Ascendant is known. The desired time is multiplied by six and increased or decreased by the hour angle units to find the degrees of the Sun's son <note>original: "arkaja"; usually refers to Saturn, but here context implies a solar derivative</note>. For those who know, the portions from the middle hour angle are multiplied by three. Alternatively, from the Ascendant, the mid-point of the night or day is determined, and by adding six signs to the Ascendant, the **tenth house** <term>daśama; the Midheaven or Medium Coeli</term> is produced.

By the sine of the Ascendant's amplitude and the **sine** <term>jīvā</term> of the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśa</term> of the **Midheaven** <note>original: "kha-lagna"</note>, being multiplied and divided, the result in degrees is found. These are the northern or southern portions. The sine of the Sun's elevation on the **vertical circle** <term>drgvṛtta</term> is divided and multiplied by the sine of the difference between the Ascendant and the Sun; the arc degrees are then determined. By the method of subtracting or adding these arc portions, the sine of the elevation of the Midheaven is multiplied and divided. This yields the **cosine** <term>koṭi-mūrvī</term> of the northern degrees of the sign. From the established degrees, the desired calculation is performed. Therefore, from the degrees of the zenith distance, the sine of the Midheaven's elevation is multiplied and the remainder found; the Ascendant is thus established. The time and hour angle are determined through the sine of the **three-sign-deficient Ascendant** <term>tribhalagna; the point 90 degrees behind the Ascendant</term> multiplied by the Midheaven’s amplitude and divided, resulting in the middle zenith distance. Depending on the direction of the place, the coordinates are established in sequence. Through this method, the three-sign-deficient Ascendant is used as appropriate.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical exposition from the previous section. While the previous page focused on the calculation of the Ascendant (Lagna) and the use of right ascension, this page shifts toward the three-dimensional geometry of the celestial sphere (Gola). It specifically defines the various "sūtras" (axes or lines) and circles like the vertical circle (drgmaṇḍala) and the nonagesimal (vibhamalagna) used to determine planetary positions and parallax.</meta>

By means of the seamless calculation, the degrees of the **cosine** <term>koṭi; the sine of the complementary angle</term> and the primary parts should be established. One who is learned in the science of the celestial sphere <note>original: "gola-śāstra-vedya"</note> knows that the Lord of the Directions, touching the **nonagesimal** <term>vibhamalagna; the point on the ecliptic ninety degrees behind the ascendant, where the ecliptic is highest</term>, and the degrees of the cosine are always to be known by the learned.

<margin>5.18</margin>In the **vertical circle** <term>drgmaṇḍala; the circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term>, when it is in contact with the nonagesimal, the correct procedure is set forth by the wise. For the division of the extent, having combined the sines <note>original: "jīvā", a synonym for jyā</note>, these multipliers and divisors lead to the calculated result. 

When the correction is applied, the arc <note>original: "cāpa"</note> becomes true or corrected <note>original: "sphuṭa"</note> in its own direction. Alternatively, according to the followers of the Sun <note>referring to the Surya Siddhanta school</note>, the calculation should be made using the multiplier and the difference between the specific divisions; thus the cosine becomes clear.

If the planet is at the first point of its own celestial sphere, having imagined the intersection, then the **equatorial horizon** <term>nāḍī-valaya; literally the 'time-circle' or celestial equator</term> is formed. From that very point, the rising of the Ascendant is determined. 

Indeed, these degrees are established by the measurements of the sphere. In the vertical circle, which is called the arc of the nonagesimal, the **zenith distance** <term>drggati; specifically the arc of the vertical circle from the zenith to the planet</term> is to be calculated from the maximum sine. Through this spiritual and physical measurement of the sphere, the vertical circle is joined to the nonagesimal; if these two are on the horizon, the degrees of the Ascendant are always at their maximum.

The degrees of the cosine and the degrees of the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśaka; the angular distance from the zenith</term> are determined by the arc. By these degrees, the true seen position is established. From the intersection of the vertical circle and the celestial equator, the arc is known. All these forms are not different from the true nature of the sphere.

For the sake of knowing the degrees of the **cosine** <term>koṭi</term>, the formulas arising from the sphere should be understood and well-grasped. Through the knowledge of these alone comes the highest understanding. On the surface of the sphere, arcs and circles are produced. The **terrestrial sine** <term>cara; the difference between the right and oblique ascension, used for day-length</term> is determined. Without doubt, the East-West axis is connected to the East-West crossroads.

<margin>5.19</margin>They call the East-West line <note>original: "pūrvāpara-sūtra"</note> that which remains equal. The line that is distant in the North and South is the **meridian line** <term>dakṣiṇottara-sūtra</term>, known by the gods and men at the horizon. Those lines which are tied to the East and West are there for the rising and setting of the heavenly bodies. The experts say that the **equatorial horizon** <term>unmaṇḍala</term> and the **diurnal circle** <term>ahorniśa-maṇḍala</term> are tied to these.

Furthermore, that which is tied to the Zenith and the Nadir <note>original: "ūrdhvādhara-svāstika"</note> is called the **vertical axis** <term>ūrdhvādhara-sūtra</term>. This vertical axis is indeed the **line of sight** <term>drksūtra</term>. Likewise, the **intermediate axis** <term>koṇasūtra; the line pointing toward the intermediate directions like Northeast or Southwest</term> is tied to the corners of the earth. These lines, which are not joined to the zenith, follow a transverse path. One should understand the interval between them. The distance between two such lines is equal.

Thus, the distance between the lines of the East-West rising and setting is always established. One should calculate the description of the rising and setting, and by the sines, the movement of the circles is understood. Just as the circumference moves through the directions, the principle of the directional circles is understood here. The line tied between the two is the line of sight; by that, in the **prime vertical** <term>sama-maṇḍala; the vertical circle passing through the East and West points</term>, the three East-West lines are established, but they do not go to the North-South corners or the vertical line.

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric description of the celestial sphere. While the previous page established the nonagesimal and the vertical circle, this page focuses on the "Great Gnomon" (Śaṅku) and the various right-angled triangles formed by the intersection of the celestial circles. It provides the mathematical definitions for the base (bhuja), perpendicular (koṭi), and hypotenuse (karṇa) used to calculate planetary positions and shadows.</meta>

<page-num>46</page-num>

From the circle of the Sun <note>original: "tigmabhacakra"; literally the circle of the hot-rayed one</note>, having moved through the **vertical circle** <term>drgmaṇḍala; the circle passing through the zenith and the planet</term>, there exists a relationship where the distance from the **base of the gnomon** <term>śaṅkumūla; the point directly beneath the center of the celestial sphere on the horizontal plane</term> to the line of the east-west poles is defined. Thus, from the tip of the gnomon to its base, or within the surface of the gnomon's shadow, one determines the north or south direction. In the middle of the day, when the sun is at the meridian, the shadow falls directly north or south; at night, the calculation is made through the **meridian circle** <term>yāmyottara-vṛtta</term>.

<margin>Note on calculation</margin> At the base of the gnomon, from the east-west line, the distance in a particular direction is called the **base** <term>bhuja; the side of a right-angled triangle</term>. Through the refinement of the **gnomon’s base and amplitude** <term>śaṅkutalāgra</term>, the base is established. In this context, the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi; the side adjacent to the base</term> is the segment of the east-west line, and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> is the distance to the planet.

<margin>Ch. 45</margin> It is not so simple: the sine of the direction <note>original: "digjyā"</note> which acts as the base, lies on the horizontal plane. The hypotenuse is the radius. From the east-west intersection to the **diurnal circle** <term>dyurātra-vṛtta; the daily path of a star</term> is the north-south distance. Between the **equatorial horizon** <term>unmaṇḍala; the circle passing through the east/west points and the poles</term> and the zenith, the latitude is found. The hypotenuse of these two is always the meridian distance.

In the same way, the degrees of the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśa; the angular distance from the zenith</term> and the **altitude** <term>unnatāṃśa</term> are determined in the vertical circle. From midday, these are the degrees of the zenith distance; from the horizon, they are the degrees of altitude. The gnomons that are famous in the north-south and other directions are known by their respective names.

The following right-angled triangles are established for the sake of calculation:
The **equinoctial shadow** <term>akṣabhā; the shadow of a 12-digit gnomon at noon on the equinox</term> is the base, the gnomon of twelve digits is the perpendicular, and the **equinoctial hypotenuse** <term>palakarṇa</term> is the hypotenuse. 

Furthermore, as described in these methods:
1. The **sine of the earth** <term>kujyā</term> is the base, and the **sine of the amplitude** <term>agrayā</term> is the hypotenuse. 
2. The gnomon of the equatorial horizon is the perpendicular, and the segment of the shadow is the base.
3. In the vertical circle, the gnomon is the perpendicular, the shadow is the base, and the radius is the hypotenuse.

In this manner, the wise should recognize the similarity of the triangles. Even in the vertical circle, because of the distance from the center of the earth <note>original: "bhū-garbha"</note> to the surface, a slight difference <term>lambana; parallax</term> occurs. Therefore, the gnomon calculated from the center of the earth is not the same as that seen by the observer on the surface. The arc of the zenith distance is corrected by this difference. When the sun has risen in the east, the base of the gnomon and the direction of the shadow are calculated to find the exact position in minutes of arc <note>original: "kalā"</note>. Thus, the shadow and the amplitude are combined to produce the true base, which is used by those learned in the science of the celestial sphere.
