<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical exposition of celestial trigonometry. While the previous page defined the basic coordinate system and the positioning of the sun on the vertical circle, this page provides the specific geometric formulas for calculating the gnomon’s shadow, the sine of altitude, and the sine of declination using the "Triad" of the right-angled triangle (base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse).</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>

By the power of the **radius** <term>trijyā; the radius of the celestial sphere, usually standardized in Indian astronomy as 3438 minutes of arc</term>, and by the power of the specific planetary position, the **sine of altitude** <note>original: "kalāprasaiva"</note> is known. Thus, in the celestial sphere <note>original: "gole"</note>, when the radius is multiplied by the desired arc and divided by the **base** <term>bhuja; the sine of the arc</term> and the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi; the cosine of the arc</term>, the resulting values follow in sequence. 

Alternatively, by using the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, the relationship between the base and perpendicular is observed. Through the calculation of the **sine of declination** <term>krāntijyā; the distance of a planet north or south of the celestial equator</term> multiplied by the hypotenuse and divided by the radius, the **gnomon at the prime vertical** <term>sama-śaṅku; the sine of altitude when the sun is exactly east or west</term> is produced.

<margin>Calculation of the Gnomon</margin> 

When the sun is in the second quadrant, the **prime vertical gnomon** <note>original: "sama-śaṅku"</note> is equal to those results. If the base and perpendicular are multiplied, then the divisor is determined otherwise. Furthermore, when the hypotenuse is multiplied by the base and divided, that result is known.

When the base is multiplied by the hypotenuse and divided by the radius, the **prime vertical gnomon** <note>original: "sama-śaṅku"</note> is known by the wise. This may also be derived through the **sine of declination** <note>original: "krāntijyā"</note> multiplied by the base and perpendicular. 

<margin>The Divisor</margin> 

The **earth-sine** <term>kujyā; a value used to calculate the length of the day</term> and the **sine of declination** <note>original: "apamajye"</note>, when divided by the hypotenuse, provide the necessary values for the sphere. The **experts in mathematics** <note>original: "gaṇita-pravīṇaiḥ"</note> should perform this by reversing the multiplier and the divisor. 

Through the pride of the **base** <note>original: "dos" or "bhuja"</note> and the **perpendicular** <note>original: "koṭi"</note>, the **hypotenuse** <note>original: "karṇa"</note> is found:
- The sum of the squares of the base and the perpendicular, when its square root is taken, becomes the hypotenuse. 
- The square root of the difference between the squares of the hypotenuse and the base is the perpendicular. 
- The square root of the difference between the squares of the hypotenuse and the perpendicular is the base.

This is the path of knowledge illustrated for those who seek to understand the three. In the absence of these three (base, perpendicular, and hypotenuse), knowledge is like something non-existent <note>original: "asatvātmakam"</note>, as the calculation cannot proceed anywhere. This internal logic is difficult to grasp for those who stay on the surface, but it is the truth that holds everywhere without deviation.

The **gnomon at midday** <note>original: "madhyāhna-śaṅku"</note> is known through this justice <note>original: "nyāya"; logic or rule</note>. In the time of the three <note>likely referring to the three signs or seasons</note>, starting from **Aries** <note>original: "meṣa"</note> and **Taurus** <note>original: "vṛṣa"</note>, when the sun has passed through the two signs and is refined by the movement, it moves through the **northern and southern celestial hemispheres** <note>original: "udag-dakṣiṇa-golo"</note>. The gnomon of the midday is then calculated by the radius multiplied by the sine of the latitude. 

<unclear>kalā</unclear>... Thus, the wise who are skilled in calculation understand the inversion of multipliers and divisors in the science of the stars.

<summary>This page outlines the fundamental trigonometric identities used in Indian astronomy, specifically the "Pythagorean" relationship between the base (bhuja), perpendicular (koṭi), and hypotenuse (karṇa) to calculate the gnomon's shadow and planetary declination.</summary>
<keywords>Sine (Jya), Radius (Trijya), Gnomon (Shanku), Declination (Kranti), Hypotenuse (Karna), Geometry, Aries (Mesha), Taurus (Vrisha)</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical exposition of celestial trigonometry. While the previous page focused on the general positioning of the sun, this page details the specific formulas for calculating the midday gnomon, the shadow's length, and the adjustments required for different times of the day and different hemispheres.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>

<margin>N. Vi. Ke. 47</margin>

The **divisor** <term>hṛti; a specialized hypotenuse used in spherical trigonometry</term>, when multiplied by ten or divided by the **equinoctial hypotenuse** <term>palakarṇa; the hypotenuse of the shadow cast by a 12-digit gnomon at the equinox</term>, becomes what is known as the **midday gnomon** <term>dinārddha-śaṅku; the sine of the sun's altitude at noon</term>. When this result is multiplied by the **radius** <term>trijyā</term>, the resulting value is the sine. 

Alternatively, if the **sine of the day** <note>original: "dinamaurvika"</note> is multiplied by ten and divided by the equinoctial hypotenuse, the result is the **sine of the sun's altitude** <note>original: "jya"</note>. This can also be derived from its own **base** <term>bhuja</term> and **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>. 

Now, if the **midday gnomon** is multiplied by the sine of the latitude and divided by the cosine of the latitude, the result obtained is the **latitude-corrected sine**. This value serves as the midday pillar <note>original: "dinamadhyayaṣṭi"; a metaphorical term for the noon altitude</note>. Here, the calculation should be performed like that of the gnomon. 

When the **sine of the altitude** <note>original: "hṛjjyā"</note> is added to or subtracted from the **hemisphere** <term>gola; the north or south celestial hemisphere</term> according to the rule, it is called the **divisor** <term>hṛti</term>. That divisor, multiplied by ten and divided by the equinoctial hypotenuse, gives the **midday divisor**. 

By subtracting the **versed sine** <term>utkramajyā; 1 minus the cosine</term> from the radius, the gnomon is produced. For the desired time, the sine is known; when divided by the gnomon, it results in the **shadow's brightness** <note>original: "bhā-dyuti"</note>. The hypotenuse multiplied by the radius and divided [by the gnomon] gives the **midday shadow hypotenuse**. 

Alternatively, twelve <note>original: "dvādaśa"</note> multiplied by the radius and divided by the gnomon's shadow gives the result. Since the equinoctial hypotenuse is the multiplier here, the result becomes the **divisor of the shadow** <note>original: "chāyā-hṛti"</note>. The sine of the sun's altitude, when divided by the shadow's hypotenuse, produces the **equal-shadow** <note>original: "samadyuti"</note>. 

In the calculation of the **zenith distance** <term>nata; the angular distance from the zenith</term> at midday, the difference between the altitude and the observer's position is used. The remaining portion of the day is called the **elevation** <term>unnata</term>, and the elapsed portion is the **zenith distance** <term>nata</term>; from these, the midday position is determined. 

Thus, for calculating the shadow and hypotenuse at any desired time: the time elapsed since sunrise or remaining until sunset, adjusted by the **ascensional difference** <term>cara; the difference between the half-day and the 90-degree quadrant</term>, according to the sun's hemisphere <term>gola</term>, becomes the **elevation**. When this is multiplied by the sine and divided by the radius, it is termed the **kalā**. When that **kalā** is multiplied by the **sine of the latitude** <note>original: "sanetrajīvayā"</note> and divided by the radius, and then multiplied by the sun's position and divided by the equinoctial hypotenuse, it becomes the **column** <term>yaṣṭi</term>. 

Alternatively, the gnomon of the sun's altitude divided by the sine of the ascensional difference results in the **desired column** <note>original: "iṣṭayaṣṭi"</note>. This is added to or subtracted from the **radius** according to the direction of the sun’s hemisphere. That result, combined with the sine, is the **desired divisor** <term>iṣṭa-hṛti</term>, which is the same as the column itself. By using the desired divisor with the gnomon, the desired gnomon is produced. 

Alternatively, the **versed sine of the zenith distance** <term>natotkramajyakā</term> subtracted from the radius gives the result. The radius multiplied by the shadow and divided by the gnomon results in the **divisor**. 

Wise scholars should perform the addition where subtraction is indicated and subtraction where addition is indicated in the case of **inverse calculation** <note>original: "viloma-śodhana"</note>, similar to the methods used for the rising sign <term>lagna</term>. However, in the case of a combination, addition remains addition and subtraction remains subtraction. 

If the zenith distance is less than five degrees <note>original: "nāḍī"</note>, it is not considered "distant." The versed sine of the zenith distance, when combined with the divisor, produces the gnomon. In the **southern hemisphere** <term>yāmya-gola</term>, the calculation is adjusted accordingly. From this point forward, the proportions are determined through observation and the mathematics of the **prime vertical** <term>samamaṇḍala</term>.

<meta>This page continues the technical exposition of spherical astronomy, specifically focusing on the geometry of the celestial sphere as viewed from the North Pole (Meru) and the calculation of coordinates like the sine of altitude and the zenith distance. It builds on the previous page's discussion of the "divisor" and the "midday gnomon."</meta>

<margin>54</margin>
<margin>55</margin>

One should perform this calculation; even if that were the case, when the degrees of zenith distance <term>natāṃśa; the angular distance from the zenith to a celestial body</term> are increased by the complement <term>koṭi; the perpendicular side of a right-angled triangle, often the cosine</term>, they are joined within the circle of the **Meru** <note>original: "meru"; the celestial North Pole or the central axis of the earth in Hindu cosmology</note>. If it is equal to the desired position on the circle, then in that very place, having multiplied and leveled it, the work should be done.

In that place, the directions are manifested at the desired time of the day and night by the half-sphere. By the line of the path seen there, which is from the east, and is like the complement in the prime vertical <term>samavṛtta; the great circle passing through the east, west, and zenith</term>, it is led to the half-sphere. That which is not the midday line <note>original: "alamam"</note> here would not be the prime vertical. Indeed, there is only one such thing there, which is known as the **divisor** <term>hṛti; a technical value used as a celestial hypotenuse</term>. The sequence of the day and night is stated here.

From the position of the sun, the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>natāṃśajīvā</term> multiplied by the **sine of the day-radius** <term>dyumaurvī</term>, and the cosine of the latitude <note>original: "tribhādikko'śvakoṭimaurvya"</note> multiplied by the half-width of the expansion, and divided sequentially by the **radius** <term>trimaurvī; the sine of three signs, or 90 degrees</term>:
* The first result is the sine of the zenith distance in the **vertical circle** <term>dṛgvṛtta</term>.
* The second result is in the **equator** <term>apavṛtta; the celestial equator</term>.
* The third result is in the **auxiliary circle** <term>phalavṛtta; literally "the circle of results"</term>. 

With these, inversely, the zenith distance is always found. All results are relative to the **zenith** <term>svastika; the point directly overhead</term>. In the vertical circle, after the zenith distance has passed, there are other results. The cosine of the zenith distance of these result-circles is what is called the **divisor** <term>hara</term>. The divisor is taken from the radius.

These are known as the **base-values** <term>bhuja; the horizontal side of the triangle</term> in the two halves. The sine of the result is their difference or sum, depending on whether the directions are different or the same. Above the cosine, the portions are divided. The sine of the base result and the cosine are divided by the radius to find the **gnomon** <term>nara; the vertical pillar used for shadows, or the sine of altitude</term>. The sine of the result multiplied by the base, or the sine of the altitude, becomes the sine of the degrees of altitude. The square root of the difference between the squares of the altitude and the divisor, or the sine of the altitude, is used in the southern direction according to the prescribed method.

The degrees of zenith distance are thus calculated in the southern portion. From these degrees, the sine of the base result in the southern direction is refined. The final values, when well-refined, provide the degrees. The sine of the base in the vertical circle and the square of the sine of the zenith distance are calculated; from that, in the result-circle, the sine of the zenith distance is divided and extracted. By the sine of altitude and the cosine of altitude, ninety degrees minus that arc is found.

The sine of the zenith distance, multiplied by the cosine, and divided separately by the day-radius, produces the sine of the zenith distance. This is how it is done at the **Meru** <note>original: "meru"</note>. By the squares of the parts and the cosine-sines, divided separately by their respective divisors, the realization of these two occurs as previously stated. This is also done in the circle of the path.

Likewise, from the sum of the half-divisors, everything is driven by the rule of time. I shall speak of that ray of light from the south. Due to the difference in directions of the earth, the sky, the zenith, and the Meru, there is a single method for the mathematical lines. In the vertical circle and the circle of directions, the meridian <term>yāmyottara</term> is not in the middle. By this, the portions of the sun's direction are shown correctly. Because of the difference in the sun's path, all zenith distances are like the midday statement. In the upper circle of the region, in the vertical circle where the three worlds are the base, from one's own zenith to the directions, these are the results. Whether in the direction of the region and time, or in the north-south, or in the prime vertical, what else is there to say?

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical exposition from Folio 47, transitioning from the calculation of shadows to the precise determination of the sun's or a planet's direction (azimuth). It references the authoritative rules established by Bhaskara II and details the intersection of the celestial equator, the vertical circle, and the horizon to define positions in the northern and southern hemispheres.</meta>

Whether through variation or distinction, the existence or absence of these states is understood by the wise as being essentially the same. The latter portion is final; even for the learned, it is <unclear>difficult</unclear> and may not be necessary. If one desires to know, it is said that because logic is infinite, one should follow the rules stated by Bhaskara <note>referring to Bhaskara II, the 12th-century astronomer</note> and others in their own works. The direction and its calculation depend on the sine of azimuth <term>digjyā; the sine of the angular distance of a celestial body from the prime vertical</term>. When multiplied by one's own rule, the result is the direction. By the combination of the directed sine and the daily motion, the two become equal. If they are always the same here, then through the distinction of the "bowl" <term>kapāla; the hemispherical division of the sky</term>, the wise should understand it as one. That very thing becomes the shadow, and by that, the difference in the "bowl" is known. In the same direction, by the combination of various observations, the differences are indeed determined. For calculating the direction, one should place the sun in its own northern or southern sphere.

When the direction is calculated in one direction, and if the sine of azimuth is greater than the sine of the sun's altitude in the southern hemisphere, then in the northern hemisphere, it disappears from the horizon <term>kṣitija; the circle where the sky meets the earth</term>. Having established the junction of the spheres <note>original: "gola sandhi"</note> in the center, the planet is located there. Whether in the south or the north, the celestial equator <term>nāḍī vṛtta; the projection of Earth’s equator into space</term> and the declination <term>krānti; the angular distance of a body north or south of the celestial equator</term> are joined by various additions at the horizon. The degrees of direction between those two are measured. The declination <term>krānti</term> is the measure of the distance from the intersection of the vertical circle <term>dṛgvṛtta; the circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term> and the equator. Those two intersections at the horizon are the points of observation on the desired circle. The desired declination <term>krānti</term> in the south or north is determined by the distance from the vertical circle to the equator.

The degrees of the points of intersection between the vertical circle and the celestial equator arise from the confusion of the spheres. Gradually, the distance between the ecliptic and the celestial equator is the maximum declination <note>original: "parama-krānti"</note>. The degrees are the form of the planet's position. In this way, the degrees from the celestial equator to the planet's position, and the distance between the two poles of the vertical circle and the equator, are the degrees of the direction. In the desired calculation, the degrees of the vertical circle are its thirty degrees from the southern point. From that, the direction and the hypotenuse of the declination are found. In the celestial equator, the "upright" <note>original: "koṭi"; the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</note> is known, or from the arc produced, the two uprights are found. There is no third one. By the degrees of the arc related to the declination <term>krānti</term> made by the difference, the intersection is produced. From the three hypotenuses, the southern expansion is determined. Directly from its own declination <term>krānti</term>, the same value is moved further; this is the declination <term>krānti</term> of the sun. When the direction of the sun and the declination are the same, they stay in one quadrant. The vertical position and the declination <term>krānti</term> are then attached to the corner. In the circle of the world, between the two, the degrees of the vertical circle appear. By the difference of those degrees, the distance is found. In the northern sphere, if the southern sine of azimuth <term>digjyā</term> of the planet located above the horizon is subtracted from the northern value, the result is clear. In the celestial sphere, the declination <term>krānti</term> and the vertical circle are attached to the junction of the spheres. When the value is greater, it is the southern position of the observer.

<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical exposition from Folio 48. Having previously discussed the sine of azimuth and the authority of Bhaskara II, the text now moves into specific geometric calculations involving the "base" and "perpendicular" of shadows to determine the sun’s exact position on the celestial globe.</meta>

Even if the ascendant <term>lagna; the point of the zodiac rising on the eastern horizon</term> is placed in two positions, the shadow is seen in the same way. However, it is through the extension of these positions that it becomes accessible to our form of reasoning. In that context, the observed planet is located in its own position in the diurnal circle <term>ahorātra-vṛtta; the daily path of a celestial body</term>.

When the ascendant is named, the measure of the shadow's tip is known through the sine of the direction. By this, the nature of the globe <term>gola; the celestial sphere</term> is understood, for without it, the circles themselves are not grasped. Here, the shadows are the observed rays. These differences 
<margin>Tattva-Viveka, Section 51</margin> 
are discarded. The remaining logic of the calculation is that when the position is not precise, the sine of azimuth <term>digjyā; the horizontal angle of a celestial body from the north or south</term> is produced by the effort of those who are attached to the science of observation. From that, the base <term>bhuja; the side of the shadow triangle representing the north-south distance</term> is formed. 

<margin>49</margin> 
When the sine of azimuth is multiplied by the shadow and divided by its hypotenuse <term>karṇa; the oblique shadow length</term>, the base is produced. Conversely, from that base, the radiance <term>vibhāsā; possibly referring to the intensity or the clear sine</term> is divided, and the hypotenuse is multiplied by the sine of the arc to become the globe's measure. When the sun is in the northern sphere <term>saumya-gole; the northern celestial hemisphere</term>, the base multiplied by the hypotenuse should be added. Thus, the northern brightness <term>saumya-kukṣī prabhā</term> is established. Alternatively, it is subtracted. When the base is in the northern part and is struck by a confusion of directions, then it is divided and the arc is surpassed. 

Having established the square of the base, one should subtract it from the square of the shadow's length; this is the method. Thus, the other perpendicular <term>koṭi; the east-west component of the shadow</term> is found here in the western bowl <term>kapāla; the western hemisphere of the sky</term>.

The same should be done on the surface of the earth. Thus, in the Kali age <note>the current historical epoch in Hindu cosmology</note>, whatever is observed on the globe—the shadow of the sun—enters the tip of the shadow's tip where it intersects the circle. Whether in the southern or northern directions, the sines of the directions at the time of death <note>likely "mṛta-kāla" refers to a specific elapsed time or the "shadow-end" time</note> are the two bases. These are divided by the hypotenuse and the human factor <term>nṛ-guṇa; often a constant like the gnomon height, usually 12 digits</term> of the shadow's tip. From the measure of the shadow, two different directions are obtained, and their sum or difference results in the true motion in the circle. 

This becomes clear through the application of the globe's mechanics. Here, the south and north are observed from the sun's entrance into Aries <term>meṣārka; the vernal equinox</term>. Even within the distance of the directional parts, the earth is established as one. When the earth and the diurnal circle are known, the southern direction is understood. All those shadows which fall on the ground are fixed there. The gnomon <term>śalākā; the vertical rod used to cast the shadow</term> is of a measured length. From that, the sky-direction is determined. Each direction is imagined and from it, the perpendicular is formed. Another line represents the base, which is the north-south line. From this, all directions are known. 

Through this very method, the sun's path is correctly grasped. Rising upwards, the knowledge of directions is attained here. By the various methods performed by the wise, that which is equal becomes steady. At the rising of the sun, the ascendant is perceived. Or, in the higher world, the moon rises. The light of the east is not seen without the sun. At that time, the direction should be perfected. Whether northern or other, the western direction is used in practical affairs. Due to the difference of local longitude <term>deśāntara</term>, if the directions are disturbed, they are not constant. Before or after noon <term>dinārddhataḥ</term>, the appearance of the shadow exists. What is the shadow of two? For one who stands in the center of the directions, it is the result of practice. Because of the union of the shadow and the direction, the tip of the shadow at the central line is the long shadow. The square root of the difference of the squares of the hypotenuse and the base is the perpendicular. From that, the shadow is known. The hypotenuse is found from the sum of the squares of the gnomon and the shadow. This is the reverse calculation of the rays.

<meta>This folio continues the mathematical derivation of planetary positions, shifting from the general theory of shadows to the specific geometric correction required because the observer stands on the Earth’s surface rather than at its center (parallax).</meta>

For a man standing on the surface of the earth <term>bhūmispṛṣṭa; an observer on the ground</term>, the influence of the divided circles of the heavens is manifested through clear observation. That which is perceived as the center of the lunar globe <term>indū-jagoḷa-kendra; the center of the Moon's sphere</term> possesses its own internal <unclear>distance</unclear>... <note>The text here discusses the difference between the 'surface-view' and 'center-view' of a planet.</note> This calculation for the movement of the planet in its orbit is clear. In the direction desired, the line of the rear direction is to be established by the expert to solve the problem. This practice for the geocentric center <term>garbha; literally 'womb,' referring to the center of the Earth</term> is difficult to achieve from the observer’s line of sight... <warning>Ink bleed: The relationship between the observed surface position and the geocentric latitude is discussed here.</warning> From the geocentric latitude, the degrees of altitude are found. These parts are produced by the calculations of the practitioner using the "Radius" <term>trijyā; the sine of three signs, or 90 degrees</term>. For the sake of the calculation, one’s own gnomon <term>śanku; a vertical shadow-pole</term> is used. 

Subtract the solar section and, seeing the remainder, the gnomon of the observed altitude is found. From that, the geocentric position of the planet <term>garbhīya-graha; the planet as seen from the Earth’s center</term> is determined. In this circle, in the middle of the heavens, one's own distance is obtained... subtracting the square and taking the root... by the surface-touching observer. <note>This refers to the Pythagorean theorem used to find the distance between the observer and the planetary center.</note> This is understood by the wise. When the "arrow" <term>śara; the celestial latitude or polar distance</term> is present, the latitude of the point is found by the degrees of altitude. The line of the direction is established through the mathematical joining of all these factors. From the "vertical arrow" and the "horizontal arrow," the center of the circle is reached. 

By these two, the calculation is made. <center>3</center> These are combined. <center>6</center> There are three vessels <note>likely referring to components of a water clock or parts of a diagram</note>. Just as it should be, I shall quickly speak of the knowledge of direction, place, and time. By the sine of the speed, the distance is determined. According to the measure of your "branch" <term>viśikhā; the secondary sine or latitude</term>, the front-wind <unclear>is calculated</unclear>. In a triangle <term>trikoṇa</term>, the measure is three and a half. One of its angles is a right angle <term>samakoṇa</term>, the other is surely irregular. In the right angle is the center; in the irregular angle is the point of the <unclear>surface</unclear>. 

Thus, the center of the Earth is the center of the circle. From the lines comes the unity of the three circles. By this, the wise should construct a set of cells <term>koṣṭha; a grid or table for calculation</term>. From the Earth's center, the degrees of the surface are found, and by that method, the minutes of time <term>nāḍikā; a unit of 24 minutes</term>. The thirty equal divisions <term>the 30 degrees of a zodiac sign</term> should be made as degrees of the Earth. By their own threads <note>referring to cords used to mark lines on an astronomical instrument</note>, the observers move... <margin>twenty measures</margin>... there, the wise have performed the calculation.

### On the Correction of Observations and Planetary Degrees

Those which are omitted <note>original: "luptāḥ"</note>, as described by the diameter, are the meridian degrees. In the zenith distance <term>nata; angular distance from the zenith</term>, through the division into two parts, the degrees for the observer are not to be grieved. Thus, the degrees of the celestial zenith do not become the highest point of the sky. By the application of corrections <term>saṃskāra; mathematical adjustments applied to astronomical data to achieve accuracy</term>, the errors regarding the angle are addressed. By multiplying these in order, the degrees of declination <term>kānti-bhāga; the angular distance of a planet north or south of the celestial equator</term> on the gnomon <term>nara; a vertical pillar used to cast a shadow</term> are to be used as sines. These sines are tied to the degrees of the Sun's own "arm" <term>bhuja; the sine of the celestial longitude</term> in the calculations.

By the number six <note>original: "rasaiḥ," a numerical code for 6</term>, those who know this 30 <note>likely referring to 30 degrees per zodiac sign</note> should calculate the "ascensional difference" <term>carakarma; the difference between the time of rising of a sign on the equator and at a specific latitude</term>. From the rising, in order, the degrees are applied. The six signs of the zodiac <note>original: "maya-ṣaṭ-sajñakā"</note> are used in direct and inverse order. By looking at these inversely, and after subtracting three signs, the rising of the signs is to be calculated. In the third part, with the division of the six signs into single parts, thirty degrees are placed in direct and inverse order. From the degrees of the Sun's position, the conjunction of the desired time occurs. 

By the inverse correction <note>original: "vyasta-saṃskāra"</note> of the zenith degrees, the time between the Sun and the planet becomes clear <term>sphuṭa; the "true" as opposed to the "mean" or average position</term>. In the Northern Hemisphere <note>indicated by red ink in the original manuscript</note>, when the motion is less, the sine should be added there. If the degrees are more, they should be added to the degrees of the signs. Three finger-breadths <term>aṅgula-traya</term> are always used for the distance. For the slow-moving equation <term>manda; the correction for the planet's elliptical orbit</term>, the center's "arm" is the interval. From that center, the own measurement is joined. 
<insert>By the correction of the equation of the center, the two positions on the instrument are made clear. In the instrument, the division of the "arm" of the planet is nine and three 3 <note>possibly 93 or a ratio of 9:3</note>.</insert> 

One's own node is in the direction of the sky. Here, the "arm" <term>bhuja</term>, "upright" <term>koṭi</term>, and "hypotenuse" <term>karṇa</term> are named; they are mutually dependent. In the calculation, where the results are equal, this should be done by the wise. In the proportion, the Radius <term>trijyā; the sine of 90 degrees, often used as a constant in Indian trigonometry</term> is the standard, and the desired result is the "fruit." If a circle is drawn from the center, one obtains it. By giving or taking away from that circle, the lines are formed. In the middle, by the result of the shadow, it is written from the center. Having given that obtained circle, it is joined to the line of the Radius in the instrument. 3 

Through the result of the equation, the difference between the two is found; the Radius then becomes the standard from the line. From the center on the ground, the division is made; that is the proportion. This is the result of the desired shadow at the center of the instrument. When there is no knowledge of this, it is obtained through the measurement of the Radius. From the center, the shadow should be reduced by the fall of the node. The Radius is said to be the standard if the result is desired. By joining the text, the degrees of the zenith are understood here.

<margin>T. Vi. Ka. 51</margin>
<margin>51</margin>

In this manner, from the center of your upward-looking eye <note>original: "ūrdhvanetrasya"; referring to the observer's zenith</note>, wherever the half-portions are mentioned, the time of that portion is situated transversely between the two midpoints of night and day in the sky. These should be inferred there regarding the "eye." The time should be understood through the divisions of time; the eye is near the day, and it is situated transversely. 

Because you strive to understand the celestial sphere <term>gola; the globe or the celestial sphere</term>, the power of the instruments <note>original: "yantra"</note> arises from the Sun. That time is always found in all places on Earth, near the great day, where the light is clear. In the sky, it is seen as double. At the equator <term>nirakṣa; the "no-latitude" line</term>, the time of the elevated position and the moment of observation should be known by those learned in the established truths <term>siddhāntajñaiḥ; experts in astronomical treatises</term> who are proficient in the logic of the spheres. This is known through various methods of poetic and logical reasoning. From this, the rationale <term>vāsanā; the underlying explanation or demonstration of a mathematical rule</term> is established. The time and place of the Earth are known from the produced results. The mathematics created here in this "vessel" of knowledge is unprecedented and causes wonder in men. Through the mathematics of time, place, and direction, the essence of the work is brought to light in its composition.

<insert>**|| Thus ends the Chapter on the Three Problems and the Dark and Light Fortnights in the Siddhāntatattvaviveka composed by Sri Kamalākara. || Om.**</insert>

Auspiciously, this is now known. Today, I shall speak of the accurate calculation <term>sphuṭa-gaṇita; the corrected or "true" mathematical position of planets</term> and the seven subtle rationales. Consider all planets to be within the sphere. Among them, the globe of the Earth is similar. These spherical bodies are made of light; the Sun is a planet of fire, and the stars are spheres of light. They are all illuminated by the rays of the Sun <note>original: "sūrya-raśmi-pradīpitāḥ"</note>. This is stated based on our own experience of the sky. 

The planets that have gone above the horizon <term>kṣitija; "earth-born," the horizon</term> are visible, while those below are invisible. In the eighth sphere, the stars are always bright. Because of the delay of the Sun's rays, they are not visible by day to the eyes of all planets. From the surface of the Earth, due to the fault of the Sun’s liquid rays <note>likely referring to the "drowning" out of light by atmospheric scattering or glare</note>, only some planets are seen together with the Thousand-Rayed One <note>original: "sahasra-raśmeḥ"; a title for the Sun</note>. Even in the daytime, some stars are seen by men whose eyes are sharp. This is seen even without the disk of the Moon <term>hima-raśmi; the "cool-rayed" one</term> acting as an obstruction. 

Thus, from the rising of the Sun, the power of its light is high. Even during the day, a person may see the planets if the obstruction of the glare is removed. When the Sun is on the meridian <term>yāmyottara-vṛtta; the north-south circle</term>, the wise say that the shadow and the light vary. By imagining the zenith, one who is under the influence of grief or focus sees the planets as bright. Others describe the appearance and diameter of the planets differently. The shadow of the celestial sphere falls in two ways. Through the rays of the Sun, the defect of glare is destroyed. The Sun's brilliance is such that it is fit to be seen by the eyes of men, yet it is always light-giving. Even when a watery orb or a sphere is in the path, the shadow of the globe moves forward.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary visibility, specifically focusing on Venus (Shukra) and Mercury (Budha) in relation to the Sun (Arka). The author, Kamalākara, explains why these planets do not cause an eclipse-like shadow when they pass the Sun and how their spherical nature and the Sun's rays affect their visibility.</meta>

Indeed, when a planet is in conjunction <note>original: "yutohi"; joined in the same longitudinal position</note>, it is not seen by the eye because of the intervening distance and the intensity of the light. The disk <term>bimba; the visible orb of a planet</term> is not visible because it is overwhelmed by the mass of solar rays. Even if this were not the case, a "transit" would not look like a solar eclipse. One might ask why the observer does not always see it when it is in the seventh house <note>original: "saptamabhavanta"; here referring to an aspect or opposition</note>, or why the Sun is not obscured by the planetary globe's shadow. It is a wonder to men how this sight occurs! 

The reason this cannot be called a "solar eclipse" <term>sūryagrahaṇa</term> is that the disk of Venus is very small. When only a tiny portion of the disk remains outside the Sun's rays, that remaining part is too small to be perceived. Because of the power of the Sun, the conjunction is not seen in the same way an eclipse is. Sometimes, due to the planet's position in the north or south, it is described by the ancient sages <term>munis</term> as being distant from the Sun's path.

**23**

Indeed, a "ring-shaped" <note>original: "valaya"</note> appearance does not occur during a conjunction of Venus and the Sun because their paths do not perfectly align. By the power of mathematical proof <term>siddhi</term>, it is known that they do not merge into one. Having calculated the middle of the Sun’s disk, one can understand that even if the dark disk of Venus were to pass in front, it would not be noticed by people in the world. 

When a planet rises in the night, people see it because its upper half is always illuminated by the Sun’s rays. That planetary globe <term>gola</term> is always situated so that it is "full" of light <note>original: "pūrṇa bimba"; a full orb</note> because the Sun's rays are always striking it. Even when Venus is near the Sun, it remains brilliant, but we cannot see it. Experts should know that the measure of the disk in the night is entirely due to the Sun’s light reflecting off the planet's globe.

**24**

Such is the nature of the distance in minutes <term>kalā; a unit of angular measurement</term> between the Sun and the "Sons of Bhrigu and Mercury" <note>original: "bhṛgu-jña"; referring to Venus and Mercury</note>. Because of the purification of the calculations <note>original: "aśuddha"; referring to the correction of planetary positions</note>, the Sun's light makes the planet appear as if it has disappeared.

**25**

When the Sun and Venus are at the same rising point, the disappearance is explained by their sequence. There is a great distinction here; at night, it is like the Moon <note>original: "śītaruce"; the "cool-rayed" one</note>. As the planet approaches the setting Sun, the intensity of the Sun's rays increases, and it is lost to our sight.

**27**

The disk of the Sun is three-fold in its power. When the planet is in the seventh house or nearby, it is opposed by the Sun's light. Thus, the visual conjunction is not easily seen.

**28**

Because the "degrees of time" <term>kālāṃśa; the specific angular distance from the Sun required for a planet to be visible</term> are not sufficient, Venus and the Sun are not seen in union. Even if the distance exceeds these degrees, the two might not be seen clearly together.

**29**

In that case, why does Venus stay fixed in its nature? Through the power of the water-like reflection of light <note>original: "ambu-saṃya-prabhāvāt"</note>, it reaches its state of visibility.

**30**

In whatever conjunction this occurs, there may be fear or a "piercing" <note>original: "bheda"; an astrological term for a planet passing through a constellation or another body</note> that brings good fortune. Therefore, one should understand the conjunction of the Sun and the "Knower" <note>original: "jña"; Mercury</note> from the highest astronomical principles.

**31**

Thus, by logic, it is shown that the planet is covered by the rays of the Sun.

...Therefore, what is the nature of human experience regarding this? **32** Our sight follows the path of the rays of the Sun's body; it proceeds in that state alone because there is no contradiction in what is perceived. By saying "they are here," we do not mean they are chosen at will. **33** Just as the light emerging from a source is seen by itself, it is also seen by everyone as a universal form. **34** 

Through the sight of solar rays <note>original: "arkaraśmi"</note>, the eye is joined with the ability to see itself; it stands here as a valid means of proof <term>pramāṇa; a standard of logic or measurement</term> through inference. **35** Just as the spheres of the stars and the spheres of the Sun's light are spherical, the light-circle <note>original: "jyotiścakra"</note> of one’s own body would be more than half illuminated. Through the eyes, three parts of the globe's surface are not seen; that sight is specifically restricted by the nature of the great distance. 

Now, concerning what is thought by the wise regarding the sphere through the sight of the eyes: the lines of sight are shown to be equal. The effort of the eye to reach the sphere allows for half of it to be seen. Some say this is due to the use of both eyes. **36** Even in that case, one does not see exactly half of the sphere; even with two eyes, one does not see the sphere's entirety as equal. This is the conclusion held here. **37**

As the sphere <term>gola; a globe or planetary body</term> goes further and further away, more of its portion is seen <note>Kamalākara is discussing the geometry of perspective; as an observer moves back, the tangent points of their vision encompass more of the sphere's hemisphere</note>. **41** Likewise, when it is near, a smaller part is examined. This smallness is what is seen by the eye in the disk <term>bimba; the visible orb of a planet</term>. **42** The sight originating from the center of the eyeball is what allows living beings to grasp an object. This is how the planetary disk is perceived. **43** 

Indeed, the distance between the lines of sight is seen to be equal. As the planetary disk moves further away, **45** the distance between the lines of sight changes; when it is near, it is always greater. By the contraction of one's own eye-sphere, the disk appears small. **45** The disk is seen in the eyeball within the space between the lines of sight. Due to the contraction of these two lines, it appears small or large. **45** 

This variation in smallness is always present in the sky in various ways. Because of the proximity of the sight, one object does not appear as a second. **48** Due to the difference in its rising and its own nature, the light appears as such; in the absence of that knowledge, the reality is explained. **49** On the back side of the planetary sphere, where the rays touch the celestial sphere <term>khagola; the sphere of the sky</term>, the spheres situated within that reach a distance measured in *yojanas* <term>yojana; a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term> and thus the disk is formed. **50** 

Because of the calculations made by the wise for the disk in terms of *yojanas*, it is established that it appears small when near and large when far <note>This refers to the apparent angular diameter vs. true physical size</note>. **51** This reason for the smallness, as published by the previous Teachers <note>original: "ācāryaiḥ"; referring to earlier astronomers like Bhāskara or Āryabhaṭa</note>, is said to be the same here, but it contradicts the logic of the sphere. **52** 

Now, according to the tradition <term>āgama</term> told by all, salutations to the Brahman <note>The Supreme Reality</note> who dwells within. The Earth is situated within the blue sphere <term>nila-gola; the atmosphere or the blue vault of heaven</term>, and the spheres are located there. The zodiacal sphere <term>bhagola</term> is infinitely equal; all of that is always destroyed by the sun's rays, yet it remains as it is. 

The blue color is famous among all people. Where the center of the sun is, there in the middle of the celestial sphere, half is always seen by those on Earth, just as at night the stars and the moon move together in one place. That blue sphere is indeed for the purpose of being seen by living beings. It is known as the best of spheres. In the blue radiance...

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s geometric analysis of astronomical optics from the Tattvaviveka. While the previous page established that we cannot see a full half of a sphere due to the nature of perspective, this page provides the specific mathematical formulas to calculate the "apparent disk" (the size the planet appears to be in the sky) based on its distance from the Earth's center.</meta>

# The Geometry of the Visual Sphere

The union of sight and the object <note>original: "darśanayoga"</note> does not occur simply through a single line, but through the interaction of the line of sight with the **Sphere of the Body** <term>bimbagola; the physical three-dimensional globe of a planet or the sun</term>. When the center of the eye and the center of that sphere are connected by the line of sight, that line marks the path. However, the sight also spreads to the edges, touching the surface of the sphere. This boundary where the sight touches the sphere is what the wise call the "disk" <term>bimba; the two-dimensional circular appearance of a planet</term>. **58**

Because the observer is at a distance, the true center of the sphere is slightly further than the point where the line of sight touches the curved surface. In the calculation of this "apparent disk," we use the distance from the center of the **Sphere of the Body**. The relationship between the visual hypotenuse <term>karṇa; the distance from the eye to the planet</term> and the radius of the planet creates a right-angled triangle. From this, the portion of the sphere that is actually visible is determined. The perceived disk is therefore slightly less than the physical half-sphere. **59**

To calculate the size of this disk in yojanas <note>A historical Indian unit of distance, roughly 5 to 9 miles</note>, one must consider the radius of the orbit. By applying the rules of trigonometry to the distance between the observer and the planet, one determines the portion of the sphere's surface that meets the eye. This calculation allows us to understand the **visible portion of the globe** <note>original: "bimbajagola-khaṇḍa"</note> as it appears to the human eye. 

The difference in appearance arises because the observer is on the surface of the Earth, while calculations are often rooted in the **Center of the Earth** <term>bhūgolakendra; the geocentric center</term>. To make the mathematics of the observation clear and easy to understand <note>original: "gaṇitaṃ suvobhaṃ"</note>, one must account for this shift in perspective.

### Calculating the Apparent Diameter in Minutes of Arc

To find the **True Minutes** <term>spaṣṭakalā; the angular diameter of a planet measured in minutes of arc</term> as seen from the Earth's center:

Multiply the diameter of the body in yojanas by the scale factor, then divide by the corrected distance <term>sphuṭakarṇa; the actual distance from the observer to the planet</term>. The result of this mathematical process yields the arc-length on the celestial sphere, which is the apparent size of the disk. **65**

The logic behind this is as follows: A line of sight is drawn from the center of the Earth <term>kugarbha</term> to touch the surface of the celestial sphere. At the point where this line meets the sphere, a right-angled triangle is formed. **66**

In this triangle:
*   The **Hypotenuse** is the distance from the Earth's center to the planet's center.
*   The **Base** <term>bhuja</term> relates to the radius of the planet.
*   The **Perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term> represents the visual line touching the surface.

Through this geometry, the physical dimensions of the planet in yojanas are converted into the "circular measure" or minutes of arc that we see in the sky. This calculation must always be performed to understand how the planet is perceived by the observer. **67**

This method, established by the universal masters, accounts for the fact that as a planet moves along its orbit, its distance from the Earth changes. Because the **Hypotenuse** <note>distance</note> varies, the apparent size of the disk also varies, even though the physical size of the planet remains constant. One must always use the line of sight that originates from the observer's eye and touches the boundary of the body to determine the true visual appearance. Without this relationship to the orbit <term>kakṣā</term>, the math of the celestial spheres would be incomplete. **71**

If the line of sight were to pass through the very center of the planet's sphere, it would be called the "Central Sight Line." In that case, the hypotenuse and the perpendicular would align differently. I have explained these divisions of the sphere and the resulting visual portions clearly for the benefit of students.

<summary>Kamalākara explains the trigonometry required to calculate a planet's angular diameter (apparent size in the sky) by forming a right-angled triangle between the observer on Earth and the planet's sphere.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s critique of earlier astronomical models. <margin>Tattvaviveka, Chapter 5, Verse 53</margin>

Even though there is a difference in the positions of the **highest and lowest points** <term>nīcocca; the apsides, or the closest and furthest points in an orbit</term>, the formula for the distance remains established as one. From the center, this line of sight is equal; however, below that center, it is singular. By observing the line of sight <note>original: "dr̥ksūtra"</note>, one sees that it is not merely an increase. In this way, the **true minutes of the disk** <term>sphuṭa-bimba-kalā; the apparent size of a planet measured in arc-minutes</term> are determined between the high and low positions. The physical measurement of that disk in **yojanas** <note>a unit of distance</note> is 76 units; this remains constant everywhere. In the visual minutes of arc produced by that planetary sphere, there is a "crawling" or shifting <note>original: "sarpirvaṃ"</note>. Where the minutes of arc are large, it is because of the relationship between the physical yojana-size of the disk and its proximity; where the minutes are small, it is due to the distance. Thus, the physical measurement in yojanas always remains the same.

<margin>53</margin>

<insert>Because of the smallness of the body, as understood by me, the methods taught by **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskara II, a 12th-century mathematician</note> and others are inconsistent with the truth and contain internal contradictions. Through the vision of one's own degrees <note>calculation based on observation</note>, one can imagine the measurements of the disks of Mars and the other planets. By this same logic, one should understand the physical yojana-height and the resulting arc-minutes. The disk-measurements of the Sun and Moon, which differ from these, must be calculated with great effort here.</insert>

When the arc is determined through the calculation of the sine of the latitude <note>original: "diggata nata"</note>, how can the distance between the lines of sight be calculated? From the distance between the two lines of sight, that arc is then derived. **81** By establishing the movement of the Sun in yojanas, the standard measurement of the arc-minutes is found through the method of the distance between the lines of sight—as seen in the work *Sārvabhauma*. **82** 

It is said that even in the case of the Sun, there is no difference in the physical size regardless of the distance <note>original: "śravaṇādi"</note>. The minutes of the disk are fixed to the physical body and do not change their nature; rather, they appear different due to the lack of a proper standard of measurement. Therefore, by the intellect of the observer, the image of the disk is divided into two parts of the sine; thus, the sum of the measurements is made. **85** 

The measurement of the minutes of the disk is always of a single form in yojanas. However, because of the varying power of the visual minutes of arc, this world has been deceived. The arc-minutes of the high and low points of the planets like Mercury and Venus were stated by **Bhāskara** and others, and similarly for the Sun and Moon. However, those measurements for the retrograde planets <term>vakra; planets appearing to move backward</term> are only the "apparent" arc-minutes of the disk. Where a calculation is brought forth without sound logic, it is not accepted as true by the wise. **88**

By the power of the **geocentric line of sight** <term>dr̥ggarbha-dr̥ksūtra; the line from the center of the Earth to the planet</term>, the distance to the perigee <note>original: "nīca-karṇa"</note> is multiplied by the physical disk of the planet. When divided by the circumference of the disk, the result is the distance in yojanas. Through the logic of the line of sight, the visual images of the dark and light portions are calculated by the wise. If there is a contradiction in the observation of retrograde motion, it is explained thus: **90** According to the lines of sight from the center of the Earth, there is only one distance in your sphere. The difference in that distance is only a perception caused by the observer's position on the surface of the Earth. **91** 

Therefore, because of the unclear nature of the observer's position, the disk on the visual circumference is not truly perceived as it is. Even though they speak of the direction of the retrograde motion from the place of sight, **92** I shall now state the calculation of those positions based on what has been perceived through experience, even when the visible disk appears different.

Having corrected that, the work of determining the true visual disk is performed. From the center of the **celestial sphere** <term>bhagola; the perceived sphere of the stars</term>, using the true diameter in **yojanas** <term>yojana; a traditional unit of distance, roughly 5-9 miles</term>, one should determine the visual size. Because of the cone-like shape of the lines of sight <note>original: "koṇyākṛteḥ"</note>, the root of the difference between the two distances becomes the visual path for the observer. The disk is calculated like a broken circle; its half-diameter is divided to find the visual minutes of arc <term>kalikā; a minute of arc, 1/60th of a degree</term> based on the observer's specific position and the time of observation [16]. Now, if the planet is situated within a **sphere** <term>golaka</term>, it appears complete only according to its true measurement; however, when calculated using the sine of the semi-diameter <note>original: "jīvādala"</note>, it is situated as such [17]. Similarly, one should imagine the center of the observed disk to be situated where the line of sight from the observer meets the center of the planetary sphere [18]. In the same way, where the tangent line of sight touches the edge of the sphere, the intelligent scholars should understand that the visual center is derived from the middle [19].

The measurement of the planet in yojanas and the true distance of the hypotenuse are used here to provide the highly accurate calculation of the disk in minutes [20]. In this way, due to the difference in mean rising and the difference in the planet's position within the sphere, the experts in altitude and observation <note>original: "antotratajñair"</note> have determined the visual disk to be free from the errors of the "middle-position" calculation [21]. 

Just as the power of the **dark moon** <note>original: "kṛṣṇa-śaśa"</note> increases as it moves upward, so too does the disk appear larger to the sight of men as it approaches the zenith [22]. The visual line from the observer's surface to the center is known as the sight-line; from the difference between the observer's own sight and the center-point, the truth of the calculation is established [23]. Because these methods were accepted in this manner, the results obtained from the union of the visual rays are indeed accurate [24]. This mathematical circle is established for the purpose of finding the true distance through calculation; from Mars and the other planets, the visual disks are derived in sequence [25].

This is the result of the time-differential at midday; the measurements are suitable for the observed positions. Through this power, existence and non-existence are understood even in the midst of planetary observation. To those who follow the logic of proportions here, I offer my salutations [29]. <meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s complex geometrical analysis of planetary disks from the Siddhānta-tattva-viveka. Having established the flaws in previous astronomical models, he now focuses on the "mānaikya-khaṇḍa" (the sum of the semi-diameters of two celestial bodies), which is crucial for predicting the exact moment of contact during an eclipse.</meta>

They say that when the disk of a planet is held within the celestial sphere, its center and heart are fixed in the sky [32]. Where the Sun rises, great and glorious, its rays are not merely scattered; the logic of determining its position through calculation is a skill that only the noble-minded can truly grasp [33]. Then, by what dark or light measurement is the visual disk of the Moon <term>yamāli-vimbam; the "twin" or "shadow" disk, often referring to the Moon in eclipse contexts</term> understood? The method for determining its visibility for men on Earth involves a subtle process that remains hidden from those who do not understand the division of the sum of the radii [34]. In the various sections of the **sum of the semi-diameters** <term>mānaikya-khaṇḍa; the combined measure of the radii of the Sun and Moon used to determine the start of an eclipse</term>, the status of the planets and the distance between their centers is not properly explained by traditional methods; therefore, I speak here of a more refined approach [35].

Now, regarding the difference in the sphere known as the **half-eaten sphere** <term>arddhabhukta-gola; a geometric construction used to calculate the partially eclipsed portion of a disk</term>, one must consider the sphere of the Moon. In its own sphere, the disk of the planet is always the true measurement; how then can the logic of their conjunction be established? [36] Therefore, a vision is imagined using two lines of sight; in the space between those two threads <term>sūtra; literally "thread," used here to mean a geometric line of sight</term>, the disk of the Sun is situated. By its extension, the Moon's disk in its own sphere is also defined [37]. If the conjunction is proven in the celestial sphere by the distance between those two lines, then the distance in **yojanas** <term>yojana; a traditional unit of distance</term> of the Sun is not necessarily equal to that of the Moon [38].

When the line of sight is directed toward the heart of the celestial sphere, it does not easily find the minutes of arc <term>kalikā; a minute of arc, 1/60th of a degree</term> of the Moon. At the time of first contact <term>sparśa-kāla; the beginning of an eclipse</term>, or at the time of separation, the line of sight of the observer does not fall in a simple straight line [39]. The center of the Sun's disk is fixed in the celestial sphere; however, the center of the Moon's disk may be in a different direction because of the nature of its own orbit [40]. The distance between these two centers is like a chord <term>jyā; a trigonometric sine or chord</term> in the celestial sphere. Only when the two lines of sight coincide does the conjunction of the edges of the disks occur [41]. When the lines of sight to the two disks are different, the minutes of arc of the Moon in its sphere must be adjusted; where the two spheres meet, that is the true conjunction [42]. This does not happen by chance, but from the union of the two lines of sight in a single sphere. From this refined understanding, one calculates the distance from the center to the edge of the disk [43]. Thus, in the section concerning the eclipsed portion, the difference between the two separate lines of sight is established; this is how the **half-illumination** <term>arddha-tāpa; the partial lighting or penumbra</term> of the Sun and Moon is determined at the moment of contact.

> From a single line of sight for all observers on Earth, when the disks are perfectly halved, then the distance between the centers of the disks is equal to the sum of their semi-diameters. According to the stated method, when this is not the case for the Sun and Moon, the line arising from the difference between the centers is not the same as the sum of the radii. Even if the distance between the centers is equal to the **sum of the semi-diameters** <term>mānaikya-khaṇḍa</term>, the intersection of the edges <term>nemi; the rim or edge of the planetary disk</term> does not occur simply by that calculation. A true union of the disks is only perceived when the edges meet at the specific minutes of arc where their visual paths intersect. Even if the centers appear close, the visual conjunction depends on the edges [2].

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical exploration of celestial diameters from the Siddhānta-tattva-viveka. Having previously introduced the visual geometry of planetary conjunctions, he now provides specific formulas for calculating the apparent size of the lunar and solar disks based on their distance from the observer's eye versus the center of the Earth.</meta>

In one's own line of sight <note>original: "svadṛggatau"</note>, when considering the visual ray <term>dṛk-sūtra; the line of sight from the observer's eye to the celestial body</term>, what indeed is the union? That which is known by the experts is the combination of factors. When the semi-diameter of the disk <note>original: "vimbadala," the radius of the planetary or solar disk</note> is taken as a single unit, and the distance to the center is considered, the visual diameter is revealed. Through the distance of the Moon <term>chandra-karṇa; the hypotenuse or distance from the observer to the Moon</term> and the distance to the stars, the sum of the radii is calculated by the wise using the centers of the celestial bodies [37-38].

<margin>S. Vi. Ka. 55</margin>

In the circle of yojanas <term>yojana-maṇḍala; a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term>, the planetary orbits are established. By dividing the product of the center and the radius by the Moon's distance, the visual disk at the center is determined. If one-and-a-half times that measure is subtracted from the disk, the remainder is increased. This relates to the visual ray of the stars and the very soul of the glorious Sun <note>original: "śrī ravi"</note> [39-40].

When there is no difference in the motion of the two bodies, the sum of their semi-diameters <term>mānaikya-khaṇḍa; the combined radii of the Sun and Moon, used to calculate the start and end of an eclipse</term> is calculated from the difference of their centers. For the sake of enlightening students on the proofs of these calculations, the wise astronomer should demonstrate this using the globe <term>gola; an armillary sphere or celestial globe</term> [41].

When positioned within the womb of the Earth <note>original: "ku-garbha," the center of the Earth</note>, the appearance of the partial eclipse <term>mānaika-khaṇḍa-darśane</term> is seen through the power of the visual rays. The centers of the two spherical disks are connected by the visual sight-lines [42].

<margin>55</margin>

In the space between the difference of those two lines, the brilliance of the light increases. That visual circle is known as the "sight-instrument." Through its observation, the true possibility of the disk’s size is realized. Even a full eclipse of the Sun occurs when the Moon's visual ray is perfectly aligned [43-44]. If the visual ray is within the measure of the parts, then the circle of sight is hindered by the larger size of the intervening body. This procedure for the planets is established as a wondrous method.

When the distance from the observer's eye <term>dṛk-sūtra</term> is greater than the distance from the Earth's center, the visual circle of the Moon and other planets appears different. There, the line of the orbit <term>valaya</term> is calculated. Without doubt, the disks of the Sun and Moon are analyzed by the intelligent in this manner. Now, the position of the Sun is established [45-47].

Specifically, when the Moon is in its position, its disk is calculated through the visual ray originating from the surface of the Earth <note>original: "pṛṣṭha-dṛk," topocentric observation</note> versus the center of the Earth <note>original: "garbha-dṛk," geocentric observation</note>. This creates a wonderful variety in how the disk is perceived. From the surface of the Earth, the rays of the Sun appear sharp and intense in the middle, yet their true power is clearly defined through this mathematical correction [48-50].

Thus, the visual disk of the light-filled bodies is seen through the difference of the centers and the observer's eye. The wise must recognize the third variation in this calculation. For the Sun, the rules are fixed. Now, regarding the diameter of the stars and planets as seen in the celestial sphere: the diameter in yojanas is multiplied by the radius and divided by the distance <term>karṇa</term> to find the true visual disk. If the distance is discarded, the calculation must follow the visual ray. This is the consensus [51-54].

If the Moon's disk is measured as a half-part, it is multiplied by the qualities and divided by the yojanas to find the disk's center. This is done for the globe. Even if a false assumption of equality is made, in the sky, the distance in yojanas remains the same even at the lowest point. If the distance is known accurately, the disk is true. The difference in the disks between the centers and the surface is a result of the varying angles of sight; to call it "untrue" is merely a matter of terminology in the conjunction of two planets [55-57].

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s technical exposition. Having just finished the calculations for the combined radii of celestial bodies, he concludes the "Chapter on Disks" and transitions into the mechanics of solar ray obstruction and the geometry of shadows (the shadow cone).</meta>

Just as a man clearly perceives a house within the blue expanse <note>original: "nīlodare," literally 'in the blue belly,' referring to the sky</note>, so too when the observer’s eye reaches a state of alignment, he sees the celestial body with his own eyes. Through this vision, the apparent decrease in size over a single day is explained through logic. One should not look at the Sun when it is yellow <note>referring to looking at the sun when it is low on the horizon or during specific atmospheric conditions</note>, for as the wind and the sight interact there, a different rule applies. Just as there is a difference in the directions of those who have traveled, the observer truly sees two planets <note>a reference to parallax or double-vision effects caused by atmospheric refraction</note> when the visual rays in the sky are aligned. Because the planet is visible to the human eye, if there is a gap in the **visual ray** <term>dṛk-sūtra; the straight line connecting the eye to the object</term>, the planet is perceived by the observer. In this way, men see objects differently due to the variation in the lines of sight.

->**Thus ends the Chapter on Disks in the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka* composed by the illustrious Kamalākara.**<-

By whatever means the rays are obstructed, the Sun’s rays are blocked for mortals. This inevitably occurs if the line between the eye and the Sun is not shorter than the visual path. Therefore, the experts have established that the Sun becomes invisible to the eye because of the obstruction of its own rays. Due to the obstruction of those rays, a cone <term>koṇa; here referring to the shadow cone or umbra</term> is formed. Its width and length are determined by the size of the globe. Just as the length of the shadow of the terrestrial globe is calculated by astronomers, so too should one calculate the length of the shadow globe of a planet. 

The distance of the planet is measured in **yojanas** <term>yojana; a traditional unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term>. When the line between the planet and the Sun is intersected, the length is determined by the difference between the two orbital diameters. This length is well-known to exist from the center of the globe to the end of the visual range. One must calculate the **cosine** <term>koṭi-jīvā; the 'clutch' or 'end' sine, used in trigonometry</term> of the difference between the Sun and the planet. By multiplying and dividing these factors, the result is the sine of the arc. 

Through these mathematical steps, the wise determine the true positions of the planets and the Sun. If the difference in degrees is known, one can find the distance between them. Whether the signs are even or odd, the calculation of the **true distance** <term>sphuṭa-antara; the corrected or 'clear' distance between two points</term> is made. The sine of the clear difference between the Sun and the planet is multiplied by the planet's distance <term>karṇa; the hypotenuse</term> and divided by the radius. This yields the arc of the planet. Thus, the hypotenuse is explained as being the "clear consecration" <note>original: "sphuṭābhiṣeka," likely a poetic or ritualistic term for a perfected calculation</note> of the two bodies. The square root of the sum of the squares of these differences provides the final distance between the two globes.

I, the intelligent one, have thus described the movements within the circle of the stars, which are calculated with total accuracy through the use of corrected sines.

<margin>T. Vi. Ka. 56</margin>Regarding the calculation of a planet and the Sun <note>original: "kheṭārkayoḥ"</note>, I shall now explain the correct method for the formula of the line between the centers of their disks <term>bimbāntara-sūtra; the straight line connecting the center of the Sun to the center of a planet</term>, whether the difference in their directions is established or not. If the north-south and east-west coordinates are not used, then at the desired time, one should multiply the base and perpendicular by the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa; the distance from the observer or center to the body</term> and divide by the **radius** <note>original: "triguṇa," literally 'three-fold,' representing the sine of 90 degrees</note>. The results are the true yojanas <note>a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-13km</note> of the base and perpendicular in their respective directions. Through the sum of the squares of the difference between these two, the true distance is found. The **gnomon** <term>śaṅku; a vertical pillar used to measure shadows</term> is calculated by some according to the opinion of Maya <note>the legendary author of the Sūrya Siddhānta</note> using the square root of the sum of the squares of the base and perpendicular of the Sun and Moon.

<margin>T. Vi. Ka. 56</margin> Multiply by the hypotenuse and divide by the radius to find the distance in yojanas. From the sum of the squares of the vertical and horizontal differences of these two, the square root yields the **hypotenuse-line between the centers of the Sun and the planet** <note>original: "karṇā-rattarka-bimbāntara-sūtra"</note>. It is because of a lack of knowledge regarding this that those who know the celestial spheres are confused by their own different theories. The line connecting the center of the Earth to the centers of the spheres of the two celestial bodies is always the same; it is not beneficial to treat it as a different hypotenuse. Regarding the formula for the line between the centers of the disks, as described in the **Bhāskara-Siddhānta** <note>referring to Bhāskara II's Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>, as the Sun’s disk becomes larger, more should be added to the calculation.

According to the mean hypotenuse of the Sun, the **length of the Earth's shadow** <term>bhū-chāyā-dairghya; the extent of the umbra</term> is 223;35. At the maximum distance (apogee), it is 2238;0;10;11. At the minimum distance (perigee), it is 215;45. Using these values based on the Sun's position, the lengths of the shadows at the center of the disks are determined: 
*   **Mercury:** 50525 (?) or 218;23;9 
*   **Venus:** 2195;12 
When the Moon is at its perigee or apogee, the length of the shadow is 480;74. When the Sun and Moon meet at the center of the celestial sphere, the rays of the Sun are obscured by the Moon. If the calculation of the shadow is greater than the distance, the eclipse is certain; otherwise, it is different. This should be carefully considered by the wise through the difference in the shadows. Since this was spoken by the sages, it is accepted here. Previously, others have stated this without accounting for the natural variation in distance, making no distinction between the large and the small. To make the unmanifest manifest, this layout is provided.

<insert>Multiply by the Sun's rays and divide by the true gnomon to find the **shadow** <term>prabhā</term>. As long as the Moon's portion is not reached, the body remains invisible. This method for calculating the visible shadow is stated in the **Śiromaṇi** <note>Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>.</insert>

The true gnomon and the Earth-center gnomon are calculated in order. By adding the minutes of the Earth's radius and dividing by the Sun's rays, the shadow to be seen is revealed. Then, the shadow's form is produced. Multiply by the upper edge of the Earth's disk and divided by the Earth's shadow portion, adding the Earth's shadow-visibility. From the sum of the squares of the Earth's shadow and the Sun's position, the square root is taken. This extremely subtle calculation for the desired man <note>the observer</note> from the center of the Earth has been described. This is the logic of the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka* by the son of Ranganātha <note>Kamalākara</note>.
