Having established the distance between planetary centers, Kamalākara now addresses the specific geometry of the solar gnomon—the vertical rod used for measuring shadows—and accounts for the difference between calculations based on the Earth's center (geocentric) and the observer's position on the Earth's surface (topocentric).</meta>

### On the Determination of the True Solar Gnomon

Following the methods established by the ancient masters, I shall now explain the proof for the measurement of the true **solar gnomon** <term>arka-śaṅku; a vertical pillar used to determine the Sun's altitude by its shadow</term> and its elevation. First, following the traditional rules, one should satisfy the requirements of the **vertical circle** <term>dṛṅ-maṇḍala; the great circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term>.

By using the cord of one's own position on the Earth's surface, one determines the **geocentric gnomon** <term>garbha-śaṅku; the gnomon as it would appear from the center of the Earth</term> and the **surface gnomon** <term>pṛṣṭha-śaṅku; the gnomon as observed from the Earth's surface</term>. Through the horizontal base and the Sun's position, the height of the gnomon is established in cubits <note>original: "haste," a unit of measure roughly 18 inches</note>. 

To calculate the Sun’s position correctly, one must understand the relationship between the observed solar distance and the true center. When the solar distance is decreased by the parallax, the resulting gnomon and its shadow are found. The shadow cast by the Sun from its specific height—multiplied by the square of the solar distance and adjusted—reveals the true nature of the shadow. This can be understood by the difference in the Sun's rays and the specific position of the observer.

For the sake of clarifying these complex rows of calculations, I provide this method of transformation:
In the middle of the calculation, one should draw a **horizontal line** <term>tiryak-sūtra</term> and a deep **earth-center line** <term>ku-ja-sūtra</term>. At the point where these meet the center of the circle, the surface-based observer is situated. From this position, one should know the horizontal line and the geocentric line through their own cords. 

<margin>T. Vi. Ka. 57</margin>
The cord known as the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> extends from the Sun to the observer's position. By applying the rule of the "Crown Jewel of Universal Principles" <note>original: "Sārvabhauma-śiromaṇi," possibly a reference to a specific astronomical authority or the author's own synthesis of knowledge</note>, the hypotenuse and the base form the **zenith distance** <term>dṛg-jyā</term>, and the perpendicular is the true gnomon. The observed shadow is of the same category as the distance from which it is cast.

Thus, through the intersection of these cords, a third value is generated. By using the rule of proportions, the geocentric calculation is refined. When this is understood, the knowledge of the distance to the Earth's center becomes clear. If one were to ignore this distinction, errors would arise due to the differing perspectives of the surface and the center. Therefore, one should apply the proportion correctly to find the desired result.

In the opinion of the wise, when the surface shadow is calculated, it must be squared and multiplied by the directions. When the result is diminished by the square of the Earth's radius, and the square root is taken, one finds the corrected value. If the distance of the Sun's hypotenuse is equal to the distance of the shadow, then the Sun itself is at the corner. The surface gnomon and the true gnomon then appear clearly as one. 

<note>Kamalākara is discussing the "parallax of the gnomon," a sophisticated concept where the observer's height and the Earth's curvature change the perceived length of a shadow compared to a theoretical calculation from the Earth's core.</note>

When the Sun is lower than this point, the surface gnomon's height and the clear hypotenuse-cord of the Sun are considered in the same plane. At the base of the instrument, where the gnomon is fixed, the shadow and the observation line must be aligned. By multiplying these values and considering the surface-to-center ratio, the true state of the shadow is determined. Upon careful consideration, it is certain that because the observer is on the level ground of the Earth, the different measures of the gnomon arise from the difference in perspective <note>original: "māna-bhedāt," difference in measurement/scale</note>.

<summary>Kamalākara provides a geometric and trigonometric proof for calculating the Sun's gnomon, distinguishing between geocentric (center of Earth) and topocentric (surface) measurements to account for parallax. He explains how to draw and calculate the various cords and lines of sight necessary for precise astronomical observations.</summary>

### On the Varieties of Shadows and the Critique of the Sārvabhauma

The distinctions in the types of shadows are observed through their specific categories. This is not merely a matter of your praise <note>The author likely addresses a hypothetical opponent or the reader</note>, for even in the division of the *Vichāra-viṃśa* <note>A specific section or method in Muniśvara's work</note>, the difference in human perception is not the only factor. The existence of these variations, as previously stated, arises from the different methods of calculation. Because the variations in these categories are so subtle, the shadow of the gnomon and its hypotenuse <term>dṛk-śukṛhantyayoḥ; the relationship between the vertical gnomon and the diagonal line to the Sun</term> are well-known to be distinct, yet they are often not recognized as such.

It is not through any other partial numerical calculation that the union or separation of these two factors occurs; rather, it arises from the application of logic. This is in accordance with the established conclusions <term>siddhānta</term> regarding the origins of human observation. Therefore, as stated by the author of the **Sārvabhauma** <note>Muniśvara, Kamalākara's primary scientific rival</note>, and following the independent reasoning regarding the Sun's position, no difference in the shadow is perceived even when the varieties of calculations are applied.

However, according to the teachings of **Bhāskara** <note>Bhāskara II, the 12th-century authority</note>, the true shadow is always properly joined with the clear movement of the Sun. This calculation of the Sun’s position, though handled by the Sārvabhauma with great care in his deliberations, actually conflicts with the geographical reality of different regions. From a standpoint of truth and affection for the science, this must be examined. The explanation provided there regarding the specific region of the solar disk <term>vivapra-deśa; the area of the Sun's body contributing to the shadow</term> is clearly expressed here: when the Sun is near the zenith, the shadow is established for the purpose of precision.

One must understand that the "place of perception" <term>vibodha-deśa; the specific point on Earth or the Sun's disk used for measurement</term> is not merely an abstract concept. In his <note>Muniśvara's</note> view, the region of the solar disk is considered distinct from the center of the sphere. If that were the case, the shadow would be easily established. Since the Sun's position is calculated from the center of the celestial sphere, that point serves as the basis for the **geocentric vertical circle** <term>garbha-dṛk-vṛtta</term>. In this calculation, the **hypotenuse** is the **square root of the sum of the squares** <term>varga-yuti-mūla; the result of the Pythagorean theorem applied to the gnomon and shadow</term> of the gnomon and its base.

One must distinguish between this geocentric vertical circle and the position of the solar disk. In the view of the surface-based calculation, the shadow does not vanish. This is because the observer is situated on the **Earth's surface** <term>bhū-pṛṣṭhage</term>. Those who are intent on understanding the subtle truth must contemplate this: because of the union of the rays from the upper region of the Sun, the calculation of the shadow involves the rays that move outward from that center.

When the place of perception is in the middle, then in that view, there is no shadow at noon. This occurs specifically at the moment of the Sun’s meridian passage. In the calculation of the Sun's position, at certain times, when the Sun is at the zenith, the "place of perception" is exactly in the middle of the midday sky. When there is no zenith distance <term>natā-bhāve</term> at midday, the shadow is said to be non-existent. However, since the Sun has a physical diameter, a shadow can still be produced from either side. Even if you claim the shadow does not exist when the Sun is at the center, we must ask: from which direction would the shadow arise? If it arises from all sides of the solar disk equally, then they would cancel each other out by mutual opposition. Therefore, the wise must accept that the "shadow" is the collective effect of these rays. Because the Sun is a physical sphere, the observer sees the center of that sphere as the point of origin for the calculation.

<summary>Kamalākara critiques Muniśvara's (Sārvabhauma) method of shadow calculation, arguing that the physical size of the solar disk must be accounted for when the Sun is at the zenith, distinguishing between geocentric and topocentric perspectives.</summary>

### The Equality of the Celestial Spheres and the Conclusion of the Shadow Analysis

From the central hypotenuse <term>madhya-karṇa; the distance from the center of the Earth to the planet</term>, it is stated that in all eight celestial shells <note>original: "sarva-golāṣṭeṣv"; referring to the nested spheres of the planets in traditional Indian cosmology</note>, these measurements remain equal. When one desires to know the position of the Sun on the horizon <term>div-nemi; the "rim of the sky" or horizon</term>, and during the time of observation from the terrestrial pole <term>meru-netra; literally "the eye at Mount Meru," the North Pole</term>, the state of the shadow is determined. Because of the inherent power of the center, the shadow falls in a specific direction and not elsewhere, due to the impossibility of it falling in any other country or region.

Thus, at any single point on the "head" <note>likely referring to the zenith</note>, even when the circle of the directions is covered by clouds, the shadow of the gnomon <term>śaṅkari-bhā; the shadow cast by the vertical pillar</term> would still exist because of the subtle rays. Why is this? Because in truth, it remains distinct. If the light were not to emerge even from the position of the horizon, then we say that for a village, there would be a shadow, yet it would not appear to be illuminated. Therefore, following the path of the celestial center, it is only through the causal factors that the shadow is produced.

Whether the assembly of astronomers <term>golajña-vargāḥ; those who know the "Gola" or sphere</term> are satisfied in their assembly or not, this is established through the principles of spherical geometry. Any contradiction regarding the half-spheres is merely an appearance; my own explanation of the causes and arrangements is correct.

<insert>Thus ends the Chapter on Shadows in the **Siddhānta-viveka** composed by the illustrious **Kamalākara**.</insert>

### On the Intersection of Solar Rays and the Celestial Sphere

With respect, the spheres are always observed. When the solar rays collide with the blue sphere <note>original: "nīla-gola"; likely referring to the atmospheric shell or the dark sky</note>, or perhaps when they move beyond it, an increase in altitude occurs. Through the union of those rays, the division between the illuminated and dark halves <term>site-asite; the "white" and "black" portions of a celestial body</term> is always maintained as equal. Whether in the bright fortnight or the dark fortnight, the two portions of the globe remain constant.

When there is a union of rays in the dark portion, the appearance changes. How can one see the "horn" <term>śṛṅga; the cusp of the crescent moon</term>? That which is shaped like a grain of barley <note>original: "śṛṅga-yavākāram"</note> appears because of the distance between the solar and lunar disks. In the chapter regarding the centers of these disks, the knowledge of the surfaces is established. For men on Earth, the distance between the disks is the hypotenuse that rests upon the rays of the Sun.

By using the square of this hypotenuse, one calculates the "horn" or the illuminated cusp. This is not a delusion of the eyes. In the section concerning the Earth's shadow, the sight of the observer is directed toward the globe. At the end of the dark phase <note>New Moon</note>, the disk becomes like a shell <note>original: "śukti"; a mother-of-pearl or conch shell</note>. As the distance from the Sun increases, the illumination grows. This has been described as the "elevation of the horns" <term>śṛṅgona-vaya; the calculation of the moon's cusps</term>. This is not a new theory, but it follows the differences in the orbits of the Sun and Moon. Knowing the six, eight, or nine-fold divisions, the brightness of the lunar half is perceived by men on Earth.

### On the Calculation of the Planetary Orbs and the Critique of Earlier Traditions

The verses beginning with the teachers' traditions <note>original: "ācāryeśa"; referring to the lineage of great astronomers</note> describe the celestial bodies as being devoid of certain qualities. These verses explain the conjunctions <term>yoga; the alignment of two celestial bodies</term> and the portions of separation <term>viyogābhāgāḥ; the angular distance between bodies</term> involving the Sun and other planets. In the tradition of the bright-rayed ones <note>original: "sitādyu"; likely referring to the moon or bright planets</note>, the divisions are made. The calculations for these portions are determined as five and three-quarters <note>original: "pādona-ṣaṭ"; 6 minus 1/4, or 5.75</note> or eight, which represent the measurements for their enjoyment of space. This great value is always derived from the power of the Sun's position, calculated by the mouths of the experts using the mean motion.

<margin>
Siddhānta-tattva-viveka
Kamalākara, page 58
</margin>

Regarding the womb of the calculation <note>original: "garbhī"; likely referring to the geocentric center or the inner workings of the sphere</note>, the pervasiveness of the visual field is explained. Some say that there are two types of these, but that is not the case; they are as they were previously established by the ancient methods. Even when the contact <term>sparśa; the first contact in an eclipse</term> is calculated here, no loss is seen in the mathematical consistency. In the calculation of the northern solar orbit <note>original: "garbha-sūtro-tare"; the northern line from the center</term>, the minutes of the orb <term>bimba-liptāḥ; the measurement of the planetary disk in arcminutes</term> remain equal if they are based on the power of the solar orbit <term>arka-kakṣā; the specific path or distance of the sun</term>. Among these, the largest and the middle ones are considered.

Whatever the internal minutes of arc <term>kalā; 1/60th of a degree</term> are, they depend on their respective celestial spheres <term>svasva-golā-śritāḥ</term>. Even so, because they do not rely on a different foundation, they are equal in both cases. This very concept should be contemplated regarding the Sun’s orb <term>arka-bimba</term> in this single chapter. In the sequence of other spheres, the threads of the Sun's orb do not lead to a different understanding. One's own distinct minutes of arc are truly perceived there. When the two are in contact, the measurement of the distance is slightly different because of the difference in the measurement of the miles <term>yojana; a traditional unit of distance</term> in the middle of the sky.

There is a difference in the number and a difference in the measurement of the minutes of arc. Before that is completed, having reached the path of their respective spheres, the measurements are not identical because of the difference in the polar longitudes. How could the shadow of that sphere be perceived? This should be examined by those with refined intellects <note>original: "dhi-dhaiḥ"</note> through the measurement of the upright side of the triangle <term>koṭi; the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term> in the calculations.

How could there be a measurement of the hypotenuse <term>karṇa</term> of the observed body? Or how could the thread between the Moon and the Sun be established through the difference of different types of conjunctions? This is surely the truth heard from tradition.

<insert>When, from the two circles like those of Venus <note>original: "bhṛgu"</note>, through the middle path, the observation of the two is equal in their inclination, then the Sun’s position would be five and three-quarters or eight parts in the *Shiromani* <note>original: "śiromaṇau"; referring to Bhāskara II's treatise, the *Siddhānta-śiromaṇi*</note>.</insert>

In that context, the difference in days is not accepted by those who know. Due to the absence of the "treasure" <note>likely a specific technical term or value</note>, the state of the hypotenuse of the observed body is just as it was stated there. <insert>Five and three-quarters and eight</insert> are the values for the sphere of the Moon. In that case, the work of the solar thread should be done by the difference of the centers of the two orbs. The "arm" of the triangle <term>bāhu; the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term> produced from that should be understood as the segment of the sum of the measurements of the Sun and Moon <term>sūryendu-māna-yuti-khaṇḍa</term>. This is the flaw <note>original: "doṣa"</note> in the earlier logic. Thus, the orb of the Sun is illuminated by the best of the wise. The arrangement of those spheres is produced from the measurement of the minutes of arc. That is well-known among the followers of this tradition. The minutes of the Sun's orb are known to the wise as being within the sphere of the Sun's rays. That which was not stated here regarding the distance between the Sun and Moon, even by the son of the Sun <note>original: "bhāskara-putra"; possibly a reference to a follower of Bhāskara</note>, what was specifically said there should be seen in the light of the equinoxes.

### On the Illumination of the Moon and the Geometric Chords

Is it not true that for the learned scholars, according to the principles established in the chapter on light <note>original: "prabhādhikāra"; the section of astronomical texts dealing with illumination and shadows</note>, much has been said? At that time, in the context of the Sun's disk, one should perform the calculation. On the path of the northern chord of the orbs <note>original: "vimbottarā-sūtra"; a mathematical line or chord connecting the upper parts of the celestial disks</note>, the distance between the Moon and the Sun is marked.

By taking that distance and multiplying it by the diameter of the orb, and then dividing it by the line connecting the centers of the Moon and Sun, the result—when viewed through the arc—becomes the illuminated portion <term>sita; the phase or the bright part of the moon's disk</term>. In this way, when the positions of the Sun and Moon are corrected <note>original: "sphuṭa"; refers to the true, calculated position as opposed to the mean position</note>, the difference between them is found. This is established for the first and second planets <note>the Sun and Moon</note>, and likewise for the third. When the two forms are separated into their arc-components, they should be grasped clearly.

<margin>
The white portion (phases) is calculated via the chords.
</margin>

Where the illuminated portion is determined by the rule of the chord, the calculation should be made with the fixed measure of the diameter. When the difference in the orb's appearance occurs, then the portion of the arc is calculated by the subtraction of the diameter. The degrees of the arc are joined together; these arc-degrees are well-known through their conjunctions. Thus, when the lunar orb is situated in its latitude <term>śara; literally 'arrow,' referring to celestial latitude or the deflection from the ecliptic</term>, the white portion is understood by those who know the sphere <term>golavidaḥ; experts in spherical astronomy</term>. This is not calculated merely by the difference between the Sun and the meridian, but through the field known as the "Area of Illumination" <note>original: "sitākhya-kṣetra"</note> in the equatorial sphere.

In the lunar sphere, from the vertical chord, the distance by which the half-illuminated part of the Sun is separated is determined. The distance-chord of the lunar orb is multiplied by the five-hundred measures <note>original: "veda-pramita"; 'Veda' often stands for the number 4, but contextually here relates to a specific large-scale calculation</note> of the light-rays, and divided by the Sun's radius <term>karṇa; the hypotenuse or distance from the observer to the planet</term> to give the result. If the Moon is located there, then by the wisdom of the scholars, in that field of the central chord, it is fixed.

Then, that which is half of the orb's diameter should not be taken as a hindrance in any way. By the degrees of separation imagined from the Sun, the illuminated part is brought to life. Thus, from the region of the Sun's rays, the "arm" <term>bhuja; the side of a right-angled triangle, often the sine</term> is calculated for the illuminated portion. This is appropriate because the minute difference of the Earth's center <term>kugarbha; the geocentric center of the Earth</term> exists within the visual sphere.

Furthermore, when the Moon has reached the calculated position relative to the Earth's center, it touches the sphere of the Sun. For the person who is observing, the difference in minutes between the Sun and Moon must be merged into a single unity through the instrument of the Sun-chord <note>original: "ravi-sūtra-yantra"</note>. There, the calculated distance of the Sun will be revealed.

In that sphere of the lunar orb, from the center, the lunar path is observed. Therefore, in the purified sphere, the corrected chord of the centers of the Sun's disk and the Moon's disk is measured by the circumference of the lunar sphere. This is clearly the corrected chord from the center of the geocentric lunar sphere. In the text, the distance between the Sun-Moon chords is marked by a line on the chord of illumination. That same distance in the sphere of the orbs belongs to those two. The "arm" <term>bhuja</term> is the form of the distance in **Yojanas** <note>a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-15 kilometers</note>. The chord of the distance between the Sun and Moon is the hypotenuse <term>karṇa</term>, and the lunar chord is the "upright" <term>koṭi; the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term>. By the union of the upright and the hypotenuse, the distance in Yojanas from the center of the lunar orb is obtained. This is the circumference. The calculated three fingers <term>aṅgula; a unit of measure, approx. 1.75 cm</term> of the measure of twenty are reduced on the chord of illumination. In the lunar chord, the chord of the orb's distance is attached; this is known as the "white portion" (phase) in the lunar sphere. When this is subtracted from the radius, then for the two...

### On the Calculation of the Illuminated Arc

It is not simply that the division is made by half; rather, wherever the upper arc <term>ūrdhva-cāpa; the superior part of the geometric arc</term> exists, that is where the elevation of the "crest" or "top" <note>original: "shikhā"; referring here to the peak of the lunar curve</note> is determined to be the upper portion. In this context, the distinct parts are clear: the multipliers for the hypotenuse <term>karṇa; the line connecting the centers of the celestial bodies</term> and the divisors are calculated. 

Because there is no total destruction <note>original: "dagdha"; in astronomy, this refers to a value becoming zero or a planet being "burnt" by the sun's proximity</note> of the arc, the explanation provided is logically sound. Regarding the intersection of that hypotenuse-line with the center of the lunar disk, the distance between the two—at the edge of the circumference of the sphere—is the primary focus.

<margin>
Tattva-viveka 5.28 <note>A reference to Kamalākara’s own masterwork, the Siddhānta-tattva-viveka.</note>
</margin>

When the Sun and Moon are positioned obliquely <note>original: "tiryak"; horizontal or slanted relative to the observer's horizon</note>, there is a certain distance between the center of the lunar disk and the established intersection line; this is the distance of the hypotenuse-lines. In the absence of latitude <term>shara; the celestial latitude or the 'arrow' of the arc</term>, this is calculated through the sequence of time.

<margin>
59
</margin>

The distance between the two hypotenuse-lines is calculated from the perspective of the center. Wherever a point is situated on the surface of the lunar sphere, if it faces the Sun, it is considered the "illuminated entry point" for the sake of calculation. Due to the lack of distance between the "Moon-line" and the "hypotenuse-line," the surface of the sphere becomes illuminated in its front half. Just as the intersection lines of the hypotenuse are separated, so too is the half of the sphere that becomes "white" <term>sita; the illuminated phase of the moon</term>. 

According to the rule, the upper portion of the lunar sphere is always illuminated in relation to the center of the Sun <note>original: "arka"; the Sun</note>. When the Sun is positioned obliquely, the distance between the "Moon-line" and the "hypotenuse-line" can be proven to be **twelve digits** <term>dvādashāṅgula; a standard measure where the full diameter of the moon is divided into 12 units</term>. Through this geometric demonstration, the increase of that illumination <note>original: "shukla"; the white/bright part</note> is observed. Therefore, the distance is established as twelve digits when the moon is full. 

All of this is clearly explained in the **Siddhānta Śiromaṇi** <note>The famous 12th-century treatise by Bhāskara II</note> for the sake of clarifying all purposes. Regarding the elevation of the lunar horns <term>shṛṅgonnati; the 'uplift' or orientation of the points of the crescent moon</term>, this is the true meaning of the "white portion" throughout the world. When the Moon is situated in its own position, the "horn" appears to tilt toward the south or the north depending on the Sun's position.

Now, regarding the distance between the Sun and Moon when it is less than six signs <note>original: "pādona ṣaṭka"; literally three-quarters of six, but here referring to the angular separation</note>, the difference in their parts <term>bhāga; degrees</term> is calculated. In all such cases where they are situated obliquely, the "horn-angle" of the illuminated portion is always at the intersection. I shall describe the direction of that illumination: if the Moon’s disk is situated otherwise, its direction is determined by the Sun. 

By multiplying by the shadow and dividing by the radius, the decrease or increase of the arc at the tips of the "horns" is calculated. This should not be calculated by mere rote counting, but by the reality of the situation in the celestial circle. Due to the transit of the disks of the Sun and Moon, the tips of the illuminated horns are spoken of as being oblique. This is the clear truth of the Sun-Moon relationship. In the lunar sphere, what is called "illumination" is simply the portion facing the Sun. 

In the sequence of the great planets, there is no difference in the calculation of the two. When the signs are joined at the horizon <term>lagna; the rising point or ascendant</term>, such as Aries and others, they appear in the circle. There, in the middle of the sky, the difference between the two circles <note>the ecliptic and the vertical circle</note> disappears, and the "horns" appear level. Thus, the two tips of the horns are seen as equal in the sky. Even in the "prime vertical" circle <term>samamaṇḍala</term>, they may appear obliquely equal.

### The Geometry of the Lunar Horns

When the pair of lunar horns <term>śṛṅga; the two pointed ends of the crescent moon</term> is considered together, they appear equal. However, when the pair of horns moves obliquely <note>original: "tiryak-gate"; moving at an angle or sideways relative to the horizon</note>, a difference arises. In that situation, based on the vertical circle <term>dṛgvṛtta; the great circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term>, if the moon is at a position less than the ascendant <term>lagna; the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</term>, the appearance of the moon's form is noted as being situated obliquely to the line of sight.

Because of the alignment of the vertical circle with the pair of horns, the upper and lower nature of the moon's tips is determined by the "arm" <note>original: "dorbāhu"; in trigonometry, the sine of the arc or the side of a triangle</note> situated between them. Therefore, from the place of observation, for one who is looking at the elevated portion, the verticality of the horns is established. By observing the horizon <term>kṛjā; the circle where the earth and sky appear to meet</term>, the specific verticality of the horns is seen. In different locations, there is a variation between the slanted and vertical positions.

This variation arises from the difference in the vertical circles. One horn may be elevated while the other is not. Regarding this, I shall state the reasoning: even if there is no elevation <note>original: "nato-shakāt"; referring to the zenith distance or depression</note>, the line of sight remains upward. In the middle of the sky, the line of sight does not follow the same path as the planetary position. Thus, the wise observer understands the sequence. In my own view, in the absence of latitude <term>shara; the celestial latitude or angular distance from the ecliptic</term>, this occurs as described. However, where latitude exists, the two horns are clearly seen as different by those who understand the science. When the pair of horns is positioned on the vertical circle, the appearance is of one kind; when positioned on different vertical circles, the logic suggests the appearance will be varied.

The horns are level only if there is no "existence of the result" <note>original: "bhāva"; here referring to a mathematical result or condition</note> regarding the upper and lower positions. 

### On the Sphere and the Prime Vertical

1. This condition of equality arises from the nature of the celestial sphere <term>gola</term> and the logic of the results. Even when there is a difference between the vertical circle and the circle of declination, the horns appear equal because of the observer's perspective. When the moon is at the ascendant, how are the "arms" of the circle described there? The equality mentioned in the theory of the elevation of the horns is sometimes questioned. Is that equality achieved through the intersection with the prime vertical <term>samamaṇḍala; the great circle passing through the zenith and the east and west points of the horizon</term>? 

<margin>
The North-South Line
</margin>

Until there is an absence of certain factors, the equality of the horns is maintained. Therefore, in the many connected lines of the north-south direction, one sees the verticality of the "side" <term>koṭi; the perpendicular side of a right-angled triangle</term>. In the east-west line, a different determination is made by those such as the author of the *Siddhānta* <note>Likely referring to Bhāskara II or other traditional authorities whose views the author is refining</note>. 

In this context, if the Sun and the Moon <term>śāśāṅka-sūryau</term> are within a small portion of the quadrant, then at the time of the elevation of the moon's horns, the knowledge of the "writing" <note>original: "lekhī"; the diagramming or graphical representation of the moon's phase</note> of the horns comes from the perpendicular. In the east-west line, the calculation follows the intersection of the formulas. For a person in a specific country, the moon appears at the ascendant. For that same observer, the equality of the horns is clear.

That graphical representation <note>original: "parilekhī"</note> is created on the formulaic line. It is visible to the eyes when the moon is on the north-south line. This also happens on the prime vertical. From the middle of the sky, the elevation of the sun and moon is calculated in the east-west direction. When the "side" <term>koṭi</term> is absent, the calculation is clear. Because of the equality of the gnomon's shadow <term>shanku</term>, the perpendicular is found here. The two horns are described as being on the line of equality, rather than the east-west line. However, in the celestial sphere, we see the opposite of this. Thus, for half of the horizon and the prime vertical, when the moon is at the ascendant, the calculation is always possible.

When the two horns <term>śṛṅga; the tips of the crescent moon</term> are considered, if they are equal, then the absence of the "perpendicular" <term>koṭi; the vertical side of the right-angled triangle used to calculate the moon's tilt</term> becomes clearly visible. However, if there is an inequality between the two horns, their regulation is governed by the following rule: this success in calculation is achieved through the universal formula. In the context of the intersection of the meridian <note>original: "yāmyottara-vṛtta"; the north-south circle passing through the poles and the zenith</note> and the top of the horns, the equality of the horns is described through one's own elevation. 

This is understood as the "clear speech" <note>original: "sphuṭa-vāk"; a reference to clear or definitive astronomical results</note> regarding the horizontal equality of the horns. It is certain that this happens in the prime vertical <term>samavṛtta; the east-west vertical circle</term>. Furthermore, when the center of the moon <note>original: "indu-kendra"</note> is at a different position, it follows the path of the observer's own horizon. Just as a slanted line is drawn from the Meru axis <note>the central axis of the earth or celestial sphere</note> at an interval of five and a half parts <note>approximately 5.5 degrees</note>, the illuminated portion of the moon is seen according to the observer's own vertical circle.

Even when the elevation of the horns is discussed, it must be viewed through the vertical circle <term>dṛgvṛtta</term>. Because of the proximity to the zenith <note>the point directly overhead</note>, the moon's position appears vertical. Where the moon's horns appear vertically one above the other, they are said to be in an "up-down" state. If the center of the moon is at the horizon <term>kṛjā; the horizon line</term>, then that verticality lasts as long as the absence of the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term>. Because of the difference between the sun and the moon, their alignment changes; therefore, the verticality of the horns on the horizon does not always result in an absence of tilt.

How then can this be? In whatever place the centers of the solar and lunar disks are aligned such that the perpendicular is absent, the verticality of the horns will truly occur. This is seen by observers in various places. Wherever the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term> is absent, there is verticality. This has been explained through the author's own observations. 

In a place at sixty-six degrees of latitude <note>original: "ṣaṭ-ṣaṣṭhi-aṃśā"; referring to the Arctic Circle latitude where unique celestial phenomena occur</note>, everything appears slanted from the vertical circle. There, the rising of the moon's horns is perceived as vertical due to the absence of the perpendicular. This is the inner secret of the calculation. In various regions, based on the local latitude, the soul of the calculation reveals itself, making the observer feel successful in their efforts. When the pair of horns is level on the vertical circle, they are visible as equal. If they shift even slightly toward the upper half of the circle, their position changes. 

This elevation of the bright portion <note>original: "sito-nnati"; the rising of the illuminated part</note> is not a mere illusion, but a "clear word" <note>accurate reality</note>. Bhaskara stated that the equality of the horns is certain. However, because of the difference between the moon's path and the observer's vertical circle, a contradiction in equality may appear. Thus, where the "clear word" (accuracy) exists, there is not always a rule of equality. From the absence of the perpendicular <term>koṭi</term>, the verticality of the sight-line arises. This is the established conclusion of the *Siddhanta Shiromani* <note>The "Crest-Jewel of Treatises," the masterpiece of the astronomer Bhaskara II</note> regarding the knowledge of the moon's illumination.

<meta>This page continues the technical debate regarding the "elevation of the horns" (śṛṅgonnati) of the moon. It specifically references the 12th-century astronomer Bhaskara II and the scholar Vishwarupa, focusing on the geometric conditions—specifically the absence of the 'koti' (perpendicular)—that determine whether the moon's crescent appears horizontal or tilted.</meta>

In the universal explanation provided there, what was done by Shri Vishwarupa <note>original: "Śrī-Viśvarūpeṇa"; a scholar or commentator on astronomical texts</note> is indeed the same. In the context of the alignment of the horns <term>śṛṅga-saṅge; the tips of the lunar crescent</term>, because the Sun <note>original: "Bhāskaro"</note> is the cause, that state always exists. Without the absence of the vertical side <term>koṭi-abhāva; the perpendicular side of the right-angled triangle formed in spherical projection</term>, there is no such alignment. 

Where some argue regarding the prior or posterior alignment of the horns, it is firmly established that in the absence of the vertical side, the two horns are not seen as being one above the other <note>i.e., they appear level to the observer</note>. This has been explained by the wise to be understood through one's own intellect. Now, the diagonal <term>karṇa; the hypotenuse</term> is discussed. In the courtyard of calculations, when this arises, it is indeed there. 

On the surface of the earth <note>original: "dṛk-pṛṣṭhiya"; relating to the observer's perspective on the surface</note> in one’s own region, when the Moon is in the sign of Capricorn <note>original: "mṛgādau"</note> and the Sun is in its respective sign, the white portion <term>sita; the illuminated part of the moon</term> will always be greater. Therefore, the appearance of the horns does not occur there according to the fall of the perpendicular. Bhaskara’s statement says that because the Moon's orbit is inclined toward the north, the fullness of the white portion appears differently, and thus the equality of the two horns does not occur.

When the vertical side <note>koti</note> is absent, the lunar horns are produced as if they were in the east-west line. Bhaskara’s explanation regarding the meaning of this definition is that the lines proceed from the center of the Moon’s disk. The line that is drawn east-west belongs to the prime vertical <term>sama-maṇḍala; the great circle passing through the east, west, and zenith</term>. Because of the interval between them, the two horns appear tilted. 

In the portion of the lunar eclipse, if it is not within six or eight degrees <note>original: "ṣaṭ-kāṣṭha-lavāṃ"</note>, by the paths brought by man, the white portion is seen in its variety. If the meridian <term>yāmyottara-maṇḍala; the north-south circle</term> is not set in motion, the fullness of the white portion is not brought into the present context. That which is known through the meridian is the "half-horn." The difference between them is the east-west or north-south division. This is a rule of the vertical and horizontal.

Those with confused minds <note>original: "mūḍha-dhiyo"; literally 'dull-witted'</note> have imagined all these things to be otherwise. Therefore, for the sake of helping those who know the sphere <term>gola-vida; experts in spherical astronomy</term>, I explain the knowledge of the auspicious and inauspicious timing of the moonrise, which tells of the elevation of the horns based on the observer's sight.

One should calculate the elevation of the horns using the corrected mathematical method: Subtract three signs from the longitude of the Sun <note>original: "arkāt"</note>, find the Ascendant <term>lagna; the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</term>, and determine the position of the Moon. Through the rays of the Moon's sight, one finds the "visible latitude" <term>dṛg-iṣu; the parallax-corrected latitude</term>. Thus, the precise calculation of the elevation of the horns should be performed by the learned.

This noble work was performed by the venerable Sripati and Bhaskara <note>two of India's most famous historical astronomers</note>. By multiplying the sine of the latitude and the sine of the declination, and dividing by the radius, the arc of the ascendant is produced... <note>the text continues into specific trigonometric ratios for calculating the moon's tilt</note>.

1
</margin>

When the positions of the Sun <term>Arka; the Sun</term> and the Moon are determined through their respective coordinates, and the subtraction of their values is performed, the result determines the alignment of the horns in the celestial sphere, especially when the Moon is being illuminated by the Sun. Regarding the path of the horns <term>śṛṅga; the points or 'horns' of the lunar crescent</term>, if the first calculation is greater than the second, the direction follows the first; otherwise, it follows the second. This rule applies everywhere to avoid confusion in direction. 

Now, when the crescent is not level <note>original: "vihinita"; diminished or shifted</note>, if there is a division by six, the result follows the diameter of the shadow. The two horns appear visible to the observer's eye, but if they are positioned in the middle of the horizon <term>hṛ-maṇḍala; the horizon or heart-circle</term>, one is seen above and the second is positioned horizontally <note>original: "tiryak"; slanted or transverse</note>. This creates the famous distinction between the "southern" and "northern" elevation, known well to those who study the horizon lines <term>dṛṣṭi-cakra; the circle of vision</term>. Where the Sun and the observer's line of sight do not align perfectly, a wise person must apply the rules of projection <term>valana; the deflection or orientation of the lunar disc</term>.

By using the centers of the orbits, one calculates the division of the spheres. This shape is known as the "pot-like" appearance. Here, for the deflection of the horns, the line of sight and the lunar disc <term>vimbā; the orb or disk</term> are considered. Through these calculations, what is the use of a mere drawing without the underlying mathematical proof <term>upapatti</term>? 

The logic here is that the line for the horns originates from the center of the Earth. <insert>(of the Sun and Moon)</insert> Where this line touches the lunar sphere, that is where the Sun's light creates the "white portion" <term>sita; the illuminated phase of the moon</term>. The distance between the centers of the Sun and Moon is the true distance. The calculated distance between the centers of the lunar orbs is what we call the "white part." If the bodies of the Sun and Moon are in the same circle, then in that circle, the white portion is the distance between them. 

When the circle of vision <term>dṛṣṭi-maṇḍala</term> is identical to the celestial equator, the two horns appear equal to the observer; this is the accepted view. However, because of the observer's latitude <term>akṣa</term>, a distortion occurs in the appearance of the white portion. When the Sun and the Moon are at different points in the circle of vision, the "elevation of the horns" <term>śṛṅgonnati</term> appears tilted due to the parallax of the eye <term>dṛg-nati</term>. If the horns are one above the other, it is due to the vertical alignment, as described by Bhaskara <note>Bhaskara II, the famous 12th-century Indian mathematician</note> and others. Where the half-horns are viewed, if the zenith distance is greater, the horns are situated on their own path. The pair of horns is then seen according to the Sun's position. This is how the visible elevation of the horns is determined, even when the calculated "white portion" suggests otherwise due to the observer's local horizon.

<summary>This page explains the geometric and mathematical rules for determining the orientation of the moon's horns, accounting for the observer's local horizon and the deflection (valana) of the lunar disc. It emphasizes that the perceived tilt of the crescent is a result of the relative positions of the Sun and Moon and the observer's latitude.</summary>
<keywords>Shringonnati, lunar horns, Bhaskara, Sita (illumination), Valana (deflection), Drishtimandala, spherical astronomy</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical mathematical proof regarding the orientation of the lunar crescent. Kamalākara is here critiquing the methods of previous astronomers—specifically Bhāskara II—while incorporating elements of Persian/Arabic astronomy which he refers to using Sanskritized terminology like "Me-ula" (inclination/declination).</meta>

Sometimes, because of the internal distance of the golden orb, one horn of the moon may appear high while the other half seems to touch the earth. This, however, does not align with the accepted theory of the celestial sphere <term>golā; the celestial sphere or globe</term>. Such a phenomenon is actually an illusion <note>original: "bhāvo kila"; indeed a state or appearance</note> produced by an error in calculating the Earth's rotation and the observer's specific position. Those who understand these principles say that this error arises from the confusion of the Earth's center <term>ku-ja; born of the earth, often referring to the horizon or parallax</term>.

Regarding the "Me-ula" <note>This is Kamalākara’s Sanskritization of the Arabic/Persian term 'Mail,' meaning inclination or declination</note>, if it were to be three-fold and associated with the white-rayed Moon, then the resulting "Me-ula" would not be the same as that measured from the top of the head <note>the zenith</note>. Therefore, in the established position of the house <note>astrological house or sign</note>, the "Me-ula" must be viewed by the wise as always being relative to the observer's eye.

If the Earth’s shadow is above the lunar disk, it results in a specific visual measurement. In the middle of the visual path of the illuminated house, the sight of the observer and the Sun must be calculated. The difference between the Sun and Moon, when calculated through the dividers of the sphere <note>referring to the use of sines and cosines in spherical trigonometry</note>, is clearly revealed. The sage <note>referring to an authority or the author himself</note> states that the distance between the Sun and Moon on the circle of vision is the true interval. From the mark of the Sun <term>Arka-cihna</term>, even if the parts of the calculation increase, the clear difference between the Moon and Sun remains the "base" <term>koṭi; the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term>. In that interval, the movement of the Moon is the "arm" <term>bhuja; the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term>. Why then, has this not been understood by you as well?

The zenith distance and the visual center of the disk produce the clear "water-parts" <note>likely referring to degrees of latitude or specific coordinates</note>. The degrees of the altitude are the "ear" <term>karṇa; the hypotenuse</term> in this calculation. This has been explained so that the "base" is established in the circle of the house. Thus, if one calculates from the center of the disk to the visual horizon, the "arm" of the Moon is clearly produced.

This method was stated by the author of the *Śiromaṇi* <note>Bhāskara II, the famous 12th-century astronomer</note> for the sake of calculating eclipses, using the existence of the "base." When the horn is high in the calculation, it follows the thread of the Sun. However, those who do not respect this rule fail to see the three-fold law of the triangles. Therefore, that method is not followed here.

The two horns may appear with one high and one low. If this is the case, it is not explained by the "base-sentence" <note>the traditional formula</note>. This does not seem correct to me based on visual mathematics <term>dṛg-gaṇita; calculation matching observation</term>. How can the horn be correctly known? A skilled calculator must look closely. Due to the influence of the Earth’s center and the observer's eye, I have described the elevation of the horns for one's own benefit through these verses. My own calculations regarding the altitude are stated here briefly for fear of making the text too long.

The producer of the Sun and Venus... if the sight has emerged on its own path, the distance of the horns is five-fold. Is that light to be known as the Sun's or the Moon's? The difference is seen. Because there is no obstruction from the Earth's shadow, the Sun's position is clear. When the "Me-ula" of the Sun is greater, it shines brightly for that year. How can I explain this further? The "Me-ula" of the Moon and the Sun follow their own respective paths, and the resulting form is as described.

**Thus ends the Chapter on the Elevation of the Moon’s Horns in the "Siddhāntatattvaviveka" composed by the illustrious Kamalākara.**

The mathematical fame of the great scholars, who are the souls of time, cannot exist without this knowledge. Therefore, this has been stated as a superior tool for those who debate these matters.

Moon: 27... Sun: 27... [Numerical values for various astronomical constants follow, including values for Venus (Śukra) and Saturn (Śani)].

...divided by their own "fast-hypotenuses" <term>śīghra-karṇa; the distance from the Earth to the planet in its epicycle</term>, not for Mars and the other planets. With the radius <term>tri-bhajyā; literally 'the sine of three signs' or 90 degrees</term> of the result of the "cool-rayed one" <note>referring to the Moon</note> [1], the arc-minutes should be the specific true position <term>sphuṭa; the corrected longitudinal position of a planet</term>, originating from the node, the "slow-true" position, the sphere, and so on. This true calculation must be performed right here.

The divisor must be applied in the calculation of the latitude <term>śara; literally 'arrow,' referring to celestial latitude</term> for Mercury and the others [4]. Alternatively, at the node or in the "fast-result" <term>śīghra-phala</term>, by performing the inverse operation and adding it to the true inclination of the circle, the calculation of the latitude should be carried out in the order stated by the rules of latitude.

Then [5], in the true [position], the others are situated in the latitude. Using the hypotenuses known as "moving" <term>cala-karṇa</term> and the three-sign radius, and the sine as mentioned, the distance... [6]. Here too, there is no deviation in the transverse inclination from the eccentric circle <term>vimaṇḍala</term>. From the house <note>astrological sign</note>, the fist-like grip <note>a metaphorical measure of arc</note> is made by the latitude of the corner [7].

If it stays within the deflected path, how is it to be known? Indeed, success in the two methods is achieved by one consistent rule, not by the varying influence of the planets [8]. From the experienced enjoyment of the house, a ninth-part <note>navāmśa</note> is born. From the planet and the rest, the distance between the Earth and the orbit, as well as the calculation of the deflection <term>valana; the variation or angle between the ecliptic and the vertical or equatorial circles</term> of the distance of the "house-pulse" <term>nāḍikā</term>, is performed.

The sine of that becomes the sine of deflection <term>valana-jyā</term>. In this calculation of the solstice <term>ayana</term>, it is the multiplier [9]. The cosine <term>koṭi-jyā</term> of the deflection-disk is multiplied by the cosine of the terrestrial latitude <term>cara-koṭi</term>, and divided by the even-multiplier. In that measurement of the arc, the deflection is produced. That, with three signs added [11], is the sine of declination <term>krānti-jyā</term>. That is multiplied by the radius and divided by the sine of the "even-planet" or simply the sine of the deflection. In the union of the spheres, that is equal to the sine of the maximum declination <term>parama-krānti</term>. In the absence of that, at the beginning of the solstice, it is called the "solstice-direction deflection" [13].

That arc-deflection is what is called the "orb-deflection" <term>vimba-valana</term>. The deflection of the nodes and the sines of the latitude-lords... [14]. Mutually, the cosine and sine become the divisor and multiplier. The multiplier is the radius, and the divisor is the result; that is measured by its arc [15]. The deflection born of latitude is that which is north of the house-equator. According to the stated order, this is somehow explained here, excluding the "cold-rayed" Moon [16].

What has been said regarding the ninety-degree distance of the Earth-lord <note>the Sun</note> is born of latitude. By the qualities of the sphere-union, that is to be accepted or rejected with straightforwardness by the fixed stars [17]. Since the calculation of the solstice was not made in the wandering circle, the true position of the two is taken for the sake of calculating the latitude. I have taken it from the eccentric circle; thus, in this inconsistency, even when there is a movement from the node-position, there is a difference [18]. As long as the circle is deflected...

...the hypotenuse, in the determination of the two corners, the calculation of latitude is not achieved by one method alone [20]. The sine of the declination of the planet, multiplied by the radius and divided by the sine-pair of the planet plus three signs, becomes the true declination in its own direction [21]. The product of the maximum declination-sines of the moving planets, divided by the sine of the planet plus three signs, gives the arc of its solstice [22]. From the remaining refinement, the direction of the correction becomes clear. The sine of the planet's "corner" <note>cosine</note> is multiplied by the sine of the radius [23].

From that arc, the true declination is established in the solstice-direction. Or, the true sine is obtained from the sine of the declination [24]. By those arcs, the true declination of the orb is known by the wise. The maximum declination-sine <term>jina-jyā; the sine of 24 degrees</term> is divided by the three-sign radius [25]... the cosine and the "moving" cosine are multiplied and divided [26]. The sines of the sum of those sixty desired arcs are the true declination degrees in the arc. Even in the true position of the place, this is certainly the case [27]. The square root of the sum of the squares of the sines...

Dividing by the radius <term>trijyā; the standard radius used in sine tables</term>, the sine is taken; in this circle, the direction and the declination are obtained. [28] By applying the rule of the seventh, and bringing it there, the true declination of the planet’s disk <term>viva-sphuṭa-krānti; the corrected declination of the planetary body</term> is calculated. [29]

With the "moving degrees" <term>calāṃśaka; degrees related to the planet's epicycle</term>, the corrected cosine of the latitude is multiplied by the radius and divided, resulting in the arc... [30] For the disk-planet, at the end of the quadrant, one should calculate the degrees and signs in order. [31] At the beginning and end of the quadrant, the disk-planet’s position is established on the even circle. [32]

The product of the radius and the latitude <term>śara; the celestial latitude</term> of the disk-planet is divided, and its arc is the maximum latitude of the moving disk-planet. [33] Its position on the sphere, when not corrected for the precession of the equinoxes <term>ayana</term>, becomes the "true-precession" position. The sine of that, multiplied by the sine of the disk-planet and divided by the radius, gives the arc of the result here. [35]

The declination becomes "true." From the "extremely true" declination, the directions change. In this way, for the disk-planet, the declination is indeed the extremely true declination. [36] This is divided by the cosine of the planet's position plus three signs <note>original: "satrimagraha-dhṛjakayā"</note>, and the arc is called the "true-moving" result. Following the methods for planets, all calculations for the disk-planet are established here. [37]

In this way, the limit of the true movement is established. By this correction, the latitude of the disk-planet is made true. [37] In the *Śiromaṇi* <note>referring to the Siddhānta-Śiromaṇi of the 12th-century astronomer Bhāskara II</note>, the method used to calculate the true latitude has some discrepancies <note>original: "vyabhicāro'sti," meaning an error or deviation from observation</note> in certain places. [38] Because the spheres and declinations are not consistent everywhere... at the pole of the ecliptic <term>kadamba; the point 90 degrees from the ecliptic</term>, the sines of the true declination are equal to the sine. [39] Even if it reaches the celestial equator <term>nāḍī-vṛtta</term>, it does not follow the rule in the north; therefore, the calculation has been performed here with this discrepancy in mind. [40]

**Now for the Demonstration:**
The two "moving" lines are created from the poles <term>dhruva</term> and the Kadamba <note>the pole of the ecliptic</note>. These are brought over the point of the planet's longitude on the ecliptic <term>krānti-vṛtta</term>. On the celestial equator <term>nāḍī-vṛtta</term>, where the true position is, the degrees of its own declination are measured. On the circle of the pole, they are called "eastward," and on the Kadamba circle, they are called "forward." 

Due to the absence of the two "sphere-junctions" <note>the equinoxes</note>, at the "precession-junctions" <note>the solstices</note>, the distance is greatest. On the ecliptic, where the planet’s mark is, that point and the Kadamba circle meet. The planet’s mark, relative to those two, becomes equal. The meeting point is at the position of the planet plus three signs and at the position of the sun. 

There, the mark of the planet on the horizon <note>original: "kuje"</note> is calculated from the celestial equator. The mark of the planet plus three signs, according to its own declination, is brought from the pole. The Kadamba circle is also brought to that junction. The distance between the two arcs of the planet plus three signs on the celestial equator is the distance between the Kadamba circle and the ecliptic. 

On the celestial equator, the degrees are the same as the equator itself. The degrees of the arc of the planet plus three signs, the degrees of the "prime cosine," the "true cosine," and the "forward cosine" are all considered as one. The "forward cosine" degrees and the true declination of the disk-planet are established in that order.

Those degrees on the sphere, fixed at the pole, are the true declination for one situated on the sphere. To bring about that calculation, the degrees of the maximum declination <term>parama-krānti; the 24-degree tilt of the Earth's axis</term> are used. For a planet in an opposite position, the declination is the declination of the planet plus three signs <note>original: "satribhaga-graha-krānti"</note>. The declination itself is the limit of the planet's day and night. Those degrees are the "earth-degrees" <term>bhūvoṃśāḥ; coordinates relative to the observer's horizon or the terrestrial sphere</term>. Here, the degrees are found at the intersection of the sphere and the opposite sign, where the planet’s marker is located at the pole. The calculation of the declination is found in the middle of the instrument. The true declination and the maximum declination are obtained by this method of calculation. Or, the rising of the planet <term>kheṭakodyayam; the time or point where a celestial body rises</term> is 48. This specific calculation is equal. On one side, it is joined with the maximum declination; elsewhere it is unequal. This is the calculation for the movement of the planet's latitude. [49]

When the Earth is considered as a sphere, the corners are equal. Now, another method for the explanation <term>vāsanā; a geometric or rational proof</term> of the true declination:

Just as the intersection of the spheres is known through the movement of the solstices <term>ayana-valana</term>, it should be understood for the planet's disk. In the circle of the solstices <term>ayana-maṇḍala</term>, the interval between the ecliptic <term>krānti-vṛtta</term> and the point on it is the maximum declination. Then, the distance between the celestial equator <term>nāḍī-vṛtta</term> and the point on it is the declination. In the calculation of those circles, when the ecliptic and the celestial equator are pierced by the latitude <term>śara; the celestial latitude of a planet</term>, the truth of the celestial equator is established.

There, in the ecliptic, where the disk is connected, those degrees represent the planet's disk. Likewise, on the circle of the Earth, the degrees of the declination connected there are the "other-true" degrees. These are to be calculated here by the experts in a detailed manner. Thus: Just as the sine of the altitude <term>namonnata-bhāga-jīve; the sine of the zenith distance or altitude</term> of the planet on its true circle is the base for the disk, so too the calculation is performed. 

As desired, for the one who has subtracted the front, one should imagine the center to be three signs away from the planet. Its cosine-sine <term>koṭi-maurvī; the cosine</term> is multiplied and divided by the radius-sine <term>tri-maurvī; the sine of 90 degrees, often 3438 in traditional Indian trigonometry</term>. This provides the result. Then the divisor is the sine of the arc. The divisor is then multiplied by three, and the arc is moved. When it is done by sixty, it is reduced... [45] 

The true declination for the centers beginning with Capricorn <term>makarādi-kendra</term> and the centers beginning with Cancer <term>karkādi-kendra</term> will be in different directions. By this calculation, the lord of the division is the true declination. The sine of that true declination is calculated as previously stated, divided by the sine of the sign. [45]

That is the cosine of the arc of the true declination on the true circle. By the sine of that cosine, there is rotation. From the sine of that cosine, the sine of the arc of the true declination is the square root of the sum of the squares <note>A reference to the Pythagorean theorem: $\sqrt{x^2 + y^2}$</note>. [46] The degrees of the arc are the degrees of the Earth. According to one’s own preference, these are the degrees of the local latitude. Because of the planet's rising, the true declination is obtained from the true circle. [47] 

As established, the rising points on the horizon are born from the planet's revolutions. This was brought about in the previous calculation, as touched upon in the *Marīci* <note>The *Marīci* is a famous 17th-century commentary by Muniśvara on the *Siddhānta-Śiromaṇi*. Kamalākara often critiques it.</note>. [48] As it was stated there, it belongs to the triumph of the sphere, but here it is equal to the other observations. The distance in the sphere of the planet's disk on its orbit <term>vimaṇḍala</term> is born from the center. [49]

Those who are skilled in the deliberation of the sphere calculate the method of mathematics there. One should write the position of the planet on the celestial sphere as desired. [50] The cosine-sine of the planet is multiplied by the sine of the difference between the Sun and the planet, and divided by the radius. The result is the arc, which is then imagined among the planets. [51] One imagines the cosine-sine of the desired point, multiplied by the result...

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s complex derivation of planetary positions from the previous page. Having established the general theory of planetary latitude, he now focuses on visibility corrections (drkkarma) and the calculation of true declination (sphuṭa-krānti). The text compares Kamalākara’s "Siddhānta-Tattva-Viveka" methods with the classic "Siddhānta-Śiromaṇi" by Bhāskara II.</meta>

At the end of the time-circle <note>the celestial equator or the diurnal circle</note>, where the half-width of the planetary disk and the celestial latitude <term>śara; the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term> are in the same direction, the difference between the two centers is calculated. By determining the authority of the light <note>referring to the calculation of the planet's visible disk</note>, the true declination <term>sphuṭa-krānti; the planet's distance from the celestial equator</term> and the true position of the Sun are established. From the power of the planet's own velocity, the movement of the planet is understood. One should perform the calculation as stated in the chapter on authority. The distance between the centers of two planets on the celestial sphere is to be understood as it was described previously. [55]

Here, for fear of making the text too long, I have not detailed the underlying geometric proof <term>vāsanā; the rational explanation or "spirit" of the mathematics</term>, but the intelligent reader can understand it through the various spherical triangles formed by the arcs on the sphere. This very result is found by the calculated fruits. [55]

When the position is determined by the signs of the Earth's shadow on the sphere, then the position of the planet's disk, located at the tip of the celestial latitude, and the resulting calculation of the base <term>bhuja; the side of a right-angled triangle</term> are found. [56]

The visibility correction <term>drkkarma; a calculation to find the point on the ecliptic that rises/sets simultaneously with a planet that has latitude</term> is declared to be of two types: one arising from the precession of the equinoxes <term>āyana-drkkarma</term> and the other from the terrestrial latitude <term>ākṣa-drkkarma</term>. Some say these two should be calculated separately, while others say they should be combined into one. [57]

Because the planet is always facing the opposite of the celestial latitude, it is always directed toward the equinoctial circle. By raising or lowering the planet according to its own base, one should apply the correction for the northern or southern latitude. [58]

In the northern solstice <term>saumyāyana</term>, this process is reversed for the rising and setting of the signs. By that lowering, the rising of the planet's own visibility is corrected. The reverse is true for the setting of the planet. [59]

Thus, where the disk of the planet emerges in its true position, one obtains the place of the visible planet. [60]

The visibility correction is established at the horizon of one's own location. At the rising of the planet, the correction for the solstice and the terrestrial latitude is applied to the planet's longitude. These two corrections are said to be additive or subtractive depending on whether the directions of the latitude and the solstice are the same or different. This visibility of the planet is considered accurate when calculated for the middle of the month. [61]

The true latitude <term>spaṣṭa-śara</term> is obtained by the sum or difference of the visibility corrections. Or, one may perform the calculation directly from the visible planet as stated previously. [62]

<note>The manuscript skips verses 63 and 64.</note>

In that calculation, following the logic of the parts of the sphere, the true latitude is determined by the "staff-multiplier" <term>yaṣṭi-hata; a geometric calculation using a pole or radius</term>. That latitude exists as the distance between the disk and the ecliptic. From the visibility correction and the disk arises the true difference in the limbs of the planet. [65]

By these two types of methods, the true latitude is known to scholars of the sphere. Both methods are useful, as stated in the *Shiromani* <term>Siddhānta-Śiromaṇi, the 12th-century masterpiece by Bhāskara II</term> and in the *Siddhanta-Tattva* <term>Kamalākara's own work, the Siddhānta-Tattva-Viveka</term>, regarding the two visibility corrections. [66]

Now, I shall describe another method for calculating the precessional visibility correction <term>āyana-drkkarma</term>, which is even more accurate than what was previously stated, for the sake of the joy of the calculators. [67]

The Sine of the planet's position <term>kheṭa-guṇa</term> is multiplied by the Radius <term>trijyā; usually the value 3438</term> and divided by its own Cosine <term>koṭi</term>. This is then multiplied by the Sine of the maximum declination <term>parama-krānti; 24 degrees</term> and divided by the Radius. The result is the arc. [68]

The first value and the second are used to find the true declination. In whatever quadrant they fall, the square root of the difference of their squares gives the true declination for that day. [69]

</margin>

Then, the Sine of the declination added to three signs is multiplied by the Sine of the planet's latitude and divided by its own multiplier. This gives the result for the visibility correction. [70]

Alternatively, the Cosine of the true latitude is multiplied by the Sine of the planet and divided by its own multiplier. This provides the calculation for the arc of the latitude. [71]

By using the general rule for the sum and difference of the mean and true positions, the distance of the disk is clearly established. This is the calculation for the declination of the signs. [72]

<meta>This page continues from Kamalākara’s *Siddhānta-Tattva-Viveka*. The previous discussion on planetary latitude transitions here into the specific mathematical procedures for visibility corrections (dṛkkarma). These calculations are necessary because a planet, having latitude, does not rise or set at the same time as the point on the ecliptic it technically occupies.</meta>

<margin>
Tattva-Viveka <note>original: "त. वि." (ta. vi.), an abbreviation for the title of the work, Siddhānta-Tattva-Viveka</note>
Chapter 64 <note>original: "क. ६४"</note>
</margin>
<margin>
85
</margin>

[73] From the distance measured in yojanas <term>yojana; a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8-9 miles</term>, the corresponding arc is determined. If the obliquity <term>ayana; the deviation or movement related to the ecliptic's tilt</term> is greater, the result produced from the planetary disk <note>original: "विव" (viva), a scribal variant for "bimba," meaning the disk of a planet</note> is calculated. 

[74] These values move mutually; having determined the direction of the planetary latitude and the declination, if they are in the same direction, they are added. In the second case, if the directions differ, the difference is taken to find the true position at the end of the sign.

[75] In the second case, one should add or subtract the degrees of the signs as appropriate. From that, the wise mathematician finds the corrected planetary position. 

[76] Having refined the position, the corrected visibility-point <term>āyana-dṛggraha; the planet's position on the ecliptic corrected for the projection of its latitude</term> is established. If the planetary latitude is very large, the calculation must be performed according to the methods previously mentioned in the text. 

[77] The time for the visibility correction <term>dṛkkarma-kāla</term> is determined at the moment of the planet's rising. Using the rising periods <term>udaya-lagnas</term> of the signs at the equator <note>original: "nirakṣa-udaya," referring to the rising times at the terrestrial equator where the celestial poles are on the horizon</note>, the movement of the planet is established.

[78] This applies to the southern and northern paths. By calculating the time according to the stated method, and by intelligently applying the reverse for the southern and northern directions of the two obliquities <note>ayana</note>, the position is found. [78]

[79] Here, the rising degrees of the obliquity are observed. The disk of the planet <note>original: "graha-saṃbimba"</note> is located on the horizon. The position is determined by the intersection of the celestial equator and the horizon. [79]

[80] If the measures of the hours <term>horā-māṇa</term> are made precise, the minutes of arc <term>kalikā</term> for the visibility correction are released. From this effort, the middle position is corrected. 

[81] Now, for a planet moving on its own path, the degrees of right ascension <term>viṣuvaṃśakāḥ; degrees measured along the celestial equator</term> are determined. The time for the visibility correction is then added or subtracted to the planetary position. 

[82] In the various obliquities, if the directions are the same, they are unified; otherwise, they are subtracted from the circle of 360 degrees. By adding this to the planet's corrected visibility position, the degrees of right ascension are derived. 

[83] From that obliquity, the planet's visibility-position is produced; these are the degrees of right ascension. This should be understood by the wise in the same way the true position <term>sphuṭa</term> is calculated. 

[84] Now, I shall describe the method for calculating the visibility-position of the planetary disk <note>original: "bimba-graha"</note> and the time of the visibility correction as they truly are. 

[85] Whichever point lies directly above the planet's disk, and where the two poles <term>dhruva</term> meet their own circles, that is where the corrected visibility-position of the planet is said to be. 

[86] The distance between these two on the circle is the visibility correction. The celestial latitude <term>śara</term> of the planet's disk is on the polar circle, whether to the left or right of the pole. 

[87] The true visibility-position of the disk is established where the latitude meets the center of the disk. This transition of the visibility-position occurs when the latitude is neutralized. 

[88] The difference between the planetary disk and the celestial equator is measured. Those are the equinoctial degrees <term>viṣuvaṃśakāḥ; Right Ascension</term> located in the middle, for the corrected visibility-position of the planet's disk. 

[89] By the intersection of the circles of the two poles, the minutes of the visibility correction <term>dṛk-kalā</term> are found. Thus the planet's disk is situated on its path. 

[90] From the true cosine <term>sphuṭa-koṭi</term> and the sine of the declination, the equinoctial degrees for the planetary disk's position are determined by the intelligent. 

[91] By multiplying the sine of the portion of the hour by its own radius <term>tri-jyā</term>, the result is the visibility-position. By taking the arc of that, one finds the right ascension for the visibility-position of the planet's disk. 

[92] Using its own cosine and dividing by the radius, the arc is obtained. The visibility-position is thus established on its own circle.

The movement of the planetary disks toward their rising and setting is always determined thus: if the rising ascendant is greater than the desired ascendant, the planet's rising is yet to come. 18 The difference between them, converted into ghatikas <term>ghaṭikā; a unit of 24 minutes</term>, shows the time that has passed or is remaining. By dividing the difference between the rising and setting ascendants, the time-based ghatikas are obtained by the learned. 19 If the planet is between its rising and setting at the desired time, the expert should calculate its position based on its proximity to the horizon; otherwise, it is not visible. 20 Wherever I have used the methods of trigonometry for these calculations, it is surely by the grace of the Supreme Soul that this knowledge is provided to the intelligent. 21
