<meta>This page continues the technical exposition from the *Siddhānta-Tattva-Viveka* by the 17th-century astronomer Kamalākara. Following the previous discussion on visibility corrections (dṛkkarma), the text now moves into the specific conditions under which planets become visible (heliacal rising) or disappear (heliacal setting) based on their angular distance from the Sun.</meta>

Due to continuous observation, because of the influence of the celestial latitude <term>vikṣepa; the angular distance of a planet north or south of the ecliptic</term>, the sudden appearance or setting of a planet is described by the wise. 30 Therefore, even if it remains unseen for some time, its visibility should be calculated by the wise. Generally, planets are seen by men when they are clear <note>original: "śuddho," meaning purified or cleared of the Sun's overpowering light</note> of the solar rays, emerging from the state of being obscured. 31

When they are in close proximity, the specific degrees of setting <note>original: "astam-bhāgāḥ"</note> are stated, from which the beginning of the rising occurs. These are produced by the movement of the circles of the Sun. 32 From the rising of the Sun until its setting, the time is calculated in degrees <term>kālāṃśa; "time-parts," a unit where 1 degree equals 4 minutes, used to measure the Sun's depression below the horizon</term> according to the visibility of the disk <note>original: "bimba"</note>. 33

The learned ones say that this is the decider of the day of setting. It is not merely a single cause; rather, through the math produced by this relationship, the setting is determined. 34 Knowing that for different locations, the visibility varies based on the observer's own position relative to the Sun. 35 Therefore, because of these differences, the times are distinct; they are called the times of visibility by those who understand these measures. 36 

<margin><unclear>illegible notes on planetary latitude</unclear></margin>

In the end, for how much time are these parts of the degrees distinct due to their planetary conjunctions? That is known through logic, by understanding the days of setting. 37 The degrees for the Moon <note>original: "indu"</note> and others are added or subtracted as stated. For Venus <note>original: "Bhṛgu"</note> and Mercury <note>original: "Saumya"</note>, the values are modified by their own motions. 38 

The disappearance of the disk <note>original: "bimbāstamanād"</note> for Venus, Jupiter, and Mars occurs at their own specific times. These time-degrees determine the visibility of the planet's eye <note>original: "dṛg-īdoḥ," referring to the observable celestial body</note>. 39 When the actual distance is greater than the stated time-degrees, then the disk is visible; if it is less, it is not visible by any means. 40 

At the time when the planet's own time-degrees are equaled, the setting occurs. Before the rising of the Sun and after its setting, this calculation should be applied with discernment. 41 Through this stated method, the visibility is determined. When the distance from the Sun is known, and it is within the required time-degrees, visibility is impossible. 42 When the distance between the Sun and the planet is at the edge of the calculated time-degrees, then it becomes visible on that day. 43

If the distance is greater, it is certainly visible. This is calculated from the true positions of the planets <note>original: "sphuṭa-graha"</note> at the desired time. 44 Through the observation of the latitude, this truth is established by those who know the principles. The distance between the Sun and the planet is thus halved. 45 By the grace of the Sun <note>original: "raviṇā," likely an invocation or credit to solar observation</note>, this has been told. When there is only the difference between the clear planet and the Sun, 46 then by the stated time-degrees, the planet is always inclined toward visibility. When the distance between the Sun and the planet is close to those degrees, 47 it becomes visible in the light of the dawn or the glow of twilight. This distance between the Moon and the Sun is thus stated. 48

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s mathematical exploration of planetary visibility from the *Siddhānta-Tattva-Viveka*. Having previously discussed the general theory of celestial latitude, the author now provides specific geometric calculations for the sizes of planetary disks and the precise timing of heliacal risings and settings—the moments when a planet first becomes visible or finally disappears in the Sun's glare.</meta>

<margin>New
Correction
58</margin>
By dividing the true distance by the distance in <term>yojanas; an ancient Indian unit of distance, roughly 5-9 miles</term>, and then dividing by the number of lunar days, the results are obtained. 50 These results, when multiplied by fifteen and divided by their own time-parts <note>original: "kālāṃśa"</note>, give the measure of the planet's own disk <term>bimba; the visible diameter of a celestial body</term>. Just as the Sun’s disk was described by the ancient sages, so too is the disk of Saturn defined here through its distance and visibility in time-degrees. 52 

As it is heard in the scriptures, the fame of the planets' disks should not be calculated casually. For the wise who seek the true measurement of the disk, it must be found through its own time-parts and the appearance of the disk in the sky. 53 This cannot be achieved solely by the power of instruments when the observations are unequal; therefore, a slight correction is needed. Furthermore, the time-parts measured by the "fire-sages" <note>likely referring to a specific astronomical school or a value of 3</note> represent the daily motion and other clearly visible characteristics. 54 These values, when multiplied and divided according to the stated method, result in a disk size that is sometimes smaller—but never larger—than what is observed by those with keen sight, for that would contradict direct observation. 55 

Because of the calculations related to the Sun’s time, the <term>true sun; sphutarka, the calculated position of the sun accounting for anomalies</term> and the planet should be determined at the time of rising in the East. All other planetary calculations should follow the standard rules. 56 In this system, whichever planet’s daily motion <term>bhukti; the distance a planet travels in 24 hours</term> is greater than the Sun's is known as "fast" <note>original: "śīghra"</note>. Conversely, the one whose daily motion is less is known as "slow" <note>original: "manda"</note>. 57 Slow-moving planets like Mars, the Sun, and Saturn set in the West and rise in the East. Fast-moving planets, such as Venus and Mercury, rise and set differently when they are in retrograde motion. 58

<margin>66</margin>
Thus, in his own treatise, the Sun has described the sequence of rising and setting. Now, the knowledge of the time past and the time remaining for these events is explained. 60 The minutes of arc resulting from the difference between the true sun and the planet, when multiplied by six, are the desired time-degrees <note>original: "kālāṃśa"</note>. If these are greater than the specified limit for that planet, the rising has already occurred; if less, the rising is yet to come. The opposite applies to the setting <note>original: "asta"</note>. 61 

Based on the stated time-degrees, the minutes of arc are determined and divided by the rising sign's portion. This result, multiplied by the difference in the daily motions of the Sun and the planet, gives the timing. 62 From this, the time passed or remaining in days is known. By calculating the positions of the planet and the sun for that specific time, the result becomes stable and fit for practical use. This clarifies the exact moment of their rising and setting. 63 

One should consider the planet's position relative to the Sun: if it is behind the Sun, its setting occurs in the West. If the planet's position is greater than the Sun's and it is moving fast, the wise say its setting also occurs in the West. 64 A rising in the West occurs when the planet is ahead of the Sun, based on its own time-parts. Whether the planet is fast or slow, this rule determines the transition. 65 

Having known both these conditions from the teachings, I shall now state a special distinction of my own preference. By using the minutes of arc combined with the stated time-degrees, one can calculate the days past or remaining for the event. 66 Otherwise, the planet’s movement might seem incorrect. If the slow-moving planet is further ahead, the days are calculated as previously stated. 67 For a fast planet in retrograde, the days of rising and setting are like those of a slow planet. This specific calculation of time-degrees was not fully explained by the ancient priests or even the gods in this specific way. 68 

Because the movement of the planet and Sun follows this specific path, it is fixed and certain. Therefore, even if it was previously unknown, I have explained this special calculation for the sake of clarity. 69 Therefore, when the planet reaches its peak or its retrograde motion, it does not immediately attain stability; it follows the path of the balanced movement.

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of planetary visibility. Having established the mathematical basis for the size of planetary disks, Kamalākara now transitions to the "kālāṃśa" or time-degrees—the specific arc of separation from the Sun required for various celestial bodies to become visible (heliacal rising) or disappear (heliacal setting).</meta>

Having reached the end of its **retrograde motion** <note>original: "vakra"; the apparent backward motion of a planet in the sky</note>, the planet proceeds toward its **setting** <note>original: "asta"; disappearing into the Sun's glare</note>. 74 Looking ahead, at the time of rising, if the planet is bright and positioned further from the Sun than required, its visibility is well-known at the horizon. In the case of a slow-moving planet <note>specifically referring to Saturn</note>, after its setting relative to the Sun, its rising is seen at the end of the night in the eastern direction. Conversely, when the planet's distance decreases, its setting occurs in the west during the evening. 

In this way, during a conjunction when the planet moves quickly, if the slow planet is further ahead than the Sun, the coming together <note>conjunction</note> will occur. At that time, as the distance between the Sun and the planet decreases, one must calculate the **time-degrees** <note>original: "kālāṃśa"; a measure of the time it takes for the degree of the ecliptic to rise or set, used here to determine the threshold of visibility</note>. If the calculated degrees are greater than the required threshold, the planet is visible; if they are less, it is not. Thus, for Mars and Saturn, visibility is calculated by the increase or decrease of their distance from the Sun during the night.

73 ->**The Visibility Limits of the Stars**<-

The time-degrees for the visibility of the following stars—**Agastya** <note>the star Canopus</note>, **Lubdhaka** <note>the star Sirius</note>, **Vahni** <note>the star Krittika or Alcyone</note>, **Hṛdaya** <note>the star Regulus</note>, and **Abhijit** <note>the star Vega</note>—are measured as thirteen. 74 

The stars of **Bhaga** <note>Uttara Phalguni</note>, **Aryaman** <note>Purva Phalguni</note>, **Ashvini**, and the lords of the quarters, as well as **Hasta** <note>Corvus</note> and **Shravana** <note>Altair</note>, are assigned fourteen degrees by the solar authorities. 75 

The stars presided over by **Agni** <note>Krittika</note>, **Rudra** <note>Ardra/Betelgeuse</note>, **Ahni**, **Mitra** <note>Anuradha</note>, and the root star (**Mula**), as well as **Tiṣya** <note>Pushya</note>, are assigned fifteen degrees. For **Pūṣan** <note>Revati</note>, **Mṛga** <note>Mrigashira</note>, and **Yama** <note>Bharani</note>, the value is also fifteen. 76 

The remaining degrees are determined by their own rising times. The position of the Sun at the **ascendant** <note>original: "lagna"; the point of the ecliptic rising on the eastern horizon</note> and its own rising must be calculated. 77 By these specific degrees, the rising and setting of the stars are determined through the difference between the desired planet and the Sun. 78 

Whether a star is yet to rise or has already set is determined by whether the Sun's position is greater or less than the required threshold. 79 In northern regions, due to the great length of the days, the rising and setting are influenced by the specific latitude of the place. 80 This is not merely a general rule; it applies specifically to stars like **Abhijit**, **Hasta**, **Hṛdaya**, **Svāti**, and others. 81

...by the rays of the Sun, which are like the Lord of light, the time is increased or decreased. 82
According to the teachings, because of those solar disks, the rising of the stars is generally observed in the east,
and their **setting** <note>original: "asta"</note> is said to occur in the west. 83
In the north, the visibility is greater, while in the south it is less, as determined by the calculation of the "arm" of the celestial sphere <note>original: "bhuja"; a trigonometric base used in calculating spherical coordinates</note>. 84
Now, on the **first lunar day** <note>original: "pratipad"; the first day of the bright fortnight immediately following the New Moon</note>, when the Sun has set,
the positions of the Sun and the Moon should be calculated accurately. From the node <note>the intersection of the lunar and solar paths</note>, the Moon's latitude <note>original: "śara"</note> is determined, whether it be north or south. 85
<margin>Siddhānta Tattva Viveka 97</margin> The astronomer should calculate the planet’s position in the circle of the sky. By the method already described, he should determine the **zenith distance** <note>original: "natoṣāḥ"; the angular distance from the point directly overhead</note>. 86
By this threefold method, the time of visibility for the people is found. If the calculated time-degrees are greater than the established limits, then the **undivided crescent Moon** <note>original: "akhaṇḍuḥ"; the very first visible sliver of the moon</note>, shaped like a deer’s horn, is seen.
Without this calculation, even a great astronomer is like one who is blind. 87
If the calculation is done as prescribed, and the time since sunset is correct, if even a small part of the orb is visible,
<margin>67</margin> then the eyes of all people will witness it. 88
A man who claims to see the Moon when it is not actually visible becomes a target of mockery.
Therefore, one must be precise in the **decision of visibility** <note>original: "darśana-nirṇaya"</note>. 89
Those who speak the truth regarding this decision earn the respect of the wise. Even if half a month has passed since the setting, on the first day, the Moon is like Rāma <note>meaning beautiful or auspicious</note>. 90
If the distance is less than the limit, the Moon will not be seen.
One must investigate the rising and setting of the Sun and Moon at the precise time of their proximity. 91
Whether the Moon will be seen or not seen at the time of its rising is determined by these rules.
One should decide this beforehand. Otherwise, if one says it will be seen and it is not, or vice versa, 92
even with the use of **astronomical tables** <note>original: "sāraṇyām"; pre-calculated tables used to speed up astronomical computations</note>, he will be confused.
Such a man is mocked, just as one who describes the Moon incorrectly.
Thus ends the **Chapter on Rising and Setting** in the **Siddhānta-tattva-viveka** composed by Śrī Kamalākara. 93

Now, I shall properly describe the calculation of the **eclipses** <note>original: "grahaṇa"</note>. I will speak of their possibility, for without this knowledge, the efforts of an astronomer are as useless as a laugh in the forest. 1
A **solar eclipse** <note>original: "sūryagraha-sambhavaḥ"</note> occurs when the Moon, at its node, reaches the same longitude as the Sun. At the end of the **New Moon** <note>original: "amānta"</note>, if the degrees of separation are within the limits of the four directions, 2
the **zenith distance in time** <note>original: "nata-nāḍikā"</note> must be calculated for that moment. For the gods <note>meaning the observers at the north pole or perhaps simply the higher heavens</note>, this calculation is essential. By adding the parallax, one finds the true position of the celestial body. 3
From those calculations, the refined positions of the planets are found. By the degrees of the "arm," the possibility of an eclipse is determined. This is the truth of the science. 4
If the node is at the end of the month, and the Moon's longitude is close to it, the eclipse is certain.
This occurs in a cycle of six months. The wise should know the sequence of the nodes. 5
Because of the Earth's shadow, a **lunar eclipse** occurs at the end of the Full Moon, while a solar eclipse occurs at the end of the New Moon when the degrees are right. 6

It contains a specific critique of the astronomer Nityānanda, a rival of Kamalākara. The text then transitions into a detailed explanation of why eclipses occur, using the metaphor of a person casting a shadow in the sunlight to explain the Moon's illumination.</meta>

...By the continuity of observation at the time of the true New Moon, the Sun and Moon, when corrected for **parallax** <term>lambana; the difference in the apparent position of a celestial body when viewed from the Earth's surface versus its center</term>, are situated on the visual line. 7 Since at the conclusion of the true New Moon, 8 at the time of the node, if the observer is knowledgeable, they will see the alignment of the Sun and Moon through the visual path. 8

Regarding the correction of the positions of the Sun and Moon mentioned by **Shri Nityānanda** <note>A rival 17th-century astronomer whom Kamalākara frequently criticized for using Persian astronomical methods</note>, I do not see any logical proof for his method within the celestial sphere. 1

->**Thus ends the Chapter on the Possibility of Eclipses in the *Siddhāntatattvaviveka* composed by the illustrious Kamalākara.**<-

Now, here, whether the **Earth’s shadow** <note>original: "bhū-bhā"</note> intervenes or not, I shall properly describe the possibility of solar and lunar eclipses, so that the immense mathematical effort of the sciences does not go to waste. 1

If the degrees of the intersection of the orbits <note>the nodes</note> are within certain limits, a **solar eclipse** <term>sūrya-grahaṇa</term> becomes possible. At the time of the "horns" <note>the tips of the crescent moon</note>, the calculation must be precise at the moment of the New Moon's end. 2

The degrees of the zenith distance and the minutes of the arc are calculated... Now I speak of the Earth’s shadow... 1

The physical orb of the Moon, through contact with the **rays of the Sun** <note>original: "taraṇi-kiraṇa"</note>, shines with moonlight on the side facing the Sun. On the other side, it is always dark, just like a person standing in the sunlight whose own body casts a shadow on the side away from the light. 4

Because of this, that part of the lunar disk which is a half-sphere facing the Earth is the portion capable of being seen. The other portion is not visible. The **lunar horns** <note>original: "śṛṅga"; the pointed ends of the crescent moon</note> are described in all directions according to this visibility. 5

When the Moon enters the **Earth's shadow** <term>bhū-bhā; the cone of darkness cast by the Earth into space</term>, then the lunar disk is said to be in an eclipse. 6

Through this observation, the true nature of the celestial bodies is realized—a truth that cannot be understood by those who lack the "milk of knowledge." The crescent horns of the Moon are explained by this logic. 7

When the Sun is exactly six signs <note>180 degrees, or directly opposite</note> away, the Earth's shadow acts like an umbrella over the lunar disk. The observer on Earth then sees the eclipse as previously described. 8

Someone might ask: "Why is the Earth's shadow not destroyed by the cold-rayed Moon's own light?" The answer is that the **Moon's brightness** <note>original: "śitāṃśu-kara"</note> is not its own; it is darkened because it is not in contact with the Sun's rays when it enters the shadow. Therefore, the Moon and the Earth's shadow meet in the sky. 9

The solar disk in the celestial sphere...

He references the classical Siddhāntic tradition while offering his own refinements.</meta>

<page-num>68</page-num>

The **Earth-globe** <note>original: "bhū-gola"</note> is always illuminated on one half by the Sun’s rays; because of its solid mass, the **Earth’s shadow** <note>original: "bhū-bhā"</note> exists perpetually on the side opposite the Sun. 11 This fact is well-known to those learned in the science of the celestial sphere <note>the "Golavids"</note>, as demonstrated through the methods of calculation. 12

When the Moon enters into the Earth’s shadow, it becomes invisible to the sight of those on the night-side of the Earth. This darkness occurs when the Moon is at its node. 13 Therefore, at that specific location of the Moon, through its concealment by that shadow, the **lunar node** <term>pāta; the point where the Moon's orbit crosses the ecliptic</term> is understood. This is my established view. 14

If the Sun were to be obscured by the Earth’s shadow, one would not see it; however, the Sun is covered by the intervention of a physical body <note>the Moon</note> coming between the observer and the Sun. 15 If one asks what creates this obstruction, it is the Moon, which acts like a screen. It is not the node itself that creates the darkness, for the node is merely a mathematical point of intersection. 16

In my view, when looking at the solar sphere, there is an obstruction of the Sun's rays. This is observed to be caused by the alignment of the spheres, and not by some unseen body devoid of length and breadth. 17 Without a physical obstruction, how can the Sun’s rays be blocked? Thus, the parts of the celestial sphere are the cause; the node is simply the location where this alignment occurs. 18

One might ask: why does this obstruction not happen at other times besides an eclipse? It is because the Moon is only at the specific position of the **node** <note>original: "pāta-sthāna"</note> during the New and Full Moon. 19 The alignment <note>conjunction</note> of these two orbits at the node is the cause; it is there that the covering of the light occurs due to the obstruction. 20

<margin>Tantra-Vibhāga
Verse 94</margin>

Regarding the solution provided in the **Siddhānta Shiromani** <note>The famous 12th-century astronomical text by Bhāskara II</note>, I find it to be not entirely firm, because they claim the obscuring body <note>the grāhaka</note> exists outside the orbit, which is illogical. 21 It is an established fact that the Moon, moving below the Sun, covers the Sun; thus, the Moon is the "coverer" in a solar eclipse. 22

Ancient teachers have declared that the Earth’s shadow is what obscures the Moon. In both types of eclipses, the cause of the darkness is clearly determined. 23 Therefore, in this context, the term **Rāhu** <note>Traditionally the demon who swallows the Sun/Moon; Kamalākara treats it as a technical synonym for the shadow or the Moon</note> is used to describe the covering of the Sun and Moon. 24

The Moon, moving eastward, enters the Earth’s shadow <note>original: "ku-bhā"</note> at the end of the **Full Moon** <note>original: "pūrṇimānta"</note>. Because of this, the eclipse of the Moon begins from its eastern edge. 25 Conversely, in a **solar eclipse** <note>original: "ravi-graha"</note>, the first contact <term>sparśa</term> is always from the west, and the liberation <term>mokṣa</term> occurs at the east. 26 Because the Moon always moves faster than the Sun toward the east, it approaches from the west and covers the Sun like a cloud. 27

Now, I shall explain the calculations for the length of the Earth's shadow cone for the benefit of those who know the celestial sphere. 28

The difference between the Sun and Earth's radii is the base; the distance between the Sun and Earth is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, and the **cathetus** <term>koṭi</term> is also determined. From the squares of these, the length of the Earth's shadow is famously derived. 29

The **distance of the Moon** <note>original: "śravaṇa"</note> multiplied by the Earth’s radius and divided by the distance of the Sun gives the length of the shadow from the Earth’s center. 30 The length of the shadow cone is calculated from the square of the difference of the parts... by which the diameter of the shadow at the Moon's distance is shown. 31 ...the square root of the difference between the Earth's radius and the shadow's hypotenuse...

with the cells, the knowledge of the "Illustrious Goddess" <note>original: "Vidyā-śrī"</note> is struck by the hypotenuse. 37 From the arc of that, only the released measure is the first clear difference of the Sun; one should know it from the meeting of rays in the sphere.

Now, the distance of the centers of the solar disk and the Earth-globe, in the distance of the hypotenuses there, the solar rays are obstructed by the Earth. At the tip of the Earth, they are produced—this is the nature of the shadow. The contrary occurs; due to one's own vision, it is called by the name "Earth's Shadow" <note>original: "bhū-chāyā"</note>. And that shadow, because of the size of the globe of the solar disk and the Earth-globe, comes from the clear inequality of the solar disk; among those rays that have not reached the surface of the Earth-globe, there is a downward extension; that is the **umbra** <term>pracchāyā; the darkest part of the shadow where the light source is completely blocked</term> in this scientific treatise.

The solar rays <note>original: "ravi-kiraṇa"</note> that have emerged are obstructed by the Earth’s sphere. In those specific places where the rays are held back, the form that follows is the Earth’s shadow <note>original: "bhūcchāyā"</note>. Through this, it is established that on the path where the rays of the planet <note>the Sun</note> meet the Earth, a subtle, long shadow exists because there is no intersection of light rays <note>meaning the umbra where the Sun is completely blocked</note>. The expansion of this shadow on the Moon's sphere is utilized for the sake of understanding the "seven-leafed" eclipse <note>original: "saptacchada-grahaṇa"; likely a technical or poetic term for the phases of a total eclipse</note>.

For this purpose, a long line <term>sūtra; literally "thread," used here as a geometric line</term> is drawn from the center of the solar disk to the center of the Earth’s sphere, extending to the limit of the Earth’s shadow. Where that central line touches the spheres of the Sun and the Earth, that point is the center of their contact. **From the center of the Sun** <note>original: "arkendra"</note>, on the circumference of that sphere’s surface, at the fourth part... and where the eclipse is sought, it should be in that specific semicircle. **Now, the solar disk’s** semicircular surface: to establish the shadow from that point is obstructed, because of the lack of intersection of those rays.

Now, an **investigation into the observed semicircle**: Just as from the center of the solar disk to the region of the semicircle, there is a line measuring the **radius** <term>vyāsārdha; literally "half-diameter"</term>. In this diagram, that line is parallel to the central line. Because there is no intersection with the central line, that measurement should not be applied here. **Now, where** the "vertical side" <term>koṭi; the adjacent side in a right-angled triangle</term> is formed by the straight line touching the solar disk and the Earth’s surface—such a line reaching the semicircle—it can never exist due to the contradiction with other principles. If one follows the ancient sayings of the elders on this, even that is not established without distinguishing the surfaces of the spheres; therefore, it is not useful, as the presence of shining rays there is impossible. Thus, from the semicircle of the solar disk, there is no intersection of rays with the Earth; therefore, the knowledge of that shadow as imagined by fools <note>original: "mūḍhaiḥ kalpitam"</note> is merely a fantasy.

Therefore, a dual approach is seen. For those positioned near their respective places, one line is called the "surface line" <note>original: "pṛṣṭa-sūtra"</note>. This line touches the surfaces of both the solar disk and the Earth’s sphere... and where it touches the solar disk and Earth, it should be calculated in order. **Now, that line** where it touches the central line, there the solar disk's...

<meta>This page continues Kamalākara’s geometric derivation of the Earth’s shadow cone. On the previous page, he established the basic premise of the shadow being formed by the Sun's rays being blocked by the Earth's mass. Here, he begins the rigorous mathematical proof using the principles of similar triangles—a core method in Indian astronomy (Jyotiṣa) for calculating the size of the shadow at the distance of the Moon.</meta>

## The Geometry of the Earth’s Shadow

The rays of the sun <note>original: "ravi-kiraṇa"</note> that are obstructed by the Earth’s sphere form a cone of shadow. To determine the true nature of this Earth-shadow <note>original: "bhū-cchāyā"</note>, one must observe where the shadow meets the zodiacal path. 

From the center of the celestial sphere, one identifies the tip of the shadow. The point located within the solar disk <note>original: "arka-maṇḍala"</note> is determined by the rays, defining the shadow’s tip as the **altitude** <term>koṭi; the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term> relative to the sun's distance. 

In this calculation, other similar triangles are established. From the **center of the Earth** <note>original: "ku-garbha"</note>, we define the Earth’s semi-diameter <note>the radius</note> as the **base** <term>bhujā; the horizontal side of a right-angled triangle</term>. The distance between the center of the Earth and the Earth’s shadow is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa; the diagonal side</term>. The square of these values is used to find the result <note>a reference to the Pythagorean theorem, known in India through the Sulba Sutras and later astronomical texts</note>. 

The line representing the surface of the Earth's shadow is the altitude. From the center of the Earth to the Earth’s surface, the Earth's radius acts as the base, and the line reaching the shadow's tip is the hypotenuse. Thus, at the **Moon’s sphere** <note>original: "candra-gola"</note>, the surface line of the shadow acts as the hypotenuse. The width of the shadow at that specific distance is the base, while the distance from the center is the altitude. 

All of these values are perfected through the use of **proportional similar triangles** <note>original: "sajātīya"</note>. By using the known dimensions of the Sun and the Earth, and the distance between them, the "arm" or base of the shadow is calculated.

### Critique of Earlier Astronomers

**Bhāskara and others** <note>referring to Bhāskara II, the 12th-century author of the Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note> calculate the width of the shadow at the Moon’s sphere by using a transverse line from the central axis. However, in their calculations, they do not fully account for the reality of how the solar rays are obstructed by the physical sphere of the Earth. In reality, because the shadow is formed by the path of the Sun’s rays, the width must be calculated along the curve of the sphere. 

Therefore, the distance between the center of the Earth and the Earth’s shadow is related to the Sun’s distance <note>original: "arka-karṇa"</note>. Just as the Earth’s radius is the base in one triangle, the shadow's extent at the Moon’s sphere forms a smaller, similar triangle. By this method of simplification <note>original: "laghu-karaṇa"</note>, the base and the segments of the shadow's surface line are correctly determined.

<summary>Kamalākara explains the trigonometry of the Earth's shadow cone using right-angled triangles (bhuja, koti, karna) and critiques Bhāskara II's method for calculating the shadow's diameter at the Moon's orbit.</summary>
<keywords>Earth's shadow, lunar eclipse, Kamalākara, Bhāskara II, trigonometry, similar triangles, solar rays, Siddhānta-tattvaviveka</keywords>

--- Page 140 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric proof from page 69. Kamalākara is refining the calculation of the Earth’s shadow (bhūbha) at the Moon's distance. He specifically addresses and critiques the methods used by the "Sons of Raṅganātha" (referring to the astronomer Munīśvara and his lineage) regarding the calculation of the shadow's diameter using the solar disk and the Earth's radius.</meta>

### The Proportional Geometry of the Shadow

In the triangle formed by the base of the region and the visual distance, the **Moon's distance** <term>karna; hypotenuse</term> is equal to the line on the surface of the Earth's shadow <note>original: "kubhā-pṛṣṭha-sūtra"</note>. In this case, the line is the **vertical** <term>koṭi; the perpendicular side of a right-angled triangle</term>. This geometric figure is similar to the standard triangle formed by the **Sun's distance** <note>original: "ravi-karṇa"</note> and the Earth's radius.

If the base is the [shadow-forming] fire-base, then what is the Moon's distance? Using this rule, the half-diameter of the Earth's shadow is marked. There, the traditional form of the Earth's shadow is defined as being equal to the distance between the center of the shadow and its edge, which forms the perpendicular.

<margin>70 Tattva-viveka, Chapter 70</margin>

Since the base exists there, it is understood through the nature of the shadow itself. In the perpendicular, it is described in terms of the hypotenuse. At the point of contact, the shadow's perpendicular and the shadow's hypotenuse are used. From this similarity of triangles, the knowledge of the actual Earth's shadow is easily attained through the proof. Thus, having set aside other methods that involve illogical arrangements of the celestial sphere’s lines which do not produce a consistent shadow, this method is superior. Those other methods are incorrect because they fail to account for the actual position of the rays as they relate to the well-known lunar sphere and the shadow’s extension.

Now, regarding the views of the **sons of Raṅganātha** <note>Raṅganātha was a famous commentator on the Sūrya Siddhānta; his son Munīśvara was Kamalākara’s great rival</note> in their work *Siddhānta Sarvabhauma*: they claim that the true shadow exists because of the distance between the rays coming from the edges of the **solar disk** <note>original: "arka-vimba"</note> and the Earth’s sphere. By using the diameter of the solar disk and the Earth, they calculate the distance between the rays through proportion. Then, using the difference of the squares of the half-diameter and the shadow's half-width, they determine the distance between the center of the Earth and the ray-line. They claim these are the appropriate distances for the shadow calculation. They say that the shadow is brought out by these rays, following the principle of the "calf and the tether" <note>a logic or metaphor used to describe a fixed relationship or constraint</note>.

1. At the predicted time, the Sun and the shadow are in opposite directions. The Moon's disk moves from the East toward the North or South of the center.
2. The distance between the center of the shadow and the center of the disk is exactly the **latitude** <term>śara (or vāṇa); literally 'arrow', refers to the celestial latitude of the Moon</term> at the moment of the middle of the eclipse.
3. Using the sum of the semi-diameters of the shadow and the Moon, the contact and separation of the eclipse are determined. The latitude determines the duration of the eclipse.
4. Others say the difference of the semi-diameters should be used, but their view is not always consistent with the famous sum of semi-diameters.
5. Regarding the lunar sphere, there might be doubt about the Earth's shadow; its position depends on its own light and the Earth's position.
6. Now I shall speak with reasoning: the distance between the Earth's shadow and the Moon's disk on the **lunar sphere** <note>original: "indu-gola"</note> is precisely what falls upon the surface of the Moon from the Earth's shadow.
7. On the Moon's sphere, the size of the shadow is slightly less [than calculated at the center], and below it, it is more. The wise should first consider the contact of the shadow with the lunar sphere with a clear vision.
8. Therefore, the contact of the Earth's shadow and the Moon occurs based on the distance between their centers as generated by the spheres. This is what defines the start and end of the contact.

Thus, for the sake of the truth of the spheres, I have briefly explained the logic for the scholars regarding the nature of the disk-sphere.

<summary>Kamalākara uses the geometry of similar triangles to define the Earth's shadow and critiques the methods of his rival Munīśvara (the son of Raṅganātha). He explains how the latitude of the Moon determines the shadow's contact during an eclipse.</summary>
<keywords>similar triangles, Earth shadow (kubhā), Moon's sphere (indu-gola), Raṅganātha's sons, Munīśvara, celestial latitude (śara), lunar eclipse geometry</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric derivation from page 70. Kamalākara is here establishing the trigonometric relationships required to calculate the precise moments of an eclipse's contact and separation, specifically accounting for the curvature of the lunar sphere and the Earth's shadow.</meta>

### Calculations for the Earth's Shadow and Eclipse Duration

The value combined with <note>original: "uṃ śaru"</note> becomes the sum. Similarly, when multiplied by three and subtracted by the Earth's value, the square of the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa; the diagonal or distance</term> is halved. From this, the result produced is multiplied by the Earth’s physical limbs <note>a metaphorical term for specific numerical factors in Sanskrit astronomy</note> and then divided by the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term>. The resulting figure for that direction is the remainder of the sum. When the Earth's shadow and the **perpendicular** are multiplied and then divided by that **hypotenuse**, the results for the three types of knowledge <note>referring to the three systems of astronomical calculation</note> are obtained, and from the **hypotenuse**, its corresponding arc is determined.

In the conjunction of the two <note>the Sun and Moon</note>, the difference in **duration** <term>sthitī</term> for the **initial contact** <term>sparśa</term> and the **final release** <term>mokṣa</term> is calculated. 

Now, regarding the squares of the Earth’s shadow diameter and others: from the square of the diameter, when three are added and the result is multiplied by twelve, if that is the **hypotenuse**, then its front portion—multiplied by the number of Indra <note>the number 14</note>—yields the arc. When that is doubled and added, it equals thirteen. Through the half-diameter of the Earth’s shadow, the difference between the two is found. At the time of **initial contact** <term>sparśa</term> and **final release** <term>mokṣa</term>, the position on the lunar sphere is determined by the specific **hypotenuse** and its arc.

When the Earth's shadow is accurately perceived, the difference between them is the "shadow-break" <note>original: "kumbhedo"</note>. Since the days elapsed <term>ahargaṇa</term> are divided for the three types of knowledge and are mixed, they are also combined here. Due to the difference between the two, they arise. 

Now, the method for bringing this about follows seven rules. Just as it is done there, it is applicable everywhere. When the **sum of the semi-diameters** <term>mānekyakhaṇḍa</term> is met, that same calculation is applied by the sages everywhere. This is done to achieve accuracy in practical dealings throughout the night. Where the Earth's shadow is reduced by those calculations, my own opinion is stated here.

### The Geometric Proof

The **proof** <term>upapatti</term> is as follows: 
The central line <note>original: "madhyasūtra"</note> between the center of the Earth and the Earth's shadow meets the celestial sphere at the center of the shadow. Regarding the **initial contact and release**, the center of the lunar sphere is also considered on the same celestial sphere. When the centers of these two are connected and divided by the radius, it gives the **hypotenuse of the duration** <term>sthiti-karṇa</term>. From that, the difference between the two centers is derived; this is the proven method for calculating the "fitness" of the eclipse. 

The means to achieve this is as follows: At the moments of **contact and release**, the Earth's shadow on the lunar sphere—and the surface of the larger shadow situated above it—touches the lunar disk. There, from the three **perpendiculars** <term>koṭi</term> situated at half the distance from the shadow’s surface, and through the three **bases** <term>bhuja</term> formed by the contact points and the difference in diameters, the center of the lunar disk remains on the lunar sphere. 

Thus, wherever the surface of the Earth's shadow meets the Earth's surface, the Earth's center is located in a specific direction from the Moon's center, acting as a **base** <term>bhuja</term> through the distance of the shadow. From the aforementioned three **perpendiculars** on the shadow's surface, the Earth's surface, the lunar disk's surface, and the clear surface of the shadow are defined. In that context, there are three **hypotenuses** for the Moon and three **perpendiculars** for the two halves of the radii and the **base**. 

This is similar to the methods for calculating the Earth's shadow and other such figures. In that form of the **perpendicular**, the central line and the **hypotenuse** are assumed to be the **hypotenuse** of the half-sum of the shadow's diameter. This follows from the two methods of proof. For the sake of determining unknown values, the calculation is the same. The process is stated thus: The half-diameter of the Earth's shadow, the radius, and the three Moon-hypotenuses form the **perpendicular** which lies within the lunar sphere as the Earth's shadow. By bringing this forward and subtracting it from the shadow's surface, the remainder is considered the **perpendicular**. Using the Moon's half-diameter as the **base**, the resulting **hypotenuse** should be used for the full arc. This represents the distance between the line of the shadow's surface and the center of the lunar disk on the lunar sphere. The distance between the central lines of the shadow on the lunar sphere is the half-diameter of the shadow.

<summary>This page provides the mathematical and geometric proof for calculating the Earth's shadow during a lunar eclipse, specifically adjusting for the curvature of the lunar sphere. Kamalākara explains how the interaction between the centers of the Earth, the Moon, and the shadow determines the precise timing of the eclipse's contact and release.</summary>

### Alternative Methods for Determining Eclipse Duration

Regarding the **Earth's shadow** <note>original: "kumbha"; literally 'pot', here referring to the cone of the shadow</note>, a second method is now described for when the sight-line is positioned at the edge of the disk. If the distance at the center is known, then at the moment of the shadow’s clear contact, one must calculate the contact and separation based on the position within the celestial sphere. Wise astronomers define the distance between the centers of the two disks <note>the Moon and the Earth's shadow</note> at the moment of contact as the **hypotenuse of duration** <term>sthitikarṇa; the distance from the center of the shadow to the center of the moon at the moment the eclipse begins or ends</term>. 

In this calculation, the square of the **sine** <term>jīvā</term> of the **hypotenuse of duration** is subtracted from the square of the **sine** of the **latitude** <term>śarajyā</term>. This result is multiplied by the **radius** <term>triguṇa; the standard radius used in trigonometric tables, often 3438 minutes</term> and divided by the **perpendicular sine** <term>koṭijyā; the cosine</term>. The resulting arc gives the **minutes of the half-duration** <term>sthitikhaṇḍaliptāḥ</term>. 

Alternatively, if the **sine of the latitude** is multiplied by the **radius** and divided by the **sine of the hypotenuse of duration**, the resulting arc will similarly represent the duration. Furthermore, by dividing the given **perpendicular sine** and multiplying by the other **perpendicular sine**, the **hypotenuse** for the desired time is found. 

#### Geometric Proof on the Celestial Sphere

The reasoning here is as follows: on the celestial sphere, the center of the disks and the clear radius-circle form the **hypotenuse of duration**. In this arrangement, the center of the lunar disk follows a path determined by the **secondary circles** <term>kadambavṛtta; circles of celestial latitude passing through the poles of the ecliptic</term>. The **latitude** <note>original: "śara"</note> and the **declination** are calculated through these coordinates. 

When the **initial contact** <term>sparśa</term> and **final release** <term>mokṣa</term> occur, the sum of the **hypotenuse of duration** and the **latitude** is used. The square root of the difference of their squares provides the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term>. This is the standard method used by all ancient teachers for practical calculations. This "three-line" formula <note>referring to a right-angled triangle</note> is established for the sake of calculation, but it must be understood through the nature of **spherical triangles** <note>original: "cāpajātya"; literally 'born of arcs'</note>.

In a true sphere, the triangle is formed by arcs rather than straight lines. Therefore, the **base** <term>bhuja; the sine</term> and the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> are expressed through the **sines of their arcs** <note>original: "cāpaguṇa"</note>. This method is proclaimed by the "Universal Rulers" <note>an epithet for great ancient astronomers</note> of this science. 

In every case, the **sine of the contact arc** acts as the hypotenuse, and the **half-sine of the arc** acts as the base. Thus, the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term> is produced. In these two types of right-angled triangles, the values are scaled to the radius-circle. The difference between the square of the **hypotenuse** and the **base** yields the **sine of the perpendicular**. However, because there is a slight difference between using a full sine and a half-sine arc, one must be careful. The third type of calculation arises from this difference, where the arc and its sine are used to find the precise **perpendicular sine** derived from the **radius**.

### Calculations of Contact and Release

The duration is divided into two parts by the wise who reside here and know the truth. Even when the mathematical proof of the celestial circle is established, why is it that the specific qualities of the planets do not always manifest exactly? The ancient sages <note>original: "āryaiḥ"</note> stated that after considering everything, one must apply their own knowledge to this system. The contact of the celestial bodies is calculated using the **sine of the radius** <term>trijyā</term>, and the duration must be understood again through the movement of the planet. Through the taste of this knowledge, and by the weight of authority, the calculation of the **half-duration** <term>sthiti</term> is performed so that the time of occurrence does not become confused, even within the same **zodiac sign** <note>original: "kumbhe"; literally 'the pot', referring to Aquarius or a specific sector</note>.

The **contact** <term>sparśa</term> and **release** <term>mokṣa</term> do not occur naturally at the same time in all directions; they are caused by the specific duration and the **latitude of the planet** <term>śara; literally 'arrow'</term>. By using the **hypotenuse of duration** <term>sthitikarṇa</term>, the exact timing of the beginning and end of the eclipse is determined. In this manner, when the results of the contact and release are processed through the repeated calculation method <note>original: "asakṛd-vidhinā"</note>, they become accurate. By calculating the **half-duration** <term>sthiti-khaṇḍa</term>, the **middle of the eclipse** <term>madhya-graha</term> is found between the two.

### Religious Merits of the Eclipse

During the entire duration of the eclipse, from contact to release, is the time for **sacred bathing** <note>original: "snāna"</note> and **prayerful recitation** <note>original: "japa"</note>. If these are not performed with great devotion, there is a loss of merit. A person standing upon the Earth who observes these rites is quickly freed from all sins. Because of the influence of the celestial lights, and for the sake of naming the phenomenon, a human always watches the **solar disk** <term>arka-bimba</term> during this time.

### Observations of the Solar and Lunar Disks

When the solar disk is observed, it is diminished by the lunar disk. In the case of the Moon, it is five digits in magnitude. This same phenomenon occurs for the Sun as well. The difference between the white and dark portions of the Moon is determined by the positions of the directions. The difference between the time of rising and the time of visibility must be adjusted by the observer’s own parts. This is the difference between the two disks as seen by the eye. Therefore, in a partial eclipse <term>grāsa</term>, one should see the calculated portion as it truly is.

When the two disks meet, the result is the manifestation of the eclipse. The times of **contact** <term>sparśa</term> and **release** <term>vimukti</term> become visible to the eye when the calculated half-duration is added to or subtracted from the time. The most excellent of the modern scholars <note>original: "navīnavaryāḥ"</note> say that after leaving the calculations of the sphere, the visibility of the eclipse remains the same; this is true and there is no other reason for it.

### Critiques of Measurement

By the proportion of the **sine of altitude** <term>unnata-jyā</term>, some here provide an answer regarding the release. This is because when the disk is elevated, the strength of the sight increases. In their view, the division of the parts is appropriate and beautifully explained. However, some others have accepted a difference in the measurement of the centers of the disks based on their own observations. Astronomers who are very wise have considered this and concluded that the traditional methods of the **Siddhānta** <note>the established scientific treatises</note> are the true authority. 

Based on the imagination of these teachers, because of the Sun's own light, the shadow of the Earth <note>original: "kumbha-ardha"</note> appears slightly different. In the disk of the Moon, the Sun's rays create a **reddish-brown color** <note>original: "piśaṅga-varṇa"</note>. This is why it is called "coppery" <note>original: "tāmra"</note> by some. This distinction in the mathematical calculation is not mentioned by others because of its difficulty.

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical discussion from page 71 regarding the nature of eclipses. Having previously discussed the mathematical durations (sthiti), the author, Kamalākara, now moves into the physical theory of how the solar disk is obscured and begins a critical analysis of parallax (lambana), specifically addressing the theories of the 12th-century astronomer Bhāskara II.</meta>

Now, what is meant by the **measurement of the disk** <term>bimbavedhya: the observational piercing or measurement of the celestial disk</term> by those dwelling on Earth? Is it not the knowledge of the people regarding the phenomenon of Rahu <note>In Indian mythology, Rahu is the demon who swallows the sun; here used to denote the cause of the eclipse</note>? Why is it that in the clear sky during the day, the Sun appears to be "eaten" <note>original: "grāsana"; refers to the obscuration or "biting" of the sun during an eclipse</note>, while this does not happen to the Moon in the same way at night? This is well-known regarding the solar eclipse. Even if these things were made known by the ancients through other methods, there remains a flaw in the sequence of their explanations regarding the status of the covering of the disk. In these cases, the visibility of the disk occurs because of the lack of light from the solar rays. Without the common understanding of the world, how else could this visibility be explained? According to the **logicians** <term>tārkika: scholars of the Nyaya-Vaisheshika schools of logic</term>, this occurs due to the contact of rays. Because there is an absence of the actual object being struck, the form of the Moon, when covered by the Earth's shadow, appears in such a way. The cause of this lack of brilliance is the specific example provided, and not the diversity of many different causes. In the visible world, such a state is mentioned because of the nature of the form itself. This is stated in the "Siddhāntatattvaviveka" composed by Śrī Kamalākara.

### Chapter on Eclipses in the Siddhāntatattvaviveka

<margin>72</margin>
Now, because of the existence of the single Essence <note>original: "ekasatva"; a reference to the divine or the underlying reality of the physical laws</note>, the **Moon** <note>original: "śītakara"; literally 'the cool-rayed one'</note>, when observed during the New Moon, is situated below the path of the Sun's rays. Therefore, the observer does not see the solar lotus <note>original: "aravinda"; a poetic term for the sun</note> because it is obstructed. The **solar disk** <term>arka-bimba</term> is seen as being covered by the Moon's disk, which is what the people of the world call the "grasping" or eclipse. Furthermore, the shadow of the lunar disk during a solar eclipse, which falls upon the Moon or is otherwise visible, is projected by the wise based on the line of sight of the observer. Because of this, the covering of the Moon’s splendor during a lunar eclipse is like the cooling of its rays. At the time of a lunar eclipse, the obstruction of the Sun by the Earth’s shadow-disk <term>bhū-bha</term> is considered by the noble ones through the line of sight. When the quality of the light is absent, the true solar disk at that moment, as seen through these calculations, is established with effort. This is the first grace offered for the sake of understanding the logic of the planets. With this knowledge, the explanation is uttered.

By knowing this science, the intellect is refined through the rays of measurement. At that moment, the specific position in the **local vertical circle** <term>dṛṅ-maṇḍala</term> is determined. The motion caused by the observer's position <term>dṛggati</term> and the **parallax** <term>lambana</term> are explained here. The degrees of altitude and zenith distance, following this method, must be understood from the example of the Earth's shadow. After first understanding all the calculations regarding the movement, one should perform the computation using the solar formula at the center of the Earth. At that time, the expansion of the Moon is considered according to the theory.

When the line of sight is not a single straight line from the center of the Earth, the difference in the drawing <note>original: "lekhana"; refers to the projection or diagramming of the celestial positions</note> is what has been described by the great teacher **Bhāskara Āchārya** <note>The famous 12th-century author of the Siddhānta Śiromaṇi</note>. However, that is not entirely consistent with the truth. According to the vision of the scriptures and following the calculations, I have performed the work on **parallax** <term>lambana</term>. Those who are capable of describing the truth have proposed theories of parallax. But those theories are merely for the sake of establishing worldly practice; they are not suitable for a truly accurate experience of the reality. Therefore, I am now setting forth the consideration of the proof of parallax according to my own observations <note>original: "svadṛk-prakāraiḥ"</note>, after having understood what was known by the ancients.

# On the Determination of Parallax

In the practical application of his own treatise, for the sake of establishing the truth, the motion of the center [of the Earth] and the displacement of the observer’s position result in what is called **parallax** <term>paralambana: the apparent shift in the position of a celestial body when viewed from different locations</term>. This shift in position is measured in miles <note>original: "yojana"; a traditional Indian unit of distance, roughly 8 miles</note>.

The minutes of the **visual parallax** <term>dṛglambana: the specific parallax experienced by the observer at a given moment</term> of the Sun are seen exactly as they are. From this, the **parallax in latitude** <term>nati: the north-south component of the parallax shift</term> is derived. Some teachers, however, suggest that by using the Earth’s radius as the base and the Sun’s distance as the hypotenuse, a triangle can be formed. By finding the square of the sum of these, and then the root, one finds the distance from the observer on the Earth's surface to the Sun.

At the end of the line reaching the center of the star-circle <note>the celestial sphere</note>, the segment that represents the difference between the surface and the center is called the **parallax**. Through the method of the **rule of three** <note>trairāśika: a mathematical proportion used to find an unknown value from three known ones</note> in miles, the value is calculated at the tip of the base. There, having made the calculation of the arc, the position relative to the Earth's center is established. 

This **visual parallax** is said by the experts to be the most accurate method. It is proven by the demonstration of the combined visual triangle. Because the observer stands on the surface of the Earth, the line that goes toward the Sun is the "visual line," while the line from the Earth's center is the "true line." The difference between these two lines, when the Sun’s disk is situated at the horizon, is the maximum parallax. This is why the solar disk, when fixed at the Earth's center <term>bhūgarbha: the geometric center of the globe</term>, is not seen in the same way as from the surface. 

Due to the gap between the surface and the center, the Sun appears to have moved from its true position. By combining these visual angles, the discrepancy is corrected. Some say the solar disk is not affected by this difference in position, but they are mistaken. Because the observer is at a different location than the Earth's center, the vision of the solar disk is naturally different. In the center of the Earth, the orbital path is true; the distance between that and the surface observer is the cause of **parallax**. 

In this system, the parallax is at its center when the Sun is at the zenith <note>directly overhead</note>. But when the Sun is at the horizon, the visual parallax is at its maximum. Through calculations involving these proportions, the planet’s position is determined. This is known by the wise who understand the math of the spheres. 

To make this clear for the eyes: when the observer is on the Earth's surface, the "visual line" and the "center line" of the Sun are different. In the globe, the distance between these two lines is very small. Others assume a maximum value for this and then use a proportion to find the value for the desired time. By following this method, the difference is corrected to find the true appearance. This has been proven through the rays of the lines of sight. How could it be otherwise? The wise should contemplate this deeply. 

The maximum visual parallax is calculated from the Earth's center. Then, by applying the proportion for the desired time, the true position of the sun and stars is found. This is the **explanation of the parallax** <term>lambana-vāsanā: the underlying logic or proof of the parallax theory</term>.

When calculating the hypotenuse and the visual line of the Sun, the Earth's radius acts as the base <term>koṭi: the vertical side of a right-angled triangle</term>... <unclear>smudged text at bottom</unclear>... The junction of these lines results in the parallax. Just as the distance between the center and the surface is measured, so too is the difference in the Sun’s appearance. This is clearly visible through direct observation of the visual and center lines.

Kamalākara uses trigonometry (base and hypotenuse) to prove that the Sun's apparent position must be corrected for accurate astronomical predictions.</summary>

# The Intersection of Visual Lines and the Earth's Center

Regarding the union of the **Earth’s center** <term>kugarbha: the mathematical center of the globe</term>, the observer's eye, and the planet’s disk, an inquiry is made into whether certain errors are unavoidable. For one who dwells in the forest <note>this may refer to an isolated observer or a metaphor for a lack of proper instruments</note>, the precise calculation of a planet's degrees is not easily mastered. Because of the lack of precision in the "face" or segments of the calculation, there is no certain determination of the **minutes of parallax** <note>original: "kallīkṛtatva"; referring to the reduction of degrees into minutes for calculation</note>. In the "center-line" <term>garbha-sūtra: the imaginary line connecting the Earth's center to a celestial body</term>, this parallax does not exist; it is only accepted due to the difference in the observer's location.

<margin>T. Vi. K. 73</margin>

Through the power of the **sight-line** <term>dṛksūtra: the line of sight from the observer on the surface to the planet</term>, the calculation is made. Even so, the true visual knowledge of a planet in a specific region depends on the observer's position. Therefore, it is appropriate that the **visual parallax** <term>dṛglambana: the apparent shift in position due to the observer's perspective</term> is not accepted as a universal constant by all, as its result depends on the observer's distance from the horizon.

Or rather, let us consider the visual parallax more closely. To bring it into calculation, one must consider the planet’s disk as it stands in the sky. One line is drawn from the **Earth's center** <term>kugarbha</term>.

<margin>73</margin>

Where that line meets the region of the **star-circle** <term>bhacakra: the zodiac or the celestial sphere</term>, it marks the planet's position. Behind it, the observer—not seeing from the center—looks from his own position. The line coming from his own eyes, known as the **sight-line**, does not reach the center of the sphere. Instead, it reaches the planet's position as situated in its own orbit. The spot on the celestial sphere where this sight-line attaches is what the eye perceives.

Thus, the position on the celestial sphere is accepted as being on the **vertical circle** <note>original: "digvṛtta"; the circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</note>. Therefore, the parallax is the interval on the celestial sphere between the center-line and the sight-line. When there is one line from the Earth's center and a second from the observer's eye, the distance between them on the sphere is the **visual parallax** <term>lambana</term>. This shift occurs because of the height of the planet in its orbit.

A planet is "with degrees" <note>meaning its position is calculated</note> when it is projected onto the sphere. When the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśa: the angular distance from the point directly overhead</term> is calculated and added to the visual parallax, the true observed position of the planet is found. Some say this is necessary to achieve the union of the two lines. 

The general principle is this: the farther away a planet is, the smaller the parallax becomes. Conversely, the closer a planet is to the center of the Earth, the greater the parallax shift will be. In this way, the parallax of the Sun and Moon are distinguished by their varying distances, and the difference between those two parallaxes is explained for the sake of accurate calculation.

<summary>This page explains the geometric basis of parallax, defining it as the angular difference between a line drawn from the Earth's center and a line drawn from an observer's eye to a planet. It emphasizes that parallax is greater for closer celestial bodies and varies based on the observer's zenith distance.</summary>
<keywords>parallax, Earth's center, sight-line, celestial sphere, zenith distance, planetary orbit, Kamalākara</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric critique of parallax from the previous page, specifically focusing on the discrepancy between geocentric and topocentric observations. The author, Kamalākara (the son of Raṅganātha), is analyzing the "visual displacement" of the Sun and Moon, arguing against the methods used by his predecessor Munīśvara.</meta>

### The Calculation of Visual Displacement and the Earth's Radius

The union of the **Earth’s center** <term>kugarbha: the mathematical center of the globe</term> and the planet’s center has been discussed; however, this is not the case for the secondary observers. For the Sun and other planets, the geocentric field of the lunar mansions <term>nakṣatra-kṣetra: the specific sector of the zodiac</term> is calculated in a specific order. 

Because there is no physical distance between the Sun and the Moon during a true conjunction <note>i.e., they share the same longitude</note>, the apparent equality of their positions is disrupted by the **difference in sight** <term>dṛṣṭibheda: the shift in perspective from the Earth's surface compared to its center</term>. This shift is what we call **parallax** <term>lambana: specifically the longitudinal parallax in eclipses</term>. Similarly, at the end of the "visual sign" <term>vimonalagna: a specific point on the ecliptic used in parallax calculations</term>, the correction known as **latitude-parallax** <term>nati: the north-south shift due to perspective</term> occurs. 

When the observer’s eye, the Earth’s center, and the planet are all aligned along the **center-line** <term>garbha-sūtra</term>, the planet is seen in its true position within the zodiac. However, when the **sight-line** <term>dṛksūtra</term> deviates from the center-line, a displacement is perceived.

<margin>Calculation of Displacement</margin>

This **visual displacement** <term>vilepana: literally 'smearing' or 'coating,' here meaning the angular deviation</term> is small when the planet is high and increases as the distance increases. To calculate this correctly, one must follow these steps:
1. Take the square of the **Earth’s semi-diameter** <term>bhūvyāsadala: the radius of the Earth</term>.
2. Multiply this by the **Sun’s distance** <term>arka-śruti: the hypotenuse or distance from the Earth to the Sun</term>.
3. Using the square root of these values and the sine of the altitude, the displacement is found.

This displacement on the Sun’s disk is called the **visual displacement of the motion** <term>dṛgvilepana</term>. It is determined by the ratio of the Earth's radius to the **yojanas** <note>an ancient Indian unit of distance, roughly 5–9 miles</note> of the planet’s distance. 

<margin>Critique of Munīśvara</margin>

The great sages <note>referring to earlier astronomers</note> have described this in their own works. However, **Munīśvara** <note>a rival astronomer and author of the *Siddhānta-sārvabhauma*</note> has stated his own reasoning regarding the intersection of the center-line and the sight-line. He claims that when the distance between the two lines is equal to the Earth's radius, the displacement on the **celestial sphere** <term>bhagola</term> is at its maximum.

I, the son of **Raṅganātha** <note>Kamalākara identifies himself by his lineage; Raṅganātha was also a famous commentator on the *Sūrya Siddhānta*</note>, contend that the true visual position on the celestial sphere is only understood through the difference in the arcs of the zodiac. The theory of **parallax in motion** <term>lambana-bhukti</term> must be calculated based on the observer's specific time and location. Without this, the displacement of the Sun <note>original: "arka-levana"</note> remains incorrect. Therefore, the motion of the planets must be adjusted by the visual displacement derived from the observer's horizon.

### The Graphical Representation of Visual Conjunctions

Why should the **visibility correction** <term>dṛkkarma: a calculation to determine when a planet becomes visible relative to the horizon</term> always be applied without a proper graphical representation? Indeed, this was not properly integrated according to the style of the *Siddhānta Sārvabhauma* <note>original: "sārvabhauma-rītyeva"—referring to the rival work by the astronomer Munīśvara</note>. After much contemplation by the noble scholars regarding the difference between the two graphical representations of the Sun and Moon, it is determined that the representation here in the **lunar sphere** <term>indugola: the celestial sphere or orb of the moon</term> results from the planetary positions at that specific time.

If the graphical representation of the **visual conjunction** <term>dṛgyogalekhana: the drawing of planets appearing to meet from the observer's perspective</term> is handled differently due to a change in the method of calculating the vehicle's path, then the true essence of the **celestial sphere** <term>golatattva</term> is found right here. 

<margin>The Earth's Surface and Sight-Lines</margin>

I have explained all of this graphical representation in accordance with the observation of visual discrepancies. Without this reasoning, the calculation of the Earth's radius is merely an approximation. This phenomenon, which follows the **line of sight** <term>dṛṣṭisūtra: the straight line from the observer’s eye to the celestial body</term>, arises from the distance of the rays. It is not something that can be pulled or stretched beyond the truth; rather, it is a superior visual observation. 

When the **Earth's surface** <term>kupṛṣṭha</term> and the base of the triangle formed by the horizon and the planet's tip are in different directions, but the zenith is the same, one must determine the local sunrise and sunset. Here, the learned astronomer must understand the **visual distortion** <term>dṛṣṭi-vikṛti</term>. This value is half of the clear mark <note>original: "spaṣṭacihnārdha"—likely referring to a specific geometric marker in the diagram</note>. 

By imagining the **Earth-segment** <term>kukhaṇḍa</term> to be fixed at the axis, and through the explanation of the graphical representation suited to the sunrise and sunset of one’s own location, the truth is spoken. This truth rests upon the **sight-line** <term>dṛksūtra</term>. In the presence of the "sharp-rayed one" <note>the Sun</note>, there is a certain decrease in the visual representation. 

When the **solar distance** <term>ravikarṇa: the hypotenuse/distance to the Sun</term> is multiplied by the axis-segment, the elevation of the sight-line occurs at the circle of the local sunrise. This is similar to the value derived from the **Earth's center** <term>kugarbha</term>. One should subtract the axis-segment from the desired location to find the visual distortion. 

### Geometric Calculations of the Solar Hypotenuse

In the representation of the horizon at the Earth’s center, the calculation for the distance of the Sun's rise and set is derived from the original source. This is possible through the geometry of those specific regions. At the desired time of sunrise or sunset, where the base of the triangle is formed by the intersection of the rays, the **line of sight to the Moon** <term>śaśidṛṣṭisūtra</term> is drawn. 

In the graphical representation, the difference between the squares of the solar distance <term>karṇa</term> and the observer's position results in a second intersection. By taking the difference of the two created segments and dividing by the solar distance, the representation is clarified. 

Following the first method of calculation for the **lunar distance** <term>śaśikarṇa</term>, the same is applied in the middle. The second intersection, when divided by the solar distance, reveals the base where the **Sun’s line of sight** <term>ravidṛṣṭisūtra</term> is drawn. This represents the heart of the planet in the graphical depiction, showing the difference between the squares of the planetary distances.

### The Calculation of True Visual Parallax

Let it be that from the root of the second segment of the arc, what is the **parallax** <term>lambana: the apparent displacement of a celestial object when viewed from different positions</term> in the visual center? This is determined through the first method of calculation and its variations. In this second method, when the calculation is performed, the first value is applied to the **Sun** <note>original: "arke"</note> from the perspective of the observer. Then, based on the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa: the distance from the observer to the planet</term>, and by mutually dividing the values obtained, the first result is established.

In the same way, the first value arises from the **zenith distance** <term>natāṃśa: the angular distance from the point directly overhead</term> of the Moon. In the second calculation of parallax, the zenith distance is used for the Sun. This distinction is essential: the first value exists in the **planetary sphere** <term>grahagola</term>, while the second belongs to the **lunar sphere** <term>candragola</term>. The union of the **line of sight** <term>dṛṣṭisūtra</term> and the **geocentric line** <term>garbhasūtra</term> is calculated by the difference between them. This **visual parallax** <term>dṛklambana</term> must be proven through calculation.

Therefore, all teachers have established the true positions here. All of this is explained so that those with subtle intellects can solve the differences between the two. If a discrepancy is found, one should not simply say "it is not so"; rather, the wise should investigate where the fault lies. Thus, the methods for calculating parallax have been spoken of by me. Generally, for the sake of worldly transactions, I have made the calculation of **True Parallax** <term>spaṣṭalambana</term> clear and accessible. 

<margin>The Conjunction of Sun and Moon</margin>

Otherwise, what is the use of that confusion where a small difference in the result is ignored? This has been stated here for the sake of determining the **conjunction of the Moon and Sun** <note>original: "candrārka yoga"—referring to an eclipse</note>, in order to establish the essence of the matter. In all methods of calculating visual parallax, the difference in the planet's position relative to the local horizon and the celestial equator is considered. Even in those calculations, the distance of the planet and my own observations are included. 

There, the sight-line of the planet is the key. The **parallax** <term>avalamba</term> is the segment to be solved. When the first segment is determined by the observer, the square root of the distance, divided by the square of the hypotenuse, results in the visible segment. Thus, when the difference between the two segments is multiplied by the parallax-sine and divided by the planetary hypotenuse, the result is the first value.

From that, the calculation follows the first method of velocity. What is gained by these various circles for the Sun? Through the calculation of the visual sines as stated by the experts, the second mentioned correction arises. Thus, due to the influence of the horizon, all **visual parallaxes** <term>dṛglambana</term> are determined by these fixed methods. They are most effectively produced by the second method. By refining the sequence from the horizon to the planet’s orbit, the center-line and the sight-line are always unified where the planet stands.

The first correction exists between the Moon and the Sun. At the time of their conjunction, it arises from the difference between their two primary parallaxes. In this way, the visual parallax is solved even at the moment when the **New Moon conjunction** <term>darśa</term> ends. At that time, the zenith distance of the Sun at sunrise is used to state the parallax. Through the power of the True Motion, the visual parallax is derived from the second calculation. By matching it with the first parallax, they are made equal. Thus, when the second correction is produced at the end of the New Moon, the zenith distances are calculated. These should be made into the first "True Shadows." The second value produced at the end of the New Moon is indeed the correction. In this way, at the end of the New Moon, the first and second corrections are established. If there is a difference in parallax here, it would mean there is only one type of eclipse for the **Sun** <note>original: "tigmarashmi"—literally 'the one with piercing rays'</note>.
