# The Mathematical Proof of Visual Parallax

Regarding the three times of observation and the deity of the light, first, without delay, one should understand the knowledge of the visual sphere <term>dṛkkṣatravicakra: the observational celestial circle</term> as it relates to the planetary sphere. This is calculated through the immersion in the "bath" of mathematical knowledge. 

### Proof through Visual Degrees

Now, the proof through visual degrees <note>original: "dṛglevenopapattiḥ"</note> is explained. The observer situated on the surface of the Earth <note>original: "bhūpṛṣṭhasthito draṣṭā"</note> does not see the planet—such as the **Sun**—through the center of the Earth. Instead, because of the observer's position on the surface, the line of sight is displaced. For this reason, due to the power of the visual displacement from the Earth's surface, one sees the **Sun** <note>original: "arkatattvam"</note> as if it were in a different position than it truly is at the center. 

<margin>
T. V.
p. 75
</margin>

From the line of the visual horizon <term>dṛkkṣitija</term> until the rising Sun, that which is established on the **Earth-sphere** <term>bhūgola</term> is the basis of visual perception. From the line of sight of the observer on the Earth's surface, which extends toward the North or elsewhere, if the observer is at the horizon, the visual line and the geocentric line do not coincide. To calculate the position of the Sun at the horizon within the **celestial sphere** <term>khagola</term>, one must consider the visual line from the observer’s specific location on the Earth's surface. At the Earth's center <note>original: "bhūkendra"</note>, the distance is considered the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>. 

The difference between the squares of the distance and the radius yields the base of our triangle. The distance from the Earth's center to the planet is the **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term>, and the Earth's radius is the **base** <term>bhuja</term>. The square root of the difference of their squares <note>a reference to the Pythagorean theorem used in Indian sine tables</note> is the **altitude** <term>koṭi</term>. 

In the case of the **Moon-sphere** <term>candragola</term>, the visual line extends to the lunar distance, which serves as the hypotenuse. The same principle of the base and altitude applies. The difference between the visual line of the Sun and the visual line of the Moon creates the interval between the two spheres. Here, the solar hypotenuse and the lunar hypotenuse are used to find the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>natāṃśajyā</term>, which acts as the base <term>bhuja</term>. Through this method, the **geocentric line** <term>garbhasūtra</term> is established as the altitude.

One should not doubt how this calculation is possible. Because of the similarity in the geometric configuration, there is no obstacle in achieving the mathematical result. Thus, by using the Earth's radius and the distance to the Sun, the parallax is calculated. When the Moon's distance is known, the same **sine of the zenith distance** is applied. This is the cause of the stability of the calculation for the rising and setting of the two luminaries.

### A Second Method of Proof

Now for the second method of proof regarding the horizon. The visual line passing through the horizon is considered. The distance from the center of the Earth to the **Solar-sphere** <term>arkagola</term> remains constant, equal to the Earth's radius. A second line is drawn from the Earth's center to the Sun, and a third line is also considered. Between these two lines within the solar sphere, a specific geometric area <unclear>ड्डू</unclear> is formed...

<summary>This page provides a geometric proof for parallax, explaining how the observer's position on the Earth's surface creates a right-angled triangle (Bhuja-Koṭi-Karṇa) that accounts for the difference between observed and geocentric planetary positions.</summary>
<keywords>parallax, Earth-sphere, Solar-sphere, hypotenuse, altitude, base, zenith distance, Kamalākara, Siddhāntatattvaviveka</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the geometric proof of parallax from page 75, moving from the general concept of visual displacement to the specific trigonometric derivation using the "bhuja" (side) and "koṭi" (perpendicular) of the observer's triangle.</meta>

Through the measure of the arc, the distance between the two deities <note>The Sun and Moon</note> is determined within the sphere. Thus, the measure of the **Moon's** position within its sphere is established. By this method, the result of the line originating from the center of the planet is obtained. There, the internal line from the center of the planet is calculated through the limb of the planet; this is how it is logically established. For those with clear intellects, the same reasoning applies to the line of sight <note>original: "dṛggata-sūtra"</note>. For a planet connected to the surface of the Earth, the visual lines are calculated through the sequence of the Earth's surface. 

In every respect, due to the power of the observer’s visual position, the reasoning for parallax <term>lambana: the difference in the apparent position of a celestial object when viewed from the surface vs. the center of the Earth</term> is established at the horizon, where the rising and setting occur. Even at those points of rising and setting, the logic of parallax remains consistent. Because of the different distances to the rays, the observer sees the planet through the visual line connected to the surface of the Earth; thus, the method of parallax is derived from the planet's position relative to the horizon. 

Through the power of the planet's motion, the positions are calculated. Now, according to the **first method**, the reasoning for the visual line and parallax is as follows: it extends from the center of the Earth to the limit of the planetary orbit.

---

In this construction, the **side** <term>bhuja: the horizontal leg of a right triangle</term> is the radius of the Earth. The **hypotenuse** <term>karṇa</term> is the distance of the **Sun** from the Earth's center. The square root of the difference of their squares <note>Applying the Pythagorean theorem: $\sqrt{hypotenuse^2 - side^2}$</note> is the **perpendicular** <term>koṭi</term>, which is the line of sight. Within the zenith circle <term>hṛdvṛtta</term>, this is not a different type of calculation. Here, from the center of the Earth to the end of the visual circle of the sphere, the distance is measured by the hypotenuse. That same Earth-radius is the "side." The square root of the difference of their squares is the perpendicular, which represents the segment of the Sun’s visual line within the sphere. 

When this is subtracted from the previous perpendicular, the remainder is the distance between the spheres of the observer and the Sun in the line of sight. Here, the "side" of these two hypotenuses is known as the parallax. The proportion is as follows: If, at the distance of the **Sun's** hypotenuse, the side is the Earth's radius, then what would it be at the distance calculated in yojanas? The resulting **sine of parallax** <note>original: "lambana-jyā"</note>, when multiplied by the radius and divided by the hypotenuse, gives the parallax in minutes of arc. 

Now, through a single calculation, the proof of visual parallax is established. From the Earth's center, the side is the measure of the Earth's radius. The line of sight is the base. Just as the distance from the Earth's center to the planet's circle is the hypotenuse, the segment of the line of sight is calculated. The difference between the two—the hypotenuse and the perpendicular—is the distance between the spheres of the observer and the Sun. From this, the proof of the planet's parallax is achieved. The parallax sine, calculated in yojanas, is converted into the visual parallax. 

Thus it is proven. Previously, the parallax was described at the time of rising and setting; it is greater there than when the planet is at the zenith. For a planet situated on the surface of the Earth, the parallax is at its maximum at the horizon and decreases as the planet rises higher. If one asks how this change occurs, listen: One should draw a circle representing the Earth with its radius from the center. A second zenith circle should be drawn with a radius corresponding to the planet's distance. Then, a third and fourth circle representing the planetary spheres should be drawn. All these should be understood by the intellect as being centered at the Earth's core. There, the line of the geocentric horizon is the horizontal line, and the vertical line points upward.

<margin>76</margin>

At the great intersection of the circles relative to the position of the **Sun** <note>original: "sūrya"</note>, at the time of the other horizon, the drawing should be executed with equal intervals throughout. Here, the visual line of the observer on the **Earth's surface horizon** <term>bhūpṛṣṭha-kṣitija: the horizon as seen by an observer standing on the surface</term> is established. At the center of that line, the surface of the Earth is marked with the point of the observer’s own visual line. From there, a line is drawn to the limit of the Sun. In this way, where it touches the direction of the Earth’s surface, the line for calculating the **rising and setting** <term>udayāsta-lekhana-sūtra</term> is established. If one considers the line originating from the **center of the sphere** <note>the Earth's core; original: "garbha-sūtra"</note>, the difference in this plotted position would not be visible. Therefore, from the horizon line of rising and setting, only the visual line of rising and setting itself should be discussed. If we consider the sphere, the drawing from the center-line to the limit of that line would be different.

As the Sun rises higher <note>increases in altitude</note>, it moves outward from the initial rising and setting line. Within the sphere, the distance from the center-line to the rising and setting line is measured as **32** <note>likely referring to the solar semi-diameter or a specific arc measurement in minutes</note>. This plotting of the rising and setting, which stays on the visual line, becomes greater than that. This **increase** <term>upacaya</term> in the plotting is observed up to the Earth’s surface horizon. From there, as the visual line moves toward the point of alignment, the plotting follows a specific rule. From the center of the Earth's surface, lines should be drawn to the limit of the Sun. Regarding the line at the Earth's surface, that is its rising and setting line. Within the sphere, the plotting of the rising and setting from the center-line would be even greater when situated on the observer's own visual line. 

One line from the Sun, extending to the visual line at the half-horizon of the Earth's surface, touches the Earth’s circumference in the direction of the surface; this becomes the rising and setting line. From that location, a single visual line is drawn from the observer’s center. The line drawn from the opposite side of that point is the line that achieves the plotting for the Earth’s surface. If, in the sphere, the plotting for the surface is taken from the center-line, then that which is added from the observer's own visual line will result in an even greater plotted value. 

In this way, when the Sun is on the visual line, this displacement in plotting reaches its **maximum** <note>this refers to horizontal parallax, which is greatest when the celestial body is on the horizon</note>. As the Sun moves further from that position, a single line of the Sun reaches the visual line at a higher point, which becomes the line that achieves the plotting for the Sun. If, in the sphere, there is plotting for the visual line from the center-line, and the visual line is situated lower than that, then a **decrease** <term>apacaya</term> in the plotting is observed. This occurs when the visual line touches the line of the Sun. This is exactly what was said regarding the three lines that achieve the plotting of the apparent horizon; the plotting of the Earth's surface will be equal to that. 

Furthermore, on the circle of the observer's own visual line, there is a line for plotting the rising and setting. On one's own visual line, that rising and setting plotting would not exist <note>meaning there is no displacement at the zenith</note>. Below that rising and setting line, the visual line moves downward, and thus the **decrease** <term>apacaya</term> continues from there. Thus, at the center-line <note>the vertical line to the zenith</note>, there is a total absence of visual plotting, which is accepted because of the alignment. Therefore, on the Earth's center-horizon, the rising and setting horizon is determined by the power of the visual rays. The rising and setting plotting occurs due to that specific state, but it does not exist at the zenith because of the visual rays. There, the plotting requires the point of the Earth's surface relative to the Sun.

<summary>This page explains the geometric variation in calculating a planet's rising and setting (parallax) by comparing the line of sight from the Earth's surface to a line from the Earth's center. <meta>This page continues the geometric proof of parallax (displacement), moving from the general theory of the observer's horizon to the specific calculations required for the Moon’s sphere. Through these methods, in the sphere of the **Moon** <note>original: "candrā gole"</note>, one should calculate the measurement for the **maximum plotting** <term>parama-lekhana: the maximum visual displacement or parallax</term>. This is done because that [displacement] which exists when viewed from the **Earth's center** <note>original: "bhū-gṛha," literally 'house of the earth'</note> is not the same as that which is produced from the Earth’s surface. Because of the observer's position, a "difference in plotting" <term>lekhanāntara</term> arises; this is well-known in this system. 

The successful calculation of the **parallax in latitude** <term>nati</term> is achieved by considering the visual intersection at the Earth’s center. However, for an observer on the **Earth's surface** <note>original: "bhū-pṛṣṭha"</note>, the movement appears different. At the time of rising and setting, the plotting of the visual line is considered the "maximum plotting." If we ask: "What is the difference when measured from the Earth's surface?" then that difference is known through the minutes of the **parallax in longitude** <term>lambana-nāḍikā</term> and the rising/setting positions. In all astronomical calculations, once the displacement relative to the Earth's surface is known, it is described through various diagrams. This effort in determining the position of the planet is called the "explanation of the sphere's residence" <note>original: "vāsanā-vivaraṇe gola-tattve"</note>. This cannot be understood by any other means than this familiarized circle of logic.

The reasoning for the plotting of the lines is as follows: just as the reasoning for the plotting of the **equinoctial shadow** <note>original: "ayana-lekha"</note> was stated, so too is the reasoning for this displacement. In the calculation of the eclipse, the displacement of the two [the Sun and Moon] is what creates the timing of the contact and separation. Because the visual orbit is slightly different from the calculated orbit due to the observer's height, we must apply a correction. This is essentially what is known in the world as the "union of sight" <note>original: "dṛg-darśana-yoga"</note>, since the time shown by the calculations must match the time seen by the eyes. Therefore, the corrected time of the conjunction is shown through the **true parallax** <term>sphuṭa-lambana</term>.

Now, the method for bringing about the calculation of this **true parallax** <term>sphuṭa-lambana</term> is described:

First, calculate the difference between the **nonagesimal** <term>tribhonalagna: the point of the ecliptic ninety degrees behind the rising point/ascendant</term> and the position of the planet. This is multiplied by the maximum parallax and divided by the **radius** <term>trijyā: the sine of 90 degrees</term>. 

Alternatively, to find the **parallax in latitude** <term>nati</term>: 
The square root of the difference between the squares of the **sine of the zenith distance** <term>drigjya</term> and the sine of the ecliptic's altitude at the nonagesimal provides the basis. Or, in the minutes of the parallax, the **sine of the latitude displacement** <term>nati-liptā</term> is the side of the triangle, and the visual parallax is the hypotenuse. From the difference of their squares, the **true parallax** <term>sphuṭa-lambana</term> is established. 

Furthermore, from the geometry of the "spotless sphere" <note>original: "vimala-gola-tattvataḥ"</note>, this calculation is examined. The three sides of the triangle are formed by the **cosine** <term>kotijiva</term> multiplied by the maximum multiplier and divided by the sine of the zenith distance. Thus, the displacement is divided into its components. The true parallax is then perfected by the multiplication of the sine of the latitude and the maximum displacement. In this way, the calculation of the **zenith distance sine** <term>drig-lambana-jya</term> is obtained through the radius, resulting in the desired **parallax in latitude** <term>nati</term>.

</margin>

The sine of the visual parallax <term>drg-levanajya: likely a variant of drg-lambana-jya, the sine of the parallax in longitude</term> of the **Sun** <note>original: "ravi"</note> is calculated through the sine of the zenith distance. From this, the "True Displacement" is derived. By taking the sine of the difference between the Sun's position and the three signs <note>90 degrees or the nonagesimal point</note>, the sine of the visual parallax is multiplied and then divided by the value of the midday circle. In the case of the **triple-lagna** <term>tri-lagna: the point of the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the rising point</term>, its visual motion <term>drggati</term> is calculated. That arc, divided by the sine, is the Sun's true displacement. Regarding the parallax in latitude <term>nati</term> and the visual displacement, the arc should be calculated at the point of deflection <term>viksepa: celestial latitude</term>. In this matter, the visual parallax and the latitude parallax are combined to find the true position. The sine of the visual motion <term>drggati-jya</term> is essential here; those who seek the true displacement of the eye must find the square root of the difference of their squares <note>a reference to the Pythagorean theorem applied to the components of parallax</note>, which results in the sine of the latitude parallax. 

On the surface of the spheres of the **Sun and Moon** <note>original: "suryacandrayaya-gola-prsthe"</note>, when they are deflected in different directions, one should not simply imagine them as points, but consider the visual line of the Sun. Through the hypotenuse of these two, the displacement described by the "visual heart" is explained, similar to the parallax in latitude. In this work, the visual displacement I have presented is corrected through the calculation of the arc. For the sake of clarity, at the time of the calculation, one should perform the visual displacement once, and then refine the "true deflection" as taught by the ancient masters <note>original: "muktam-adyaih"</note>. Alternatively, whatever visual displacement occurs, one should use that to find the calculation of the place of displacement at the desired time. In this way, the "True Displacement" <term>sphuta-levana</term> of the arc and the displacement itself is explained. 

Generally, the wise say that the visual displacement is produced by the power of the gnomon <term>shanku</term>. They state that the gnomon is not merely a physical shadow-caster but a representation of the arc in the circle of the stars <term>bha-mandala</term>. For the people of ancient times, the sine of the "true plotting" was considered the "base" <term>koti</term> of the visual sine; the wise have said that the "base-sine" of that parallax exists within the celestial sphere. By using these instruments, the displacement is made "true" or "corrected" <note>original: "sphuta"</note>. Because the celestial circle does not deviate from its path, the true sine of displacement, multiplied by the radius <term>trijya</term> and divided by the "base-sine" of the zenith distance, is considered the true displacement in the arc. This is known by those who understand the spheres. For the sake of one's own purpose, the "base-sine" of the displacement is multiplied by the radius and divided by the "base-sine" of the zenith; that which is subtracted from the arc of ninety degrees <note>the nonagesimal</note> becomes the true displacement. In this way, the true displacement in minutes and seconds is not divided by sixty, but by the difference in the speeds of the Sun and Moon to find the displacement in terms of **ghatikas** <term>ghatika: a unit of time equal to 24 minutes</term>. This should be understood as the visual motion of the field. Because of the difference between the two, a displacement arises in the circle; if there is no difference, then it is said to be the same for the Sun. By the rule of three, the distance between the southern and northern points of the intersection is found; that is the middle-lagna of the celestial circle. Because the solar eclipse is calculated from that, the displacement is understood through the zenith. This is the path of the scriptures. If one asks why the "True Motion" is not seen directly, it is because the "True Displacement" is calculated through the mean motion and the power of the gnomon's base-sine.

It emphasizes the correction of these values for the Sun and Moon's spheres to accurately predict astronomical events like eclipses in units of time (ghatikas).</summary>

It specifically addresses the geometry of the nonagesimal point and the calculation of the moment of conjunction for the Sun and Moon.</meta>

### On the Calculation of Visual Displacement

To achieve the perfection of the limbs of knowledge regarding the nonagesimal <term>vibhama lagna: the point on the ecliptic 90 degrees behind the rising point</term>, one must consider the various rising signs. Thus, the meaning of the **Sun’s** <note>original: "arka"</note> statement is this: when the two motions are clear on the celestial circle, they are located between the "arm" of the Sun and the center of the Earth. 

For the purpose of calculating the difference between the Sun and the meridian lagna, one must divide by seventy-two <note>The number 72 is often used in Siddhantic astronomy as a constant related to the radius of the Earth or the conversion of degrees to specific sine values</note> according to the nature of the visual circle. In that state of displacement, where the Sun is equal, the visual displacement <term>drg-lavana: a variant of lambana or parallax</term> is not found. However, due to the union of the visual lagna and the celestial visual lagna, the clear parallax is reached.

#### The Difference in Perspectives

This visual displacement is not of a single type; it varies due to the difference in visual perspective <term>drg-bheda: parallax</term>. From the difference between the two circles, the wise should understand that what is accepted as the "geocentric" <term>garbha: literally 'womb' or 'center'</term> position is what is suitable for practical calculations. Because of the lack of a single perspective, what is called "visual displacement" <term>vilevana</term> occurs. 

<margin>
Observations of the West
</margin>

Its quality exists in the displacement of the visual celestial circle because the **Sun** is seen from the west, and there the motion is separate. Furthermore, the wise have considered that this must be understood through the "geocentric line" <term>garbha-sutra: the line connecting the center of the Earth to the center of the planet</term>. 

The difference on the celestial sphere between the visual line and the geocentric line is the true displacement. Even without imagining this difference, the **Moon** <note>original: "shashi"</note>, relying on its own path, is increased by the visual lines of sight. Therefore, the Moon is displaced from the geocentric line by the visual line. 

### The Time of Conjunction

This should be understood by the learned regarding the fixed rotations. Therefore, in the nonagesimal, the visual displacement is greater than its own stated value. This is my explanation of the time of the "true observation" <term>sphuta-darsha-kala: the true moment of the New Moon or conjunction</term>. 

The interval seen in the visual displacement is the difference between the two lines originating from the center. There is no doubt that the visual line and the geocentric line must be considered, otherwise there will be an error in the calculation of the observation. 

<column-break/>

When the geocentric line and the visual line are described, they are the two lines by which the center of the Earth and the eye of the observer are connected. The distance between the **Sun's** sphere and the center of the Earth is traversed by these lines. The interval between these two lines—the geocentric and the visual—is the reason why there is a difference in the time of conjunction <term>yuti-kala</term>. 

At that time, the interval between the **Sun and Moon** <note>original: "ravindv-antara"</note> is the form of the hours <term>ghati</term> of the geocentric line. To establish the conjunction, the wise have said that the parallax must be applied. For the perfection of the union, the distance between the Sun and Moon must be corrected for parallax. This union of the visual and celestial lines belongs to the Sun. If the calculation of the difference and union is not performed, the goal will not be achieved. 

Where the visual ecliptic does not result in a single point, the Sun and Moon are said to be on a single visual line. This creates the center of the conjunction circle. Even then, due to the difference between the two, the entire calculation is known by the mathematicians. There too, the stated conjunction occurs in the celestial visual circle when the visual line is reached.

The text specifically focuses on the iterative method (approximation) required to find the "true visual" moment of an eclipse, which differs from the "geocentric" moment calculated from the center of the Earth.</meta>

T. V. <note>likely an abbreviation for Tattva-Viveka, the title of the work</note>
Chapter 78

</margin>

### On the True Moment of Visual Conjunction

Because the difference in altitude <note>original: "natantara", referring to the zenith distance</note> for the **Sun** and the **Moon** is not identical, the time of their conjunction as seen by an observer is different from the true geocentric time. Just as the latitude of the Moon is determined through its celestial deflection <note>original: "shara", literally 'arrow', representing the celestial latitude</note>, so too in the case of the visual conjunction, the calculation of the **true parallax in latitude** <term>nati: the difference between the geocentric and topographic latitude of a planet</term> is established. This parallax in latitude is considered of two types; it is calculated for the purpose of determining the exact nature of an eclipse. 

When the visual centers of the two bodies <note>the Sun and Moon</note> meet, their conjunction is said to be complete. If their celestial latitudes are in the same direction, the difference between them is taken; if in opposite directions, their sum is taken. This determines the north-south and east-west distance between the centers of the **Sun** and **Moon** on the celestial sphere. Because of their shifting motion, there is always a potential for visual displacement. Even when they appear to be at the same longitudinal point, their actual centers may be separated by their latitude.

#### The Iterative Calculation of Parallax

The wise should understand that the time of the eclipse occurs when there is an absence of east-west and north-south distance between the disks. This is known through the calculation of the **visual displacement** <term>lambana: parallax in longitude, which shifts the apparent time of conjunction</term>. If the visual displacement of the **Sun** and **Moon** were identical, there would be no change in the time of the conjunction; however, because their altitudes and distances from the zenith differ, their displacement is unequal. 

Therefore, one might ask: how is the moment of conjunction calculated? If the displacement is calculated based on the ecliptic zenith distance <term>drkksepa: the sine of the zenith distance of the nonagesimal</term>, the Moon’s altitude is not the same as the Sun's. One might wonder why the displacement is not calculated once and then finalized. The answer is that the displacement itself changes as the time changes. 

<note>This refers to a common problem in ancient astronomy: the parallax depends on the time, but the time of the event depends on the parallax. This requires an iterative "successive approximation" method.</note>

Thus, one must repeatedly calculate the displacement starting from the **end of the New Moon** <note>original: "darsha-virama", the moment the geocentric conjunction occurs</note>. By applying the visual displacement to that moment, a new time is reached. From that new time, the displacement must be calculated again. This process must be repeated <note>original: "asakrt", meaning 'more than once' or 'repeatedly'</note> until the calculated parallax becomes stable and equal. Only then is the **true visual time of the New Moon** <term>sphuta-darsha-kala</term> achieved. This corrected time, refined by the wise through the calculation of visual displacement, is the true moment when the observer sees the centers of the disks align.

<summary>This page explains the necessity of iterative calculations (successive approximation) to determine the "visual" moment of a solar eclipse, accounting for the different effects of parallax on the Sun and Moon.</summary>
<keywords>lambana (parallax), nati (latitude parallax), darsha-virama (end of New Moon), asakrt (iteration), Sun and Moon, conjunction, celestial latitude</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of parallax (lambana) from the perspective of the Siddhanta-Tattva-Viveka. The author, Kamalākara, is moving from general principles to a specific critique of existing methods and a geometric demonstration of how the observer’s line of sight interacts with the celestial spheres.</meta>

### The Geometrical Demonstration of Parallax

The true state of the celestial bodies on any given day is not immediately obvious to the senses because the **Sun** and **Moon** are perceived through the **line of sight** <term>drksutra: the straight line connecting the eye of the observer to the celestial body</term>. It is only through the application of **parallax** <term>lambana: the apparent displacement of an object when viewed from different positions</term> that the actual truth of their positions is established. This truth is always present, yet it is through the displacement caused by the observer's two eyes <note>original: "dvinethratah", likely referring to the binocular perspective or the shift from the Earth's center to the surface</note> that the infinitesimal differences in their proximity become apparent. While these differences might seem small, they are the very reason why a perfect conjunction may not be seen even for a single moment.

To achieve success in the calculation of an eclipse <note>original: "lavana-sadhya-siddhi", referring to the cutting or duration of the eclipse</note>, one must consider the practical conventions of the world. If the visible minutes of arc are brought into harmony through the three-fold calculation <note>likely referring to longitude, latitude, and parallax</note>, only then can the means of conjunction be considered accurate. In this matter, a definitive judgment must be made by one who truly understands the **principles of the sphere** <term>gola-tattva: the mathematical laws governing spherical astronomy</term>. The true time of parallax is defined as the interval between the **Sun** and the **Moon** when their positions are corrected for the observer's perspective.

#### A Critique of Previous Errors

Regarding the **true parallax** <gloss>sphuta-lambana</gloss> as stated by **Bhaskara** <note>Bhaskara II (12th century), a famous astronomer whose work the author often critiques</note>: those who have accepted his conclusions without scrutiny are often led into contradictions. Because of a confusion in their own intellect, what can such scholars achieve? They remain ignorant of the **nature of the terrestrial globe** <gloss>bhugola-anabhijnana</gloss> and the various types of parallax. Therefore, I shall now explain the underlying logic <gloss>vasana</gloss> according to the correct geometry of the sphere.

It is well known that "parallax" is the distance between the line of sight passing through the **Sun** and the line originating from the **center of the Earth**. Similarly, in the **Lunar sphere**, "visual parallax" is the interval between the **vertical circle** <term>drnmandala: the great circle passing through the zenith and the celestial body</term> and the line of sight from the Earth's center. 

I will now explain the method for calculating these two types of true parallax.

<insert>It is as follows:</insert> In the **Lunar sphere**, the circle of its orbit is imagined to be identical to the **ecliptic** <term>krantivritta: the apparent path of the Sun across the sky</term>. Then, one should imagine the poles of these circles. By the power of the observer’s position, when the line of sight from the Earth’s center is marked on the East-West vertical circle, it creates a specific geometry. In this same way, the point known as the **nonagesimal** <term>tribhonalagna: the point on the ecliptic ninety degrees behind the rising point (ascendant)</term> must necessarily be at the center of this arrangement. This is because from the rising and setting points of the zodiac, all points are at an equal distance of ninety degrees. 

In this vertical circle, the distance between the line of sight and the Earth’s center is determined by a distance of ninety degrees. By placing the degrees of that arc upon the ecliptic, and considering the perspective from the center, the arc of the **zenith distance** <gloss>nati-koti</gloss> is formed. This arc is similar to the declination, and its sine <gloss>jya</gloss> is used to calculate the precise position within the sphere...

<summary>Kamalākara critiques Bhaskara II's understanding of parallax and begins a technical geometric explanation of how the observer's line of sight and the Earth's center create the phenomenon of parallax.</summary>
